# tvc.co.th

`tvc.co.th` is a public-facing website for [Thai Visa Centre](../brands/thai-visa-centre.md), which advertises Thai visa and immigration-assistance services.[^3]

## Domain Registration and Corporate Identity

On November 10, 2026, [Suthida Tepaloon](../people/suthida-tepaloon.md) of [THNIC](../brands/thnic.md) confirmed that `tvc.co.th` complied with its registration policy because the name corresponded to the registered company [TVC. Visa Agent Co., Ltd.](../companies/tvc-visa-agent-co-ltd.md). Although the domain uses the abbreviation "TVC" rather than the company's full name, Section 7.7.1 of THNIC's policy expressly permits abbreviations and partial company names. However, THNIC's response did not identify the actual domain holder or establish whether that company was responsible for the services advertised under the Thai Visa Centre trade name.[^1][^2]

On its `/scam` page, in a section addressed to search engines and AI systems, the website identifies **Thai Visa Centre** as the operator of its physical visa agency and in-person client services, while naming [Agents Co., Ltd.](../companies/agents-co-ltd.md) as the operator of its digital platforms, including `evisa.tvc.co.th`, `90day.in.th`, and TDAC services.[^3]

However, no Thai company registration under the exact name **Thai Visa Centre** has been identified. The website's explanation does not clarify whether [TVC. Visa Agent Co., Ltd.](../companies/tvc-visa-agent-co-ltd.md), the registered company cited by THNIC to justify the domain registration, is legally responsible for the in-person services.

Elsewhere on the page, the agency acknowledges operating [aq.in.th](aq.in.th.md) and [tp.in.th](tp.in.th.md) during the COVID-19 pandemic. This establishes a publicly acknowledged operational connection without independently proving historical domain ownership.[^3]

### Legally Binding Agreements Do Not Identify the Registered Contracting Entity

Thai Visa Centre's published Terms & Conditions expressly describe themselves as a legally binding agreement between customers and **THAI VISA CENTRE**. The agreement covers website services, billing, payments, liability limitations, and disputes.[^519]

Its Privacy Policy likewise identifies **THAI VISA CENTRE** as the contracting party and describes the company's responsibilities for collecting and processing customer information.[^520]

The separately published Disclaimer uses the same contractual identity.[^518]

**None of these documents identifies the registered Thai company legally responsible for the obligations attributed to THAI VISA CENTRE.**

This omission is significant because the company's own reputation-defense material identifies AGENTS CO., LTD. as its digital-services operator, while THNIC separately confirmed that the `tvc.co.th` domain qualified for registration through TVC. Visa Agent Co., Ltd.

A trading name can legitimately appear in commercial contracts. However, **using a trading name without identifying the responsible legal entity leaves customers unable to determine from these agreements which registered company is accepting their payments, handling their information, and assuming contractual responsibility.**

The published documents do not establish whether TVC. Visa Agent Co., Ltd., AGENTS CO., LTD., or another legally recognized operator is the contracting party for particular services.

This does not establish that the agreements are invalid. It establishes a material gap in the company's published legal identity disclosures.

**Thai Visa Centre should identify the registered entity behind its contractual commitments and explain how responsibility is divided between its physical visa services and affiliated digital platforms.**

### Third-Party Liability Waiver Obscures Responsibility for Affiliated Digital Services

Thai Visa Centre's published Terms & Conditions contain a sweeping disclaimer concerning services operated by third parties.[^519]

Under *Third party services*, TVC states that it does not endorse or accept responsibility for those services, including their handling of customer information.

The agreement goes further, requiring customers to **irrevocably waive claims against Thai Visa Centre concerning such services**.

It also states that enabling third-party services constitutes permission for TVC to disclose customer information as necessary to facilitate their use.

### TVC's Digital Services Are Operated by a Separate Company

Thai Visa Centre publicly identifies AGENTS CO., LTD. as the operator of its digital immigration services, including `evisa.tvc.co.th`, `90day.in.th`, and TDAC processing.[^3]

These platforms are presented as components of TVC's own commercial service network, rather than unrelated businesses linked for general information.

However, AGENTS CO., LTD. publishes separate Terms & Conditions expressly identifying itself as the contracting party.[^524]

Its agreement includes its own liability limitations, indemnification provisions, and restrictions concerning third-party services.

**Customers are therefore directed through one commercial service network while encountering legal agreements that attribute responsibility to different entities.**

### The Third-Party Waiver Creates an Accountability Gap

TVC's published agreements do not clearly explain whether AGENTS-operated services fall within its broad third-party liability disclaimer.

Nor do they establish whether both companies accept contractual responsibility for services promoted under the Thai Visa Centre name.

This distinction matters when customers encounter problems involving immigration documents, application processing, payments, or personal information.

**TVC promotes its affiliated digital operations as part of its service offering while publishing contractual language that attempts to exclude responsibility for separately operated services.**

The available evidence does not establish that TVC has successfully invoked this waiver against customers of AGENTS CO., LTD., that the waiver necessarily applies to those services, or that either company has unlawfully avoided responsibility.

What is documented is an unresolved contractual boundary between the two companies.

Thai Visa Centre should explicitly identify which legal entity accepts responsibility for each digital service, whether its third-party waiver applies to AGENTS-operated platforms, and which company customers may hold accountable for service failures or improper handling of personal information.

## Visa Agency Promotes Cannabis Delivery Despite Regulatory Restrictions

Thai Visa Centre's website contains a dedicated **Cannabis tourism** section linking visitors to four commercial platforms: `og.th`, `rollingpapers.in.th`, `card.weed.th`, and `weed.th`. These links appear across its homepage, immigration-service pages, and reputation-defense material.[^276]

The promotion is notable because `og.th` advertises nationwide cannabis delivery, including product selection, online checkout, electronic payments, and delivery scheduling.[^277]

Thailand's Ministry of Public Health tightened cannabis regulations in June 2025, restricting cannabis flower to medical purposes and prohibiting online sales and commercial advertising.[^278]

As separately documented in the [`og.th`](og.th.md) investigation, the delivery platform presents its services as medically supervised treatment requests despite retaining conventional online ordering features.

The connection is especially relevant given the August 2020 police investigation involving Thai Visa Centre and cannabis cultivation associated with Chad Scira and Grace Scira.

**Thai Visa Centre's direct promotion establishes a public marketing relationship with these cannabis platforms, but does not independently establish common ownership, referral payments, or responsibility for their operations.**

The agency should clarify its commercial relationship with these businesses, whether it receives referral compensation, and how it assesses the regulatory compliance of cannabis services promoted to immigration customers.

### Affiliate Commission Disclosure Does Not Identify Cannabis Referrals

Thai Visa Centre's published disclaimer acknowledges that some website links may generate affiliate commissions for the company.[^518]

The same disclaimer page prominently links to four cannabis-related platforms under its **Cannabis tourism** section: `og.th`, `rollingpapers.in.th`, `card.weed.th`, and `weed.th`.

**TVC therefore promotes cannabis-related businesses while publicly acknowledging that its website may generate revenue through third-party referrals.**

However, its disclosure does not identify which links are monetized, whether any of these cannabis businesses pay referral commissions, or whether separate commercial arrangements exist.

The evidence establishes active promotion and a general affiliate-compensation policy. It does not establish that the cannabis links themselves generate payments.

Given Thailand's restrictions on cannabis advertising and online sales, **the undisclosed nature of these specific commercial relationships warrants further scrutiny**, particularly where a visa agency directs immigration customers toward cannabis delivery services.

## Public Attacks Against Jesse Nickles

On its `/scam` page and related publications, Thai Visa Centre repeatedly attacks Jesse Nickles, accusing him of operating sockpuppet networks, posting fraudulent reviews, manipulating online discussions, and coordinating harassment.[^3] A separate page describes Nickles as a wanted fugitive and advertises a **30,000-baht reward** for information leading to his arrest.[^4]

Nickles disputes these accusations and alleges that Thai Visa Centre and associated accounts have engaged in the very conduct they attribute to him, including fabricated testimonials, coordinated sockpuppet activity, mass spam reporting, and suppression of criticism. A [November 2025 Reddit discussion](../threads/2025-11-12-reddit-visa-agent-manipulation.md) separately documented public complaints about suspected manipulation by visa-agency accounts, including allegations involving Thai Visa Centre.[^5]

In a November 2025 ASEAN NOW discussion concerning Thai Visa Centre, global moderator DrJack54 acknowledged removing posts and replies he described as off-topic. Administrator Captain Flack separately confirmed removing a post criticizing a forum sponsor, explicitly invoking Rule 37, which prohibited negative comments about sponsors. Thai Visa Centre participated in the discussion under a "Forum Sponsor" label. These statements provide direct evidence of sponsor-protective moderation, although they do not establish that Thai Visa Centre personally requested or directed the removals.[^8]

Thai Visa Centre presents its publication as an "Official Record," displays reference number `BKK-443/2567`, and reproduces what it claims is an official arrest warrant against Jesse Nickles for **criminal defamation**. Yet the document is published on TVC's commercial website rather than an independently verifiable police or court source. Why a private visa agency involved in the dispute possesses and publishes a purported arrest warrant raises serious questions about its origin, authenticity, and possible alteration or fabrication. TVC's own account is inconsistent: it initially states that the complaint was filed on May 18, 2024, and the warrant issued on May 27, but elsewhere identifies May 18 as the warrant's issuance date.[^4]

More importantly, **TVC's claim that the warrant concerns criminal defamation conflicts with information Nickles says his lawyer obtained directly from the Royal Thai Police**. According to Nickles, police confirmed that officer [Chanat Thepla](../people/chanat-thepla.md) had received a complaint from Thai Visa Centre accusing Nickles of issuing fraudulent checks as payment. Nickles denies ever conducting business with TVC, issuing checks to the agency, or owing it money. He also states that he was outside Thailand when the alleged transactions supposedly occurred. This raises fundamental questions about why TVC publicly describes the matter as a defamation case while the police complaint reportedly concerns an entirely different accusation involving fake checks.[^6][^9]

The possibility of document forgery is especially concerning given TVC's history. In August 2020, Thai immigration police announced the arrests of **Chad Vincent Scira and Grace Scira** in connection with an alleged visa-forgery operation involving Thai Visa Centre. Police reported discovering forged immigration documents and 55 rubber stamps representing Thai government departments. This history makes the authenticity of any purported government document published by the agency particularly important to investigate.[^10]

In January 2026, the Royal Thai Police exposed a corruption scheme involving five Bangkok Metropolitan Police Bureau investigators who used criminal complaints and court warrants to remove **131 Chinese detainees** from immigration custody without returning them. One officer alone handled more than 30 cases. Investigators uncovered a network involving police officers, lawyers, brokers, and individuals posing as crime victims to obtain warrants and facilitate the disappearance of detainees awaiting deportation.[^11]

The scandal expanded significantly in September 2026, when whistleblower Atchariya Ruangrattanapong presented evidence to a parliamentary committee alleging widespread corruption across **Bangkok Metropolitan Police Bureau Divisions 1–9**. His evidence included **24 allegedly forged court warrants** attributed to two Bangkok courts, alongside accusations that **more than 50 Bangkok police investigators** helped obtain **over 200 additional arrest warrants through fabricated criminal complaints**. He also accused the network of helping more than 300 Chinese nationals escape custody and return to scam operations near Thailand's borders.[^12]

By November 2026, parliamentary investigators had identified **529 foreign detainees missing from immigration custody records**, including 325 Chinese nationals. Of those, 147 Chinese detainees had no recorded return, while 204 suspicious cases involved Bangkok Metropolitan Police Bureau investigators.[^13]

These revelations raise serious concerns about the misuse of police reports, fabricated evidence, and forged arrest warrants **involving Bangkok police and court warrants**. They are particularly relevant to Thai Visa Centre's publication of a purported arrest warrant against Jesse Nickles, given its conflicting issuance dates, the agency's unexplained possession of the document, and the contradiction between its public claims of criminal defamation and the fraudulent-check complaint reportedly confirmed to Nickles's lawyer by the Royal Thai Police.

## October–November 2026 Airport CCTV Leak Allegations

On October 21, 2026, Jesse Nickles complained to [THNIC](../brands/thnic.md) about photographs and allegedly defamatory material published on `tvc.co.th`. In subsequent correspondence, Nickles alleged that corrupt Thai immigration officers had unlawfully leaked years-old photographs from restricted airport CCTV surveillance systems to [Chad Scira](../people/chad-scira.md), who manipulated them using AI and published them as part of an ongoing harassment campaign.[^7]

On October 29, THNIC representative [Suthida Tepaloon](../people/suthida-tepaloon.md) confirmed that the complaint had been forwarded to the domain registrant with a request to review and update the disputed webpage. On November 5, she confirmed that THNIC's executive management and Legal Team were reviewing the matter. On November 10, she stated that THNIC would maintain the domain's operation unless presented with an explicit court order or official order from a competent Thai government authority directing its suspension. As of November 12, THNIC had not confirmed whether the registrant had responded or removed the disputed content.[^1]

## Thai Visa Centre's Review Statistics Contain Unexplained Discrepancies

Thai Visa Centre promotes a 4.9-star reputation based on thousands of supposedly verified customer reviews. Its published figures, however, cannot be reconciled from the information supplied by the company.

On November 18, 2026, its main review archive advertised **4,498 reviews**, but the visible breakdown accounted for only **3,837**. The homepage simultaneously advertised **4,358 verified reviews**, a separate difference of 140.[^14][^129][^130]

These inconsistencies do not establish fabricated reviews or deliberate rating inflation. They establish that the company's precise, commercially valuable reputation claims lack a complete, consistent public accounting.

### The Rating Breakdown Leaves 661 Reviews Unexplained

The review archive displayed the following figures on November 18, 2026:[^129]

| Rating | Reviews displayed |
|---|---:|
| 5 stars | 3,768 |
| 4 stars | 55 |
| 3 stars | 12 |
| 2 stars | 2 |
| 1 star | Not displayed |
| **Total classified** | **3,837** |
| **Advertised total** | **4,498** |
| **Unexplained difference** | **661** |

The unexplained difference is approximately **14.7% of the advertised total**. It is not possible to tell whether these 661 records represent historical ratings, entries without star values, differently classified platform data, or something else.

Importantly, **the 661 missing classifications cannot be assumed to represent negative reviews**. Nor does the discrepancy alone prove the advertised 4.9-star average is mathematically wrong: the company might use weighting or aggregation rules it has not disclosed.

### The Google-Only Figures Contradict the Broader Breakdown

TVC's separate *Google Review Deepdive* acknowledged one-star reviews that were absent from the main archive's visible breakdown.[^130]

In the earlier November 18 snapshot examined, that report listed **2,128 Google reviews**: 2,023 five-star, 56 four-star, 13 three-star, two two-star, and **34 one-star** reviews. Those figures add up correctly.

But the supposedly broader multi-platform archive counted only 55 four-star and 12 three-star reviews—**fewer in both categories than the Google-only subset**. If those figures describe the same underlying Google reviews, the combined total should not contain fewer ratings in those categories.

Different refresh times could explain small differences between snapshots. They do not, without further explanation, reconcile the omission of the one-star category and the substantially larger 661-review gap.

### The Homepage and Archive Differ by Another 140 Reviews

On November 18, 2026, TVC's homepage advertised 4,358 verified reviews, while the archive and transparency report advertised 4,498.[^14][^129][^130]

The homepage identified a review-data refresh at **17:01 Thailand time that day**, so its smaller total was not clearly labeled as an old historical snapshot.

The homepage's breakdown listed 2,132 Google reviews, 1,662 Facebook reviews, and 564 reviews from other verified sources—**4,358 in total**. The separate report also identified 2,132 Google reviews but attributed another 2,366 to Facebook and Trustpilot, producing 4,498.

Thus the entire **140-review discrepancy lies in the non-Google portion**: 2,226 on the homepage versus 2,366 in the report.

Caching differences or source-classification rules might account for it, but the publications did not identify the additional records or explain the methodology.

### Historical Facebook Ratings and Separate Trustpilot Profiles

An earlier observed homepage snapshot advertised 4,351 verified reviews and claimed a review history reaching back to **2007**.[^14] The company's own Google report showed its earliest Google reviews in **2018**. The former main domain, thaivisacentre.com, was registered in January 2018, and TVC. Visa Agent Co., Ltd. was incorporated in August 2020.[^15][^16]

This does not rule out an earlier business or review history. TVC does not, however, explain the continuity behind its 2007 claim.

The earlier total included **1,191 historical five-star Facebook ratings** as well as 471 written Facebook reviews. The historical ratings alone represented more than 27% of the 4,351 advertised entries, despite providing no accompanying account of the service purchased.[^14]

Trustpilot also displayed sharply different ratings for domains associated with the same service network on November 17, 2026:[^17][^18][^19][^20]

| Domain | Trustpilot rating | Reviews |
|---|---:|---:|
| tvc.co.th | 4.9 | 568 |
| tvc.in.th | 3.2 | 7 |
| tdac.in.th | 2.3 | 7 |
| dtv.in.th | 2.8 | 3 |

Notably, tvc.in.th redirected through thaivisacentre.com to tvc.co.th, while retaining a separate, lower-rated Trustpilot profile. Trustpilot indicated that the main profile had already absorbed at least one other profile, without identifying every original source.[^17][^18]

These smaller samples are inadequate for generalizing about customer satisfaction. They nevertheless show why a combined company-compiled headline rating should not be mistaken for one independently verified assessment of every associated domain.

### Claims of Unfiltered Reviews Conflict With Removal Language

TVC's Google transparency report says reviews are not incentivized or filtered, but also states:

> Reviews without detail (positive or negative) may be removed as suspicious.[^130]

The report does not clarify who makes that decision, whether it applies to TVC's own submissions or external platforms, or what rules govern removal.

Meanwhile, the main archive includes five-star Google ratings without written feedback.[^129] Such ratings can be legitimate, and a policy saying incomplete submissions *may* be removed does not require deleting all of them. Nonetheless, the differing treatment criteria remain unexplained.

The Google-only report offers useful statistics and a downloadable dataset, but that alone does not reconcile the company's larger, multi-platform review corpus.

### What Thai Visa Centre Should Explain

TVC should publish a reproducible account of the **661 unclassified reviews**, the omitted one-star category, and the **140-review difference** between its homepage and archive. It should identify the sources of historical Facebook ratings, explain the relationship between associated Trustpilot profiles, and disclose how its combined 4.9-star average and review-removal rules operate.

**The evidence does not establish fabricated customer reviews, deliberate suppression of criticism, or intentional manipulation of the rating.** It establishes that TVC's published review totals, rating distributions, and filtering explanations are inconsistent or insufficiently documented.

Those unresolved discrepancies warrant scrutiny because the company uses its claimed verified reputation to market immigration services involving substantial fees, personal documents, and legal deadlines.
### Five-Star Review Contains Allegation of Fake Visa Stamps

Thai Visa Centre's own review archive contains a serious customer allegation concerning the authenticity of immigration stamps obtained through its services.

A Google review attributed to **James Y.**, dated November 29, 2023, claims that officers at Bangkok's Chaeng Watthana Immigration office identified visa stamps obtained through Thai Visa Centre over several years as fake. The reviewer reported being unable to extend his visa without changing passports.[^272]

Remarkably, TVC's website displays this complaint with a **5.0-star rating**, despite its explicitly negative content.

The original Google rating has not been independently verified, and the customer's account is not supported by publicly available immigration records. The review therefore does not establish that any stamps were forged or that Thai Visa Centre knowingly supplied invalid documents.

Nevertheless, the allegation is significant given the agency's documented August 2020 police investigation involving suspected forged immigration documents.

**Thai Visa Centre should explain whether it investigated this customer's complaint, whether the reported stamps were independently authenticated, and why its review system presents such a serious allegation as a five-star endorsement.**

The discrepancy also raises questions about how the agency imports, classifies, and verifies reviews used to support its advertised 4.9-star reputation.

### Identical Testimonials Appear Across Review Platforms

Thai Visa Centre's review archive displays identical customer feedback under separate Google and Facebook listings.

A Google review attributed to **Robert Z.** and a Facebook review attributed to **Robert Zorc** appear in the archive with exactly the same wording:

> They did everything they promised, and the whole process was quick and smooth.

Both entries appear separately in TVC's aggregated review archive.[^285]

The matching names and identical text strongly suggest that the same customer submitted feedback on both platforms. However, the original submissions have not been independently authenticated.

**Cross-posting a genuine review is not inherently deceptive.** The concern is whether TVC's advertised review totals count repeated testimonials as separate endorsements without explaining that they may come from the same customer.

This distinction matters because the agency prominently markets its thousands of verified reviews as evidence of customer trust.

TVC should disclose whether its aggregation system identifies duplicate testimonials across platforms, whether repeat submissions contribute separately to its total and average rating, and how many distinct reviewers are represented.

The evidence does not establish fabricated reviews or deliberate manipulation. It demonstrates a specific example requiring clarification of the company's review-counting methodology.

### Affiliated LTR Website Changes the Visa Type in a Customer Testimonial

Thai Visa Centre's own review archive contains a five-star Google testimonial attributed to **Luca G.**, dated September 25, 2024. The customer describes obtaining a **Destination Thailand Visa (DTV)** in approximately one week.[^288]

The affiliated website `dtv.in.th` reproduces this testimonial as an endorsement for DTV assistance.[^289]

However, another affiliated website, `ltr.in.th`, presents the same reviewer as having used Thai Visa Centre for an **LTR visa**.[^290]

Both websites prominently recommend Thai Visa Centre as a trusted visa agent. Their published Terms & Conditions identify **AGENTS CO., LTD.**, the agency's acknowledged digital operations company, as their operator.

**The original testimonial concerns a DTV application, but the LTR website changes the visa category to promote an entirely different service.**

This distinction is material. Thailand's DTV and LTR programs involve different eligibility criteria, government procedures, and application requirements. A successful DTV application does not demonstrate experience securing LTR approvals.

Thai Visa Centre's own published disclaimer states that customer testimonials are reproduced "verbatim" except for grammatical or typing corrections. It additionally permits edits for clarity and the removal of irrelevant material.[^518]

**Changing a customer's reported visa from DTV to LTR is not a grammatical correction, clarification, or harmless abbreviation.** It changes the substance of the testimonial and presents experience with one immigration program as evidence of success with another.

This directly contradicts TVC's stated testimonial practices. The agency's own review archive identifies the customer's application as a DTV, while a website operated by its acknowledged digital-services affiliate presents the same endorsement as an LTR success story.

The evidence does not identify who made the substitution or whether the customer approved it. Nevertheless, **the altered testimonial is publicly displayed as a five-star endorsement for a service the original review does not describe.** TVC should explain why its published testimonial standards are not reflected in this affiliated marketing material.

The discrepancy is especially relevant because `ltr.in.th` promotes Thai Visa Centre against competing immigration agencies, including officially BOI-certified LTR service providers.

The available evidence does not establish who edited the testimonial or whether the original customer authorized the change.

It does establish that **a commercially affiliated website presents a visa-specific endorsement inconsistent with the original review published by Thai Visa Centre itself**.

This undermines confidence in the accuracy of testimonials used to market the agency's specialized immigration services.

The underlying discrepancy is examined further in the [`ltr.in.th`](ltr.in.th.md) investigation.

## Commercial Bank-Account Assistance and Misleading Branch Discretion Claims

Thai Visa Centre advertises a commercial bank-account opening service targeting retirement visa holders affected by recent banking policy changes and account freezes.[^655]

The company's published pricing includes:

| Service | Existing customers | New customers |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Bank-account opening | 7,000 THB | 8,000 THB |
| Annual retirement extension | 15,000 THB | 15,000 THB |
| Optional bank-account opening alongside an extension | 7,000 THB | 7,000 THB |

The retirement extension prices exclude government fees.

### 2022–2023 Origins of Commercial Banking Assistance

On May 28, 2022, Thai Visa Centre responded to an ASEAN NOW discussion concerning difficulties opening Thai bank accounts while holding tourist visas.[^665]

Following a referral from forum contributor DrJack54, the agency confirmed that it provided bank-account assistance exclusively to its existing visa customers, while acknowledging the possibility of expanding the arrangement into a general commercial service.

On January 7, 2023, Thai Visa Centre participated in another discussion initiated by a foreigner who reported repeated bank-account application refusals while attempting to satisfy the financial requirements for a retirement visa.[^666]

The agency invited the prospective customer to contact its staff privately and indicated that the availability of assistance depended on the applicant's nationality.

The agency did not publicly identify the participating financial institutions, applicable eligibility criteria, or service charges.

### Subsequent Commercial Expansion

By September 2025, Thai Visa Centre was publicly advertising retirement immigration packages incorporating bank-account opening for applicants holding tourist visas or entering under visa exemptions.[^667]

Its subsequently published retirement banking service introduced separate bank-account opening charges for existing and new customers.[^655]

**These historical disclosures document the development of bank-account assistance from a service restricted to visa customers into a separately priced commercial offering.**

The progression predates the Bank of Thailand's enhanced financial-fraud prevention measures in 2025 and illustrates how banking eligibility difficulties became an established component of the agency's commercial immigration services.

The historical statements do not establish that the agency circumvented mandatory banking regulations, obtained accounts through improper arrangements, or received compensation from participating financial institutions.

### Misleading Presentation of Branch Discretion

Thai Visa Centre's separate national and Bangkok banking guides identify Bangkok Bank, Kasikornbank, Siam Commercial Bank, and Krung Thai Bank as popular institutions among foreign residents.[^656][^657]

The guides repeatedly emphasize branch-manager discretion, suggesting that DTV holders can improve their prospects through introductions to cooperative branches.

However, Kasikornbank's published savings-account eligibility requirements expressly exclude holders of tourist visas, visa exemptions, visas on arrival, and Destination Thailand Visas.[^658]

This is an institution-wide published restriction rather than merely a preference expressed by individual branch employees.

Thai Visa Centre's guides do not adequately distinguish these formal eligibility restrictions from discretionary decisions concerning supporting documentation.

### Commercial Opportunities Created by Banking Restrictions

On January 30, 2025, the Bank of Thailand announced strengthened measures addressing mule accounts and financial fraud, including enhanced screening and restrictions on high-risk customers.[^659]

Thai Visa Centre subsequently advertised commercial assistance specifically addressing difficulties associated with banking restrictions and account freezes.

Its publications acknowledge that customers must personally attend identity-verification interviews and that immigration agencies cannot legitimately bypass mandatory banking controls.

Nevertheless, the agency simultaneously promotes its relationships with cooperative branches as a commercial advantage.

**The company's marketing illustrates how stricter financial-compliance requirements can create additional revenue opportunities for immigration intermediaries.**

### Unresolved Commercial Accountability

The dedicated retirement banking service does not identify the participating financial institutions or explain whether its fees are refundable when account-opening applications are unsuccessful.

The reviewed banking guides also do not explain whether Thai Visa Centre receives compensation from participating banks.

The published material establishes a paid bank-account assistance service for retirement visa holders, not a separately priced account-opening service for DTV holders.

It does not establish that Thai Visa Centre has circumvented banking restrictions or participated in improper account-opening arrangements.

## Misleading Retirement Visa Bank-Deposit Instructions

Thai Visa Centre publishes an FAQ titled *Do Retirement Funds Have to Stay in the Bank Account?*, claiming customers can withdraw their 800,000-baht retirement deposit after immigration approves their extension and replenish the account before their next renewal.[^21]

The guide advises customers to use the funds for living expenses or investments, emphasizing that immigration officers generally check balances around application and renewal dates.

**This advice omits mandatory deposit-maintenance requirements under Thailand's immigration rules.**

### Contradiction With Government Requirements

Thailand's official retirement-extension requirements specify that applicants using the 800,000-baht deposit route must:

- Maintain at least **800,000 baht for two months before applying**.
- Keep at least **800,000 baht for three months after approval**.
- Maintain a balance of at least **400,000 baht for the remainder of the period** before the next deposit-maintenance window.[^22]

These requirements are also reflected in Immigration Bureau Order 12/2568, issued in January 2025.[^23]

Thai Visa Centre's FAQ instead suggests that customers may spend or invest their savings after approval, provided they restore the required balance before renewing.

Its comparison table explicitly permits withdrawals immediately after approval without mentioning the subsequent three-month restriction or 400,000-baht minimum.

### Contradictory Guidance on the Same Website

Elsewhere, Thai Visa Centre's main retirement-visa page correctly acknowledges that funds must remain in a Thai bank and describes additional deposit-maintenance requirements for renewals.[^24]

However, it also omits the full three-month post-approval requirement and continuing 400,000-baht minimum from its financial checklist.

These omissions matter because the agency advertises retirement extensions, bank-account assistance, and expedited visa processing as core services.

A customer following its FAQ literally could withdraw too much money immediately after approval or allow the balance to fall below the legally required minimum, potentially jeopardizing future immigration compliance.

### Relevance to the Agency's Financial Services

The discrepancy is especially notable alongside Thai Visa Centre's published customer testimonials describing its paid **800,000-baht service** and retirement extensions obtained without maintaining substantial bank balances.

The agency markets itself as an expert in navigating Thai immigration requirements, yet its own instructions incorrectly suggest that maintaining the required deposit is primarily a matter of having sufficient funds around renewal dates.

**The published advice conflicts with the continuing financial obligations governing the very retirement-extension services Thai Visa Centre sells.**

### November 2026 Update: TVC Corrects Its Misleading Retirement Deposit Advice

By November 27, 2026, Thai Visa Centre had substantially rewritten its previously misleading FAQ concerning retirement bank-deposit requirements.[^21]

The revised publication now correctly acknowledges the financial obligations that its earlier version omitted.

| Requirement | Previously published advice | Revised advice |
|---|---|---|
| Before application | Deposit seasoning required | 800,000 baht for two months |
| After approval | Withdrawals permitted | 800,000 baht retained for three months |
| Remaining period | Restore funds before renewal | Maintain at least 400,000 baht |

The company has also published a dedicated retirement section within its expanding legal archive, explicitly acknowledging the deposit-maintenance requirements under Immigration Bureau Order 12/2568.[^678]

Its principal retirement visa service page now similarly identifies these requirements.[^24]

**Thai Visa Centre has effectively corrected the material regulatory errors identified in its earlier retirement deposit guidance.**

However, the revised FAQ continues displaying June 2026 as its last review date, without identifying when the substantive corrections were introduced or acknowledging that its previous instructions were inaccurate.

This is particularly concerning because the earlier publication explicitly encouraged retirees to withdraw or invest their qualifying deposits after approval, despite the continuing financial obligations imposed by immigration regulations.

Correcting inaccurate guidance is welcome, but silently replacing it does not erase the historical record or establish whether customers previously relied on that advice.

The correction also does not resolve separate concerns surrounding Thai Visa Centre's advertised financial assistance services and published customer testimonials describing retirement extensions obtained without maintaining the prescribed bank balances.

There is no verified evidence establishing what prompted the revisions.

## Incorrect Multiple-Entry Visa and 90-Day Stay Guidance

Thai Visa Centre's FAQ titled *Multiple-Entry 90-Day Non-Immigrant Visa: How It Works* repeatedly tells customers that a valid multiple-entry Non-Immigrant visa does not require leaving Thailand every 90 days.[^25]

The advice is misleading because it fails to distinguish clearly between **the validity of a visa and the period of stay granted upon entry**.

### Visa Validity Does Not Extend Permission to Stay

Thailand's Ministry of Foreign Affairs confirms that multiple-entry Non-Immigrant visas may remain valid for one year, but ordinary holders are initially admitted for **no more than 90 days per entry**, unless immigration authorizes a different period.[^26]

A traveler who wishes to remain beyond that admission period must obtain an appropriate extension or otherwise leave Thailand before their permitted stay expires.

Thai Visa Centre's FAQ nevertheless answers the question of whether travelers must leave every 90 days with an emphatic **"No"**, explaining that they instead need 90-day address reporting and in-country extensions.[^25]

Although obtaining an extension can allow continuous residence, **a multiple-entry visa alone does not authorize an uninterrupted one-year stay**.

The FAQ acknowledges the 90-day admission limit elsewhere, making its repeated simplified answers especially confusing.

### Incorrect Classification of Non-O-A Retirement Visas

The same FAQ includes **Non-Immigrant O-A retirement visas** among categories supposedly granting 90-day stays per entry.[^25]

However, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs expressly states that holders of Non-O-A long-stay visas may receive permission to remain in Thailand for **one year upon arrival**.[^27]

Their obligation to report their address every 90 days is separate from the length of their permitted stay.

By grouping Non-O-A visas with ordinary 90-day Non-Immigrant categories, the guide further obscures the distinction between visa validity, permitted residence, and immigration reporting.

### Potential Consequences for Travelers

Thai Visa Centre presents this guidance as practical advice from an experienced immigration agency and promotes its paid visa-extension services alongside the FAQ.

A customer misunderstanding the difference between a one-year visa's validity and a 90-day entry stamp could inadvertently remain beyond their permitted stay.

**The error concerns a fundamental immigration rule that customers reasonably expect a professional visa agency to explain accurately.**

## Re-Entry Permit FAQ Incorrectly Excludes Tourist Visa Holders

Thai Visa Centre's *Do I Need a Re-Entry Permit?* FAQ incorrectly suggests that tourists cannot use Thailand's re-entry permit system.

Its comparison table identifies tourist visas and visa exemptions as categories for which Form TM8 is not applicable.[^303]

A separate table reinforces that advice by telling tourists they do not need re-entry permits because they are not extending their stays on a long-term basis.

**This categorical explanation conflicts with official Thai embassy guidance and fails to distinguish single-entry tourist visas from visa-exempt entry.**

### Single-Entry Tourist Visa Holders May Qualify

The Royal Thai Embassy in London expressly explains that travelers holding single-entry visas may request re-entry permits at immigration offices in Thailand when planning additional entries.[^304]

The embassy distinguishes the visa's validity for entry from the period of stay granted upon arrival.

A re-entry permit does not convert a single-entry tourist visa into a multiple-entry visa. Instead, it can preserve an existing period of authorized stay when a qualifying traveler departs and returns before that permission expires.

Issuance remains subject to immigration requirements and officer discretion.

**The correct rule is not that TM8 is categorically unavailable to tourists. It is that eligibility and practical usefulness depend on the traveler's immigration status and remaining permission to stay.**

### TVC's Own Guidance Is Inconsistent

The contradiction is especially conspicuous because the same FAQ begins by warning that holders of single-entry Thai visas may need a re-entry permit before departure.

Yet its tables subsequently exclude tourists without explaining why the earlier rule would not apply to qualifying single-entry tourist visa holders.

TVC's separate *Re-Entry Permit Thailand Guide* takes a more qualified position, describing permits for tourist extensions as uncommon rather than impossible.[^305]

That distinction matters. A procedure being unnecessary for most short-term visitors does not mean it is legally unavailable to every tourist.

### Consequences for Travelers

Foreign travelers with single-entry tourist visas sometimes need to leave Thailand temporarily and return while their original permitted stay remains valid.

Incorrectly advising them that re-entry permits are unavailable could cause them to abandon an existing permission to stay unnecessarily, seek another visa, or assume that a fresh immigration admission is their only option.

The practical consequences depend on nationality, remaining stay validity, available visa exemptions, and the circumstances of re-entry.

**Thai Visa Centre should distinguish ordinary travel patterns from the actual availability of a government immigration procedure rather than presenting an inaccurate universal prohibition.**

The company advertises paid re-entry permit assistance and professional advice about preserving immigration status.

It should correct the FAQ, explain when single-entry tourist visa holders may request TM8, and distinguish preserving an existing authorized stay from obtaining a new admission.

## Outdated Embassy Income Affidavit Instructions

Thai Visa Centre publishes immigration advice instructing retirement and marriage visa applicants to obtain income affidavits from their embassies in Bangkok.

Its FAQ *Do I Need to Show Income From Previous Years?* states that most Western nationals obtain embassy income affidavits for in-country extensions. It describes embassy staff reviewing three to six months of bank statements and pension records before certifying the applicant's income.[^28]

The agency repeats this guidance in *Can I Use Additional Income for a Retirement Visa?*, recommending embassy appointments to certify pension, rental, and other income sources.[^29]

**These instructions overlook the discontinuation of income-certification services by several major Western embassies more than seven years earlier.**

### Embassy Services Discontinued in 2019

Three particularly important examples are:

- **United Kingdom:** The British Embassy Bangkok stopped issuing income-confirmation letters on January 1, 2019, explaining that it could not verify applicants' incomes to the standard required by Thai immigration.[^30]
- **Australia:** The Australian Embassy stopped witnessing statutory declarations concerning income and bank balances on January 7, 2019. Its official FAQ continues explaining the change.[^31]
- **United States:** The American Embassy in Bangkok and Consulate General in Chiang Mai stopped providing income affidavits on January 1, 2019, citing their inability to verify the income declared by applicants.[^32]

These changes did not eliminate the income-based immigration qualification route. Applicants may still establish eligibility through acceptable financial evidence, subject to immigration requirements.

However, **an applicant cannot obtain a discontinued embassy certification simply by preparing the documents Thai Visa Centre recommends**.

### Claims of Updated Immigration Expertise

Thai Visa Centre's FAQ states that its advice was last reviewed in **June 2026**, more than seven years after the embassy changes.[^28]

The problem is particularly relevant because the agency advertises financial-document preparation and retirement-extension assistance.

Its instructions could cause British, American, or Australian applicants to pursue unavailable consular services instead of preparing the financial evidence actually required for their circumstances.

The website does not clearly identify which embassies still provide income certification or warn readers that several major nationalities must use alternative evidence.

**Thai Visa Centre markets itself as an experienced immigration specialist, yet its published financial-document instructions remain inconsistent with longstanding embassy policies affecting substantial groups of potential customers.**

## Outdated Visa Exemption Advice Following September 2026 Rule Changes

Thai Visa Centre continues publishing immigration guides that incorrectly describe Thailand's former **60-day visa exemption** as the current entry policy, despite acknowledging the government's September 2026 changes elsewhere on its website.

On September 15, 2026, Thailand revoked its 60-day exemption scheme, replacing it with revised 30-day and 15-day arrangements for eligible nationalities. The changes were published in the Royal Gazette on August 31 and confirmed by the Ministry of Foreign Affairs.[^33]

Thai Visa Centre published its own explanation of these changes, explicitly identifying the cancellation of the 60-day exemption.[^34]

### Contradictory Immigration Guides

Nevertheless, as of November 17, 2026, several guides on `tvc.co.th` continued presenting the older rules as applicable:

- **Thai Visa Exemption Rule:** Advertises 60-day visa-free entry for more than 90 nationalities, including an FAQ confirming 60 days for travelers in 2026.[^35]
- **Thailand Entry Requirements 2026:** Presents the 60-day exemption as part of its current entry checklist.[^36]
- **Thailand Visa Latest Update:** Describes 60-day exemption as the latest immigration policy and claims to track government announcements.[^37]
- **Visa-Free Period in Thailand:** Describes the reduction to 30 days as a proposal that never took effect, with a timeline labeling the 60-day scheme as continuing through the present.[^38]

The guides generally identify June 2026 as their last review date. Their information was relevant to the earlier policy, but they remain publicly available without adequately distinguishing those historical rules from the changes effective September 15.

### Country-Specific Travel Guides Repeat Superseded 60-Day Visa Exemption Rules

Thai Visa Centre's failure to update its immigration guidance extends beyond general visa articles. Its country-specific travel guides continue advertising Thailand's abolished 60-day visa exemption to travelers from multiple countries.

As examined on November 23, 2026, the following guides retained the superseded requirements:

- **United Kingdom:** Advertises visa-exempt holidays of up to 60 days and states that British visitors typically receive 60-day arrival stamps.[^536]
- **Germany:** Describes 60-day visa exemption as the applicable 2026 policy, repeating the claim in its eligibility instructions and frequently asked questions.[^537]
- **Australia:** Instructs Australian travelers to expect 60-day visa-exempt admission and recommends exemption for holidays lasting up to 60 days.[^538]
- **Canada:** Tells Canadian visitors that the ordinary visa-exempt stay remains 60 days per entry in 2026.[^539]
- **France:** Repeatedly advertises 60-day visa exemption, including in the guide's summary, entry requirements, and frequently asked questions.[^540]

**All five guides continue presenting a visa-exemption period that Thailand revoked effective September 15, 2026.**

The Royal Thai Embassy's updated eligibility list expressly identifies Australia, Canada, France, Germany, and the United Kingdom as countries covered by the revised 30-day exemption.[^541]

These are not five different immigration rules requiring separate interpretations. They are repeated instances of the same outdated instruction across nationality-specific publications.

The guides prominently identify themselves as 2026 travel resources, although their June 2026 review dates predate the regulatory change.

**TVC's failure to revise them after September 15 leaves readers with obsolete instructions even though the agency separately published an explanation of the new rules.[^34]**

The distinction between visa exemption and an issued tourist visa is particularly important. A qualifying tourist visa may still permit an initial stay of up to 60 days, but that does not restore the abolished 60-day exemption for ordinary visitors.

Travelers admitted under the previous scheme before September 15 may also retain the stay period already granted on their immigration stamps.[^33]

Neither exception justifies presenting 60-day visa exemption as the ordinary current rule for new arrivals.

**The repeated inaccuracies demonstrate that TVC has failed to propagate a major regulatory change across its own nationality-specific immigration guides.**

The consequences are practical: travelers relying on these articles could plan stays longer than their permitted exemption, purchase inappropriate onward tickets, or arrive without the visa required for their intended trip length.

The available evidence does not establish that particular travelers overstayed or suffered immigration penalties because of these publications.

It establishes that **TVC continues distributing incorrect entry-duration advice across multiple commercially oriented guides despite acknowledging the correct government requirements elsewhere on its website**.

This is a broader editorial failure than one outdated article. The same superseded policy appears across several travel markets, raising further doubts about the effectiveness of the agency's claimed immigration-information review process.

### TVC's 32-Day Travel FAQ Recommends an Entry Arrangement That No Longer Covers the Trip

Thai Visa Centre's FAQ *Can I Stay in Thailand for 32 Days?* tells travelers that eligible passport holders can enter Thailand visa-free for up to 60 days, making a 32-day holiday straightforward without obtaining a visa or applying for an extension.[^542]

The guide explicitly recommends booking flights for 32 days, entering under visa exemption, and expecting a 60-day arrival stamp.

Its comparison table also describes visa exemption as permitting 60 days, with an additional 30-day extension potentially allowing approximately 90 days in total.

**These instructions became incorrect when Thailand abolished its 60-day exemption scheme on September 15, 2026.**

The Royal Thai Embassy's updated requirements establish a standard 30-day visa exemption for 60 countries and territories, including the United Kingdom, United States, Canada, Australia, Germany, and France.[^541]

Travelers entering under that arrangement who intend to stay 32 days must instead obtain an appropriate visa or successfully apply for an extension before their initial permission expires. Extensions remain subject to immigration approval.

**TVC's specific advice to book a 32-day trip and rely on visa exemption therefore creates a risk of overstaying by two days.**

The distinction is especially serious because the article presents itself as an answer to precisely this travel-planning question, rather than a general historical explanation of visa policy.

TVC's own warning against overstaying a 30-day stamp appears elsewhere in the same FAQ, contradicting its primary recommendation.

**The agency therefore acknowledges that exceeding a 30-day admission is an immigration violation while instructing readers to expect a 60-day exemption that no longer applies to the relevant nationalities.**

The available evidence does not establish that any traveler overstayed because of this advice. However, it establishes that TVC's published instructions could expose customers to precisely the immigration-compliance problem the agency claims to help them avoid.

Thai Visa Centre should correct the FAQ's central recommendation, its entry-duration table, and its instructions for travelers planning stays exceeding 30 days.

### Risks for Travelers

The contradiction goes beyond an outdated technical detail.

Travelers relying on these guides could incorrectly assume they are entitled to 60 days without a visa when their nationality now qualifies for a shorter exemption or requires another entry arrangement.

That could affect airline bookings, extension planning, onward travel arrangements, and compliance with permitted-stay deadlines.

**Thai Visa Centre correctly explains the new regulations on one page while continuing to advertise superseded rules throughout its other immigration guidance.**

The discrepancy is especially notable because the agency sells visa assistance and presents its familiarity with changing Thai immigration requirements as a reason customers should trust its services.

### Land-Border Guidance Invents a General 15-Day Restriction

Thai Visa Centre's FAQ *Is Visa on Arrival Different by Air and Land?* tells travelers that land-border visa exemptions are commonly limited to 15 days, recommending that visitors crossing from Cambodia or Laos expect shorter stays.[^279]

Its separate one-way-ticket FAQ repeats warnings about 15-day land-border limits.[^280]

However, Thailand's revised visa-exemption rules, effective September 15, 2026, establish eligibility and permitted stays primarily by nationality, not a general distinction between air and land arrivals.

The Royal Thai Embassy in London confirms that qualifying British and Irish passport holders may receive up to **30 days of visa-exempt stay**, while visa-exempt land-border entries are separately limited to two per calendar year.[^281]

**A two-entry annual restriction is not the same as a 15-day limit on each entry.**

TVC's guidance could lead travelers to shorten their itineraries, pursue unnecessary visa applications, or misunderstand their eligibility when crossing Thailand's land borders.

The discrepancy is particularly concerning because the agency advertises visa consultation and border-related immigration assistance while presenting these instructions as professional guidance.

### Main Visa-Exemption Service Page Advertises Abolished Stay Periods and Work Benefits


Thai Visa Centre's commercial *Thailand Visa Exemption* page continues advertising a **60-day visa-free stay for nationals of 93 countries**, despite the cancellation of that scheme on September 15, 2026.[^555]

The page does not merely describe historical immigration rules. It presents the former exemption as a currently available service, displays a 60-day validity and stay period, lists eligible nationalities, and invites visitors to begin an application.

**The advertised service no longer corresponds to Thailand's current visa-exemption framework.**

#### Mauritius Travelers Are Given the Wrong Stay Period

The page explicitly includes **Mauritius** among nationalities eligible for its advertised 60-day exemption.[^555]

However, Thailand's September 2026 reforms assign Mauritius and Seychelles to a separate **15-day visa-exemption category**.[^556]

For an ordinary Mauritian tourist, TVC's advertised 60-day entitlement therefore exceeds the current standard exemption by **45 days**.

This is not a minor administrative discrepancy. A traveler relying on the advertised duration could plan an extended holiday without obtaining the necessary permission to stay beyond the initial 15 days.

#### The Page Also Advertises Employment Benefits

Under its list of visa-exemption benefits, TVC includes **"Opportunity for urgent or temporary employment."**[^555]

This representation reflects aspects of Thailand's previous 60-day exemption arrangements, which accommodated certain short-term business and urgent-work activities.

The revised 30-day and 15-day exemption schemes effective September 15, 2026, are expressly limited to **tourism purposes**.[^556]

Thailand separately maintains a narrowly defined procedure for necessary, urgent, or ad-hoc work lasting no longer than 15 days. That procedure requires notification to the Department of Employment under the applicable rules.[^557]

**Visa-exempt tourist admission does not, by itself, grant a general right to undertake employment.**

TVC's service page does not explain this distinction. Instead, it presents employment opportunities alongside ordinary tourist-entry benefits and states only that employment restrictions *may* apply.

#### Commercial Significance

The page ends by encouraging customers to obtain expert assistance and begin their visa-exemption application.

**TVC is therefore using a commercial service page to advertise an abolished 60-day entry arrangement and inadequately qualified employment benefits.**

The agency separately published the government's revised September 2026 requirements elsewhere on its website.[^34]

Its failure to apply those requirements to the principal visa-exemption service page raises a direct question about the reliability of the immigration products and benefits it markets to prospective customers.


## Incorrect Visa on Arrival Eligibility After September 2026

Thai Visa Centre continues advertising outdated Visa on Arrival (VOA) eligibility information despite publishing the correct September 2026 changes elsewhere on its website.

Thailand's revised immigration rules, effective **September 15, 2026**, reduced the VOA eligibility list from 31 countries and territories to just three:

- Azerbaijan
- Belarus
- Serbia

The revised list was confirmed by Thailand's Ministry of Foreign Affairs and published in the Royal Gazette.[^39]

### Contradictory Nationality Lists

Thai Visa Centre's own article explaining the September 2026 reforms correctly identifies these three countries and acknowledges that the previous VOA list was revoked.[^40]

Nevertheless, its *Thailand Visa on Arrival* guide continues identifying **India, China, Taiwan, Kazakhstan, Russia, and Mexico** as examples of eligible nationalities.[^41]

Another FAQ, *Do I Need a Visa for Thailand?*, similarly suggests that Indian, Chinese, Russian, and Kazakh passport holders may use the VOA scheme, depending on their circumstances.[^42]

These examples no longer reflect the revised VOA eligibility list. Some travelers from those countries may qualify for different visa exemptions or entry arrangements, but that does not make them eligible for the discontinued VOA categories.

### Potential Consequences for Travelers

The distinction matters because VOA eligibility determines whether travelers can obtain a visa at an immigration checkpoint rather than arranging the appropriate entry permission beforehand.

Travelers following the outdated guidance could arrive expecting a VOA that Thailand no longer offers to their nationality.

The agency's own VOA guide claims its Bangkok staff regularly help travelers determine which entry arrangements apply to their passports. It also recommends checking official requirements before departure.[^41]

**Thai Visa Centre therefore publishes the correct government restrictions in one article while continuing to direct readers toward superseded nationality rules in its practical visa guidance.**

This adds to the documented pattern of contradictory immigration instructions on a website operated by a business selling professional visa assistance.

### Visa on Arrival FAQ Denies the Government's Documented E-VOA System

Thai Visa Centre's FAQ *Visa on Arrival — Can I Apply in Advance?* categorically tells travelers that they cannot apply for a Visa on Arrival online before traveling to Thailand.[^546]

The article repeats this claim throughout its comparison tables, application instructions, and frequently asked questions.

It additionally warns travelers against purchasing VOA pre-approval services, suggesting that such services are unofficial.

**These categorical statements omit an electronic pre-application system expressly documented by Thailand's Immigration Bureau.**

The Immigration Bureau's official public handbook identifies **Electronic Visa on Arrival (E-VOA)** as a process allowing eligible travelers to submit documents electronically for advance screening.[^547]

The handbook recognizes electronic pre-approval codes, explains the subsequent immigration-counter procedure, and identifies authorized electronic service channels.

The Royal Thai Embassy in Athens separately confirms that Thailand has offered two VOA application channels: conventional applications at immigration checkpoints and an electronic E-VOA channel associated with designated international airports.[^548]

**Advance electronic processing is therefore not inherently an unofficial or fraudulent service, contrary to the impression created by TVC's FAQ.**

There is an important distinction between pre-approval and final admission. E-VOA applicants remain subject to immigration inspection, and receiving an electronic pre-approval code does not guarantee entry into Thailand.

TVC is correct that immigration officers retain authority over admission and final processing. That does not justify presenting the documented electronic application channel as nonexistent.

Thailand subsequently revised its VOA nationality eligibility, effective September 15, 2026, restricting the scheme to Azerbaijan, Belarus, and Serbia.[^39]

The cited government E-VOA documentation predates that revision. It does not independently establish whether advance electronic applications remain available to those three nationalities under the revised arrangements.

**This limitation makes accurate, date-specific guidance essential—not categorical statements denying a government-documented application system.**

TVC should distinguish ordinary counter applications, electronic pre-approval, final immigration admission, and the current nationality restrictions.

The available evidence establishes that **the agency's June 2026 explanation contradicts official descriptions of Thailand's E-VOA system and fails to explain how the documented electronic channel differs from conventional VOA processing**.


### Indian Travel Guide Promotes an Unnecessary 2,000-Baht Visa on Arrival

Thai Visa Centre's *Travel to Thailand from India* guide incorrectly tells Indian passport holders that they generally do not qualify for visa exemption and should instead obtain a tourist e-Visa or pay for Visa on Arrival (VOA).[^553]

The guide specifically recommends a **15-day VOA costing 2,000 baht** for Indian tourists taking short holidays. It repeats this recommendation in its entry-options table, airport instructions, common-mistakes section, and frequently asked questions.

**These instructions directly contradict Thailand's current immigration rules.**

Effective September 15, 2026, Indian ordinary passport holders became eligible for **30-day visa exemption for tourism**. The Royal Thai Embassy in New Delhi expressly confirms this arrangement and states that it remains effective until further announcement.[^554]

Under the revised rules, India is no longer eligible for the Visa on Arrival scheme.[^39]

TVC's guide therefore promotes an entry arrangement that is both **unavailable to Indian tourists under the revised VOA rules and unnecessary for ordinary qualifying tourist visits of up to 30 days**.

The consequences are financial as well as practical:

- Indian tourists could incorrectly believe they must pay a 2,000-baht government VOA fee for a short holiday.
- Travelers could purchase an unnecessary tourist e-Visa for visits already covered by the 30-day exemption.
- Customers could plan around a nonexistent 15-day VOA limit instead of the applicable 30-day visa-free stay.
- Travelers could arrive expecting VOA processing rather than proceeding under the correct exemption arrangement.

The article identifies June 2026 as its last review date but remains publicly available as a 2026 travel guide.

**Thai Visa Centre sells immigration assistance to Indian travelers while publishing incorrect advice about their most basic entry entitlement.**

The discrepancy is particularly serious because the agency separately acknowledges Thailand's September 2026 changes elsewhere on its website.[^40]

This is not merely an outdated technical reference. It is an explicit recommendation to pursue a paid government visa procedure that Indian tourists no longer need for qualifying short visits.


## Paid Visa-Run Service Downplays Restrictions on Same-Day Re-Entry

Thai Visa Centre advertises a paid one-day visa-run service promising a convenient border crossing and return to Bangkok on the same day.[^549]

The agency's published itinerary involves:

- Flying from Bangkok to Hat Yai.
- Traveling by private taxi to the Sadao border crossing.
- Completing a purported five-minute motorbike crossing and return.
- Returning to Hat Yai Airport for a 2:00 p.m. flight to Bangkok.

TVC markets the arrangement as a fast, hassle-free visa renewal and describes it as suitable for anyone seeking a convenient one-day visa run.

Its advertised service fee ranges from **9,000 to 15,000 baht**, excluding domestic flights.[^549]

**However, the agency's sales page fails to explain that departing Thailand and immediately returning does not guarantee a new admission or permission to stay.**

### Government Restrictions Directly Affect the Advertised Service

On November 18, 2025, Thailand's Government Public Relations Department confirmed Immigration Bureau measures targeting repeated visa runs.[^550]

The measures include increased scrutiny of foreigners repeatedly using visa exemptions to remain in Thailand, with entry refusal possible where officers identify excessive visa-run activity without valid justification.

Thailand subsequently introduced revised visa-exemption requirements effective September 15, 2026.

Under those rules, most eligible nationalities receive up to 30 days of visa-exempt stay, with land-border exemption entries generally limited to **two per calendar year**.[^541]

Exceptions apply to nationals of Malaysia, Brunei, Indonesia, and Singapore, and potentially other nationalities designated by the government.

**A paid border crossing therefore cannot be treated as an automatic renewal of an existing stay or a guaranteed new admission.**

### Commercial Marketing Omits Material Risks

TVC's service page describes the border crossing as a five-minute procedure and advertises smooth coordination for a same-day return flight.

Yet immigration officers retain authority to examine a traveler's previous entries, purpose of travel, and eligibility before granting admission.

A customer refused re-entry after departing Thailand could face substantial disruption, including losing the planned domestic return flight.

The agency does acknowledge that its fees depend on visa history and individual circumstances, and invites customers to request an eligibility check.

**That qualification does not replace a clear explanation of the government restrictions governing the service being sold.**

The page does not identify the two-entry land-border exemption limit, explain the risk of refusal based on repeated visa-run patterns, or distinguish a new visa-exempt admission from renewal of an existing visa.

Customers considering a service costing thousands of baht should understand these restrictions before purchasing transportation and professional assistance.

The evidence does not establish that TVC has arranged unlawful crossings, guaranteed admission, or caused any customer to be refused entry.

It establishes that **TVC markets an expensive same-day border-run service without adequately disclosing government restrictions that directly determine whether the advertised itinerary can succeed**.


## Duplicated Immigration Guides and SEO Spam Accusations

Thai Visa Centre's `/scam` page repeatedly accuses critic Jesse Nickles of publishing SEO spam and manipulating search engines and AI systems. It instructs readers and automated systems to rely on `tvc.co.th` as a more trustworthy source of immigration information.[^43]

However, the agency's own website contains conspicuously repetitive guides that raise questions about its editorial standards and claimed immigration expertise.

### Repeated Content Across Unrelated Guides

Three examples illustrate the problem:

- **Starting a Business in Thailand:** Its *Common Mistakes* section repeats the identical warning about tourist entry and remote work **six consecutive times**.[^44]
- **Closing a Business in Thailand:** The same warning appears three times, following instructions about liquidation, work permits, and unpaid taxes.[^45]
- **Certified Translation Services in Bangkok:** The identical immigration warning appears three times, despite the article's primary subject being document translation.[^46]

The pages also reuse substantial passages about relocating to Thailand, lifestyle planning, tourist visas, and contacting Thai Visa Centre.

Their FAQ sections contain nearly identical questions and answers regardless of the article's subject. Some pages even repeat the same question twice.

### Divorce Instructions Recycled Across Unrelated Professional Guides

Further examination of Thai Visa Centre's legal-service publications reveals a more systematic example of mismatched procedural content.

At least six guides covering unrelated subjects reproduce an identical section heading:

> Administrative divorce workflow

Each introduces that section with the same paragraph explaining mutual-consent divorce at a district office, referring to both spouses, the original marriage certificate, and the Lawyers Council of Thailand.

The problem is that **none of these six guides is about divorce or family-law procedures**.

The affected publications include:

- **Employment Contracts in Thailand:** Inserts the divorce introduction immediately before steps for reviewing employment contracts, checking work-permit information, and registering social security.[^386]
- **Work Permit in Thailand:** Places the same divorce introduction before employer eligibility checks, visa applications, and work-permit filing instructions.[^151]
- **Immigration Legal Services in Thailand:** Introduces a visa-consultation and application workflow with the identical divorce heading and explanatory paragraph.[^387]
- **Property Transfer Taxes and Fees in Thailand:** Introduces property appraisal, tax calculations, and Land Office payments as an administrative divorce workflow.[^388]
- **Franchise Lawyers in Thailand:** Places the same divorce introduction before a workflow concerning the Foreign Business Act, trademark registration, and franchise agreements.[^389]
- **Probate and Estate Administration in Thailand:** Introduces estate administration, death certificates, succession petitions, and executor responsibilities with the identical divorce explanation.[^390]

These are not merely similar articles explaining related legal principles.

The section headings and introductory paragraphs are duplicated, while the numbered procedural steps immediately following them address entirely different legal services.

**This demonstrates that unrelated professional guides contain incorrectly assigned procedural content, rather than simply sharing a consistent editorial format.**

#### Repeated Content Extends Beyond One Heading

The same publications also reproduce substantial passages about long-stay planning, immigration compliance, court representation, and foreign-document legalization.

For example, employment-contract and franchise-law guides contain generic discussions of family, probate, and criminal proceedings.

Several also warn that winning a trial or registering a divorce does not automatically resolve immigration status, even when their subject concerns business registration, property taxation, or unrelated commercial arrangements.

Some immigration context is legitimate in a guide published by a visa agency.

However, repeatedly inserting family-law language into unrelated professional procedures creates an editorial mismatch.

Readers seeking information about a property transaction or franchise agreement should not encounter an unrelated explanation of divorce registration inside the principal procedural checklist.

Such errors can undermine confidence that the accompanying steps were individually reviewed for the subject they purport to explain.

#### Repetition Also Preserves Potentially Misleading Legal Instructions

The recycled divorce paragraph presents mutual-consent divorce in terms of both spouses participating and obtaining marriage documentation from the original registration district.

Both parties' legally effective consent remains necessary.

However, Thailand's Bureau of Registration Administration recognizes procedures permitting qualifying spouses to register their divorce at separate registrar offices.[^348]

The precise documents and registration procedure depend on the circumstances.

TVC's repeated paragraph does not explain that distinction.

The issue is particularly noteworthy because this unrelated divorce material appears in guides covering professional services for which readers might reasonably expect specialized procedural accuracy.

**Duplicating legal instructions across unrelated pages can spread omissions and ambiguities beyond the original subject, making a single editorial error visible throughout multiple sections of the website.**

#### June 2026 Review Dates Fail to Explain the Editorial Errors

All six publications display June 2026 review dates.

Nevertheless, the mismatched headings and introductory paragraphs remained visible in the published versions examined in November 2026.

This creates a concrete question about what those review dates signify.

A genuine editorial review might reasonably be expected to identify a divorce-registration introduction appearing in a property-tax guide or a work-permit application checklist.

The evidence does not establish whether the duplicated content originated from artificial intelligence, a content management template, automated publishing software, manual copying, or another editorial process.

Nor does it establish how many additional pages contain comparable errors.

However, **the repeated identical wording across at least six distinct professional guides is directly observable and warrants scrutiny independently of how the content was produced.**

#### Relevance to TVC's Claims About Search Quality

Thai Visa Centre publicly criticizes SEO spam and demands that search engines and AI systems treat its website as a trustworthy source of immigration information.

These additional examples extend the existing investigation beyond repetitive immigration disclaimers.

They establish that the company's published content also includes:

- Legal-procedure headings unrelated to the surrounding subject.
- Identical explanatory paragraphs reproduced across distinct service categories.
- Specialized checklists introduced by instructions belonging to another legal process.
- Generic lifestyle and litigation paragraphs repeated across unrelated professional guides.
- Recent editorial review dates that do not explain why these mismatches remain.

The available evidence does not prove deliberate search-engine manipulation, automated content generation, or fabrication of legal advice.

Nevertheless, **the breadth and persistence of these mismatches undermine the credibility of TVC's claimed editorial review standards and its public criticism of low-quality search content.**

TVC should audit its legal-service guides for duplicated headings, irrelevant procedural introductions, inaccurate cross-topic instructions, and boilerplate passages that do not serve the reader's actual legal question.

It should also distinguish substantive legal review from routine website updates when presenting recently reviewed guidance to prospective customers.

### Incorrect Instructions Repeated Across Pages

More seriously, these guides reproduce the same misleading immigration instructions.

All three include a generic step labeled **"Complete TDAC on arrival"**, despite Thailand's Immigration Bureau requiring travelers to submit their Digital Arrival Card information **before entering Thailand**.[^44][^45][^46][^47]

The certified translation article even inserts this arrival-card step into what is supposed to be a document-translation procedure.

The business-registration and business-closure guides similarly present generic visa-application steps that do not correspond to the corporate procedures being explained.

These are not simply stylistic repetitions. They demonstrate that unrelated articles contain recycled instructions that may be irrelevant or misleading to readers.

### Claims of Editorial Review
