All three guides state that they were last reviewed in **June 2026**.

Yet the published pages retain obvious duplication, mismatched procedural steps, and inaccurate TDAC terminology.

The repeated structure suggests extensive use of standardized content templates. It does not establish whether the material was generated by AI or another automated process.

**The contradiction is that Thai Visa Centre condemns SEO spam and demands that search engines trust its publications, while its own website distributes visibly repetitive immigration content containing substantive errors.**

For an agency selling professional immigration assistance, these examples raise questions about the reliability of its claimed editorial review and the accuracy of information used to attract prospective customers.

### Published Terms Undermine Confidence in Review Dates

Thai Visa Centre's Terms & Conditions, updated February 9, 2025, introduce another qualification to its claimed editorial review process.[^185]

The agreement states that published update or refresh dates should not be understood as confirmation that all website information has been modified or updated. It also disclaims any general obligation to update or clarify published information, except as required by law.

This matters because numerous immigration guides display recent review dates while retaining incorrect government fees, outdated eligibility requirements, and contradictory application instructions.

A review date does not necessarily mean every statement was revised. However, Thai Visa Centre provides no public explanation of what its editorial reviews verify or why documented regulatory errors survived them.

**The concern is not simply that individual articles contain mistakes, but that the agency uses recent review dates to support the credibility of commercially important immigration guidance without demonstrating that the underlying information has been checked against current requirements.**

### TVC Disclaims Responsibility for the Accuracy of Its Professional Guidance

Thai Visa Centre markets professional immigration assistance, including visa strategy consultations, document reviews, embassy liaison services, and guidance on immigration compliance.[^147]

Yet its published Disclaimer states that website information is provided without guarantees of accuracy, completeness, or timeliness. It further denies responsibility for errors, omissions, and decisions made in reliance on that information.[^518]

Most notably, **TVC explicitly states that information published on its website is not intended to provide professional advice.**

This disclaimer stands in striking contrast to the agency's commercial presentation.

TVC publishes extensive immigration and legal guidance, advertises specialist assistance, and attaches recent editorial review dates to articles covering complex government procedures.

As documented throughout this investigation, those publications contain repeated errors concerning visa eligibility, application fees, immigration forms, statutory deadlines, and legal requirements.

**TVC therefore uses its published guidance to demonstrate professional expertise and attract customers while simultaneously attempting to disclaim responsibility for the reliability of that information.**

Its Terms & Conditions reinforce this position by disclaiming any general obligation to correct or update published information, except where legally required.[^185]

A distinction exists between freely published information and individualized paid professional services. The disclaimer does not establish that TVC can lawfully exclude responsibility for negligent paid services, nor does it establish that the agency knowingly published false information.

However, **the agency's documented inaccuracies make its broad disclaimers especially consequential: customers are encouraged to trust information that the company itself refuses to guarantee.**

The published documents do not adequately explain where TVC's responsibility for its advertised immigration expertise begins and where its disclaimers concerning inaccurate advice end.

## Four Advertised Offices Match Siam Legal's Locations

Thai Visa Centre publishes a guide titled *Thailand Law Firms and TVC Office Locations*, claiming to operate immigration and legal-coordination offices in **Bangkok, Phuket, Pattaya, and Chiang Mai**.[^48]

The guide describes these locations as Thai Visa Centre's own regional branches, invites customers to book consultations, and explicitly states that walk-in visits are available at all four offices.

However, **all four advertised locations match offices independently identified with Siam Legal International**, a separate Thai law firm and immigration consultancy.

### Identical Office Listings

Comparing Thai Visa Centre's guide against Siam Legal's published office information reveals the following:

| City | Finding |
|---|---|
| Bangkok | Identical building, floor, and office unit |
| Phuket | Identical commercial premises and unit numbers |
| Chiang Mai | Identical shopping complex, floor, and room numbers |
| Pattaya | Identical to Siam Legal's previously advertised office location |

Siam Legal's official website continues identifying the Bangkok, Phuket, and Chiang Mai locations as its own offices.[^49]

The Pattaya location appears in an older office directory advertising Siam Legal's services. Its current website identifies a different Pattaya office, suggesting that Thai Visa Centre's listing may have reused outdated information.[^50]

**The agreement across four cities, including specific suite and room numbers, is far more significant than two businesses coincidentally operating in the same neighborhood.**

### Contradiction With Thai Visa Centre's Actual Office

Thai Visa Centre's homepage identifies its customer-facing office as being at **The Pretium Bangna in Samut Prakan**.[^51]

That location does not match the supposed Bangkok head office described in its four-branch guide.

The homepage also emphasizes nationwide assistance through couriers and postal services rather than advertising the four regional offices.

The conflicting presentations leave customers with two substantially different accounts of the agency's physical operations.

### Undisclosed Relationship With Siam Legal

The four-office guide does not identify Siam Legal as the operator of the listed premises or explain any commercial arrangement between the businesses.

Instead, it describes the locations as Thai Visa Centre offices, identifies regional services supposedly available there, and encourages customers to visit them.

The available publications provide no evidence that Siam Legal authorized Thai Visa Centre to represent its offices as TVC branches.

**Unless a genuine office-sharing or partnership arrangement exists, Thai Visa Centre appears to be passing off another law firm's business locations as its own.**

This discrepancy raises a more fundamental credibility issue than the agency's previously documented inaccurate immigration guides: customers may be directed to offices that belong to an entirely different business.

The identical listings also warrant scrutiny of whether Thai Visa Centre has copied additional material concerning legal services, professional qualifications, or commercial credentials from Siam Legal.

### Customer Contact Pages Direct Visitors Toward Nana BTS

The misleading office information extends beyond Thai Visa Centre's four-branch guide.

Several pages specifically intended to help prospective customers contact or visit the agency identify its Bangkok office as being near **Nana BTS on Sukhumvit**.

Examples include:

- **Contact Thai Visa Centre:** Identifies Nana BTS as the office location and recommends scheduling in-person consultations there. Its FAQ repeats the location.[^61]
- **Company Profile:** Lists the Bangkok office as near Nana BTS in its contact information.[^62]
- **Contact an Immigration Specialist:** Gives the same location for appointments and document handovers.[^63]
- **Contact a Thai Family Lawyer:** Repeats the Nana BTS location for consultations involving marriage, divorce, and related immigration matters.[^64]

These pages identify **June 2026** as their review date.

### The Same Area as Siam Legal

Siam Legal International independently identifies its Bangkok headquarters as being beside Nana BTS.[^49]

Thai Visa Centre's separate four-office guide goes further by advertising Siam Legal's exact Bangkok office premises as its own head office.[^48]

However, **Thai Visa Centre's homepage identifies its actual customer-facing office as The Pretium Bangna in Samut Prakan**, a different part of the metropolitan area.[^51]

The contact pages neither explain this discrepancy nor identify a separately operated TVC office near Nana BTS.

### Misleading Customers Seeking Appointments

This distinction matters because these are not abstract descriptions of the company's regional coverage.

The pages explicitly encourage prospective customers to schedule consultations, visit the office, and bring passports, visa documents, and other sensitive records.

**TVC is directing prospective customers toward an area associated with Siam Legal's office while identifying a different location as its actual premises.**

Taken together, the office directory and customer-contact pages indicate a repeated pattern of inaccurate business-location claims, not merely an isolated address error.

The published information does not establish that TVC operates a separate office near Nana BTS or has authorization to receive customers at Siam Legal's premises.

The agency's failure to reconcile these conflicting locations raises serious questions about the accuracy of its advertised physical presence and the reliability of its instructions to customers.

## Thai Visa Centre's Thailand Privilege Accreditation and Award Claims Lack Verification

Thai Visa Centre advertises itself as an authorized General Sales and Services Agent (GSSA) for Thailand Privilege, the government-owned long-term residency membership program. It also claims to have received Thailand Privilege's **Best Sales Performance Awards in both 2024 and 2025**.[^52][^91][^92]

These are commercially important credentials for an intermediary handling memberships costing hundreds of thousands or millions of baht. Yet TVC's examined publications do not identify the registered company holding its claimed authorization or receiving the awards. The program's published corporate agent list identifies another immigration firm, Siam Legal, whose documented office and award claims overlap with TVC's representations.

### August 2023 Thailand Elite and LTR Service Promotion

On August 30, 2023, Thai Visa Centre participated in an ASEAN NOW discussion concerning Thailand Elite membership applications ahead of anticipated membership price increases.[^669]

Several participants expressed uncertainty about which websites represented the official membership provider and which belonged to commercial intermediaries.

Thai Visa Centre entered the discussion through its designated Forum Sponsor account, provided its commercial contact channels, and stated:

> We have assisted with many Elite, and LTR cases.

The agency did not identify an official accreditation number, registered sales-agent identity, or authorized intermediary through which it handled Thailand Elite applications.

On August 31, forum contributor DrJack54 directed the prospective applicant toward Thai Visa Centre after initially recommending direct applications to the membership provider.

### Distinction Between Commercial Assistance and Official Accreditation

**The historical advertisement establishes that Thai Visa Centre was commercially promoting Thailand Elite and LTR application assistance by August 2023.**

However, providing application assistance is not equivalent to holding official General Sales and Services Agent accreditation.

The distinction is relevant to the agency's subsequent representations that it is an authorized Thailand Privilege sales agent and recipient of sales performance awards in 2024 and 2025.

The historical correspondence does not establish whether Thai Visa Centre held accreditation at the time, subsequently obtained authorization, or handled applications through another authorized business.

### GSSA Claims Versus the Published Agent List

TVC's *Thailand Elite Visa Application Forms* guide calls it an authorized GSSA and directs readers to obtain application documents through the business.[^52] Another guide names **Thai Visa Centre** as the GSSA through which customers should select membership tiers.[^53] A four-office guide claims its Bangkok, Phuket, Pattaya, and Chiang Mai locations handle Thailand Privilege applications as authorized agents.[^54]

Thailand Privilege Card Co., Ltd.'s **2025 annual report** lists 50 corporate GSSAs, but does not identify **Thai Visa Centre**, **TVC. Visa Agent Co., Ltd.**, or **AGENTS CO., LTD.** among them.[^55]

It does identify **Siam Legal (Thailand) Co., Ltd. as agent number 37**. Siam Legal separately publishes its authorization identifier, **SA22/013**.[^55][^56]

An annual report is a dated record, not a definitive registry of every later appointment. TVC could have received subsequent authorization or operated through an intermediary. However, its cited guides provide no equivalent appointment number, registered GSSA name, or explanation of such an arrangement.

**TVC's claimed direct accreditation is not substantiated by the examined official agent list or the identifying details it publishes about itself.**

### Claims of Awards in 2024 and 2025

TVC's Japanese-language *Thailand Privilege Long-Stay Visa: Elite Membership Tiers (2026)* guide claims that it received **Best Sales Performance Awards in 2024 and 2025**. The Korean-language version repeats the assertion.[^91][^92]

Both pages also describe TVC as an authorized GSSA and advertise regional offices. Neither identifies the award category, corporate recipient, ceremony date, accepting representative, certificate, or official announcement substantiating TVC's wins.

Siam Legal, by contrast, published specific accounts of similarly named awards for those years:

- **2024:** An April 2024 promotional announcement published by *The Nation* identifies Siam Legal (Thailand) Co., Ltd. as the winner of **Best Sales Performance Award (Western)** at the Thailand Privilege GSSA Awards ceremony in Bangkok.[^93]
- **2025:** Siam Legal's February 2025 announcement identifies the same Western-category award, a **January 24, 2025** ceremony at the Grand Hyatt Erawan Bangkok, and **Rex Baay**, head of its Thailand Privilege program, as the recipient's representative.[^94]

These accounts are principally Siam Legal's promotional material, not independently authenticated government award records. Nevertheless, they specify a category, recipient, and ceremony that TVC's award claims do not.

**Siam Legal's documented awards do not prove TVC could not have won in another category.** The missing evidence is the corporate identity and verifiable award record behind TVC's own assertions.

### Overlap With Advertised Office Locations

The credential claims intersect with a separate finding documented earlier in this investigation: TVC advertises offices in **Bangkok, Phuket, Pattaya, and Chiang Mai** at locations publicly associated with Siam Legal.[^49][^54]

Its Thailand Privilege guides combine those locations with claims of GSSA authorization and successive sales awards. Meanwhile, the examined official GSSA list identifies Siam Legal, not any of TVC's named companies.[^55]

This overlap warrants clarification, but **does not establish that TVC copied Siam Legal's achievements, shares management with Siam Legal, or lacks any legitimate relationship with an authorized agent**. The underlying office addresses and customer-visit implications are covered in the separate office-location section rather than repeated here.

### What TVC Should Substantiate

Thai Visa Centre should identify the registered entity and authorization number supporting its GSSA claims, explain whether it submits applications directly or through another accredited company, and provide independently verifiable evidence of the **2024 and 2025 awards**, including their categories and recipients.

It should also clarify any commercial relationship with Siam Legal relevant to the overlapping office and credential representations. This would let customers distinguish direct authorization from referral or intermediary arrangements before applying for membership.

The available evidence does not establish fabricated awards or deliberate impersonation. It establishes that TVC advertises specific official credentials without the identifying documentation needed to reconcile them with the published agent list and Siam Legal's separately documented record.

**Until those details are supplied, prospective customers cannot independently verify which company holds the accreditation and awards TVC uses to promote its Thailand Privilege services.**
## Warning Against Paid TDAC Websites While Promoting an Affiliated Service

Thai Visa Centre's *Thailand Entry Requirements 2026* guide warns travelers against unofficial websites charging money for Thailand Digital Arrival Card (TDAC) registration.

It explicitly instructs travelers to submit their arrival cards through the official immigration website only. Its list of common travel mistakes also warns that third-party TDAC websites may collect personal information without delivering valid confirmations.[^57]

However, the same guide promotes Thai Visa Centre's own TDAC assistance.

### Directing Readers to AGENTS CO., LTD.

The linked TDAC service page advertises premium submission assistance and directs applicants to `agents.co.th`, operated by [Agents Co., Ltd.](../companies/agents-co-ltd.md).[^58]

That company's TDAC comparison website advertises:

- Free assisted submissions within the government's 72-hour registration window.
- **US$8 (270 baht)** early submissions for travelers registering farther in advance.
- A list of 29 competing websites labeled scams or untrusted operators.[^59]

Thai Visa Centre separately acknowledges that AGENTS CO., LTD. operates its digital immigration services, including TDAC processing.[^60]

These are not independent businesses merely advertising similar services. They belong to the same publicly acknowledged commercial network.

### Applying Different Standards to Competitors

The contradiction is not that charging for optional assistance automatically constitutes fraud.

Rather, **Thai Visa Centre tells travelers to avoid third-party TDAC websites charging fees while directing them toward its own affiliated intermediary, which also advertises paid TDAC processing**.

The entry guide does not clearly explain that the recommended assistance leads to a related commercial business or distinguish its own paid offering from the fee-charging sites it warns readers against.

Meanwhile, AGENTS CO., LTD. presents itself as a trusted alternative and publicly categorizes competing providers as scams or untrusted operators.

The agency's own website confirms that its private service is not affiliated with the Thai government. Nevertheless, customers following TVC's recommendations are exposed to a commercial submission option after being warned about precisely that category of intermediary.

**This selective treatment of competing TDAC services raises questions about whether TVC's scam warnings are impartial consumer guidance or also function as promotion for its affiliated business.**

### TDAC Fine Warnings Lack an Identified Official Basis

Thai Visa Centre's immigration-forms guides warn that failing to complete the Thailand Digital Arrival Card (TDAC) can result in fines and immigration delays. The warnings appear alongside promotions for the agency's TDAC assistance services.[^273]

However, the Immigration Bureau's official TDAC FAQ does not identify a specific monetary penalty for failing to submit the form before arrival. Instead, it explains that airport kiosks and internet access are available for travelers requiring assistance.[^274]

The Royal Thai Embassy in Hong Kong similarly confirms that travelers unable to complete registration beforehand can receive help at designated immigration checkpoints.[^275]

**TDAC registration remains mandatory**, and travelers should complete it before arrival. Late registration can cause delays, while admission ultimately remains subject to immigration authorities.

Nevertheless, TVC does not identify the legal provision establishing the fines it warns travelers about or distinguish possible immigration consequences from a specific TDAC penalty.

This omission matters because Thai Visa Centre promotes TDAC assistance through its affiliated AGENTS CO., LTD. network, including paid early-submission services.

**Presenting unspecified financial penalties alongside commercial registration assistance risks creating unnecessary anxiety among travelers who might otherwise complete the government's free procedure themselves.**

The evidence does not establish that TVC deliberately exaggerated penalties to generate sales. It does establish that the company publishes a financial warning without substantiating its legal basis.

### TDAC Guides Misstate Family Submissions and Official Exemptions

Thai Visa Centre publishes misleading instructions about who must submit Thailand Digital Arrival Cards (TDAC) and how families can complete the registration process.

Its *How to Travel to Thailand as a Family* guide tells readers that every foreign passport holder must complete a separate TDAC submission, including infants and young children.[^299]

Its separate travel-requirements guide explicitly states that parents cannot submit once for the entire family.[^300]

**These instructions confuse the requirement for individual traveler records with the government's ability to accept multiple records in a single group submission.**

#### Immigration Allows Groups of Up to Ten Travelers

Thailand's Immigration Bureau explicitly permits **up to 10 travelers in one TDAC group submission**.[^301]

Each traveler must still provide the necessary passport and arrival information, including accompanying children.

However, families are not required to complete an entirely separate submission process for every family member.

TVC's instructions omit this official capability and incorrectly suggest that submitting family information together is prohibited.

This can create unnecessary administrative work for families already navigating complicated travel arrangements.

#### The Official System Recognizes Transit Exemptions

TVC also repeatedly describes TDAC as mandatory for every foreign traveler on every arrival.

However, the Immigration Bureau's official FAQ identifies important exceptions.

Travelers transiting Thailand without passing through immigration do not need TDAC registration. Technical landings and qualifying transit flights are similarly exempt.[^301]

The official FAQ also confirms that flight crew are not required to submit TDAC.

These exemptions do not apply to transit passengers who clear immigration to enter Thailand.

Indeed, TVC's separate *Do I Need a Visa for Thailand?* FAQ correctly recognizes that distinction, stating that transit passengers must complete TDAC when passing through immigration.[^302]

**The agency therefore publishes broad, categorical TDAC instructions while acknowledging a relevant exception elsewhere on its website.**

#### Relevance to Commercial TDAC Assistance

The discrepancies are particularly relevant because Thai Visa Centre promotes TDAC assistance through AGENTS CO., LTD., its acknowledged digital operations company.

AGENTS offers free assisted registration within the government's ordinary submission window and paid early-submission services for travelers registering farther in advance.

Accurate guidance should help travelers determine whether they actually need a TDAC and whether they can use the government's free group-submission facility.

Overstating filing obligations or presenting unnecessary separate applications as mandatory risks directing customers toward assistance they do not need.

The available evidence does not establish that AGENTS has charged exempt transit passengers or deliberately prevented families from using the official group-submission system.

Nevertheless, **a business commercially promoting immigration-registration assistance should accurately explain both the government's filing exemptions and its free group-submission capabilities**.

TVC should revise its family guides to distinguish individual traveler records from group submissions and consistently identify the official exemptions for qualifying transit passengers and crew.

## Misleading Foreign-Visa Requirements, Applications, and Fees

Thai Visa Centre promotes assistance for foreign visa applications from Thailand, but several of its published service guides contradict official entry requirements, omit simpler travel authorizations, or present incorrect government fees. The problems involve both **whether an applicant needs a visa at all** and **what the correct application route or cost would be**. The examples below cover Schengen fees, application jurisdictions and appointment providers, Greek embassy booking instructions, misleading foreign-visa directory links, ETIAS rules, Romania and Bulgaria's Schengen integration, and French overseas-territory procedures; visitor entry requirements for Singapore, South Korea, China, and Japan; India's fee-free e-Tourist visa; Canada's conditional eTA route; Italian family-visa categories, French appointments, and Portuguese embassy links; Australia's prospective marriage visa validity; UK digital documentation and fiancé-visa fees; and U.S. visa fees. They distinguish exemptions from application rules and actual government charges.

### Schengen Area: Outdated Visa Application Fees Despite Claimed 2026 Reviews

Thai Visa Centre advertises document preparation and application assistance for European Schengen visas, but repeatedly publishes application fees that became obsolete in June 2024.

The European Commission increased the standard short-stay visa fee from **€80 to €90 for adults**, and from **€40 to €45 for children aged six to eleven**, effective June 11, 2024.[^65]

Nevertheless, TVC continues publishing the former €80 adult fee across multiple guides:

- **Schengen Visa:** Lists €80 as the standard fee in both its introductory summary and requirements table.[^66]
- **Schengen Visa FAQs:** Repeats the €80 fee in its requirements table, additional guidance, and FAQ.[^67]
- **How to Apply for a Schengen Visa in Thailand:** Advertises €80 plus service charges.[^68]
- **How Do I Apply for a Schengen Visa From Thailand?:** Repeatedly identifies €80 as the standard short-stay application fee.[^69]

Additional country-specific sales pages repeat the same obsolete **€80 standard adult fee**, despite the EU-wide €90 rate:

- **Italy:** The *Italy Tourist Visa* page quotes €80 for most adults, while the separate *Italian Schengen Visa* guide also advertises €80 plus service charges.[^584][^588]
- **Spain:** The *Spain Tourist Visa* page quotes €80.[^585]
- **Sweden:** The *Sweden Tourist Visa* page quotes €80.[^586]
- **Netherlands:** The *Netherlands Tourist Visa* page quotes €80.[^587]

These are **additional published instances of one Schengen-wide fee error**, not different national fees. The European Commission's standard short-stay adult fee has been €90 since June 11, 2024, subject to exemptions and reduced categories.[^65]

These are not obscure historical articles. They are active service guides intended to attract customers seeking European visas from Thailand.

#### Contradiction With Official EU Requirements

The revised fees are established by Commission Delegated Regulation (EU) 2024/1415 and remain reflected in the European Commission's published visa guidance.[^65]

Certain applicants qualify for exemptions or reduced fees under specific arrangements, but these exceptions do not change the standard €90 adult fee.

TVC's guides do not explain that their €80 figure is outdated. Instead, they present it as the applicable standard fee while claiming their information was reviewed in **June 2026**.

#### Misleading Application Costs

The discrepancy directly affects customers budgeting for visa applications.

Applicants following TVC's figures would underestimate the standard government fee before accounting for appointment-center charges, translations, insurance, and the agency's own assistance fees.

**Thai Visa Centre markets professional Schengen application assistance while publishing a government fee that has been incorrect for more than two years.**

The repetition across four separate pages raises questions about the agency's claimed editorial reviews and whether its outbound visa guidance is actually checked against current immigration regulations.

#### Schengen FAQ Gives Incorrect Application-Centre Providers

TVC's *How Do I Apply for a Schengen Visa From Thailand?* FAQ groups **France, Germany, Italy, Spain, Portugal, and the Netherlands** under a single instruction to apply through **“VFS Global Bangkok – book online.”**[^618] This is not a reliable destination-specific appointment directory:

- **France:** France-Visas instructs applicants in Thailand to book with **TLScontact**, not VFS Global.[^619]
- **Spain:** The Spanish Embassy in Bangkok identifies **BLS International** as its short-stay visa application provider.[^620]
- **Portugal:** Its visa application centre in Bangkok is operated by **TLScontact**, not VFS Global.[^621]

Other entries in the table, including Germany, do use VFS Global; the problem is the FAQ's treatment of all six destinations as though they shared that provider. TVC also publishes a separate French Embassy guide with incorrect VFS instructions, documented below.[^593][^594]

**An applicant following the table could try to book the wrong provider rather than the authorized centre for the intended destination.** The difference is practical, not merely a branding detail, because appointment platforms and submission procedures are country-specific.

#### Greece: VFS Appointment Instructions Contradict Embassy Booking Guidance

TVC's *Greece Tourist Visa* page repeatedly describes the lodging route as **“Embassy or VFS Bangkok”** and instructs applicants to book a VFS or embassy slot. It uses this formulation in the summary, application stages, and step-by-step appointment instructions, despite also telling applicants to follow the official Greek mission appointment system.[^626]

The **Embassy of Greece in Bangkok** instead directs visa applicants through its own **“Book an appointment”** link on the embassy's official website, last updated October 20, 2025.[^627] Its published procedure identifies an embassy-specific booking service; the official instructions reviewed do not identify VFS Global as an alternative Greek visa appointment provider in Thailand.

**TVC's repeated VFS recommendation is not supported by the embassy's published instructions and could send applicants to the wrong appointment platform.** The discrepancy should not be interpreted as proof that no third-party appointment arrangements have ever existed, nor that specific customers lost money or missed appointments. The page claims a June 2026 review, after the embassy had already published its booking instructions.

#### First Entry Is Not a General Alternative to the Main-Destination Rule

The same FAQ reduces the choice of consulate to **“Country of main destination or first entry,”** without qualifying when first entry determines jurisdiction.[^618] TVC's separate ETIAS/Schengen travel guide likewise says travelers can apply through the first Schengen entry country when touring multiple states.[^625]

Under the European Commission's rules, travelers visiting several Schengen countries must normally apply to the country of their **main destination**, determined by purpose or length of stay. **First entry applies when the visits are of equal length and no main destination can be determined**, not simply whenever the traveler enters another Schengen country first.[^622]

This distinction can change which mission is legally responsible for the visa application. A traveler staying a week in France and two days in Spain does not ordinarily apply to Spain merely because the flight enters Schengen there. Presenting main destination and first entry as interchangeable choices can send applicants to the wrong consulate.

#### Tourist Status in Thailand Does Not Automatically Establish Filing Eligibility

TVC's Schengen FAQ explicitly answers **“Yes”** to whether someone visiting Thailand on a tourist visa can apply for a Schengen visa in Bangkok, provided the Thai stay remains legal through processing.[^618]

**Legal presence is not the same as residence for Schengen filing jurisdiction.** Article 6 of the EU Visa Code ordinarily assigns applications to the competent consulate for the applicant's place of **legal residence**. A person legally present but not resident there may apply only with an acceptable justification for not applying in the country of residence; the consulate assesses that justification.[^623]

For example, France-Visas states that foreign applicants who are not ordinarily resident in Thailand, including those with less than three months' local residence, need **unforeseeable and compelling reasons** to request filing in Bangkok.[^624] Thus, a valid Thai tourist entry or extension does not by itself make a Schengen application acceptable there.

**TVC's unconditional answer risks directing foreign tourists in Thailand toward appointments that they are not eligible to use without additional justification.** This does not mean every non-resident application is prohibited: justified exceptions exist and requirements depend on the competent member state's practice.

#### Foreign-Visa Directory Link Redirects to the Homepage

TVC's commercial *Schengen Visa - Short-Stay Travel from Thailand* page displays a **“Foreign visas hub”** navigation link to the agency's `/foreign-visas` section.[^628] Indexed versions of its Greece, Croatia, and Romania tourist-visa pages also advertised a foreign-visa hub or **“All foreign visas”** navigation option.[^626][^629][^630]

However, **requesting `https://tvc.co.th/foreign-visas` redirects to the main TVC homepage**, rather than presenting the advertised directory of outbound visa guides.[^631] The destination is not a 404 error, but it does not provide the country-by-country directory the link promises.

Some of the indexed examples are older than the currently retrievable page renderings, so the evidence does **not** prove that every historically displayed directory link remains visible. The live Schengen guide and the redirecting destination are sufficient to establish a current navigation inconsistency. It can frustrate users attempting to compare destination requirements, but does not by itself establish incorrect visa advice or charges.

### Italy: Family-Visa Sales Page Conflates Visits With Family Reunification

TVC's *Family Visa for Thai - Apply from Thailand* page presents the route as a routine **Schengen short-stay visa** for up to 90 days, advertises a **€80 adult fee**, and instructs applicants to book an embassy or VFS appointment, pay the Schengen fee, and prepare €30,000 travel insurance. Its page title and family-visa branding do not explain the different rules applicable to qualifying relatives joining Italian or EU citizens.[^609]

**Italy distinguishes temporary family visits from actual family reunification.** Since June 1, 2024, qualifying non-EU spouses and other family members who intend to reunite with an Italian or EU citizen permanently residing in Italy must generally apply for a **national type D visa for family reasons**, rather than a short-stay tourist/visit visa. The Italian government's consular guidance says this qualifying visa is **issued without a visa fee**; qualifying short visits by those family members likewise receive the applicable visa free of charge.[^611]

The **Italian Embassy in Bangkok** also specifies that qualifying Article 2 family members accompanying or joining an Italian or EU citizen may submit directly at the embassy's Visa Office **without an appointment**, while ordinary short-stay Schengen applications use VFS. National type D visa applications are submitted at the embassy, not at the ordinary Schengen VAC.[^610]

Not every person visiting relatives in Italy qualifies for these family-member protections, and not every family visit involves permanent reunification. The failure is TVC's **one-size-fits-all presentation** of a family-visa service using standard tourist-visa fees, insurance, and appointment rules without distinguishing the protected family categories or the national family-reunification route. Following those instructions could result in an incorrect application category, unnecessary fees, or the wrong filing channel.

#### Romania and Bulgaria: Partial-Schengen Wording Retained After Full Accession

TVC's *Romania Tourist Visa* page describes Romanian Schengen participation specifically for **air and sea borders**.[^612] Its *Thailand TDAC vs Schengen ETIAS* guide repeats that **Bulgaria and Romania joined air and sea borders in early 2024**, telling travelers to verify the land-border rules.[^613]

**The EU removed internal land-border checks with and between Romania and Bulgaria on January 1, 2025, completing both countries' full Schengen integration.**[^614] Neither page explains that subsequent change despite claiming a June 2026 review.

The problem is incomplete historical guidance for overland travel and Schengen itinerary planning. The pages do not explicitly say that land travel is prohibited, and this evidence does not establish unnecessary visa sales.

#### ETIAS: Outdated €7 Fee and Unconfirmed Launch

TVC's *Thailand TDAC vs Schengen ETIAS* guide describes ETIAS as a **“rolling launch”** and estimates the application fee at **roughly €7**.[^613]

**The European Commission's live notice says ETIAS is not yet operating, applications are not being accepted, and a confirmed launch date will be announced in advance.** The official fee has increased from €7 to **€20**, payable when ETIAS becomes operational.[^615]

ETIAS is a future travel authorization for **visa-exempt** travelers to participating European countries, not a substitute for the Schengen visa generally required of ordinary Thai tourists. TVC's launch wording and obsolete fee risk confusing readers about whether they can apply today and what the authorization will cost.

The Commission announced the higher fee in 2025, before TVC's claimed June 2026 review. The available evidence does not indicate that TVC actually sold ETIAS authorizations.

#### Portugal: Embassy Information Link Opens British Government Website

TVC's *Portuguese Embassy Bangkok* page has a prominent **“Embassy information”** link directing readers to `https://www.gov.uk/world/thailand`—the UK government's services page for Thailand, **not the Portuguese Embassy**.[^616][^617]

The TVC page elsewhere names Portuguese government visa resources, but the incorrect embassy link can send applicants seeking Portugal's official instructions to an unrelated government. This is a verifiable destination-link mistake on the commercial service guide, not proof of incorrect visa decisions or charges.

### New Caledonia: Schengen Visa Advice for a Territory Outside Schengen

TVC's *New Caledonia Visa Thailand* page repeatedly claims that a France-issued **Schengen short-stay visa covers New Caledonia**. It instructs Thai visitors to treat the French overseas collectivity as their main Schengen destination, obtain €30,000 Schengen medical insurance, pay Schengen visa fees, and observe the Schengen 90/180-day stay limit.[^582]

**France's official visa authority explicitly states that a Schengen visa does not by itself authorize entry to France's non-European territories, including New Caledonia.** Travelers who need visas for these territories must obtain the appropriate French overseas-territory visa. Someone visiting mainland France and an overseas territory may need two distinct visas.[^583]

Eligibility for an overseas-territory exemption can depend on nationality, residence status, qualifying long-stay visas, or specific multiple-entry visas. **The documented failure is TVC's identification of an ordinary Schengen visa as the correct authorization**, not a claim that every Thai passport holder must apply for a new visa.

TVC advertises document preparation based on the wrong visa rules, potentially causing unnecessary applications or travel disruption. The page claims a June 2026 review while using Schengen policies for a destination outside the Schengen Area. No specific paid applications or refused entries have been established.

### France: Visa Appointment Guidance Names the Wrong Provider in Thailand

TVC's *French Embassy Bangkok - Apply from Thailand* guide tells applicants that most French visa applications are completed with an appointment at the **French Embassy Bangkok or VFS**, presenting VFS as an official application route.[^593]

**France-Visas directs applicants in Thailand to register with TLScontact**, the designated application centre for French visa submissions in Bangkok. Its country-specific instructions identify the French Embassy and TLScontact—not VFS—as the relevant channels.[^594]

A traveler following TVC's VFS instructions could search for a non-existent French application appointment there, waste time, or be misdirected to an unrelated provider. This is separate from whether the traveler requires a French visa: the documented error is **which provider receives visa applications in Thailand**.

TVC claims the guide was reviewed in June 2026 and sells translation and document-preparation assistance alongside its appointment guidance. The available evidence does not establish that TVC booked incorrect appointments for particular clients.

### Singapore: Visa Requirements Invented for Thai Tourists

TVC's *Singapore Visa for Thailand - Apply from Thailand* page claims Thai citizens need advance Singapore visas for tourism, business visits, and certain transit arrangements. It says Thailand is a visa-required nationality, warns that travelers departing Bangkok without visa approval could be denied boarding, and directs applicants through Form 14A, financial documentation, authorized agents, and government processing fees.[^558]

**Singapore's Immigration & Checkpoints Authority (ICA) does not include Thailand on its visa-required nationality list.**[^559] Thailand's Ministry of Foreign Affairs independently confirms visa-free visits of up to **30 days** for qualifying Thai ordinary passport holders.[^560] Travelers still must meet Singapore's entry conditions, including submitting the SG Arrival Card where applicable; immigration officers determine admission and permitted stay. The exemption does not authorize employment or other activities requiring separate permission.

The same commercial page includes a **Schengen Area “main destination” rule** and **€30,000 Schengen medical insurance requirement** in its Singapore application instructions.[^558] Singapore is not in the Schengen Area, and neither requirement applies to an ordinary Singapore tourist visit. The unrelated material raises additional questions about the page's claimed June 2026 editorial review.

For qualifying Thai tourists, the promoted Singapore visa application and accompanying professional preparation are unnecessary.

### South Korea: Denial of Visa-Free Entry and Incorrect K-ETA Advice

TVC's *South Korea Visa Thailand - Apply from Thailand* page says Thai passport holders cannot use K-ETA, must obtain C-3 embassy visas for tourism, and make a mistake if they pursue K-ETA instead. Its instructions promote visa forms, financial and employment records, appointments, and document-preparation assistance.[^561]

**South Korea has a bilateral visa-waiver agreement with Thailand covering qualifying ordinary passport holders for visits of up to 90 days.**[^562] The Korean Embassy in Bangkok confirms that eligible K-ETA travelers retain the 90-day stay entitlement.[^563] K-ETA is an electronic travel authorization, not an embassy visa. Thailand is not covered by the temporary nationality-wide K-ETA exemption through December 31, 2026, so eligible adult Thai tourists ordinarily require approved K-ETA before boarding, subject to individual exceptions and admission requirements.[^564]

The Korean Embassy's July 2026 C-3-9 tourist-visa guidance additionally requires eligible applicants to show a **prior K-ETA refusal** before pursuing that visa route.[^565] TVC therefore not only misidentifies Thai travelers as needing visas; it presents the embassy application as the normal first step when the embassy ordinarily requires an unsuccessful K-ETA application first.

A C-3 visa or another appropriate visa may still be necessary after K-ETA refusal or for activities outside the visa waiver. That does not support TVC's categorical claim that Thai tourists cannot use visa-free entry.

### China: Tourist-Visa Sales Page Contradicts a Bilateral Waiver and TVC's Own Guide

TVC's *Chinese Tourist Visa - Apply from Thailand* page claims Thai holidaymakers need L-category visa stickers. It directs travelers to complete visa applications, prepare employer letters and bank statements, attend appointments, pay fees, and use document-preparation and translation services. Its discussion of limited visa-free transit also omits the separate exemption for ordinary Thai tourists.[^566]

**Thailand and China have had a reciprocal visa-exemption agreement in force since March 1, 2024.** Thailand's Ministry of Foreign Affairs confirms that qualifying ordinary passport holders may visit without visas for **up to 30 days per entry**, subject to a cumulative **90 days in any 180-day period**.[^567] The Chinese Embassy in Bangkok confirms the exemption and distinguishes activities or longer stays that require visas.[^568]

TVC's separate *Thailand and China Visa Waiver* guide itself acknowledges that Thai passport holders benefit from this bilateral arrangement.[^569] Its commercial Chinese tourist-visa page therefore contradicts another page on the same website.

The Chinese Embassy reaffirmed in September 2026 that the bilateral exemption remained effective.[^570] The exemption is not merely a transit concession. Qualifying Thai tourists do not need the L-category visas presented as standard requirements by TVC's commercial page.

### Japan: Tourist Visa Sales Page Denies Exemption in Force Since 2013

Thai Visa Centre's *Japan Visa for Thai Citizens - Apply from Thailand* page falsely claims that Thai passport holders must obtain temporary visitor visas before traveling to Japan for tourism or short family visits.[^576]

The page explicitly states that Japan does not grant Thai citizens visa-free entry and repeatedly directs travelers to obtain visa stickers through the Japanese Embassy or accredited travel agencies.

**Japan has permitted qualifying Thai ordinary electronic passport holders to enter visa-free for short visits of up to 15 days since July 1, 2013.**[^577]

The Embassy of Japan in Thailand confirms that the exemption covers tourism, visits to relatives, and qualifying short-term business activities.[^578]

The exemption requires a valid ordinary IC passport, an eligible short-term purpose, and a stay not exceeding 15 days. Travelers intending longer stays, employment, or activities outside the exemption must obtain appropriate visas.

Immigration officers may request evidence of return travel, accommodation, sufficient funds, and an itinerary. Visa exemption does not guarantee admission.

TVC's commercial page nevertheless instructs ordinary Thai tourists to:

- Complete Japanese visitor visa application forms.
- Prepare bank statements, employment certificates, and detailed itineraries.
- Arrange visa application appointments.
- Pay application charges and wait for visa processing.
- Collect embassy-issued visa stickers before departure.

TVC promotes its own document-preparation, translation, and application-review assistance alongside these requirements.[^576]

**For Thai tourists qualifying for Japan's 15-day visa exemption, the advertised embassy visa application process is unnecessary.**

The page also describes direct applications at the Japanese Embassy in Bangkok as a standard option, despite the embassy's published instructions directing ordinary visa applications through the Japan Visa Application Centre (JVAC) and designated intake centres.[^578]

TVC claims its guidance was reviewed in June 2026, more than 12 years after Japan introduced the exemption.

The available evidence does not establish deliberate deception or completed unnecessary sales.

However, **TVC commercially promotes Japanese tourist visa assistance while categorically denying a visa exemption that Japan's government has officially recognized since 2013.**

### India: Official Visa-Fee Instructions Omit Free Tourist e-Visas Introduced in September 2026

TVC's *Indian Visa for Thai Citizens* page correctly explains that Thai tourists can apply for Indian electronic tourist visas. Its application process nevertheless instructs travelers to **“Pay official visa fee”** and says to pay online for e-Visa applications, without identifying the free short-stay option for Thai citizens.[^579]

The Embassy of India in Bangkok announced on **September 11, 2026** that Thai nationals could receive **30-day, double-entry e-Tourist visas without a visa fee** until **December 31, 2027**, unless the government issues further orders.[^580] **Travelers must still apply for and obtain the visa** through the official government portal; this is a fee exemption, not visa-free entry. Other visa categories and durations can have different charges.[^581]

For eligible Thai tourists using the 30-day e-Tourist category, TVC's categorical payment step is outdated. The page also promotes its own document-preparation assistance, although India's official electronic application guidance states that intermediaries are not needed to submit an e-Visa application.[^581]

The page's latest claimed review was **June 2026**, before the September announcement, so this is a **failure to update later guidance**, not evidence that its earlier review ignored a rule that had not yet been announced. The evidence does not establish that TVC charged customers the waived government fee or intentionally concealed the change.

### Canada: Mandatory Visitor-Visa Claims Omit an eTA Alternative

TVC's *Canada Tourist Visa: Apply from Thailand* page labels Thai passport holders as **“Visa required”**, states that they need an approved Temporary Resident Visa (TRV) before boarding, and marks tourism and family visits **“Yes: TRV”** in its eligibility table.[^571] The guide promotes document preparation, certified translations, financial evidence, and biometric appointments, although it also contains a general reminder to confirm eligibility with Immigration, Refugees and Citizenship Canada (IRCC).

**Canada has permitted certain Thai passport holders to fly to Canada with an electronic travel authorization (eTA), instead of a visitor visa, since June 6, 2023.**[^572] IRCC confirms that eligible travelers must fly to or transit through a Canadian airport, intend a temporary visit (normally up to six months), and have held a Canadian visitor visa in the preceding 10 years **or** hold a valid U.S. nonimmigrant visa when applying for eTA.[^573]

The eTA costs **CAD $7**, while a visitor visa application ordinarily costs **CAD $100**, plus **CAD $85** for biometrics when required.[^574][^575] An eligible eTA traveler generally need not complete the full TRV application or attend a biometric appointment.

This is a **conditional** exemption, unlike the broader short-visit arrangements for Singapore, South Korea, and China. Thai travelers who do not meet eTA criteria, or who plan to enter Canada by land or sea, ordinarily still require a visitor visa. TVC's introductory mention of alternative authorization does not correct its categorical nationality table or explain the eTA route for eligible Thai tourists.

**The omission could direct eligible travelers toward more expensive government applications and professional assistance that they do not need.** It does not prove that TVC intentionally concealed eTA eligibility or sold unnecessary services to particular customers. The page's claimed June 2026 review came three years after Canada introduced the program.

### Australia: Prospective Marriage Visa Timeline Incorrectly Fixed at Nine Months

TVC's *Australian Visa: Apply from Thailand* guide lists the **subclass 300 Prospective Marriage visa** for fiancés marrying in Australia **“within 9 months.”**[^591]

Australia's Department of Home Affairs instead states that subclass 300 visas permit eligible holders to live, work and study in Australia for **9 to 15 months from the date of grant**, and the holder must marry the specified partner while the visa remains valid.[^592] The exact period is determined by the grant, not a universal nine-month deadline.

**TVC's fixed nine-month description understates the potential permitted period and omits the need to check the individual visa's expiry date.** That distinction can affect wedding planning, international travel, and when a couple must pursue a subsequent partner visa.

The error is a misstatement of visa conditions, not evidence that a Thai fiancé can enter Australia without a visa or that TVC has mishandled an individual application.

### United Kingdom: Repeated Physical-Visa Instructions and Incorrect Fiancé IHS Charges

TVC's *UK Visa from Thailand - Guide for Thai Citizens* directs new applicants to receive a passport **vignette** and then **collect a biometric residence permit (BRP) in the UK**.[^589] This is not an isolated outdated checklist; the same physical-document assumptions recur across the agency's UK service guides.

#### Physical Visa Vignettes and BRP Collection Repeated Across UK Guides

The following pages direct applicants to collect a visa vignette, receive a passport entry sticker, or travel on a newly issued vignette:

- **UK Visa Overview:** Describes the successful application as a passport returned with a vignette.[^595]
- **UK Visitor Visa:** Directs approved applicants to collect a passport with vignette.[^596]
- **UK Tourist Visa:** Instructs applicants to collect an entry vignette and travel during its validity.[^597]
- **UK Visa Application Centre / VFS Thailand:** Describes passport collection as receiving a vignette or refusal.[^598]
- **British Embassy Thailand:** Includes collecting a passport with a vignette in its UKVI application sequence.[^599]
- **British Embassy Visa Applications:** Repeats vignette collection as the normal result of approval.[^600]
- **UK Family Visa:** Directs approved applicants to receive a vignette and then collect a BRP after arrival.[^601]
- **UK Spouse Visa:** Again instructs visa holders to collect a vignette and a BRP.[^602]
- **UK Unmarried/De Facto Partner Visa:** Describes approval as receiving a passport vignette.[^603]
- **Both dedicated UK Fiancé Visa guides:** Tell applicants to enter the UK using an issued vignette.[^604][^605]

**The UK Home Office confirms that new visa vignette stickers stopped being issued to successful applicants on July 1, 2026.** Successful new applicants instead receive an **eVisa**, accessed through a UK Visas and Immigration (UKVI) account. The Home Office had already stopped issuing new BRPs in 2024.[^590][^607] Applicants should check their UKVI account and link their current travel document, rather than expect a fresh entry sticker or a new BRP collection appointment.

Legacy physical documents can remain valid until expiry, and certain exceptional travelers may receive a **form accompanying an eVisa (FAV)** that requires collection at a Visa Application Centre. Those exceptions do not support presenting an ordinary new passport sticker and BRP as the standard route for all applicants.

TVC's separate *UK Visa Vignette Transfer* service also describes replacing an expired 30-day entry sticker and then collecting a BRP in the UK.[^606] Cases involving still-valid legacy documents require individual verification against UKVI rules; the guide should not imply that vignette replacement and BRP collection remain routine features of new visa grants.

#### Fiancé-Visa Guides Incorrectly Require the Immigration Health Surcharge

TVC's two dedicated fiancé-visa pages explicitly instruct Thai applicants to **pay the visa fee and Immigration Health Surcharge (IHS)** when applying from Thailand for six-month permission to enter the UK to marry.[^604][^605]

**GOV.UK expressly exempts applications made from outside the UK for visas lasting six months or less from the IHS.**[^608] A qualifying initial six-month fiancé or proposed civil partner entry visa therefore does **not** require this payment. If the person marries and subsequently applies inside the UK for further permission under the spouse or partner route, the IHS may then become payable as part of that later application.

The distinction directly affects cost planning. TVC conflates the initial temporary fiancé entry process with the later in-country family-visa application, despite presenting the fee instruction as a mandatory step in the initial route.

#### Significance Across the UK Service Pages

The pattern spans visitor, spouse, fiancé, family, embassy, and VAC guides. The eVisa change took effect **after** the pages' claimed June 2026 reviews, so the absence of a later update is the issue for new vignette claims; the discontinued BRP process was already obsolete before those reviews. The fiancé IHS exemption is a separate longstanding rule, not a consequence of the digital transition.

These pages market document preparation and checklist reviews while giving applicants outdated instructions about visa evidence or incorrect payment obligations. The evidence documents the errors, **not** specific unnecessary payments, actual immigration refusals, or intentional deception.

### United States: K-1 and Immigrant Spouse Visa Application and Fee Errors

Thai Visa Centre's FAQ *US Spouse Visa Cost: CR-1, IR-1, and K-1 Fees*, marked reviewed June 2026, presents a combined breakdown of U.S. government charges for couples pursuing different immigration pathways.[^251]

The guide identifies two routes:

- K-1 fiancé visas for couples intending to marry in the United States.
- CR-1/IR-1 immigrant spouse visas for couples already legally married.

However, its consolidated fee table lists charges without adequately distinguishing which apply to each route.

**The resulting guidance could lead applicants to budget for the wrong government fees or overlook charges actually required for their visa category.**

#### K-1 Applicants Do Not Pay the Standard NVC Immigrant-Visa Fees

TVC's government-fee table lists:

| Fee | TVC's quoted amount |
|---|---:|
| Form I-129F petition | $675 |
| NVC immigrant-visa processing | $325 |
| NVC Affidavit of Support processing | $120 |

The FAQ describes the $325 fee as covering Form DS-260 processing and the $120 fee as covering Form I-864 financial-document review.[^251]

These NVC fees relate to immigrant-visa processing, including applicable CR-1/IR-1 cases.

**K-1 fiancé applicants follow a different consular process.**

The U.S. Department of State confirms that K-1 applicants complete Form DS-160 rather than the immigrant-visa Form DS-260.[^252]

They pay the K-category nonimmigrant visa application fee, currently $265 per applicant.[^253]

The National Visa Center still assigns and forwards approved K-1 petitions to the appropriate embassy. However, this administrative role does not make the ordinary NVC immigrant-visa processing and Affidavit of Support fees applicable to K-1 cases.

TVC's fee breakdown fails to clearly explain that distinction.

#### Its Fee Table Omits the $265 K-1 Consular Charge

The Department of State's published fee schedule expressly identifies $265 for K-category visa applications.[^253]

That charge is separate from the initial USCIS Form I-129F petition fee.

TVC identifies the I-129F fee but omits the $265 K-1 consular charge from its table of typical government fees.

It instead directs attention to the $325 immigrant-visa processing fee and $120 NVC Affidavit of Support fee.

**The problem is not merely a missing fee: the table places charges from different visa procedures together without a clear indication of which customers must pay them.**

#### Financial-Support Forms Apply at Different Stages

The same confusion affects the financial-support requirements.

The Department of State explains that K-1 applicants may be asked to provide Form I-134 during consular processing.[^252]

Form I-864 ordinarily becomes relevant later, when the K-1 beneficiary marries the petitioner and applies for adjustment of status in the United States.

By contrast, qualifying CR-1/IR-1 immigrant-visa cases use Form I-864 during the immigrant-visa process and may require payment of the NVC Affidavit of Support fee.[^254]

TVC correctly acknowledges that K-1 beneficiaries ordinarily need adjustment of status after marriage.

However, it does not adequately separate the financial-support documentation and fees applicable before entry from those arising after marriage.

#### I-130 Online Filing Is Also Cheaper Than TVC States

TVC quotes $675 as the Form I-130 filing fee without distinguishing paper and online submissions.[^251]

USCIS's official fee schedule identifies:

- **Paper filing:** $675.
- **Online filing:** $625.[^255]

The $50 difference is relatively modest, but it illustrates the same problem: the FAQ presents a single government charge where the official schedule provides different options.

#### CR-1 Spouse-Visa Guide Repeatedly Directs Applicants to the Wrong Form

TVC's *US CR1 Visa Thailand - Apply from Thailand* guide, marked reviewed June 2026, repeatedly tells immigrant-spouse applicants to complete **Form DS-160**. The instruction appears in its visa-journey overview, numbered application process, detailed walkthrough, and FAQ.[^634]

**The U.S. Department of State instead requires Form DS-260, the Immigrant Visa and Alien Registration Application, for CR-1 and IR-1 immigrant spouse visas.** DS-160 is the application form used for nonimmigrant visas, including K-1 fiancé visas.[^635][^252]

TVC's separate *US Spousal CR1 Visa Requirements in Thailand* guide correctly identifies DS-260, including in its National Visa Center checklist and step-by-step instructions.[^636]

**The same agency consequently publishes contradictory instructions about the principal government application required for the same immigrant-spouse procedure.** This is particularly relevant because TVC advertises paid document preparation, certified translations, and checklist reviews. The evidence does not establish that any customer actually submitted the wrong form or incurred a processing delay.

#### CR-1 and IR-1 Guidance Misstates the Two-Year Marriage Cutoff

TVC's dedicated CR-1 guide describes CR-1 as appropriate when the marriage is under two years old **at visa issuance** and IR-1 as appropriate once the marriage has reached two years at issuance.[^634] Its separate spouse-visa requirements guide repeats that issuance-date distinction and associates the categories with conditional or unconditional residence on entry.[^636]

The State Department explains that a spouse's permanent resident status is **conditional if the marriage is less than two years old when the spouse enters the United States on an immigrant visa**. For conditional residents, the spouses generally must petition USCIS to remove conditions during the 90-day period preceding the second anniversary of admission.[^635]

Visa classification printed at issuance and the legal consequences of admission should not be conflated. If the couple reaches its second wedding anniversary after the visa is issued but before the immigrant spouse enters the United States, the two dates produce different answers to TVC's simplified rule.

**TVC should distinguish visa classification at issuance from conditional permanent-resident status at admission**, and advise applicants to verify the correct classification when the relevant anniversary falls between those events. This discrepancy does not establish that a particular applicant received the wrong status.

### Commercial Significance of the Foreign-Visa Guidance Errors

Across these guides, TVC presents visas as mandatory for travelers who may qualify for visa-free entry or simpler electronic authorization, recommends a Schengen route that does not authorize travel to New Caledonia, quotes outdated Schengen and Indian tourist-visa fees, repeats superseded ETIAS pricing and Schengen-border information, misstates Schengen filing jurisdiction and appointment providers, directs Greece applicants toward an unsupported VFS route, advertises a foreign-visa hub that redirects to its homepage, misdirects Portuguese embassy enquiries, misidentifies the French visa appointment provider, conflates Italian family reunification with ordinary Schengen visits, and gives outdated Australian and UK instructions (including incorrect UK fiancé surcharge advice) while conflating separate U.S. visa charges, repeatedly directing CR-1 applicants to the wrong application form, and misstating the conditional-residence cutoff. Its advertised preparation and translation services make these errors commercially consequential.

Singapore, South Korea, China, and Japan involve short-visit entry exemptions, with Japan limited to qualifying Thai IC passport holders and stays up to 15 days. Canada's eTA route depends on additional travel and visa-history criteria. India's 30-day e-Tourist visa still requires approval, but no visa fee under its specific current scheme. New Caledonia has distinct French overseas-territory visa rules. The Italy, Spain, Sweden, and Netherlands tourist-visa pages repeat **one EU-wide fee mistake**; Italy's family-visa page separately misstates the protected family-member filing route and fee. Romania and Bulgaria's Schengen integration and ETIAS illustrate outdated European entry guidance; Portugal and France illustrate faulty application links or procedures. Australia, the UK, and U.S. examples concern visa conditions, documents or costs.

**The evidence documents inaccurate commercial guidance and potential unnecessary costs; it does not establish deliberate deception, completed unnecessary sales, or incorrect charges in private consultations.** Additional verified foreign-visa errors should be incorporated as subsections here, with their individual official sources, instead of multiplying top-level investigations.

## Misleading Five-Year O-A Retirement Extension Claims

Thai Visa Centre repeatedly advertises a supposed **five-year extension of the Non-Immigrant O-A retirement visa**, claiming that foreigners become eligible after holding O-A status for three years.

Its published guidance confuses two legally distinct immigration categories: the standard O-A retirement visa and the longer-term O-X visa.

### Repeated Claims Across Retirement Guides

Several examples appear on the agency's website:

- **Retire in Thailand:** Lists a "5-year O-A extension" for retirees with at least three years on O-A, promising up to five years per extension.[^71]
- **Thailand Retirement Visa Requirements:** Claims the five-year extension requires three years on O-A and a 3 million baht bank deposit.[^72]
- **Retiring in Thailand:** Advertises the same five-year O-A extension as an alternative to ordinary annual renewals.[^73]
- **Options for a 60-Year-Old Traveling Freely:** Again claims that five-year O-A extensions become available after three years, subject to a higher deposit.[^74]

These statements present the supposed extension as a progression available to established O-A retirees.

### Contradiction With Official Visa Categories

Thailand's official retirement visa framework distinguishes:

| Category | Permitted stay |
|---|---|
| Non-Immigrant O | Initially 90 days, with qualifying extensions |
| Non-Immigrant O-A | One-year long-stay category |
| Non-Immigrant O-X | Five years per period, up to ten years total |

The Royal Thai Consulate-General in Los Angeles explicitly identifies O-A as a one-year category and O-X as the five-year category.[^70]

O-X has separate eligibility restrictions, including a limited list of nationalities, substantial financial requirements, and mandatory insurance.

**There is no general government rule allowing O-A holders to obtain five-year extensions simply because they have maintained their status for three years.**

### Thai Visa Centre Contradicts Itself

The most revealing evidence comes from Thai Visa Centre's own dedicated *5-Year Thai Retirement Visa* guide.

That article correctly identifies the five-year category as **Non-Immigrant O-X**, explains that it differs from annual O-A extensions, and specifically warns against describing O-X as a simple five-year extension of an existing O-A visa.[^75]

Yet the company's other retirement guides continue doing precisely that.

The conflicting pages all claim to have been reviewed in June 2026.

### Financial Consequences for Retirees

This is particularly concerning because the misleading guidance links the supposed extension to a **3 million baht deposit**.

A retiree following these instructions could plan major bank transfers or immigration applications around a benefit that does not exist in the advertised form.

The distinction also affects nationality eligibility, health insurance, visa application procedures, and whether an applicant must continue renewing annually.

**Thai Visa Centre markets professional retirement-visa expertise while repeatedly advertising an O-A extension pathway contradicted by official government rules and by its own specialist guide.**

The inconsistency raises questions about the accuracy of the agency's financial planning advice and whether customers are receiving reliable explanations before committing substantial funds.

## DTV Application Guides Contradict the Agency's Own August 2026 Rule Announcement

Thai Visa Centre publicly acknowledged major changes to Thailand's Destination Thailand Visa (DTV) application requirements effective **August 31, 2026**.

However, several of its other immigration guides continue providing instructions based on the superseded requirements, including advice concerning applications from neighboring countries and documentation that no longer satisfies the revised rules.

**The contradiction is particularly serious because the agency published a correct explanation of the changes while continuing to promote inconsistent application instructions elsewhere on its own website.**

### Thai Visa Centre Acknowledged the New Restrictions

In its article *DTV Application Changes From 31 August*, Thai Visa Centre correctly identifies two important changes:

- New DTV applications must be submitted in the applicant's country of citizenship or permanent residence.
- Applicants must provide a police clearance certificate.

The article explicitly warns that applications submitted while visiting a third country are generally refused, including applications at nearby embassies used by foreigners living in Thailand.[^76]

These restrictions are independently confirmed by announcements from Thailand's Ministry of Foreign Affairs and overseas diplomatic missions.

The Royal Thai Embassy in Moscow confirms that the previous requirement to document an applicant's current location was replaced with proof of permanent residence. It also confirms that criminal-record clearance became mandatory from August 31, 2026.[^80]

The Royal Thai Embassy in Vientiane provides an especially clear example: DTV applicants applying there must be Lao nationals or permanent residents of Laos.[^81]

**Thai Visa Centre therefore had direct knowledge of the new requirements and published them for its customers.**

### Its Main DTV Sales Page Still Lists Superseded Documents

Despite that announcement, Thai Visa Centre's primary DTV service page continues listing a **"Document indicating current location"** as a required document for workcation, Thai soft-power, and dependent applications.[^77]

That was the earlier requirement expressly replaced by the August 2026 changes.

The same page provides extensive additional-document checklists without identifying the new mandatory police clearance certificate.

Instead, it lists requirements including:

- Six months of salary or income evidence.
- Employment contracts authenticated by embassies.
- Company-registration documents authenticated by embassies.
- Six months of residence in Thailand for dependent applicants.

Some embassies may request additional documentation depending on the application category and jurisdiction.

However, these additional requirements do not substitute for the two revised requirements introduced nationally in August 2026.

**The agency's primary commercial DTV page still instructs customers to prepare superseded location evidence while omitting a newly mandatory criminal-record document.**

### Its Step-by-Step Guide Encourages Applications From Third Countries

The contradiction extends to Thai Visa Centre's guide *How to Get a Destination Thailand Visa*.[^78]

Its document checklist recommends proof that the applicant is outside Thailand, including an entry stamp from another country.

The guide also tells people currently in Thailand that they must leave and apply from another country.

Later, it specifically identifies Vientiane, Phnom Penh, Ho Chi Minh City, and Kuala Lumpur as nearby application locations, explaining that its team advises customers on embassy selection.

But simply traveling to a neighboring country no longer establishes eligibility to apply there.

The Royal Thai Embassy in Vientiane expressly requires DTV applicants to be Lao nationals or permanent residents of Laos under the rules effective August 31, 2026.[^81]

**An entry stamp proving that an applicant has temporarily entered Laos is not evidence of permanent residence in Laos.**

Thai Visa Centre's general recommendation to apply from another country therefore omits a decisive limitation that the agency itself acknowledges elsewhere.

The distinction matters because applicants following the earlier border-run approach could incur travel expenses, visa fees, and document-preparation costs for applications they are not eligible to submit.

### The Claimed 2026 Requirements Guide Also Omits the Change

Thai Visa Centre publishes another article titled *DTV Visa Thailand 2026: Official Specs, Documents, Financial Proof, and Refusal Risks*.[^79]

The introduction describes that article as the agency's requirements reference for 2026.

Yet its document checklist treats proof of legal residence as something required only **"where required"**, rather than explaining the revised permanent-residence requirement.

It also omits the mandatory criminal-record clearance certificate from its general applicant checklist.

These omissions are especially conspicuous because the article presents itself as authoritative guidance about application requirements and reasons for refusal.

**Thai Visa Centre's supposedly comprehensive 2026 requirements reference does not consistently reflect the changes its own separate announcement correctly describes.**

### Confusing Alternative Visa Categories With Mandatory Conditions

The agency's main DTV sales page contains another significant eligibility problem.

Under its general eligibility criteria, the page states that applicants must have proof of employment or freelance work and must participate in Thai soft-power activities.[^77]

However, these are different qualifying pathways.

Thailand's official DTV framework distinguishes between:

1. Workcation applicants, including remote workers and freelancers.
2. Participants in qualifying Thai soft-power activities, including cultural training and medical treatment.
3. Eligible spouses and dependent children of DTV holders.

The Royal Thai Embassy in Helsinki identifies these categories separately, with different supporting documents applicable to each.[^82]

**A digital nomad does not have to enroll in a Muay Thai or cooking course merely to qualify through the workcation category. Likewise, a medical-treatment applicant does not have to establish foreign employment solely to qualify through the soft-power category.**

Presenting both conditions as generally mandatory misstates the structure of the visa.

### Contradictions Across the Same Commercial Website

The most revealing feature of these inconsistencies is that Thai Visa Centre does not need to rely on outside commentators to identify the correct rules.

Its own August 2026 announcement already explains them.

Yet visitors encountering its commercial application pages and detailed guides receive materially different information.

The contradictions concern:

- Where applicants are permitted to submit DTV applications.
- Whether temporary presence in another country is sufficient.
- Which identity and residence documents are mandatory.
- Whether criminal-record clearance is required.
- Whether separate qualifying visa categories impose cumulative requirements.

The available publications do not establish that Thai Visa Centre submitted applications contrary to the new rules or that particular customers suffered financial losses.

**They do establish that the agency promotes professional DTV application assistance while publishing inconsistent instructions about fundamental eligibility and documentary requirements.**

This is not an obscure technical distinction. Applying in the wrong country or omitting mandatory supporting documents can directly affect whether an application is accepted.

The continued publication of contradictory guidance also raises questions about the agency's editorial controls, especially because its other articles claim to provide current immigration expertise.

**Thai Visa Centre correctly warned customers about the new DTV restrictions, then left commercial guidance online that could direct those same customers toward the very application mistakes it had identified.**

### DTV Extension Page Confuses Government Fees With Visa and Agency Charges

Thai Visa Centre's commercial *DTV Visa Thailand* page repeatedly lists the cost of extending a DTV holder's stay for another 180 days as **1,900–10,000 baht**.[^221]

Under its frequently asked questions, the company explicitly describes this range as a fee payable at immigration.

However, Thailand's Department of Consular Affairs distinguishes two separate charges:

- **10,000 baht:** Government fee for initial DTV visa issuance.
- **1,900 baht:** Immigration fee for extending an existing DTV stay by up to 180 days.[^222]

The agency's advertised 10,000-baht upper limit therefore does not reflect the published government extension fee.

#### TVC's Own Pricing Page Confirms the Correct Amount
