The discrepancy is particularly significant because TVC's dedicated DTV assistance page publishes a detailed price breakdown.[^223]

| Charge | Existing customers | New customers |
|---|---:|---:|
| Government extension fee | ฿1,900 | ฿1,900 |
| TVC service fee | ฿10,100 | ฿12,100 |
| Advertised total | ฿12,000 | ฿14,000 |

Unlike its general visa sales page, this breakdown correctly distinguishes government charges from commercial assistance.

TVC's separate 2026 DTV requirements guide also identifies approximately **1,900 baht** as the extension fee.[^224]

**The company therefore publishes the correct government fee in its dedicated pricing materials while advertising a substantially higher government-fee range on its commercial visa overview.**

#### Consequences for Customers

The discrepancy could mislead applicants comparing self-service immigration extensions with paid professional assistance.

A customer relying on the visa overview might incorrectly believe the government itself can charge up to 10,000 baht for an ordinary DTV extension.

The distinction is especially important when TVC separately sells extension packages costing 12,000–14,000 baht.

Those packages include substantial private service charges, which should not be confused with mandatory immigration fees.

The available evidence does not establish that TVC has collected 10,000 baht while representing the entire amount as a government fee.

Nevertheless, the contradiction is concrete and financially relevant.

TVC should correct its DTV service page to identify the 1,900-baht government extension fee, distinguish it from the initial visa-issuance charge, and disclose optional agency assistance costs separately.

### November 2026 Update: TVC Adds Legal References Without Correcting Its DTV Application Advice

On November 22, 2026, Thai Visa Centre captured and republished the Department of Consular Affairs' official announcement concerning the revised Destination Thailand Visa application requirements.[^682]

The archived publication explicitly acknowledges that, effective August 31, 2026:

- Permanent-residence evidence replaced the previous requirement to demonstrate an applicant's current location.
- Criminal-record clearance became a mandatory supporting document.
- Applications submitted before the effective date remained subject to the previous requirements.

Despite publishing this information, Thai Visa Centre's commercial DTV application guides continued providing outdated instructions as of November 27, 2026.[^78][^79]

Notably, the articles now incorporate extensive legal reference tables and links to the company's expanding law archive.

Its dedicated 2026 DTV requirements guide includes eleven official references, while its step-by-step application guide includes twelve.

Nevertheless, the former continues presenting residence documentation as conditional and omits police clearance from its core document checklist.

The latter continues recommending nearby embassies in Laos, Cambodia, Vietnam, and Malaysia to applicants leaving Thailand, without clearly explaining the permanent-residence restriction.

**Thai Visa Centre has added an appearance of greater legal authority to its immigration publications without resolving the substantive regulatory errors contained within them.**

The problem is no longer simply that the agency has failed to publish the relevant government announcement. It has now incorporated that announcement into its own legal archive while continuing to distribute incompatible commercial advice.

Adding legislative citations does not make inaccurate application instructions reliable.

This discrepancy is especially concerning because the company advertises professional document review and embassy selection as services intended to help customers avoid unsuccessful applications and wasted visa fees.

Whether these inconsistencies result from automated content production, inadequate editorial oversight, or another cause remains unverified.

## Obsolete Retirement Visa Insurance Requirements Repeated Across Multiple Guides

Thai Visa Centre repeatedly publishes outdated health-insurance requirements for Thailand's Non-Immigrant O-A retirement visa, advising applicants that policies providing **40,000 baht in outpatient coverage and 400,000 baht in inpatient coverage** satisfy the relevant minimums.

Those figures no longer reflect the general insurance requirement for new O-A applications.

Thailand increased the required medical coverage to **3 million baht or US$100,000**, effective October 1, 2021.

The revised requirement has been confirmed by multiple Thai diplomatic missions, including government guidance updated during 2026.[^87][^88]

**Thai Visa Centre continues presenting the superseded insurance limits as current requirements, despite marketing itself as a specialist in retirement visas and advising customers on the purchase of qualifying insurance.**

### The Dedicated Retirement Insurance Guide Uses the Old Figures

Thai Visa Centre's article *Health Insurance for Thailand Retirees: O-A Requirements and Alternatives* identifies itself as guidance specifically for Non-Immigrant O-A applicants.[^83]

It prominently advertises:

- Outpatient minimum: 40,000 baht.
- Inpatient minimum: 400,000 baht.
- Effective date: October 2019.

The article explicitly instructs customers to purchase policies meeting both limits and describes those amounts as the minimum coverage necessary for approval.

It discusses initial embassy applications, annual extensions, insurance renewals, and the risk of applications being rejected for inadequate coverage.

However, **the article never explains that the insurance requirement introduced in 2019 was subsequently increased in October 2021**.

The page claims to have been reviewed in June 2026, nearly five years after the change took effect.

### The Same Error Appears Throughout the Website

The outdated requirements are not confined to one article.

Thai Visa Centre repeats them across several publications:

**Do I Need Health Insurance for Thailand?**

The agency's FAQ identifies O-A applicants as requiring 40,000 baht outpatient and 400,000 baht inpatient coverage. It also instructs customers that their insurance certificates should demonstrate those specific minimum amounts.[^84]

**Health Insurance in Thailand**

This guide explicitly groups O-A and O-X applicants together, claiming that both visa categories require the same 40,000/400,000-baht insurance coverage.[^85]

**Thailand Retirement Visa**

The agency's principal retirement guide repeats the same lower limits while describing the insurance customers should purchase for O-A applications and extensions.[^86]

**Best Places to Retire in Thailand for Couples**

Even this retirement-planning article identifies the same outdated insurance thresholds when advising couples about qualifying for O-A visas.[^90]

**At least five separate publications repeat the obsolete figures, demonstrating a recurring problem with the agency's retirement-visa guidance rather than an isolated typographical error.**

### Thai Government Requirements Are Substantially Different

Thailand's current insurance requirements are readily available from official government sources.

The Royal Thai Consulate-General in Los Angeles, in guidance updated September 1, 2026, states that Non-Immigrant O-A applicants must maintain health insurance covering their stay, including COVID-19, with a minimum insured amount of 3 million baht or US$100,000.[^87]

The Royal Thai Embassy in Budapest confirmed the same requirement in February 2026.[^88]

These documents reflect the increase introduced on October 1, 2021, rather than the original 2019 insurance framework.[^89]

The difference is material.

A policy offering only 400,000 baht in inpatient benefits and 40,000 baht in outpatient benefits does not automatically satisfy a requirement for 3 million baht in overall medical coverage.

**A customer purchasing insurance solely on the basis of Thai Visa Centre's stated minimums could discover that the policy does not meet the requirements for an O-A visa application.**

This could result in additional insurance costs, replacement policies, delayed applications, or refusal to accept inadequate supporting documents.

### Confusing O-A and O-X Insurance Requirements

The source of the error becomes clearer when the government's separate retirement-visa categories are compared.

The Royal Thai Embassy in Copenhagen publishes distinct requirements for the two categories:

| Visa category | Published insurance requirement |
|---|---|
| Non-Immigrant O-A | At least US$100,000 or 3 million baht in medical coverage |
| Non-Immigrant O-X | At least 40,000 baht outpatient and 400,000 baht inpatient coverage |

These requirements appear separately in the same official embassy guidance.[^89]

The distinction is important because O-A and O-X are different visa categories with different eligibility conditions, financial requirements, and permitted periods of stay.

Yet Thai Visa Centre's general health-insurance guide combines the two categories and explicitly assigns both the lower insurance limits.[^85]

**The agency has effectively presented the insurance thresholds associated with the O-X category as sufficient for O-A applicants.**

This mirrors other documented problems with Thai Visa Centre's retirement guidance, including its repeated confusion between O-A and O-X visa durations and its promotion of a supposed five-year O-A extension pathway.

### Incorrect Restrictions on Foreign Insurance

Thai Visa Centre's retirement guide also states that O-A applicants require Thai health insurance from approved providers.[^86]

That description is unnecessarily restrictive.

The Royal Thai Consulate-General in Los Angeles and Royal Thai Embassy in Budapest both recognize qualifying insurance issued by Thai or foreign insurance companies, subject to the required coverage and documentation.[^87][^88]

Foreign insurance applicants may be required to obtain the prescribed Foreign Insurance Certificate, completed and endorsed by their insurer.

**A qualifying foreign insurance policy is not automatically disqualified merely because the insurer is not based in Thailand.**

By presenting Thai insurance as mandatory, the agency risks directing customers toward unnecessary replacement policies or narrower insurance choices than the official rules permit.

### Claimed Expertise Versus Repeated Regulatory Errors

Thai Visa Centre advertises retirement visas as one of its principal services.

Its website promotes assistance with insurance selection, bank documentation, visa applications, and annual extensions.

The company also repeatedly advises customers that its immigration guidance is current and that supporting documents must satisfy official requirements.

However, its insurance publications continue reproducing requirements superseded years earlier.

The consequences are more significant than outdated terminology.

Customers rely on an immigration agency's advice to decide which insurance policies to purchase, which financial commitments to make, and whether their applications are ready for submission.

**Thai Visa Centre presents itself as a retirement-visa specialist while repeatedly directing customers toward insurance limits that do not satisfy the current general O-A application requirement.**

The published record does not establish that particular customers purchased inadequate insurance or suffered application refusals because of this advice.

It does establish that the same material error appears across multiple pages claiming to provide current professional guidance.

The central question is why an agency whose business depends on understanding retirement-visa requirements continues publishing obsolete insurance figures nearly five years after the government increased the required coverage.


### November 2026 Update: TVC's Own Legal Archive Confirms Its Retirement Insurance Advice Is Incorrect

By November 2026, Thai Visa Centre had expanded its website to include an extensive legal archive featuring government regulations, Royal Gazette publications, and explanatory guides covering Thai immigration and expatriate affairs.

Notably, its dedicated guide concerning mandatory health insurance and Social Security correctly acknowledges the revised insurance requirements for Non-Immigrant O-A retirement visas.[^677]

The archive explicitly identifies Royal Thai Police Order 548/2562 as superseded by Royal Thai Police Order 654/2564 and correctly distinguishes between the insurance requirements applicable to O-A and O-X visa holders.

| Visa category | Requirements acknowledged in TVC's legal archive |
|---|---|
| Non-Immigrant O-A | 3 million baht or US$100,000 |
| Non-Immigrant O-X | 40,000 baht outpatient and 400,000 baht inpatient |

However, as of November 27, 2026, the company's dedicated retirement insurance guide, general health insurance guide, and health insurance FAQ continued presenting the superseded insurance thresholds as applicable to O-A applicants.[^83][^84][^85]

**Thai Visa Centre is now publishing the very regulations that contradict its own professional immigration advice.**

While expanding public access to Thai immigration legislation is potentially useful, publishing an extensive legal archive does not compensate for continuing to distribute materially incorrect advice to prospective paying customers.

The contradiction is particularly concerning given that Thai Visa Centre promotes its expertise in retirement applications and insurance compliance, while warning customers that inadequate insurance documentation can result in application delays or refusals.

The company should prioritize correcting its existing commercial guidance rather than allowing contradictory information to coexist across its expanding website.

Whether this inconsistency results from inadequate editorial oversight, automated content production, or another cause remains unverified. Nevertheless, the company is responsible for the accuracy of the immigration advice published under its brand.

## Inflated Government Visa Fees and Contradictory Service Prices

Thai Visa Centre publishes substantially inaccurate financial comparisons in its guide *Thai Elite Visa Cost: Thailand Privilege Membership Fees, Family Add-ons, Hidden Costs*.

The article promotes Thailand Privilege memberships costing between 650,000 and 5 million baht while comparing those memberships against alternative immigration options, including retirement visas and routine immigration-reporting services.[^95]

However, two of its advertised costs conflict directly with published government fees and the prices charged by a business within Thai Visa Centre's own network.

**The discrepancies concern figures customers may rely upon when deciding whether to purchase expensive immigration services.**

### O-A Embassy Visa Fee Exaggerated Tenfold

Under its comparison of Thailand Privilege memberships against alternative visas, Thai Visa Centre lists the upfront cost of a Non-Immigrant O-A retirement visa as approximately **US$2,000 in embassy fees**, alongside an 800,000-baht bank-balance requirement.[^95]

That description substantially exaggerates the actual government application fee.

The Royal Thai Consulate-General in Los Angeles publishes the following fees:

| Visa category | Official consular fee |
|---|---:|
| Non-Immigrant O retirement, single entry | US$80 |
| Non-Immigrant O retirement, multiple entry | US$200 |
| Non-Immigrant O-A long-stay retirement | US$200 |
| Non-Immigrant O-X long-stay retirement | US$400 |

These amounts appear together on the consulate's official visa-fee schedule.[^96]

**Thai Visa Centre's claimed US$2,000 O-A embassy fee is ten times the US$200 charge published by the Thai consulate.**

Consular fees may vary by country, but the agency provides no explanation or government reference supporting its US$2,000 figure.

The discrepancy is especially conspicuous because the article specifically describes the amount as an embassy fee, not as a combined estimate for private legal assistance, insurance, travel, document preparation, or other optional expenses.

### Confusing Financial Qualification With Application Costs

The same comparison adds an 800,000-baht bank-balance requirement to the purported embassy fee.

However, applicants' financial qualifications and the government's actual application charges are fundamentally different categories.

Thailand's O-A framework permits qualifying applicants to demonstrate sufficient funds through bank deposits, monthly income, or an acceptable combination, subject to the relevant consulate's documentation requirements.[^97]

A qualifying bank deposit remains the applicant's money. It is not a payment to the Thai government or an immigration agency.

The funds may need to remain available for specified periods, and applicants must satisfy the applicable financial requirements, but this does not convert the balance into an application fee.

**Thai Visa Centre's financial comparison combines an exaggerated government fee with a bank-balance requirement without adequately distinguishing actual expenditure from proof of financial eligibility.**

This matters because Thailand Privilege membership fees are substantial payments for a commercial membership, while retirement-visa financial requirements generally involve demonstrating access to qualifying funds.

Customers comparing the programs need to understand that distinction before deciding which route is financially appropriate.

### Its Own 90-Day Reporting Prices Contradict the Guide

Another discrepancy appears in the article's table of additional expenses.

Thai Visa Centre identifies 90-day immigration reporting as free when completed independently but lists its own paid reporting service at **3,959 baht through `90day.in.th`**.[^95]

However, the referenced website publishes substantially different prices:

- 500 baht per individual report.
- 375 baht per report for packages of four or more.
- Non-expiring prepaid reporting credits.

These prices are explicitly advertised by AGENTS CO., LTD., the operator of `90day.in.th` and Thai Visa Centre's publicly acknowledged digital operations company.[^98]

The 3,959-baht figure is nearly eight times the operator's advertised 500-baht single-report price.

Thai Visa Centre's cost guide does not identify a special reporting package, additional services, or any other basis explaining the difference.

**The agency's financial guide quotes a price for its affiliated immigration-reporting service that cannot be reconciled with the prices published by the service operator itself.**

The discrepancy does not establish that customers have actually been charged 3,959 baht for a single report.

It establishes that the company's published cost guidance is inconsistent with its associated service's advertised pricing.

### Outdated Membership Deadline Creates Additional Confusion

The same cost-comparison guide repeatedly states that Thailand Privilege's 650,000-baht Bronze membership was available only until **September 30, 2026**.[^95]

That deadline was subsequently extended.

On September 30, 2026, Thailand Privilege Card Co., Ltd. reportedly issued a further extension making Bronze memberships available until **December 31, 2026**.

Authorized sales agents including Siam Legal and Daimaru Trading published the revised deadline. Daimaru's announcement identifies the Thailand Privilege Card company notice as reference **TPC: SD26/4288**.[^99][^100]

Nevertheless, Thai Visa Centre's guide continues presenting September 30 as the closing date, including in its pricing introduction and frequently asked questions.

Its article identifies June 2026 as its last update, indicating that the published guidance has not been brought into line with the subsequently announced extension.

The outdated date could lead readers to conclude that the lowest-priced Thailand Privilege membership is unavailable when authorized agents continue offering it.

### The Direction of the Financial Errors

These discrepancies are not all equivalent.

The inflated O-A embassy fee makes a relatively inexpensive government visa application appear substantially more expensive.

The contradictory 90-day reporting price exaggerates the advertised cost of an associated commercial service.

The outdated Bronze deadline creates uncertainty about the continued availability of Thailand Privilege's least expensive membership.

Together, they undermine the article's stated purpose of helping customers make informed financial comparisons between immigration options.

**Thai Visa Centre publishes a cost guide intended to justify expensive long-term visa decisions while misstating a government application fee by a factor of ten and contradicting the published prices of its own affiliated reporting service.**

The available evidence does not establish that these discrepancies were introduced deliberately, that customers paid the inaccurate amounts, or that they were induced to purchase more expensive memberships.

Nevertheless, the errors are concrete, independently checkable, and directly relevant to the financial decisions the article is designed to influence.

### Relevance to the Broader Investigation

Thai Visa Centre has already been documented publishing incorrect retirement-visa durations, outdated insurance requirements, contradictory financial-deposit instructions, and unsupported Thailand Privilege accreditation claims.

The cost-comparison discrepancies add a distinct problem: **the reliability of the monetary figures the agency uses to compare immigration services and promote expensive membership products**.

A business advertising professional immigration expertise should distinguish government fees from private service charges, identify financial qualifications accurately, and reconcile its own published prices.

Thai Visa Centre's guide fails those basic tests.

The company should explain the basis for its US$2,000 embassy-fee claim, clarify why its reporting-service price differs from that published by AGENTS CO., LTD., and update its Thailand Privilege deadline information.

### Immigration Law Archive Misrepresents Statutory Fee Ceilings as Actual Charges

Thai Visa Centre's immigration law archive publishes a dedicated *Visa and Extension Fees* guide that presents the fee schedule established by the Immigration Act (No. 3) B.E. 2542 (1999) as an applicable government price list.[^660]

However, the guide fails to distinguish between statutory maximum fees and the actual charges prescribed by subsequent ministerial regulations.

#### Inflated Government Fees

A comparison with the Immigration Bureau's published fee schedule identifies several discrepancies:

| Immigration procedure | TVC law archive | Official fee |
|---|---:|---:|
| Extension of temporary stay | ฿2,000 | ฿1,900 |
| Single re-entry permit | ฿2,000 | ฿1,000 |
| Permanent-residence application | ฿8,000 | ฿7,600 |
| Standard residence certificate | ฿200,000 | ฿191,400 |
| Qualifying reduced-fee residence certificate | ฿100,000 | ฿95,700 |

The official charges are published by Samut Prakan Immigration, which identifies Ministerial Regulation No. 27 B.E. 2546 (2003) as the applicable regulatory basis.[^661]

#### Confusing Statutory Maximums With Applicable Fees

Section 5 of Thailand's Immigration Act authorizes the establishment of fees through ministerial regulations, subject to the maximum amounts specified in the Act's attached schedule.[^662]

The amounts appearing in the 1999 statutory schedule therefore do not necessarily represent the fees payable by applicants.

Nevertheless, TVC describes that schedule as a price list and instructs readers to pay the fee corresponding to their immigration procedure.

Its separate *Residence under Section 41* legal guide repeats the inflated permanent-residency figures without adequately explaining the distinction.[^663]

#### Thai Visa Centre Contradicts Its Own Commercial Website

Thai Visa Centre's dedicated permanent-residency service page correctly identifies the application fee as 7,600 baht and the standard approval fee as 191,400 baht.[^664]

Its commercial service page also identifies the reduced approval fee of 95,700 baht.

**Thai Visa Centre's own immigration law archive therefore contradicts the government fees published elsewhere on its commercial website.**

#### Commercial Significance

Thai Visa Centre advertises paid assistance with visa extensions, re-entry permits, and permanent-residency applications.

Accurate disclosure of government fees is particularly important when customers must distinguish mandatory immigration charges from optional agency fees.

The discrepancy does not establish that TVC actually charges customers inflated government fees. It establishes that its legal information archive presents statutory maximums without adequately explaining the lower applicable charges, despite publishing correct figures elsewhere.

## Immigration Law Archive Includes Irrelevant References and Contradictory Fee Guidance

As of November 27, 2026, Thai Visa Centre's legal guide concerning extensions of temporary stay lists 130 supporting legal instruments, government publications, and archived documents.[^670]

However, numerous documents concern extensions of taxation incentives rather than extensions of immigration permission.

### Unrelated Corporate Tax Legislation

Examples include:

| Legislation | Actual subject |
| --- | --- |
| Royal Decree No. 611 (2016) | Corporate tax deductions for domestic seminars and tourism |
| Royal Decree No. 635 (2017) | Tax exemptions for property fund conversions |
| Royal Decree No. 658 (2018) | Corporate tax exemptions for qualifying startups |
| Royal Decree No. 669 (2018) | Extension of reduced VAT rates |
| Royal Decree No. 726 (2021) | Tax deductions for employing former prisoners |
| Royal Decree No. 800 (2025) | Tax incentives for hotel renovations |

These documents are presented alongside genuinely relevant immigration legislation without explaining their relevance to temporary-stay extensions.

The apparent association is consistent with an overly broad interpretation of the word "extension," encompassing unrelated financial and administrative procedures.

### Correct Government Fees Appear Within the Archive

The extension-of-stay guide incorrectly identifies 2,000 baht as the applicable extension fee.[^670]

The separate re-entry permit guide similarly presents 2,000 baht as the single-entry permit fee under the 1999 statutory schedule.[^671]

However, reference 11 within that same re-entry permit guide correctly identifies the applicable single-entry permit fee as 1,000 baht and the multiple-entry permit fee as 3,800 baht.

Thai Visa Centre's archived copy of Bangkok Immigration's official fee schedule also identifies the ordinary temporary-stay extension fee as 1,900 baht.[^672]

These figures correspond with the official Immigration Bureau fee schedule previously examined in this investigation.[^661]

### Reliability of the Legal Archive

**Thai Visa Centre's legal archive contains relevant government information that contradicts its own explanatory guidance, alongside unrelated legislation presented as supporting material.**

The findings raise questions about document classification, source selection, editorial review, and whether the published summaries have been adequately reconciled with their underlying references.

They do not establish that the documents themselves are fabricated, that the archive was generated using artificial intelligence, or that the agency intentionally inflated its reference counts.

Nevertheless, the number of cited documents should not be interpreted as evidence of legal accuracy.

## Incorrect Immigration Form Instructions Contradict Official Requirements and the Agency's Own Guides

Thai Visa Centre publishes contradictory instructions concerning two fundamental Thai immigration forms: **TM7 and TM87**.

Its guide *Important Immigration Forms in Thailand* repeatedly identifies TM87 as a form used to extend an existing visa or permission to stay.[^101]

However, Thailand's Immigration Bureau identifies TM87 as an application for obtaining a visa, while TM7 is the standard application for extending temporary permission to stay.

The distinction is particularly significant because Thai Visa Centre claims to process immigration forms daily and markets professional assistance to customers navigating these procedures.

**The agency is instructing readers to use a visa-application form for an extension procedure that requires a different form.**

### TM87 Incorrectly Listed as a Visa Extension

Thai Visa Centre's guide begins with a quick-reference table identifying each major immigration form and its purpose.

The table describes:

- **TM87:** Visa extension application, submitted before the current permitted stay expires.
- **TM7:** General extension of stay, including tourist and business extensions.

The same article subsequently groups both forms under a heading describing extensions of stay.

It states that TM87 is used in certain extension scenarios and that immigration officers will direct applicants to the appropriate form.[^101]

The error appears again in the article's illustrated immigration timeline.

When describing what foreigners should do as their visas approach expiration, the guide instructs them to proceed with a **TM7/TM87 extension**.

These are not passing references. The incorrect classification appears in the reference table, explanatory section, and practical application sequence.

**A reader following the guide could reasonably conclude that TM87 is an alternative extension-of-stay application rather than a form used to obtain a visa under a different procedure.**

### Official Immigration Forms Establish Different Purposes

Thailand's Immigration Bureau maintains a published collection of immigration application forms.

The Samut Prakan Immigration Office identifies them separately:

| Form | Official purpose |
|---|---|
| TM7 | Application for extension of temporary stay |
| TM86 | Application for change of visa |
| TM87 | Application for visa |
| TM88 | Application for visa on arrival |

These classifications are published on the immigration office's official government website.[^102]

The distinction between TM7 and TM87 is not merely terminology.

The Thai government's immigration guidance explains that TM87 applies to foreigners who entered Thailand without a visa but received temporary permission to stay and subsequently wish to apply for a qualifying Non-Immigrant visa.[^103]

For example, a qualifying foreigner who entered under visa exemption and wishes to obtain an initial Non-Immigrant O visa may need TM87.

By contrast, a foreigner seeking to extend existing permission to stay for tourism, retirement, marriage, or another eligible purpose generally uses TM7 with the applicable supporting documentation.

The government's published instructions for tourist extensions explicitly require TM7 and identify a 1,900-baht application fee.[^104]

Its published procedures for obtaining or changing certain Non-Immigrant visas instead distinguish TM86 from TM87 and identify a 2,000-baht fee.[^103]

**These procedures have different purposes, eligibility conditions, documentation requirements, and government charges. They are not interchangeable simply because both involve applications submitted to immigration.**

### Thai Visa Centre's Other Guide Gets It Right

The strongest evidence of inconsistency comes from the agency's separate publication *Thai Immigration Forms: Complete Guide to TDAC, TM30, TM7, TM8, TM47, TM86, TM87 and TM88*.[^105]

That article correctly classifies:

- TM7 as an extension-of-stay application.
- TM86 as an application to change visa category.
- TM87 as an in-country visa application.

It also explains that TM87 applies to selected in-country visa applications rather than functioning as a universal alternative to applying through an embassy.

Its step-by-step immigration timeline correctly instructs foreigners to use TM7 when extending their existing permission to stay.

**Thai Visa Centre therefore publishes two guides about the same government forms, one correctly distinguishing TM87 from extension applications and the other repeatedly misclassifying it.**

Both articles describe themselves as professionally reviewed in June 2026.

Neither explains why its instructions differ from the other publication.

### Consequences for Retirement and Marriage Visa Applicants

The distinction directly affects services Thai Visa Centre actively sells.

A foreigner entering Thailand without a visa and subsequently applying for an initial retirement-purpose Non-Immigrant O visa may require TM87, subject to eligibility and supporting documentation.

That initial visa application does not itself grant the same permission as a subsequent one-year retirement extension.

The later extension generally involves TM7, separate financial evidence, and the applicable extension requirements.

Marriage-based applications likewise require distinguishing initial visa issuance or conversion from a subsequent extension of temporary stay.

**Confusing these stages risks sending applicants to immigration with the wrong form, inappropriate supporting documents, or an incorrect understanding of the procedure they are eligible to request.**

For applicants approaching the expiration of their permitted stay, such errors may be especially consequential.

An incorrectly prepared application can require additional appointments or document preparation, while missing the applicable deadline can expose the applicant to overstay consequences.

The published material does not establish that Thai Visa Centre has actually submitted the wrong form for a customer or caused a particular application to fail.

Nevertheless, its public instructions introduce precisely the kind of confusion customers pay professional immigration advisers to prevent.

### Claiming Daily Expertise While Publishing Contradictory Instructions

Thai Visa Centre's incorrect guide explicitly tells readers that its Bangkok team handles these forms daily.

It also claims that the information was reviewed against official sources in June 2026.

Yet the government has maintained the distinction between TM7 and TM87 for years, and the agency's own separate guide identifies the difference correctly.

**This is not a newly announced immigration rule that the agency has simply failed to incorporate. It is a basic procedural distinction contradicted by the official forms themselves.**

The issue also follows a broader pattern documented throughout the investigation.

Thai Visa Centre has published contradictory instructions involving retirement-visa durations, financial deposits, health insurance, DTV eligibility, and official application fees.

The incorrect TM87 instructions introduce another category of concern: the accuracy of the basic government paperwork that its advertised services are intended to handle.

**Thai Visa Centre promotes professional assistance with immigration applications while publishing conflicting explanations of which government form customers must submit.**

The company should correct the misclassification, reconcile the two guides, and explain how its claimed editorial-review process allowed contradictory filing instructions to remain published simultaneously.

## Contradictory Claims About Submitting Immigration Applications on Customers' Behalf

Thai Visa Centre publishes conflicting descriptions of its role in preparing and submitting immigration applications.

Several guides explicitly state that the agency does not submit applications on customers' behalf and provides only document preparation, checklist reviews, and related assistance.

However, other publications describe application submission through Thai Visa Centre, immigration representatives attending government offices, and documents being filed on customers' behalf.

**These contradictory representations concern a fundamental aspect of the service being sold: whether the agency actually handles official submissions or leaves that responsibility with the customer.**

### Multiple Guides Explicitly Deny Submitting Applications

Thai Visa Centre's *Thailand Visa Guide — Complete Overview for Visitors and Long-Stay Residents* describes the agency's assistance as document preparation in Bangkok.

Its application checklist explicitly states that customers must lodge their applications through official government channels and that Thai Visa Centre does not submit on their behalf.[^106]

The same assertion appears in the article's frequently asked questions.

The guide does not describe submission as an optional additional service or identify which visa categories might receive direct representation.

Instead, it presents document preparation as the extent of TVC's role.

This language is repeated across other immigration publications.

The agency's *Top 10 Thai Visa Application Mistakes to Avoid* guide again states that the customer must lodge the application personally or through official government channels, while TVC provides preparation and review services.[^107]

**The repeated wording gives readers the impression that Thai Visa Centre does not act as the party formally submitting their immigration applications.**

### The Contradiction Extends to Thailand Privilege

The inconsistency is especially apparent when examining Thailand Privilege applications.

Thai Visa Centre's guide comparing Thailand Privilege, DTV, and LTR visas specifically addresses customers choosing between long-term immigration programs.

Under its frequently asked questions, the article again states that TVC prepares documents and checklists but does not submit applications on customers' behalf.[^108]

Its separate guide to upgrading Thailand Privilege memberships repeats the same restriction.[^109]

However, another TVC publication makes a materially different representation.

The guide *Thailand Elite Visa Application Forms: Documents, GSSA Workflow, and Compliance in 2026* expressly states that Thailand Privilege forms may be submitted through an authorized General Sales and Services Agent, naming Thai Visa Centre as an example.[^110]

The article identifies the application pathway as submission through a GSSA and invites customers to begin the process with TVC.

Its related-service links specifically advertise application submission through the agency.

**Thai Visa Centre therefore tells Thailand Privilege customers that applications are not submitted on their behalf while separately promoting submission through TVC as part of its Thailand Privilege service.**

These statements cannot be reconciled merely by distinguishing Thai visas from foreign visa applications, because both sets of instructions address the same Thailand Privilege program.

The publications do not explain whether TVC provides formal submission services for some applicants, whether an external accredited agent handles submissions, or whether the statements denying representation are inaccurate.

### The Agency's Reputation-Defense Page Describes Direct Immigration Filing

Thai Visa Centre provides a substantially different account of its operations on its own reputation-defense page, `tvc.co.th/scam`.[^3]

That publication describes a customer case-management system tracking immigration applications from initial document intake through submission, collection, and return of passports.

It advertises:

- Photographs recording when documents are prepared, submitted, and collected.
- Digital copies of official immigration receipts.
- Records showing what has been filed on customers' behalf.
- Passport submissions made directly through Thai Immigration.
- Case-status updates documenting interactions with immigration authorities.

The company also states that its documents and passports are handled through official immigration channels.

These representations go considerably beyond providing a checklist for customers to use independently.

**Thai Visa Centre's own account of its operations describes active participation in immigration submissions, including handling passports, filing documentation, and collecting official records.**

The publication does not establish that TVC acts as an authorized representative for every visa category or every customer.

However, its broad description conflicts with the categorical statements elsewhere on the website that the agency does not submit applications on customers' behalf.

### A Clearly Documented Example: 90-Day Reporting

Thai Visa Centre's 90-day reporting service provides an additional illustration.

Its guide *90-Day Reporting Step-by-Step* states that the company files hundreds of TM47 reports for customers through `90day.in.th`.[^111]

The guide specifically describes the operator submitting reports on customers' behalf using their address information.

Thai Visa Centre's reputation-defense page independently confirms that representatives physically attend immigration offices for customers who purchase the reporting service.[^3]

AGENTS CO., LTD., the operator of `90day.in.th`, describes a comparable in-person proxy submission arrangement.

This is a routine immigration notification rather than an initial visa application, so the procedures and eligibility rules are different.

Nevertheless, **it establishes that the business network publicly advertises direct government submissions as a paid service**, rather than limiting all assistance to document preparation.

The important distinction is which services involve direct representation and which require customers to attend or submit applications themselves.

TVC's general statements do not clearly explain that division.

### Different Services May Legitimately Require Different Arrangements

There are legitimate reasons why an immigration agency might submit some documents but not others.

Certain procedures require applicants to appear personally, provide biometrics, attend interviews, or use designated government submission channels.

Other procedures may permit authorized representatives or accredited intermediaries to perform specific tasks.

For example, a customer may need to attend a foreign embassy personally while an agent prepares supporting documentation.

A Thai immigration representative may separately be permitted to submit routine reporting paperwork.

**The problem is not that every immigration service must use an identical submission procedure. It is that TVC publishes incompatible statements without clearly identifying which arrangements apply to its different advertised services.**

Its Thailand Privilege guidance is particularly revealing because the contradictory submission statements appear within publications addressing the same membership program.

Customers should not have to determine whether the agency will handle their application by comparing unrelated pages on the same website.

### Implications for Fees, Refunds, and Responsibility

The distinction between document preparation and formal representation is commercially significant.

A document-preparation service might provide checklists, translations, eligibility reviews, and supporting paperwork while leaving the actual application entirely to the customer.

A submission or representation service may additionally involve handling original passports, arranging government appointments, paying official charges, delivering documents, and tracking decisions.

Those activities create different expectations concerning:

1. What customers receive for the amount paid.
2. Who is responsible for submitting the correct application before a deadline.
3. Whether the customer must attend immigration or an embassy personally.
4. How official submission receipts and application records are obtained.
5. Whether refunds remain available when the agency has not completed a promised filing.

Thai Visa Centre's published Refund Policy separately distinguishes situations in which an application has or has not been submitted to an embassy or consulate.[^112]

That contractual distinction implies that the timing of submission can materially affect customers' refund rights.

However, the company's public guides do not consistently identify when TVC itself is responsible for performing that submission.

**A customer purchasing professional immigration assistance needs to know whether the agency promises to complete the filing or merely prepare documents for the customer to submit.**

The distinction becomes even more important when the agency accepts advance payments or retains customers' passports.

### Relevance to the Broader Investigation

The existing investigation documents uncertainties concerning Thai Visa Centre's corporate identity, government accreditation, contractual responsibility, and advertised processing arrangements.

The submission discrepancy adds a more fundamental question about the scope of the service itself.

Thai Visa Centre cannot provide a reliable explanation of its commercial responsibilities while simultaneously publishing that:

- Customers must submit their own applications.
- Thailand Privilege forms can be submitted through TVC.
- Immigration paperwork is filed on customers' behalf.
- Its representatives perform physical government submissions.

These statements may describe different service categories, but the company does not consistently distinguish them.

The available evidence does not establish that applications were submitted without authorization, that the company falsely claimed to perform particular filings, or that customers were charged for services not delivered.

**It does establish that Thai Visa Centre publishes contradictory descriptions of its role in the official application process while selling services whose value and contractual obligations depend on precisely that distinction.**

The company should identify which immigration procedures it submits directly, which are handled through external accredited agents, and which must be lodged by customers themselves.

It should also reconcile those distinctions with its refund provisions and the representations made through its customer case-tracking system.

**For an agency advertising professional visa processing, the inability to provide consistent public explanations of who actually submits the application raises significant questions about transparency and accountability.**

## TVC's 90-Day Reporting Guidance Contradicts Immigration Rules and Its Own Pages
Across its LTR, Thailand Privilege, and paid TM47 reporting guides, TVC publishes contradictory or unsupported instructions about reporting obligations and the consequences of missed reports. The evidence below identifies each discrepancy against the applicable government guidance and TVC's own pages.

### LTR Guide Misstates TM47 Requirements

Thai Visa Centre publishes contradictory instructions concerning the immigration-reporting obligations of Thailand's Long-Term Resident (LTR) visa holders.

Its guide *90-Day Report Online: Thailand TM47 Filing Guide* explicitly tells readers that LTR holders are not exempt from the standard 90-day reporting requirement.[^113]

However, Thailand's Board of Investment confirms that LTR holders follow a different procedure, submitting an annual TM95 notification instead of the ordinary TM47 report.[^115]

Thai Visa Centre's own LTR visa service page correctly advertises this annual-reporting benefit.[^114]

**The agency therefore tells LTR holders they must complete a reporting procedure that the government expressly replaces with annual reporting for their visa category.**

#### The 90-Day Reporting Guide Gives the Wrong Answer

Thai Visa Centre's dedicated reporting guide describes TM47 as a requirement for long-stay foreigners who remain in Thailand for 90 consecutive days.

Its frequently asked questions specifically address whether Thailand Privilege or LTR status changes that obligation.

The answer is categorical:

> Q: Does Elite or LTR exempt me from 90-day reporting?

The article answers **"No"**, states that most long-stay categories still require TM47, and directs readers toward online or in-person filing.[^113]

The guide also advertises Thai Visa Centre's paid reporting assistance, including:

- 500 baht for individual reports.
- 375 baht per report when purchasing four-report packages.
- Deadline reminders and document preparation.
- Submission assistance and delivery of immigration receipts.

These are legitimate commercial services for foreigners actually required to submit TM47 reports.

**But an LTR holder who relies on the article's answer could incorrectly conclude that quarterly TM47 submissions are mandatory and that purchasing reporting assistance is an appropriate way to satisfy that obligation.**

#### The BOI Explicitly Replaces TM47 With TM95

Thailand's Board of Investment provides an unambiguous explanation in its official LTR frequently asked questions.

Under the section addressing one-year reporting, the BOI explains that LTR holders who remain in Thailand continuously for one year must submit **TM95 instead of TM47**.[^115]

The distinction is a formal benefit of the LTR program.

| Reporting requirement | Ordinary qualifying long-stay visas | LTR visa |
|---|---|---|
| Immigration address notification | TM47 | TM95 |
| Reporting interval | 90 days | One year |
| Departure and re-entry | Resets applicable reporting period | Resets annual reporting period |
| TM30 accommodation notification | Still applies | Still applies |

The BOI separately confirms that TM30 accommodation reporting remains applicable to LTR holders.

Accordingly, annual reporting does not eliminate every immigration-notification obligation.

It does, however, expressly replace the quarterly TM47 procedure.

**The government's instruction is not a discretionary exemption or informal accommodation. It is a published feature of the LTR visa program.**

#### Thai Visa Centre's Own LTR Page Gets It Right

The contradiction is particularly revealing because Thai Visa Centre already publishes the correct rule elsewhere.

Its main *Long-Term Resident Visa (LTR)* service page lists the replacement of 90-day reporting with annual reporting among the program's principal benefits.[^114]

The same page identifies annual reporting to immigration as an ongoing obligation.

Thus, two publications on the same commercial website offer incompatible instructions:

1. **LTR visa sales page:** 90-day reporting is replaced by annual reporting.
2. **90-day reporting guide:** LTR holders are not exempt and should continue following TM47 requirements.

Both publications promote professional immigration assistance through Thai Visa Centre.

Neither explains why its reporting instructions differ from the other.

**Thai Visa Centre correctly advertises reduced reporting obligations when marketing the LTR visa, but contradicts that benefit when promoting immigration-reporting assistance.**

#### Another Guide Recognizes Annual Reporting Exceptions

The inconsistency extends to the company's broader immigration guidance.

Its article *Thai Immigration Crackdowns 2026* explicitly acknowledges that some long-stay visa categories use annual reporting rather than the ordinary 90-day procedure.[^116]

The article identifies LTR holders among those benefiting from different reporting arrangements.

This demonstrates that the company has published the relevant distinction in multiple places.

Nevertheless, its dedicated reporting guide continues answering the LTR question incorrectly.

A separate article, *90-Day Reporting Step-by-Step*, also broadly states that anyone remaining in Thailand for 90 consecutive days must file TM47, without clearly identifying the LTR exception.[^117]

**The problem is not a recent regulation that the agency has failed to discover. It is inconsistent application of a rule the agency already acknowledges.**

#### Financial and Compliance Implications

The discrepancy matters for two reasons.

First, customers may pay for unnecessary assistance.

A foreigner holding an LTR visa could encounter Thai Visa Centre's reporting guide, conclude that quarterly TM47 filings remain mandatory, and purchase reporting credits or professional filing assistance.

Four individual reports at the advertised 500-baht rate would represent 2,000 baht in annual commercial service charges.

That expense would serve no ordinary TM47 reporting requirement for an LTR holder whose obligation is annual TM95 reporting.

Second, inaccurate advice can confuse customers about their actual immigration responsibilities.

An LTR holder needs to understand the annual TM95 deadline and applicable TM30 obligations.

Submitting or attempting to submit unnecessary TM47 reports does not establish that the appropriate annual reporting requirement has been fulfilled.

**Professional immigration guidance should identify the correct form and reporting interval before encouraging customers to purchase filing services.**

The published evidence does not establish that Thai Visa Centre has actually sold unnecessary TM47 submissions to LTR holders or charged customers for filings they did not require.

It establishes that its public guidance creates that risk by presenting contradictory instructions.

#### The Broader Accountability Problem

Thai Visa Centre advertises LTR applications and immigration-reporting assistance as specialist services.

Its website repeatedly emphasizes familiarity with government procedures, professional document preparation, and avoiding costly compliance mistakes.

Yet the company publishes incompatible explanations of a fundamental LTR benefit.

The contradiction is especially concerning because the reporting guide does not merely omit an exception.

**It asks whether LTR holders are exempt from ordinary 90-day reporting and expressly gives the wrong answer.**

The BOI's guidance is clear, and Thai Visa Centre's own LTR sales page demonstrates that the correct information was available to its editorial team.

The company should correct the reporting FAQ, distinguish TM47 from TM95, and ensure that its paid reporting services are not promoted as necessary to customers whose visa categories follow different procedures.

**Thai Visa Centre markets reduced immigration bureaucracy as an advantage of the LTR visa while simultaneously publishing guidance that could lead those same customers to purchase unnecessary quarterly reporting assistance.**

The discrepancy raises questions about the reliability of its immigration advice and the safeguards used to ensure customers are sold services appropriate to their actual legal obligations.

### Thailand Elite Guides Incorrectly Suggest Annual Reporting Exemptions

Thai Visa Centre publishes misleading instructions suggesting that certain Thailand Elite, now Thailand Privilege, visa holders qualify for annual immigration reporting rather than the standard 90-day notification requirement.

Its guide *Top 10 Thai Visa Application Mistakes to Avoid*, marked reviewed June 2026, instructs long-stay foreigners to file quarterly reports unless their visa category permits annual reporting, explicitly identifying **"some Elite and LTR members"** as examples.[^295]

The same claim appears in its *How to Upgrade Your Thailand Elite Visa* guide.[^296]

**These instructions incorrectly conflate Thailand Privilege membership benefits with the annual reporting exemption available under the separate LTR visa program.**

#### Thailand Privilege Confirms Quarterly Reporting Remains Mandatory

Thailand Privilege Card Co., Ltd., the government-owned program operator, expressly states that members who remain continuously in Thailand for more than 90 days must submit the ordinary 90-day immigration notification.[^297]

Its official membership guidance distinguishes between:

- Membership categories receiving complimentary 90-day reporting assistance.
- Membership categories using Privilege Points to obtain reporting assistance.
- Members who must complete their own reporting.

None of these arrangements creates a general annual-reporting entitlement for Thailand Privilege visa holders.

Members remain responsible for monitoring deadlines and complying with immigration requirements. Departing Thailand resets the applicable reporting period, but merely possessing an Elite visa does not eliminate quarterly reporting during a continuous stay.

#### Thai Visa Centre Contradicts Its Own Bronze Guide

The inconsistency is particularly apparent because TVC's dedicated Thailand Privilege Bronze Membership guide correctly explains that 90-day reporting remains necessary during continuous residence.[^298]

That publication separately advertises assistance with reporting through Thailand Privilege offices.

**The agency therefore acknowledges the correct requirement on its membership sales pages while suggesting annual-reporting exceptions in its broader immigration guidance without explaining the separate qualifying conditions of the Flexible Plus Program.**

#### Legal Archive Misapplies the Flexible Plus Reporting Exemption

Thai Visa Centre's subsequently published 90-day reporting law archive categorically states:

> Long-Term Resident and Privilege file once a year instead.[^673]

The publication cites Clause 8 of a Ministry of Interior notification published in the Royal Gazette on January 6, 2022.

However, the cited notification concerns the **Flexible Plus Program**, rather than ordinary Thailand Privilege membership.

Thai Visa Centre's own archived English translation identifies several relevant conditions:[^674]

- Certification of eligibility by the Tourism Authority of Thailand.
- Conversion to a special Non-Immigrant visa for employment.
- Compliance with applicable work-permit requirements.
- Annual residence notifications for qualifying participants and dependents.

Thai Visa Centre's separate legal guide concerning Thailand Privilege correctly describes the notification as an employment-related arrangement requiring additional certification.[^675]

**The agency's general reporting guide therefore omits the qualifying conditions contained in its own cited legislation.**

Ordinary Privilege Entry visa holders remain subject to the standard 90-day reporting requirement, as confirmed by Thailand Privilege Card Co., Ltd.[^297]

The documented error is not the existence of an annual reporting exemption, but its presentation as a general benefit of Thailand Privilege membership.

#### Consequences for Visa Holders

A Thailand Privilege member relying on the incorrect annual-reporting statement could miss a legally required TM47 notification.

Thailand Privilege's official guidance warns that late reporting can result in a 2,000-baht fine and additional administrative requirements.

The company should correct its published instructions and clearly distinguish the following:

1. Ordinary TM47 notifications required of Thailand Privilege members.
2. Optional reporting assistance provided through membership benefits.
3. The separate annual TM95 reporting arrangement applicable to qualifying LTR holders.

**The documented problem is not the availability of reporting assistance. It is TVC's suggestion that certain Elite membership tiers change the underlying legal reporting interval when the program operator expressly confirms otherwise.**

### TM47 Guide Incorrectly Treats Day 91 as Automatically Overdue

Thai Visa Centre's *90-Day Reporting Step-by-Step* guide publishes an inaccurate deadline table for foreigners completing Thailand's mandatory TM47 address notifications.[^111]

The guide instructs readers that:

- **Day 80:** Submit the report online, by post, or through an agent.
- **Day 90:** The reporting deadline expires.
- **Day 90+:** The report is overdue, with a potential 2,000-baht fine.

**This presentation incorrectly suggests that a foreigner becomes liable for late reporting immediately after the 90th day.**

### Immigration Officially Allows Seven Days After the Reporting Date

Thailand's Immigration Bureau provides a broader notification window.

Its published procedure allows qualifying foreigners to notify Immigration:

- Up to 15 days before the 90-day reporting date.
- Up to seven days after that date.[^525]

A person submitting the required notification within the permitted seven-day period is not automatically overdue simply because Day 90 has passed.

The distinction concerns the permitted TM47 notification window. It does not extend the validity of a visa or permission to remain in Thailand.

Different submission channels have separate procedural restrictions. In particular, postal and online reporting may require earlier submission, and the availability of those methods should not be confused with the permitted in-person reporting window.

**TVC's deadline table fails to explain these distinctions and instead treats every day following Day 90 as a reporting violation.**

### The Incorrect Deadline Accompanies Commercial Reporting Assistance

Thai Visa Centre markets paid TM47 reporting services involving document preparation, deadline reminders, and submissions handled on customers' behalf.[^111]

Accurate deadline information is particularly important when a business charges customers to help them avoid penalties.

Someone consulting TVC's guide on Day 91 could incorrectly believe they have already missed the permitted reporting period and face an immediate fine.

The government's procedure provides a different answer.

This does not establish that TVC has charged customers unnecessary penalties or misrepresented an individual customer's filing status.

It establishes that **the agency publishes a deadline warning that omits an express allowance in the Immigration Bureau's reporting procedure**.

### The Error Reinforces TVC's Other TM47 Warnings

The same investigation documents TVC's unsupported suggestions that repeated TM47 violations can lead to blacklisting.

The inaccurate Day 90 deadline creates an additional problem: readers may misunderstand when a reporting violation has occurred in the first place.

**TVC combines an unnecessarily restrictive reporting deadline with warnings about immigration penalties while promoting commercial assistance for the same procedure.**

Thai Visa Centre should distinguish the 90-day reporting date from the permitted seven-day notification period and explain the separate requirements for in-person, postal, and online submissions.

### TVC Mislabels Paid In-Person Reporting as an Online TM47 Filing Method

Thai Visa Centre publishes misleading instructions that blur the distinction between Thailand's official online immigration-reporting system and the agency's own paid reporting service.

Its *How to Do the 90-Day Report with a Thailand Elite Visa* guide explicitly presents `90day.in.th` as the destination for online TM47 filing.[^526]

The guide's comparison table identifies three supposedly distinct options:

- **Online TM47:** Directs eligible users to `90day.in.th`.
- **In-person reporting:** Directs users to Immigration.
- **TVC filing service:** Directs users to TVC's separate service page.

Its *Important Immigration Forms in Thailand* guide repeats the claim, identifying online TM47 reporting as available through `90day.in.th`.[^527]

**These instructions incorrectly present a private, paid intermediary as the online filing channel while treating the same company's in-person submission service as a separate option.**

### TVC's Own Operator Admits the Service Works Differently

The actual `90day.in.th` service is operated by AGENTS CO., LTD., which Thai Visa Centre publicly identifies as its affiliated digital-services company.[^3]

On its own service-information page, AGENTS explains that its product is a physical proxy service. Customers purchase reporting credits and submit requests through an online dashboard, after which company representatives attend immigration offices and submit TM47 notifications on their behalf.[^98]

The company further acknowledges that Thailand already operates a separate official online reporting portal, which applicants can use without purchasing its assistance.

That government service is available through the Immigration Bureau's TM47 system.[^528]

**Submitting a service request through an intermediary's website is not the same as submitting a TM47 notification electronically through the Immigration Bureau's official system.**

### TVC Explicitly Claims Its Paid Service Uses the Government's Online Portal

Thai Visa Centre's *90-Day Report Online: Thailand TM47 Filing Guide* goes further than merely confusing its private reporting website with the official immigration portal.[^113]

Under *Option 1: Online via the official TM47 portal*, the guide identifies the Immigration Bureau's online reporting system and then states that TVC's commercial reporting service uses that same official portal.

Later, under *Professional 90-day reporting in Bangkok*, the company advertises **online submission through the official system** as an included feature of its paid service.

These are explicit representations about how the agency handles customers' reporting applications.

**However, TVC's own service page directly contradicts them.**

### The Actual Service Is Advertised as Physical Immigration Reporting

On its dedicated reporting-service page, TVC describes `90day.in.th` as a physical proxy service in which its representatives attend immigration offices and submit TM47 notifications in person.[^529]

The page specifically distinguishes its commercial product from free online filing through the government's portal.

It explains that the service is intended particularly for customers who have experienced online filing rejections, technical problems, or other difficulties requiring personal assistance.

The affiliated operator, AGENTS CO., LTD., confirms that its standard procedure involves purchasing reporting credits, submitting a request through an online dashboard, and having company representatives physically visit Immigration.[^98]

**The company's service descriptions therefore contradict each other on a fundamental point: whether customers are paying for online submission through the government portal or physical submission by an authorized representative.**

Submitting a request through TVC's dashboard does not itself constitute electronic submission to the Immigration Bureau.

### This Is a Material Difference in the Service Being Sold

The distinction is not merely technical.

Customers who cannot successfully use Immigration's online portal may specifically purchase professional assistance because they need a different submission method.

For those customers, physical filing can provide a legitimate service that differs substantially from submitting the same information electronically.

Yet TVC's reporting guide tells readers that its paid service uses the official online system, while its dedicated commercial page identifies in-person filing as the product's defining feature.

A provider could potentially offer both methods. However, **TVC does not explain in these conflicting descriptions whether different customers receive different submission methods or when either procedure applies.**

The available evidence does not establish that TVC has failed to submit reports, charged customers for services it did not perform, or exclusively uses one filing channel in every case.

It establishes something simpler and directly verifiable: **TVC publishes incompatible explanations of the method used to deliver a paid immigration-compliance service.**

That contradiction undermines the company's claims of transparent service descriptions and reliable immigration expertise.

Customers should be told whether their reports will be submitted electronically through Immigration's official portal or physically by an authorized representative. TVC's published materials should consistently identify the actual service being purchased.

### Customers Are Directed Toward a Paid Alternative

The distinction matters because `90day.in.th` charges customers for services that differ materially from free direct online filing.

Its published prices include 500 baht for individual reports and 375 baht per report when purchasing qualifying packages.[^98]

Those charges can be justified when a customer knowingly purchases physical submission assistance, document handling, and the return of official reporting receipts.

**They are not charges for accessing Thailand's official online reporting system.**

Yet TVC's immigration guides direct readers searching for online TM47 filing toward its affiliated commercial platform without consistently explaining that customers are purchasing a separate service.

A reader could reasonably mistake `90day.in.th` for the appropriate online submission portal rather than a private intermediary that may send an employee to Immigration.

The misleading distinction also affects what customers understand about processing methods, document handling, and which organization receives their personal information.

### Contradictory Instructions Undermine TVC's Claimed Expertise

This discrepancy is particularly revealing because TVC's different publications describe the same service in incompatible ways.

The immigration guides label it online TM47 filing. The service operator identifies it as physical proxy reporting. TVC's dedicated service page correctly distinguishes its paid assistance from free government filing.

**The company knows how to describe its own service accurately, yet continues publishing guides that misidentify the commercial intermediary as an online immigration-filing channel.**

The available evidence does not establish that customers were deliberately deceived or that AGENTS failed to perform purchased reporting services.

It does establish that TVC's published guidance obscures a financially important distinction between a free government filing method and a paid private intermediary.

For an agency presenting itself as an immigration specialist, repeatedly confusing those services is an unacceptable failure of accuracy in information used to attract paying customers.

### Paid TM47 Guide Makes Unsupported Blacklisting Claims

Thai Visa Centre publishes warnings about severe immigration consequences for foreigners who fail to complete their 90-day address notifications.

Its guide *90-Day Report Online: Thailand TM47 Filing Guide* claims that repeated reporting violations may lead to immigration blacklisting and that unresolved reporting failures can cause visa extensions to be refused.[^118]

However, Thailand's published immigration guidance identifies financial penalties for failing to submit TM47 notifications, while the government's established re-entry bans concern different violations, particularly overstaying permission to remain in Thailand.

**Thai Visa Centre presents possible blacklisting as a consequence of repeated address-reporting failures without identifying a government regulation, official determination, or documented case establishing that consequence.**

The warnings appear in material promoting the company's paid immigration-reporting services.

#### The Guide Links Missed Reports to Blacklisting

Under the heading *What happens if you miss your 90-day report?*, Thai Visa Centre presents three escalating outcomes.[^118]

| Circumstance | Consequence advertised by TVC |
|---|---|
| Overdue report submitted voluntarily | Typically a 2,000-baht fine |
| Violation discovered during an extension | Extension may be delayed or refused |
| Repeated reporting violations | Increased scrutiny and possible blacklisting |

The first warning generally corresponds to the published administrative fine.

The second may reflect the requirement to resolve an outstanding reporting violation before completing certain immigration procedures.

The third introduces a considerably more serious consequence: the possibility of being prohibited from entering Thailand.

Yet the guide does not identify any official rule explaining when repeated TM47 reporting failures independently justify an entry ban.

It also provides no example of a foreigner blacklisted solely because of repeated late 90-day address notifications.

**The article places ordinary reporting fines, extension-processing problems, and immigration blacklisting in the same escalation sequence without establishing the legal relationship between them.**

#### Official Immigration Guidance Identifies Financial Penalties

The Royal Thai Consulate-General in Los Angeles publishes official guidance explaining Thailand's 90-day reporting obligations.[^119]

Its instructions distinguish voluntary late reporting from failures discovered through enforcement.

The published penalties include a 2,000-baht fine for late reporting and higher penalties where a foreigner is apprehended without having fulfilled the notification obligation.

The guidance also describes an additional daily penalty that may apply until compliance.

Crucially, its explanation of TM47 reporting does not identify automatic visa cancellation, deportation, or blacklisting as the ordinary penalty for failing to submit an address notification.

The same publication explicitly distinguishes 90-day reporting from applying for an extension of stay.

These are separate immigration procedures serving different legal purposes.

A foreigner can hold valid permission to remain in Thailand while being overdue for a required address report.

**Missing a TM47 notification does not itself mean that the foreigner has overstayed their visa or extension.**

That distinction is essential when explaining the risks of failing to report.

#### Overstay Blacklisting Is a Different Legal Issue

Thailand's government separately publishes specific re-entry-ban thresholds for foreigners who remain in the country beyond their authorized period of stay.[^120]

For individuals voluntarily departing after an overstay, the published framework includes:

| Duration of overstay | Re-entry ban |
|---|---|
| More than 90 days | 1 year |
| More than 1 year | 3 years |
| More than 3 years | 5 years |
| More than 5 years | 10 years |

More severe consequences can apply when individuals are apprehended rather than voluntarily departing.

These penalties concern **overstaying permission to remain in Thailand**, not simply failing to complete an address notification.

The two violations should not be conflated.

A foreigner whose authorized stay expired months earlier may face re-entry restrictions regardless of whether they completed TM47.

Conversely, a foreigner with valid immigration permission who misses an address-reporting deadline has not necessarily committed an overstay.

Thai Visa Centre's own *Visa Overstay in Thailand* guide correctly identifies the distinction between immigration permission, overstay fines, and re-entry bans.[^121]

It explains that overstay begins when the permitted stay expires and connects blacklist consequences to the duration and circumstances of that violation.

**Thai Visa Centre correctly explains the legal basis for overstay-related entry bans in one guide while separately invoking possible blacklisting in its paid reporting guide without establishing an equivalent basis for late TM47 notifications.**

#### Visa Extensions May Require Outstanding Fines to Be Cleared

The reporting guide also warns that unresolved TM47 violations can delay or prevent visa extensions.

This part of its warning requires a more careful distinction.

Immigration officers may require a foreigner to settle an outstanding reporting fine before proceeding with an extension application.

Official immigration procedures recognize the need to address such violations during application processing.

However, requiring payment of an outstanding fine is not necessarily the same as refusing an otherwise eligible extension because a reporting deadline was missed.

Thai Visa Centre does not identify the specific circumstances in which a TM47 violation alone would justify refusing an extension.

Nor does it distinguish temporary processing delays from a substantive rejection of the applicant's eligibility.

**The agency presents extension refusal as a possible consequence without explaining whether the problem is an outstanding administrative fine, a separate immigration violation, or another ground for refusing the application.**

That omission risks making an ordinarily remediable reporting problem appear more severe than the published procedure establishes.

#### Thai Visa Centre's Other Guide Describes the Penalties Differently

The company's *Thai Immigration Crackdowns 2026* guide provides another comparison.[^122]

Its penalty table identifies TM30 and 90-day reporting failures as matters involving fines, extension-processing problems, and cumulative compliance records.

In the same table, it separately associates overstay, unauthorized work, fraudulent documents, and incorrect visa categories with potentially more severe consequences.

That presentation more clearly distinguishes address-reporting failures from conduct that may justify visa cancellation or entry prohibitions.

Yet the dedicated TM47 guide introduces blacklisting as a possible consequence of repeated reporting violations.

Neither publication explains the difference.

**The same agency publishes different descriptions of the consequences of missing TM47 reports, with its dedicated paid-reporting guide presenting the more alarming warning.**

#### The Warnings Accompany a Paid Reporting Service

The commercial context is important.

Thai Visa Centre's 90-day reporting guide repeatedly encourages customers to use professional filing assistance.

It advertises:

- 500 baht for individual reports.
- 375 baht per report when purchasing packages of four.
- Deadline tracking and reminders.
- Preparation of TM47 submissions.
- Address verification against immigration records.
- Submission through official channels.
- Delivery of reporting receipts.

The guide acknowledges that eligible customers can complete their reporting through the government's free system.

However, its descriptions of potential extension refusal and blacklisting introduce additional reasons for customers to regard professional assistance as necessary.

**The agency combines routine paid filing assistance with warnings about severe immigration consequences that are not adequately substantiated for the reporting violation being discussed.**

This does not establish that the business deliberately exaggerated those consequences to increase sales.

It establishes that the claims appear in promotional material and may influence customers' decisions about purchasing reporting assistance.

#### Repeated Violations Do Not Eliminate the Need for Evidence

Repeated reporting failures may legitimately attract additional attention from immigration authorities.

They may also occur alongside unrelated violations, including overstays, false statements, or failure to comply with an immigration order.

Such circumstances can involve consequences more serious than an ordinary TM47 fine.

However, the possibility of additional misconduct does not establish that repeated late reporting alone is a recognized ground for blacklisting.

Thai Visa Centre has not published the legal provision, official instruction, or immigration decision supporting that particular claim.
