**The question is not whether serious immigration violations can result in blacklisting. They clearly can. The question is whether TVC has a factual basis for presenting blacklisting as a possible escalation of ordinary TM47 reporting failures.**

The available government materials do not establish that connection.

#### Relevance to the Broader Investigation

The existing investigation documents Thai Visa Centre's incorrect instructions for LTR holders, who are generally subject to annual TM95 reporting rather than quarterly TM47 notifications.

The new discrepancy concerns customers who actually must file TM47 reports.

It examines whether the agency accurately describes the consequences of failing to use the free government service or its paid commercial alternative.

The issue is particularly relevant because the company sells reporting packages, maintains customers' immigration records, and presents itself as a specialist in preventing immigration complications.

Customers are entitled to accurate explanations of both the underlying obligation and the consequences of noncompliance.

The available evidence does not establish that late TM47 filing can never contribute to a broader adverse immigration decision.

It does establish that **Thai Visa Centre has not substantiated its published suggestion that repeated address-reporting failures can lead to blacklisting independently of other immigration violations**.

The company should identify the legal authority supporting that claim, explain when an outstanding reporting fine can delay an extension, and distinguish administrative reporting penalties from overstay-related re-entry bans.

**Thai Visa Centre promotes paid 90-day reporting assistance while warning customers about possible blacklisting without demonstrating that ordinary TM47 reporting failures carry that consequence under Thailand's published immigration rules.**

## Blacklist Appeal Guide Omits Harsher Arrest-Based Penalties

Thai Visa Centre's *Thailand Immigration Blacklist Appeal* guide advertises professional assistance with overstay resolution, deportation, and immigration appeals.[^286]

Its published penalty table lists re-entry bans ranging from one year for overstays exceeding 90 days to ten years for overstays exceeding five years.

However, **these penalties describe voluntary surrender, not every overstay case**.

Thailand's official immigration rules distinguish between foreigners who voluntarily surrender and those arrested and prosecuted for overstaying.[^287]

For arrested offenders, the penalties are substantially harsher:

- **Overstay under one year:** Five-year re-entry ban.
- **Overstay over one year:** Ten-year re-entry ban.

By comparison, someone voluntarily surrendering after six months would ordinarily face a one-year ban, while an arrested person with the same overstay could face five years.

TVC's guide discusses detention, deportation, and arrest but does not explain this crucial distinction alongside its penalty table.

**Customers facing immigration enforcement could substantially underestimate their potential exclusion period if they rely on the agency's simplified figures.**

The omission is especially concerning because TVC expressly markets professional blacklist appeals and urgent immigration assistance to customers whose legal circumstances may be complicated.

The agency should distinguish voluntary departure from arrest and prosecution, explain the applicable penalty schedules, and avoid presenting surrender-based penalties as general blacklist durations.

## Overstay Guide Misstates Penalties for Children and Re-Entry Ban Thresholds

Thai Visa Centre's *Visa Overstay in Thailand* guide contains incorrect statements about financial penalties for children and the duration of overstays triggering re-entry bans.[^121]

These errors appear in a guide through which TVC advertises urgent immigration assistance for people facing overstays, deportation, and potential blacklisting.

### Falsely Claims Overstay Fines Apply to Children

Under *Special Categories*, TVC tells readers that parents are responsible for children and that overstay fines still apply.

**This blanket warning contradicts the Immigration Bureau's express exemption for children under 15.**

Thailand's official Immigration Division 2 bilingual handbook states that children younger than 15 are not charged an overstay fine.[^551]

The handbook separately identifies the ordinary fine of 500 baht per day, capped at 20,000 baht.

**TVC therefore presents a financial penalty as applicable to children whom Immigration expressly exempts from that penalty.**

This exemption does not mean that minors automatically possess lawful immigration status, that parents are exempt from penalties for their own overstays, or that families can disregard immigration requirements.

Those are separate questions that professional guidance should distinguish.

Instead, TVC's categorical warning could lead families to believe they must pay fines that Immigration does not impose on children under 15.

### Incorrectly Includes Exactly 90 Days in the One-Year Ban Threshold

The same guide's re-entry-ban table identifies overstays of **90–365 days** as triggering a one-year ban.[^121]

However, Thailand's official rules establish the one-year exclusion threshold for voluntary surrender at **more than 90 days**.[^552]

The Immigration Division 2 handbook confirms the same distinction.[^551]

**Exactly 90 days and more than 90 days are not interchangeable legal thresholds.**

A person who voluntarily surrenders after exactly 90 days of overstay does not automatically fall within the one-year ban category merely because of that duration.

The distinction is particularly important because the guide advertises emergency assistance and advises customers to determine whether a re-entry ban applies before making further visa applications.

Separate, substantially harsher restrictions apply when an overstayer is arrested and prosecuted rather than voluntarily surrendering, as documented elsewhere in this investigation.[^287]

### Consequences for Customers

These errors concern two practical questions that customers may reasonably rely on a professional immigration agency to answer correctly:

- Whether an overstaying child is subject to a financial penalty.
- Whether a particular overstay duration triggers a mandatory re-entry ban.

**TVC provides inaccurate answers to both questions while marketing assistance to foreigners facing serious immigration consequences.**

The evidence does not establish that TVC collected unnecessary fines, caused a customer to be blacklisted, or deliberately misstated these rules.

It establishes that **the agency publishes penalty information inconsistent with express Immigration Bureau guidance, including an exemption protecting children and a precisely defined re-entry-ban threshold**.

TVC should correct its explanation of children's overstay penalties and distinguish exactly 90 days from more than 90 days when explaining voluntary-surrender restrictions.


### November 2026 Update: TVC Archives Official Immigration Guidance Contradicting Its Own Advice

On November 22, 2026, Thai Visa Centre captured and republished Immigration Division 1's official FAQ as part of its expanding legal archive.[^679]

The archived government publication correctly identifies the standard overstay fine as 500 baht per day, capped at 20,000 baht, and distinguishes between re-entry bans applicable to voluntary surrender and arrest.

However, TVC's previously indexed commercial overstay guide contains materially different instructions.[^121]

| Issue | TVC's commercial guide | Official guidance archived by TVC |
|---|---|---|
| Maximum overstay fine | Presented under overstays exceeding 90 days | 20,000 baht regardless of duration once the ceiling is reached |
| One-year re-entry ban upon voluntary surrender | 90–365 days | More than 90 days |
| Arrest and prosecution | General warning about blacklisting | Separate mandatory five-year or ten-year exclusion periods |

The standard daily fine reaches its maximum after 40 days, not 90 days.

Although TVC's commercial guide mentions the correct maximum amount, its presentation misleadingly associates that ceiling with overstays exceeding 90 days.

**Thai Visa Centre has now republished official immigration guidance that directly contradicts the penalty information in its own previously indexed commercial publication.**

This discrepancy is particularly concerning because the company claims to handle urgent overstay cases every week and actively encourages foreigners facing immigration penalties to purchase professional assistance.

Expanding its legal archive is not a substitute for ensuring the accuracy of the commercial guidance through which it attracts customers.

The available evidence does not establish whether TVC deliberately misstated these penalties or whether customers suffered financial losses as a result.

## Digital Work Permits Advertised for LTR Applicants Who Are Ineligible to Receive Them

Thai Visa Centre repeatedly advertises digital work permits as a benefit of Thailand's Long-Term Resident (LTR) visa for Work-from-Thailand Professionals.

Its dedicated guide to this category describes the permit as an official document authorizing remote employment for a foreign company while residing in Thailand.[^123]

The article goes further, instructing applicants to obtain a digital work permit after receiving their LTR visa.

However, Thailand's Board of Investment (BOI), which administers the LTR program, explicitly states that **Work-from-Thailand Professionals are not eligible to receive work permits**.[^124][^125]

The discrepancy is not a minor technicality.

**Thai Visa Centre advertises the issuance of a government document that the responsible government agency expressly refuses to issue to applicants in the advertised visa category.**

### The Dedicated Guide Promises Digital Work Permits

Thai Visa Centre's article *LTR Visa Thailand for Work-from-Thailand Professionals: Remote Employer Guide* presents the digital work permit as a central benefit of the category.[^123]

Its introduction describes remote employees of overseas companies as receiving a digital work permit instead of a traditional Thai employment permit.

A summary table identifies the category's work-permit type as **"Digital only."**

The article subsequently states that the Work-from-Thailand category issues a digital work permit authorizing remote employment for an overseas employer.

Under its application instructions, Thai Visa Centre includes a specific fifth step titled **"Digital work permit issuance."**

That step tells successful LTR applicants to apply for the permit through the program after receiving their visa.

The claim is repeated throughout the article:

- The category comparison identifies digital work permits for remote workers.
- The benefits table describes the permit as proof of legal remote employment.
- The comparison against DTV identifies digital work permits as a distinctive LTR advantage.
- The FAQ promotes the category as suitable for professionals who need documented authorization to work remotely.

**The promised work permit is not incidental to the sales presentation. It is repeatedly described as an important reason to select this visa category.**

### The BOI Explicitly Says No Permit Is Issued

The BOI's official LTR visa issuance instructions establish a different procedure.

Under its section concerning work-permit applications, the BOI explains that eligible LTR holders working for entities in Thailand must apply for employment authorization through the LTR system.[^124]

However, the same section contains an explicit exclusion.

**Work-from-Thailand Professionals do not receive work permits because their category is intended for remote employment with companies located outside Thailand.**

The BOI's official FAQ independently reinforces this distinction.[^125]

It specifically asks why Work-from-Thailand Professionals cannot apply for digital work permits.

The answer explains that the category is intended for foreign employees working remotely rather than undertaking local employment.

The FAQ also states that these visa holders cannot undertake income-generating work for Thai employers because the category does not provide work-permit eligibility.

The official distinction is therefore:

| LTR category | Digital work permit |
|---|---|
| Work-from-Thailand Professionals | Not issued |
| Highly Skilled Professionals working for qualifying Thai entities | Available subject to approval |
| Other LTR holders undertaking eligible employment with Thai entities | Subject to applicable work-permit requirements |

The existence of a digital work-permit system for some LTR visa holders does not establish that every category qualifies.

**Thai Visa Centre has applied a benefit available to certain LTR holders to a category that the BOI specifically excludes.**

### Remote Work Permission Is Not the Same as Receiving a Work Permit

An important distinction must be preserved.

The BOI created the Work-from-Thailand Professionals category specifically to accommodate qualifying foreign employees who perform remote work for overseas companies while residing in Thailand.

That activity is permitted within the category's conditions.

The absence of an issued Thai work permit does not automatically make their qualifying remote employment unlawful.

Instead, the category provides an immigration framework designed for that particular arrangement without requiring or issuing the same employment permit used for work involving Thai entities.

This is different from a Highly Skilled Professional employed by a qualifying company or institution in Thailand.

Those applicants may require a digital work permit because their employment involves activities within the Thai labor market.

**The problem with Thai Visa Centre's explanation is not that Work-from-Thailand LTR holders cannot legally work remotely. It is the claim that their authorization is documented through a digital work permit the government will not issue.**

That distinction directly contradicts the central explanation in TVC's guide.

### The Same Error Appears in Other Visa Comparisons

The incorrect claim is not confined to one publication.

Thai Visa Centre's guide *Thai Elite Visa vs LTR Visa* describes the LTR program as providing a **digital permit for remote workers** while presenting a separate work-permit pathway for highly skilled professionals.[^126]

Its application checklist goes further, instructing remote workers to obtain a digital work permit after visa issuance.

That guidance repeats the same procedural error found in the dedicated Work-from-Thailand article.

The agency's *DTV Visa Thailand FAQs* similarly compares DTV, LTR, and Thailand Privilege visas.

Its comparison table describes LTR remote work as permitted **with a digital work permit**.[^127]

The company's primary LTR service page also promotes digital work permits among the program's general benefits and describes a digital permit as required for work activities, without clearly explaining the exclusion affecting Work-from-Thailand Professionals.[^128]

These publications reinforce one another.

A reader researching remote work, comparing long-term visa categories, and reviewing the application process could encounter essentially the same incorrect representation repeatedly.

**Thai Visa Centre presents the unavailable permit as a defining advantage of Work-from-Thailand LTR status across several independently published immigration guides.**

### The Application Instructions Describe an Unavailable Step

The most consequential error concerns the procedure applicants are told to follow.

Thai Visa Centre's dedicated Work-from-Thailand guide presents the following sequence:

1. Establish eligibility through foreign employment and income.
2. Register through the BOI's LTR application system.
3. Obtain qualification endorsement.
4. Receive the LTR visa.
5. Apply for a digital work permit.
6. Maintain post-arrival immigration compliance.

The fifth step is incorrect for this category.

The BOI's published procedure specifically excludes Work-from-Thailand Professionals from work-permit issuance.[^124]

Applicants following TVC's guidance could therefore attempt to complete an unavailable government procedure.

They might also incorrectly assume that receiving a digital permit is necessary before beginning qualifying remote work.

Alternatively, applicants could choose the Work-from-Thailand category partly because they believe it provides a formal employment document that could be presented to banks, employers, insurers, or other institutions.

**Thai Visa Centre advertises both a government benefit and a procedural step that do not exist for the applicants its article is specifically targeting.**

### A Misleading Comparison With Other Immigration Options

The false permit claim also distorts the financial and practical comparison between LTR visas and competing long-stay arrangements.

Work-from-Thailand LTR applicants must satisfy relatively demanding financial and employment criteria.

Thai Visa Centre's guide identifies an annual income threshold generally beginning at US$80,000, with alternative qualifications for some applicants earning less.

It also identifies the LTR visa issuance fee in Thailand as 50,000 baht.

The digital work permit is presented as an additional reason to satisfy those requirements rather than choose the less expensive Destination Thailand Visa.

That distinction is commercially significant.

Customers might reasonably value an official work-permit document when comparing long-term immigration options.

However, **a benefit that cannot be issued should not be used to justify choosing one visa over another**.

The BOI does charge separate digital work-permit fees for eligible LTR applicants working in Thailand, including a published maintenance fee of 3,000 baht annually.[^124]

Those charges concern the work-permit process available to eligible applicants, not an additional permit that Work-from-Thailand Professionals are required or entitled to obtain.

The available evidence does not establish that TVC has charged remote workers for nonexistent work permits.

It does establish that its public advertising inaccurately identifies a government-issued document as part of their expected immigration process.

### Claimed Professional Expertise Versus Official Instructions

Thai Visa Centre's Work-from-Thailand guide repeatedly encourages readers to consult its Bangkok team before submitting applications.

The company claims to assess overseas employers, financial documentation, income qualifications, and work-permit requirements against BOI rules.

It also lists the BOI's official LTR portal among its supporting references.

Yet the BOI's published instructions explicitly contradict the guidance.

This is particularly significant because the agency markets its expertise as a way to avoid applications based on incorrect eligibility assumptions.

A professional adviser handling LTR cases should distinguish between permission to work remotely under an approved visa category and eligibility for a separate Thai employment permit.

**Thai Visa Centre repeatedly fails to make that distinction while claiming expertise in the very government program whose official instructions contradict its advice.**

### The Missing Explanation

The company should explain why its publications describe digital work-permit issuance for Work-from-Thailand Professionals when the BOI expressly excludes those applicants.

It should also correct the application sequence, clarify which LTR categories may apply for digital permits, and identify whether customers have been advised to pursue or pay for unavailable work-permit processing.

The evidence does not establish that TVC has fabricated work permits, submitted unauthorized applications, or caused a particular customer financial loss.

Those outcomes would require additional documentary evidence.

**What is established is that Thai Visa Centre repeatedly advertises an official employment document that the responsible government authority states will not be issued for the visa category being promoted.**

Unlike a disputed customer testimonial or an unsupported accreditation claim, this discrepancy can be resolved directly through the government's published eligibility rules.

It adds another substantial inconsistency to the existing investigation into Thai Visa Centre's LTR advertising, immigration advice, and representations about professional processing expertise.

**An agency claiming to specialize in complex immigration applications should not direct customers toward a work permit that Thailand's own investment authority explicitly states they cannot obtain.**


### November 2026 Update: TVC Archives Official Guidance Contradicting Its Digital Work Permit Claims

By November 27, 2026, Thai Visa Centre's expanding legal archive included a dedicated publication reproducing and summarizing the Board of Investment's official LTR visa frequently asked questions.[^680]

The archived material acknowledges that Work-from-Thailand Professionals cannot obtain Thai work permits under that visa category.

This directly contradicts the company's previously indexed commercial guide, which repeatedly advertises digital work permits as a benefit of the Work-from-Thailand Professionals category.[^123]

The contradiction extends to the application procedure itself, with the commercial guide instructing successful applicants to apply for a digital work permit after receiving their LTR visa.

**Thai Visa Centre is now archiving official government guidance explaining why prospective customers cannot obtain a document that its own commercial publication promises.**

The distinction is particularly significant because the company promotes digital work permits as an advantage of choosing the more financially demanding LTR visa over the Destination Thailand Visa.

Publishing government legislation and official guidance may improve public access to immigration information. However, it does not establish professional expertise when the company's own commercial advice contradicts the government material it publishes.

Thai Visa Centre should reconcile these publications, remove the unavailable work-permit benefit from its advertising, and clarify whether customers have previously been advised to pursue unnecessary work-permit processing.

The available evidence does not establish deliberate deception, the issuance of fraudulent permits, or financial losses suffered by individual customers.

## Mandatory LTR Health Insurance Claims Contradict Official BOI Alternatives

Thai Visa Centre publishes misleading instructions concerning the health-insurance requirements of Thailand's Long-Term Resident (LTR) visa program.

Its principal LTR sales page identifies health insurance as a required application document and states that visa holders must maintain valid health insurance throughout their stay.[^131]

However, Thailand's Board of Investment (BOI), which administers the LTR program, explicitly permits eligible applicants to satisfy the corresponding financial and healthcare requirement through alternatives to private health insurance.[^132]

Those alternatives include qualifying Thai social security coverage and bank deposits maintained for at least 12 months.

**Thai Visa Centre presents private health insurance as mandatory even though the responsible government authority explicitly permits applicants to qualify without purchasing an insurance policy.**

The error also affects dependents, whose official requirements include a separate, lower bank-deposit alternative.

### Health Insurance Presented as a Universal Requirement

Thai Visa Centre's dedicated *Long-Term Resident Visa (LTR)* page advertises immigration assistance across the program's four principal categories.

Its category-specific documentation lists repeatedly identify health insurance with minimum coverage of US$50,000 as a required document.[^131]

The same page includes a separate section titled *Health Insurance*, instructing applicants to provide a policy meeting that coverage threshold for their stay in Thailand.

Under *Restrictions*, the website makes an even more categorical claim:

> Must maintain valid health insurance

This instruction appears alongside legitimate continuing obligations, including maintaining qualification criteria, completing annual immigration reporting, and complying with employment requirements.

The page does not explain that the insurance requirement can be satisfied through other forms of evidence.

**A prospective applicant reading TVC's service description would reasonably conclude that obtaining and maintaining private health insurance is a non-negotiable condition of LTR approval.**

That conclusion conflicts with the government's published requirements.

### The BOI Explicitly Permits Three Alternatives

The BOI's official LTR program website identifies three acceptable methods of satisfying the healthcare-related requirement for principal applicants.[^132]

| Qualification pathway | Official requirement |
|---|---|
| Private or qualifying group health insurance | At least US$50,000 in medical coverage |
| Thai social security | Valid benefits covering hospitalization and medical treatment in Thailand |
| Bank-deposit alternative | At least US$100,000 maintained for a minimum of 12 months |

These are alternatives rather than cumulative requirements.

Applicants do not automatically need private health insurance if they provide qualifying social security evidence or satisfy the bank-deposit option.

The BOI repeats this arrangement across its descriptions of Wealthy Global Citizens, Wealthy Pensioners, Work-from-Thailand Professionals, and Highly Skilled Professionals.

The agency's official document checklist for Work-from-Thailand Professionals independently confirms the alternatives.[^133]

That checklist specifies that qualifying bank evidence must demonstrate a balance of at least US$100,000 maintained for at least 12 months.

It also clarifies that investment portfolios and fixed deposits are not accepted for that particular form of evidence.

**The BOI does not impose a universal requirement to purchase private health insurance. It requires applicants to demonstrate compliance through one of several accepted pathways.**

### The Official Rules for Dependents Are Different

The distinction is equally important for spouses and children applying as LTR dependents.

The BOI's published dependent requirements allow applicants to satisfy the healthcare-related condition through:

- Qualifying insurance providing at least US$50,000 in coverage.
- Valid Thai social security benefits covering hospitalization and medical treatment.
- A qualifying bank deposit of at least US$25,000 maintained for a minimum of 12 months.

The BOI's November 6, 2025 document checklist expressly identifies these three alternatives.[^134]

The US$25,000 threshold is substantially lower than the US$100,000 bank-deposit alternative applicable to principal applicants.

However, Thai Visa Centre's insurance FAQ states that each dependent carries their own policy.[^135]

That wording again suggests private insurance is mandatory for every family member.

**A dependent satisfying the BOI's US$25,000 deposit requirement does not automatically need to purchase a separate private health-insurance policy merely because they are applying under the LTR program.**

The eligibility of any particular dependent remains subject to the BOI's documentary requirements and assessment.

Nevertheless, the government's published rules expressly permit alternatives that TVC's categorical instructions fail to describe.

### Dependent Guide Confuses Insurance Coverage With Deposit Requirements

Thai Visa Centre's *LTR Visa Thailand for Spouses and Dependents* guide repeatedly instructs applicants to obtain health insurance providing at least **US$25,000 in coverage**.[^294]

The figure appears in its application summary, document checklist, insurance requirements, and warnings about insufficient coverage.

However, Thailand's Board of Investment establishes a **US$50,000 minimum for qualifying health insurance** under the LTR dependent category.[^132]

The BOI separately permits dependents to qualify through valid Thai social security coverage or a bank deposit of at least **US$25,000 maintained for 12 months**.

**TVC appears to confuse the US$25,000 bank-deposit alternative with the US$50,000 insurance-coverage requirement.**

These are fundamentally different qualification methods.

An applicant purchasing a US$25,000 insurance policy based on TVC's instructions could submit inadequate financial or healthcare evidence and experience delays or rejection.

Conversely, applicants with sufficient qualifying deposits or social security benefits might unnecessarily purchase insurance because the guide presents it as mandatory.

The same publication also incorrectly instructs LTR dependents to submit 90-day immigration reports, despite the BOI's published annual-reporting procedure for LTR holders.[^115]

This adds another inconsistency to the agency's previously documented LTR advice.

**Thai Visa Centre's dependent-specific guidance contains errors affecting both application eligibility and ongoing immigration compliance, despite the correct requirements being publicly available through the BOI.**

The agency should correct the insurance threshold, explain the accepted alternatives, and distinguish annual LTR reporting from ordinary quarterly notifications.

### Thai Visa Centre's Own Insurance FAQ Acknowledges the Alternative

The inconsistency is particularly revealing because Thai Visa Centre has published the correct distinction elsewhere on its website.

Its FAQ *Do I Need Health Insurance for Thailand?* discusses insurance requirements across different immigration categories.[^135]

Under its LTR section, the article acknowledges that applicants may use an equivalent cash-deposit alternative under BOI rules.

That acknowledgment directly undermines the main LTR sales page's statement that valid health insurance must be maintained.

The company's separate guide *How to Stay in Thailand for 20 Years* introduces the same confusion.[^136]

Its planning checklist states that health insurance is mandatory for O-A retirement visas, required for LTR, and recommended for Thailand Privilege.

The guide repeats that classification without explaining the BOI's alternatives.

The result is three materially different presentations:

1. **Main LTR sales page:** Health insurance is required and must be maintained.
2. **Twenty-year residence guide:** Health insurance is required for LTR applicants.
3. **Insurance FAQ:** A cash-deposit alternative exists under BOI rules.

**Thai Visa Centre already acknowledges that the LTR insurance requirement has exceptions, yet continues advertising private insurance as compulsory in its main commercial guidance.**

The company does not explain why prospective customers receive different information depending on which article they consult.

### Health Insurance and Financial Evidence Are Not Interchangeable Costs

The BOI's alternatives have different financial implications.

Purchasing private health insurance generally involves paying premiums to an insurer.

A qualifying bank deposit instead involves maintaining a substantial balance that remains the applicant's own money, subject to the government's requirements.

These are materially different financial commitments.

A bank deposit is not the same as an application fee or an insurance premium.

However, maintaining US$100,000 in qualifying funds can impose a substantial liquidity requirement and may be impractical for applicants who would otherwise prefer purchasing insurance.

Likewise, Thai social security coverage is available only where applicants can demonstrate valid qualifying benefits.

**The alternatives are not necessarily cheaper or more convenient for every applicant, but their existence is central to determining which LTR application strategy is appropriate.**

A professional visa adviser should explain those choices rather than presenting one option as universally mandatory.

### The Error Can Affect Families and Older Applicants

The omission may be particularly consequential for applicants who face difficulties obtaining private health insurance.

Some older applicants may encounter high premiums, exclusions, or difficulty obtaining coverage for pre-existing medical conditions.

Other applicants may already possess qualifying financial resources or valid Thai social security benefits.

For these individuals, the BOI's alternatives may materially affect whether an LTR application is practical.

A family applying for LTR status may also need to compare the cost of multiple insurance policies with the separate financial-evidence requirements for dependents.

**Telling every applicant and dependent that private health insurance is mandatory can cause unnecessary concern, discourage otherwise eligible applicants, or lead customers to purchase coverage that the government does not require under their chosen qualification pathway.**

The public evidence does not establish that Thai Visa Centre has sold unnecessary insurance policies, received insurance commissions, or charged customers for avoidable documentation.

Those conclusions would require transaction records or customer testimony.

The documented problem is the agency's inaccurate explanation of the government's eligibility requirements.

### Maintaining Qualification Does Not Necessarily Mean Maintaining Insurance

The BOI requires LTR visa holders to maintain the conditions under which their applications were approved.

These continuing obligations may include employment arrangements, investment amounts, bank balances, and insurance coverage, depending on the applicant's specific qualification pathway.[^132]

That requirement does not establish that everyone must maintain private insurance.

An applicant approved through the bank-deposit alternative must satisfy the applicable financial conditions.

An applicant relying on qualifying insurance must maintain the required coverage.

An applicant relying on Thai social security must satisfy the corresponding eligibility requirements.

**Thai Visa Centre's blanket instruction to maintain valid health insurance fails to distinguish between the different methods of satisfying the BOI's underlying requirement.**

The company should explain which continuing obligations apply to each pathway instead of describing one option as universal.

### The Broader Pattern of LTR Misinformation

The existing investigation has documented two other important discrepancies in Thai Visa Centre's LTR guidance.

First, the agency advertises digital work permits for Work-from-Thailand Professionals despite the BOI explicitly excluding that category from work-permit issuance.

Second, its 90-day reporting guide incorrectly tells LTR holders to submit quarterly TM47 notifications even though the government replaces that procedure with annual TM95 reporting.

The health-insurance discrepancy adds a third independent problem.

All three concern material benefits, obligations, or eligibility conditions associated with the same government immigration program.

They also share a common characteristic: **the correct information is available through official BOI publications, and some of it is acknowledged elsewhere on TVC's own website**.

This raises questions about whether the agency's published immigration guidance is reviewed consistently against the primary government requirements it claims to explain.

### Questions the Company Should Answer

Thai Visa Centre should clarify why its main LTR sales page requires private health insurance despite the BOI's officially permitted alternatives.

It should also:

1. Identify the US$100,000 bank-deposit alternative for principal applicants.
2. Explain the qualifying Thai social security pathway.
3. Identify the US$25,000 deposit alternative available to eligible dependents.
4. Correct categorical statements requiring all LTR holders to maintain insurance.
5. Distinguish bank-balance maintenance from insurance-premium payments.
6. Reconcile its main sales page, insurance FAQ, and long-term residence guides.

The company should further clarify whether its professional document-preparation service advises applicants about all accepted qualification pathways before recommending particular insurance arrangements.

The available evidence does not establish intentional deception, unnecessary insurance sales, or financial harm to a specific customer.

**It does establish that Thai Visa Centre repeatedly presents one optional method of satisfying the BOI's healthcare-related requirements as though it were mandatory for all LTR applicants.**

This is a consequential factual error because the government expressly provides alternatives for applicants who meet the relevant criteria.

**An immigration agency advertising expertise in the LTR program should not tell customers they must purchase or maintain private health insurance when the responsible government authority explicitly permits approval through qualifying social security benefits or maintained bank deposits.**

## Categorical T8 Health-Form Advice Omits Continuing Disease-Control Requirements

Thai Visa Centre publishes categorical statements declaring Thailand's T8 health declaration discontinued.

Its dedicated guide *Health Declaration T8 Form: What Happened to It?* tells travelers that the form is no longer required, describes it primarily as a COVID-era document, and instructs visitors not to download or print it.[^137]

A separate travel-planning guide repeats that T8 was a pandemic measure replaced by Thailand's Digital Arrival Card (TDAC) for all arrivals.[^138]

These statements correctly reflect the removal of routine paper health declarations for ordinary travelers.

However, they omit a consequential distinction.

**Thailand continues to impose special health-declaration, vaccination, and disease-control requirements on travelers arriving from certain countries. Official 2026 guidance also warns that some affected passengers may still need a separate T8 declaration before boarding.**

The discrepancy matters because an immigration agency advising international travelers must distinguish the removal of routine paperwork from the continuing requirements applicable to travelers with specific epidemiological risks.

### Thai Visa Centre Declares T8 Discontinued

Thai Visa Centre's dedicated T8 guide opens with a categorical explanation:

> Here is the straight answer for 2026: the T8 is no longer required.

The article identifies the form's status as **"Discontinued"** and describes it as a paper pandemic document no longer used at the border.[^137]

Its FAQ repeats that travelers do not need to download T8 because the form has been discontinued.

The guide additionally characterizes the T8 primarily as part of Thailand's COVID-19 border-control arrangements between approximately 2020 and 2022.

Its separate article *20 Essential Tips Before Traveling to Thailand* gives substantially the same answer.

That publication states that the paper T8 declaration was a pandemic measure replaced by TDAC for all arrivals.[^138]

**The combined effect is to present T8 as obsolete paperwork whose relevance ended with Thailand's pandemic entry restrictions.**

That explanation is incomplete.

### T8 Has a Separate Yellow-Fever History

Thailand's T8 health declaration has also been used for screening travelers arriving from yellow-fever-affected countries.

It was not exclusively a COVID-19 form.

The Thai Health Pass platform identifies the declaration as a health-screening process associated with Thailand's Department of Disease Control.[^139]

The form's historical applications include the reporting of travel history and health information for visitors from designated disease-control areas.

This distinction is important because the removal of pandemic-wide requirements does not necessarily eliminate disease-specific health controls.

Thailand continues identifying countries and territories subject to yellow-fever vaccination and screening requirements.

The government's 2026 international-meeting travel guidance explicitly confirms that travelers arriving from designated yellow-fever-affected countries must present an International Health Certificate establishing qualifying vaccination.[^141]

The certificate must be presented to the relevant health-control and immigration authorities.

**The fact that ordinary arrivals no longer complete a standalone T8 paper form does not establish that every traveler has been released from special health-documentation requirements.**

### T8 Registration Was Integrated Into TDAC

The present position is more nuanced than either a universal requirement or complete disappearance.

The Thai Health Pass website now explains that T8 health-declaration registration has moved into the Thailand Digital Arrival Card system.[^139]

The official Immigration Bureau TDAC guidance likewise describes health declarations as part of the arrival-card process.[^140]

It explains that travelers may be asked for additional health information according to their nationality, recent travel history, departure location, or flight.

In other words, the TDAC system does not simply collect passport and accommodation details.

It also serves public-health screening purposes.

| Issue | Published official position |
|---|---|
| Routine paper arrival card | Replaced by TDAC |
| Ordinary T8 health declaration | Incorporated into digital arrival procedures |
| Health questions for designated travelers | May be required through TDAC |
| Yellow-fever vaccination certification | Continues for qualifying travelers |
| Separate T8 requested by an airline | May still apply in specified circumstances |

**The government's current system incorporates health declarations into TDAC rather than abolishing all health-screening obligations formerly associated with T8.**

Thai Visa Centre's general explanation of TDAC does not adequately identify this distinction.

### November 2026 Guidance Warns That Separate T8 Forms May Still Be Requested

The discrepancy becomes more significant when compared with contemporary travel instructions.

A United Nations information note dated November 4, 2026, prepared for participants traveling to Thailand, confirms the continued application of special health-control measures.[^142]

Its health-control section explains that travelers from designated yellow-fever-affected countries must present valid vaccination documentation.

It also describes additional screening measures for travelers arriving from specified Ebola-affected areas.

Crucially, the publication warns:

> Travellers from Yellow Fever and Ebola affected countries may also be required by the airline to present a separate completed Health Declaration Form T.8 form before boarding.

This is not a statement that every traveler must complete T8.

It is a conditional warning for passengers with particular travel histories.

Nevertheless, **the warning directly undermines an unqualified instruction that T8 has been completely discontinued and should never be prepared for a 2026 journey**.

The distinction matters at check-in, where airlines may verify health documents before permitting a passenger to board.

A traveler relying on TVC's categorical explanation could fail to investigate additional documentation required for their itinerary.

### Ebola Controls Demonstrate the Continuing Risk

Thailand's 2026 health-control requirements extend beyond yellow fever.

Government-published guidance for international visitors identifies special Ebola screening and quarantine arrangements affecting travelers arriving from designated countries, including the Democratic Republic of the Congo.[^141]

The November 2026 United Nations travel instructions separately identify restrictions affecting travelers arriving from or through specified Ebola-affected areas.[^142]

These measures are distinct from the former general COVID-19 quarantine rules.

They reflect disease-specific public-health powers and can change as outbreak conditions develop.

Thai Visa Centre's T8 guide acknowledges in general terms that Thailand may introduce additional screening during outbreaks.

However, it does not identify the existing yellow-fever requirements or the Ebola-related measures relevant during 2026.

**The article presents disease-specific controls as something that might return in the future without adequately explaining the health restrictions already relevant to certain travelers.**

That omission is particularly important because the consequences may include additional screening, mandatory observation, quarantine, or boarding-document requirements.

### The Misleading Historical Explanation

Thai Visa Centre's account also compresses two different historical developments into one narrative.

The first was Thailand's use of health declarations and disease-control documentation for travelers from designated infection-risk areas.

The second was the broader use of health questionnaires and exceptional entry paperwork during the COVID-19 pandemic.

Although both involved health screening, they were not identical programs.

Ending the pandemic's general entry restrictions did not automatically repeal every earlier disease-control requirement.

The Thai Health Pass platform's continuing reference to T8 health declarations demonstrates that the form's terminology and public-health purpose extend beyond the pandemic.[^139]

The government's continued yellow-fever screening requirements independently establish that the underlying disease-control obligations remain relevant.[^141]

**Describing T8 solely as an obsolete COVID-19 form obscures its separate role in Thailand's longstanding infectious-disease screening arrangements.**

### The Correct Advice Depends on Travel History

For most ordinary arrivals, Thai Visa Centre's practical conclusion is broadly correct.

Travelers generally complete TDAC online rather than obtaining a separate paper T8 declaration.

However, a traveler arriving from a designated yellow-fever area or another country subject to special disease-control measures should verify additional requirements.

Those requirements may involve:

1. Completing health declarations through TDAC.
2. Presenting a qualifying international vaccination certificate.
3. Undergoing screening at a port-health checkpoint.
4. Complying with additional outbreak-specific public-health instructions.
5. Providing a separate T8 declaration when requested by the airline or competent authority.

Not all five requirements apply to every affected traveler.

The applicable conditions depend on the disease, recent travel history, public-health notices, and current instructions from the authorities and carrier.

**The critical distinction is between ordinary immigration arrival paperwork and special public-health obligations that may remain applicable independently of ordinary TDAC registration.**

A professional travel adviser should explain that distinction rather than simply declaring the older form irrelevant.

### The Importance of Accurate Arrival Advice

Thai Visa Centre markets professional assistance with visas, entry documentation, and TDAC submissions.

Its T8 guide specifically encourages travelers to seek its help with current arrival procedures.

Customers may therefore encounter the company's explanations while preparing documentation for an international flight.

For travelers arriving from countries subject to yellow-fever or Ebola-related measures, incomplete health-documentation advice can have consequences beyond administrative inconvenience.

Depending on the applicable requirements, travelers may face boarding difficulties, additional health checks, or other restrictions imposed by public-health authorities.

The available evidence does not establish that a traveler has been refused boarding or entry because of Thai Visa Centre's published guidance.

It also does not establish that a separate paper T8 form remains universally mandatory.

**What is established is that Thai Visa Centre presents the form as categorically discontinued while contemporary official guidance retains circumstances in which additional health declarations, including a possible separate T8 request, remain relevant.**

### Questions the Agency Should Answer

Thai Visa Centre should clarify whether its statement that T8 is discontinued applies only to ordinary arrivals.

It should also distinguish the electronic integration of T8 health declarations into TDAC from continuing requirements for designated infection-risk travelers.

Its travel guides should explain the current yellow-fever vaccination requirements and direct travelers with relevant itineraries to official outbreak-specific instructions.

The company should further acknowledge that airlines may impose additional document checks for travelers arriving from affected countries, as identified in the November 2026 United Nations guidance.

These corrections would not require treating T8 as universally mandatory again.

They would simply distinguish ordinary procedures from the exceptional requirements still applicable to some passengers.

**Thai Visa Centre tells travelers that T8 is discontinued while failing to explain continuing public-health requirements under which travelers from specified countries may still be asked to provide a separate health declaration.**

The discrepancy adds another example of incomplete immigration and entry guidance from a company advertising specialist knowledge of Thailand's changing travel requirements.

## Claimed Embassy Connections and DTV Approval Assurances Contradict Official Procedures

Thai Visa Centre advertises special embassy relationships, fast-track submission arrangements, and assurances of approval when promoting its Destination Thailand Visa (DTV) services.

However, Thailand's official visa procedures reserve approval decisions to authorized consular officers.

The agency's published DTV processing times are also internally inconsistent, describing official embassy review as taking only two to three days while separately advertising substantially longer standard and express processing periods.

**Thai Visa Centre uses claims of privileged embassy coordination and approval certainty to promote paid immigration services without identifying an official procedure establishing those advantages.**

### The Agency Claims It Can Ensure Approval

On its dedicated DTV assistance page, Thai Visa Centre describes the services available to applicants who are already in Thailand.[^143]

It states:

> We work closely with our nearby embassy contacts to confirm eligibility and ensure approval before arranging a visa run.

This is a consequential commercial representation.

An immigration adviser can assess eligibility, prepare documentation, contact consular offices, and identify potential weaknesses in an application.

However, those activities do not establish authority to approve a government visa.

The wording also leaves an important procedural question unanswered.

**How can the agency ensure approval before arranging the applicant's departure when DTV issuance requires a formal application to the competent Thai embassy or consulate outside Thailand?**

The publication does not identify a preliminary approval system, an official embassy agreement, or a government-issued authorization supporting the advertised arrangement.

### Visa Decisions Remain With Consular Officers

Thailand's official e-Visa platform explains that applications remain subject to government approval.[^144]

Embassies and consulates may request further documents or interviews, and submitting a complete application does not guarantee issuance.

The Royal Thai Embassy in Washington, D.C., independently confirms that consular officers retain authority to reject applications and request additional evidence.[^145]

These rules do not prevent immigration agencies from offering legitimate document-preparation services.

Nor do they prohibit ordinary communications between agencies and embassy personnel.

Nevertheless, **a commercial agency's ability to contact an embassy is not equivalent to the authority to guarantee a consular decision**.

Thai Visa Centre's published description does not adequately explain this distinction.

### The Company Advertises Fast-Track Embassy Channels

The agency's primary DTV visa page makes a related claim.[^146]

Its application process includes an embassy-submission stage described as:

> Fast-track submission through our embassy channels

The page assigns one day to this stage.

However, it does not identify the relevant embassy, an official expedited-submission program, or the conditions under which customers qualify for preferential handling.

The existence of embassy contacts would not independently establish access to an officially recognized fast-track procedure.

**Thai Visa Centre advertises a potentially valuable government-processing advantage without documenting the administrative basis for that advantage.**

The claim is especially relevant because the company charges customers for professional application assistance and promotes its expertise as a way to reduce rejection risks.

### Contradictory Processing Times on the Same Page

The main DTV sales page contains another independently verifiable inconsistency.

At the beginning of the publication, Thai Visa Centre advertises the following estimated processing periods:

| Advertised service | Processing time |
|---|---|
| Standard | 2–5 weeks |
| Express | 1–3 weeks |

Yet the same page's application-process section describes official embassy review and processing as taking **2–3 days**.[^146]

The publication does not explain whether the shorter estimate applies only to particular embassies, previously reviewed applications, internal agency processing, or another exceptional arrangement.

In fact, it explicitly labels the shorter stage as official embassy review.

The discrepancy matters because the claimed time advantage could influence customers deciding whether to purchase expedited assistance, schedule international travel, or commit to nonrefundable expenses.

Some Thai consular offices may process straightforward applications quickly.

That possibility does not reconcile the different estimates published on the same sales page.

**Thai Visa Centre presents materially different processing expectations without identifying the conditions under which either estimate applies.**

### The Embassy Warns Against Assuming Rapid Processing

The Royal Thai Embassy in Washington, D.C., advises visa applicants to plan well in advance because application processing can take longer during periods of high demand.[^145]

Its October 2026 guidance recommends applying at least 30 days before the intended departure date.

The embassy also explains that requests for additional documents can extend the processing schedule.

These warnings demonstrate why a universal two-to-three-day expectation would be unreliable.

A professional intermediary may help customers avoid document errors, but it cannot independently control an embassy's workload, discretionary review, or requests for additional evidence.

**The published government guidance does not support treating Thai Visa Centre's two-to-three-day processing figure as a generally reliable expectation for DTV applicants.**

### Thai Visa Centre Contradicts Its Own Disclaimer

The agency's broader immigration guidance acknowledges the limits of professional assistance.

Its *Thailand Visa Services* guide states:

> No agency can guarantee immigration outcomes.

That explanation correctly distinguishes private application assistance from government decision-making.[^147]

However, its DTV marketing simultaneously claims that embassy contacts allow the company to ensure approval before arranging travel.

The two representations create different expectations about the same underlying service.

One correctly acknowledges that immigration outcomes cannot be guaranteed.

The other suggests that TVC's embassy relationships can provide certainty before an application proceeds.

**Thai Visa Centre acknowledges the limits of private visa assistance in its general guidance while using stronger approval assurances in its commercial DTV advertising.**

### Refund Protection Does Not Establish Approval Authority

Thai Visa Centre also advertises a refund policy for unsuccessful DTV applications.

Its DTV sales pages state that agency service fees can be refunded if the application is unsuccessful, while government visa fees remain nonrefundable.[^143][^146]

This distinction may provide customers with a degree of financial protection.

However, a refund promise is not evidence that the agency can guarantee government approval.

Indeed, the company's acknowledgment that applications may be rejected demonstrates that unsuccessful outcomes remain possible despite its professional assistance.

Customers must also distinguish between refundable service fees and nonrefundable embassy fees, travel costs, accommodation expenses, and other commitments made in anticipation of approval.

**The availability of an agency-fee refund does not eliminate the financial consequences of relying on an unsupported processing or approval assurance.**

### Why the Claims Matter

The existing investigation documents Thai Visa Centre's outdated DTV application instructions following the August 31, 2026 changes to application-location and documentation requirements.

The company's claims about embassy contacts and pre-approval add a separate concern.

They suggest a degree of access or influence over government visa decisions that the published evidence does not substantiate.

These statements could be particularly influential for customers considering an international visa run.

Such customers may incur expenses for flights, accommodation, embassy fees, professional services, and time away from employment.

They need to understand which parts of the process an agency can legitimately coordinate and which decisions remain exclusively with government authorities.

The available evidence does not establish improper relationships with embassy employees, preferential government treatment, bribery, or actual interference with visa decisions.

Nor does it establish that the company has failed to secure approvals for particular customers.

**The documented issue is that Thai Visa Centre advertises approval assurances and fast-track embassy arrangements without publishing evidence identifying the official basis for those claims.**

### Questions the Company Should Answer

Thai Visa Centre should identify the government procedures underlying its advertised embassy relationships and explain precisely what it means by ensuring approval before arranging a visa run.

It should also clarify whether its fast-track submission service uses a formally recognized government channel or simply expedited document preparation.

Its DTV sales page should reconcile the advertised two-to-three-day embassy-processing estimate with its substantially longer standard and express processing periods.

Finally, the company should distinguish eligibility screening from formal consular approval and explain the practical limits of its refund protection.

**Thai Visa Centre promotes paid DTV assistance through claims of embassy connections, advance approval assurances, and expedited processing, while its own guidance and official government procedures acknowledge that visa decisions remain discretionary.**

The discrepancy raises legitimate questions about whether prospective customers receive an accurate explanation of the authority, speed, and certainty of the immigration services being advertised.

## TVC's Work-Permit Guides Misstate Filing Procedures and Legal Requirements

Thai Visa Centre advertises professional work-permit assistance for foreign employees, company directors, and employers operating in Thailand.

However, four of its guides continue describing traditional paper-based work-permit procedures without explaining Thailand's nationwide e-WorkPermit system, introduced in October 2025.[^148][^149][^150][^151]

All four publications display June 2026 as their most recent review date.

The discrepancy is significant because Thailand had already introduced electronic applications, online fee payments, appointment scheduling, and a standardized permit-card system.

Although temporary exemptions allowed some manual applications to continue, **Thai Visa Centre's publications do not distinguish those exceptions from the government's new electronic procedures.**

Instead, they repeatedly describe obtaining a traditional work-permit booklet as the expected outcome of an application.

### Thailand Introduced e-WorkPermit in October 2025

Thailand's Ministry of Labour launched e-WorkPermit nationwide on October 13, 2025.[^152]

The official platform, https://eworkpermit.doe.go.th/, introduced electronic processing for work-permit applications and related employment notifications.

The Ministry of Labour described a workflow involving online registration, electronic application submission, digital payment, government verification, and electronic approval notifications.[^155]

Applicants could subsequently arrange service-center appointments for identity verification, biometric registration, and permit collection.

The system included 54 service centers and support units nationwide.

The government also introduced a standardized electronic work-permit card, identified as Form BorTor. 42/1.[^154]

**These changes directly affected the application and documentation procedures that professional work-permit advisers were expected to explain.**

The new system did not eliminate work-permit requirements, employer sponsorship obligations, or the need to obtain authorization before beginning employment.

### Four TVC Guides Continue Describing Traditional Booklets

Thai Visa Centre's *Thailand Work Permit: Requirements, Process, and Renewals* instructs applicants to submit documentation through the Department of Employment or an eligible One Stop Service office.[^148]

Its approval stage states:

> Work permit booklet issued specifying employer, position, and location.

The article does not explain e-WorkPermit registration, online applications, or the new card format.

A second publication, *Thailand Work Permit Requirements: Documents and Checklist*, instructs employers or authorized representatives to file applications at provincial or central Department of Employment offices.[^149]

It subsequently tells applicants to collect a work-permit booklet after approval.

The guide describes its document requirements as applicable in 2026 but does not explain when electronic submission is required or when manual filing remains permitted.

A third guide, *Thailand Work Permit Basics: Quotas, Exceptions, and Rules*, introduces the booklet as the document defining an employee's authorized employer, job description, and workplace.[^150]

It also describes carrying the permit during working hours and physically returning it following resignation.

Some of these obligations may remain relevant to existing paper permits or particular employment categories.

However, the guide does not distinguish those circumstances from applications processed through the electronic system.

**Across these three publications, readers are given an older administrative model without being told how it relates to the government's new procedures.**

### TVC's Commercial Work-Permit Guide Contains Additional Contradictions

The fourth publication, *Work Permit in Thailand: Complete Legal and Immigration Guide*, explicitly advertises work-permit filing as a core Thai Visa Centre service.[^151]

It claims that TVC coordinates work permits end-to-end with employer documentation.

Yet its application instructions direct customers to submit documents at a government office and then:

> Receive permit book

The guide tells customers to check the employer, job title, and work location against that physical booklet.

There is also an internal inconsistency.

An earlier summary panel tells readers to wait for a permit card before working, while the detailed application workflow instructs them to receive a permit book.

Neither passage explains the difference between the two formats or identifies which procedure applies to which applicant.

The same publication contains an unrelated section headed *Administrative divorce workflow*, including instructions concerning divorce registration before introducing its work-permit application steps.

This is a separate, visible editorial error in a publication presented as professional legal and immigration guidance.

**The conflicting permit terminology and unrelated divorce material raise additional questions about the quality of the publication's editorial review.**

### Manual Work-Permit Applications Remained Possible in 2026

The transition to e-WorkPermit was complicated by technical difficulties and government exemptions.

In November 2025, the Department of Employment announced temporary arrangements allowing certain applicants to continue using older procedures.[^153]

The initial deadline for qualifying paper submissions was January 28, 2026.

However, that deadline was subsequently extended.

Immigration advisory firm Fragomen documented a February 2026 extension to April 28, followed by an April extension allowing qualifying manual applications until July 28, 2026.[^156]

The extensions covered initial applications, renewals, cancellations, and amendments in specified circumstances, including technical problems with the electronic system.

Separate arrangements also permitted certain employers using the Thailand Investment and Expat Services Center's Rapid Process to continue submitting applications manually.[^157]

In July 2026, the Department of Employment extended the general technical-exception deadline again, to October 28, 2026.[^156]

These developments are important.

**The introduction of e-WorkPermit did not immediately make every manual application invalid or obsolete.**

Consequently, it would be inaccurate to accuse Thai Visa Centre of describing an entirely prohibited process simply because its guides mention government-office submissions.

The documented problem is narrower.

TVC does not explain the electronic system, distinguish normal procedures from temporary exceptions, or identify which applicants should follow each route.

That omission matters particularly in publications marked as reviewed in June 2026, when the electronic system and the transitional arrangements were already established.

### TVC's Paper-Filing Instructions Outlasted the General Exception

The latest general extension identified in the cited sources allowed manual work-permit submissions only when technical problems prevented use of e-WorkPermit, with proof of the problem required. The deadline was October 28, 2026.[^156] That deadline has passed.

TVC's four work-permit guides, each marked reviewed June 2026, describe office-based filing and physical work-permit booklets without explaining that manual submission was a limited, time-bound exception or identifying e-WorkPermit as the new system.[^148][^149][^150][^151]

The Department of Employment's SupportEWP notice says its issue-reporting service closed on October 1 and directs users to report problems through e-WorkPermit.[^516]

A separate government renewal window remains open through December 11, 2026 for qualifying Lao, Myanmar, and Vietnamese workers. Those applications must be submitted electronically through e-WorkPermit.[^517] That limited renewal program does not extend the general paper-filing exception.

**TVC's guides leave readers with an outdated filing process and omit the deadline that ended the general paper exception.**

### The New Permit Format Creates Further Confusion

In December 2025, professional-services firm Forvis Mazars analyzed the electronic work-permit system and its standardized smart-card format.[^154]

The analysis explained that the new card differs from the traditional booklet, including in how employer-specific authorization information is displayed and verified.

The standardized card does not display the employer's name directly on its face.

Instead, electronic verification provides access to the underlying authorization records.

This distinction matters because Thai Visa Centre repeatedly instructs applicants to inspect employer and workplace details physically printed in a permit booklet.

Those instructions may be relevant to older permits or particular issuance arrangements, but they do not adequately describe the newer standardized card.

The correct distinction is between the legal conditions attached to employment authorization and the physical or electronic documents used to verify those conditions.

**TVC's guides fail to explain that distinction despite presenting work-permit compliance as an area of professional expertise.**

### The June 2026 Review Claims Raise Questions

The four affected publications all identify June 2026 as their most recent review date.

That was approximately eight months after the electronic system's nationwide launch.

By then, official information describing e-WorkPermit was publicly available, and the Department of Employment had already introduced and extended transitional filing arrangements.

Yet none of the four publications explains e-WorkPermit or clearly distinguishes traditional booklet procedures from newer permits.

The review dates do not establish that TVC performed a substantive examination of the material in June.

They establish that the company presents the information to readers as having been reviewed at that time.

**The omission is particularly difficult to reconcile with a claimed 2026 review because the change concerns the actual process for obtaining the service TVC advertises.**

### Why the Discrepancy Matters

Work-permit applicants and employers rely on procedural guidance when preparing documentation, selecting filing channels, arranging appointments, and determining when employment may lawfully begin.

Incomplete instructions can cause confusion about whether electronic registration is necessary, whether manual filing is available, and which type of permit the applicant should expect.

The available evidence does not establish that Thai Visa Centre has improperly filed applications, obtained invalid work permits, or failed to follow the government's current procedures when representing customers.

Its internal operations may be more current than its website.

The evidence does establish that **four publicly available guides, each marked reviewed June 2026, omit a major government processing reform and repeatedly present traditional booklet procedures without explaining the applicable distinctions.**

One of those guides also contains conflicting permit terminology and unrelated divorce instructions.

### Questions Thai Visa Centre Should Answer

Thai Visa Centre should clarify which work-permit application channels it currently uses and how it determines whether an applicant qualifies for electronic or manual processing.

It should explain why its June 2026 guidance omits e-WorkPermit and whether its references to traditional booklets apply to older permits, special cases, or material that has not been substantively updated.

The company should also clarify how customers are informed about electronic verification, permit-card collection, and changes affecting their employment authorization.

Finally, it should explain the conflicting terminology and unrelated divorce material in its commercial work-permit guide.

**The central finding is not that every paper-based procedure disappeared in October 2025. It is that Thai Visa Centre continues presenting incomplete and internally inconsistent work-permit guidance while advertising professional assistance and claiming to have reviewed the material after a major government reform.**

### Work-Permit Guidance Omits Reduced Capital Requirements for Thai Spouses

Thai Visa Centre's guide *Thailand Work Permit: Requirements, Process, and Renewals*, marked reviewed June 2026, identifies 2 million baht in paid-up capital as the usual employer requirement for hiring a foreign worker.[^148]

Its separate *Thailand Work Permit Basics* guide repeats the 2-million-baht-per-foreigner calculation while acknowledging that marriage to a Thai citizen may justify special consideration.[^150]

However, neither guide clearly explains an important financial concession expressly recognized in official government work-permit criteria.

#### Government Guidance Reduces Capital Requirements by Half

The Bangkok Metropolitan Administration's published work-permit guidance identifies the following employer-capital requirements:

| Employer category | Standard | Qualifying Thai spouse |
|---|---:|---:|
| Thai-registered company | ฿2,000,000 | ฿1,000,000 |
| Overseas-registered employer operating in Thailand | ฿3,000,000 | ฿1,500,000 |

The government expressly provides that foreigners legally married to Thai nationals, holding a marriage certificate, and cohabiting with their spouse qualify for the 50% capital reduction.[^233]

This is a defined concession in the published work-permit criteria, not merely an unspecified discretionary exception.

Other employment, documentary, and immigration requirements remain applicable.

**TVC advertises the full capital requirement without explaining the reduced threshold potentially available to its marriage-visa customers.**

#### Work-Permit and Immigration-Extension Rules Are Different

TVC's work-permit guide also describes four Thai employees per foreign worker as a standard work-permit requirement.[^148]

However, the government's published immigration guidance identifies the 4:1 ratio in its separate criteria for extending permission to stay on employment grounds.[^234]

The distinction matters:

- The Department of Employment assesses work-permit eligibility, including applicable employer-capital concessions.
- Immigration separately assesses whether a foreign worker qualifies for an extension of stay.
- Foreign spouses extending their stay on marriage grounds may follow different immigration requirements from applicants extending on employment grounds.

The marriage-based reduction in work-permit capital does not automatically waive the 4:1 staffing test where that test applies to an employment-based immigration extension.

**These are related but legally distinct decisions, and TVC's guidance does not adequately separate them.**


#### Government-Recognized Exceptions to the Four-Employee Ratio

TVC's work-permit guide presents four Thai employees per foreign worker as the standard staffing requirement.[^148]

However, Thailand's official immigration guidance identifies specific business categories that qualify for substantially different requirements.

**Representative Offices, Regional Offices, and Multinational Branches**

Government guidance expressly provides that qualifying representative offices, regional offices, and multinational company branches may receive:

- A reduced ratio of **one permanent Thai employee per foreign employee**.
- Exemptions from specified employer-capital requirements.
- Exemptions from certain financial-statement and business-necessity criteria applicable to ordinary company applications.[^502]

These concessions are recognized in published government guidance for employment-based stay extensions, rather than being discretionary promises from private immigration agents.

The precise requirements depend on the employer's legal structure and the applicable immigration category.

Nevertheless, TVC's published work-permit instructions fail to explain these established alternatives.

**Long-Term Resident Visa Holders**

Thailand's Board of Investment separately identifies exemption from the **four-Thai-employees-per-foreigner ratio** as a benefit of the Long-Term Resident (LTR) visa programme.[^503]

This exemption is particularly relevant to qualifying Highly Skilled Professionals employed in Thailand.

LTR applicants remain subject to their own eligibility and employment-authorization rules. The exemption does not establish that every LTR category qualifies for a Thai work permit; for example, the BOI says Work-from-Thailand Professionals do not receive a Thai work permit for their remote employment with overseas employers.[^503]

However, the exemption demonstrates that the ordinary staffing ratio cannot be presented as a universal prerequisite for foreign professionals working legally in Thailand.

**Financial Consequences for Foreign Employers**

A company relying on TVC's general staffing advice could incorrectly assume that sponsoring one foreign professional requires hiring four Thai employees, even where its business structure qualifies for a reduced ratio.

That misunderstanding could influence hiring decisions, company budgets, investment planning, and whether an entrepreneur considers employing a foreign specialist financially viable.

For example, a qualifying representative office employing two foreign professionals may be eligible for a 1:1 arrangement requiring two permanent Thai employees rather than the eight suggested by applying the ordinary 4:1 calculation.

This illustration assumes the office satisfies the applicable eligibility requirements; it does not override other relevant labour or immigration rules.

TVC promotes work-permit coordination and employer-compliance assistance as core commercial services.

**Its guidance should identify the recognized 1:1 categories, explain applicable LTR exemptions, and distinguish immigration staffing conditions from Department of Employment work-permit eligibility.**

Foreign employers should not be led to believe that every qualifying employment arrangement requires the same staffing structure.

#### Consequences for Customers

Incorrectly assuming that every foreign worker requires 2 million baht in employer capital could discourage eligible spouses from applying or lead businesses to arrange unnecessary additional capitalization.

Confusing work-permit approval with employment-based extension requirements can also produce inaccurate assessments of staffing obligations.

The available evidence does not establish that TVC has demanded excess capital from customers or incorrectly processed work permits.

Nevertheless, its published guidance omits a substantial financial concession while promoting professional work-permit and marriage-visa assistance.

TVC should identify the reduced capital thresholds for qualifying foreign spouses and distinguish Department of Employment requirements from Immigration Bureau extension criteria.


### Office Guide Falsely Claims Thai Lawyers Are Mandatory for Work Permits

Thai Visa Centre's *Thailand Law Firms and TVC Office Locations* guide tells prospective foreign workers that obtaining a work permit requires a Thai lawyer.[^461]

Its list of common mistakes warns:

> Work permits require a Thai lawyer and employer coordination, not just an agent.
