**Thailand's ordinary work-permit procedures do not impose a blanket requirement to hire a Thai lawyer.**

#### Government Procedures Permit Employer and Worker Applications

Thailand's Ministry of Labour launched the nationwide e-WorkPermit system on October 13, 2025, expressly allowing employers and foreign workers to register and submit work-permit applications through the official platform.[^462]

The Ministry's description of those application channels does not make licensed legal representation a prerequisite.

The Board of Investment's official work-permit instructions also explain that a prospective employer may file an application on behalf of a foreign employee. Its published documentation requirements recognize applications handled through authorized representatives without stating that the representative must be a lawyer.[^463][^464]

The precise filing route depends on the applicant's employment category, applicable requirements, and any current procedural exceptions. None of those distinctions supports TVC's categorical claim that hiring a Thai lawyer is mandatory for work permits.

Legal advice may be valuable for complicated immigration histories, employment disputes, company-formation issues, or questions involving restricted occupations. But the practical value of professional legal assistance does not make it a universal statutory requirement.

#### Confusing Optional Legal Services With Government Requirements

TVC promotes coordination with legal professionals while warning readers that work permits require a Thai lawyer rather than an immigration agent alone.[^461]

That statement could lead foreign workers and employers to believe that they must purchase legal representation before using otherwise available government application channels.

**The distinction is between a legal obligation to obtain appropriate work authorization and an optional decision to hire a lawyer to assist with the process.**

The claim also adds to the problems documented above: TVC's work-permit guides describe outdated filing procedures, fail to clearly explain the e-WorkPermit system, and confuse employment-permit requirements with immigration-extension criteria.

These findings concern TVC's published guidance; they do not establish that the agency has improperly processed any individual customer's application.

Thai Visa Centre should remove its blanket statement that work permits require Thai lawyers, describe the government-authorized application routes, and distinguish mandatory employer and employee obligations from optional legal assistance.


### Work Permit Guide Repeats Restrictions Abolished by Thailand's 2018 Labour Reform

Thai Visa Centre's *Thailand Work Permit Basics*, marked reviewed June 2026, publishes outdated instructions about carrying work permits, changing job duties, and working at different locations.[^504]

The guide describes restrictions substantially changed by Thailand's 2018 amendments to the Foreigners' Working Management Emergency Decree.

**These are not recent or obscure regulatory changes. The reforms took effect more than eight years before TVC's stated review date.**

#### Physical Work-Permit Carrying Requirement Was Removed

TVC instructs foreign employees:

> Keep your work permit on your person or at the office during working hours.

It further claims that failure to comply carries a fine of up to **1,000 baht**.[^504]

This guidance is outdated.

The Foreigners' Working Management Emergency Decree (No. 2) B.E. 2561, effective March 28, 2018, replaced Section 68.

The amended provision requires permit holders to be able to present their work permit to an authorized official or registrar **within a reasonable period** upon request.

It does not universally require the physical permit to remain on the worker's person or at the workplace throughout working hours.

Section 120 establishes a maximum fine of **5,000 baht** for failing to satisfy the actual presentation requirement.[^505][^506]

**TVC identifies the wrong obligation and the wrong penalty.**

#### Former Restrictions on Work Duties and Locations Were Repealed

The guide also insists:

> Job title, employer, and location on the permit define legal work.

Its compliance instructions warn that working at another location, undertaking additional duties, or accepting a promotion requires modification or separate authorization.[^504]

These categorical statements conflict with the 2018 amendments.

Section 37 of the amending decree expressly repealed Sections 70, 71, 72, 73, and 74 of the original 2017 legislation.

Those repealed provisions included restrictions on work differing from the type, employer, locality, or conditions specified in a permit.

Amended Section 59 instead permits qualifying work-permit holders to undertake work not otherwise prohibited to foreigners, subject to other applicable legislation.[^505][^507]

**An ordinary foreign worker's legal permission is no longer universally confined to one job title or one registered workplace under the former statutory restrictions.**

#### Employer Notification Still Applies

The removal of the former restrictions does not eliminate employment reporting requirements.

Under Section 64/2, ordinary work-permit holders must notify the registrar about their employer, workplace, and characteristics of employment within **15 days** of commencing work. Changes of employer also trigger notification requirements.

Employers have separate notification responsibilities.[^505][^507]

Other rules may still affect particular workers, including immigration conditions, professional licensing, occupations prohibited or conditionally restricted to foreigners, and specific BOI or other statutory authorizations.

Those requirements should not be confused with the repealed general restrictions.

**The proper distinction is between work requiring prior authorization and changes requiring notification or compliance with another applicable law.**

TVC's guide fails to explain it.

#### Government and Legal Sources Confirm the Changes

The amended legislation is published by Thailand's Department of Employment.[^505]

Thai law firm Tilleke & Gibbins documented the reforms in May 2018, explaining that changes to job type, employer, location, and working conditions no longer universally required prior approval under the previous procedures. It also confirms the relaxed presentation requirement and **5,000-baht** maximum fine.[^506]

These sources establish that TVC's instructions are not simply an alternative interpretation of recent administrative practice.

They repeat legal restrictions substantially changed in 2018.

#### Misleading Foreign Workers About Their Rights

TVC advertises work-permit coordination, renewals, employer changes, and immigration assistance.

Its incorrect instructions could cause foreigners to believe that:

- They risk fines simply by leaving their physical work permit at home.
- They cannot perform additional lawful job duties without advance permission.
- They cannot work at another location without applying for a permit modification.
- Routine employment changes necessarily require additional authorization rather than the applicable notification procedures.

These misunderstandings could create unnecessary administrative work, interfere with legitimate employment, and encourage reliance on paid assistance.

The evidence does not establish that TVC charged particular customers for unnecessary modifications.

It establishes that the agency publishes outdated legal instructions while marketing services intended to help foreigners comply with those same laws.

#### Required Corrections

Thai Visa Centre should remove its obsolete physical-carrying requirement and correct the applicable penalty.

It should also revise its descriptions of workplace and job-duty restrictions, explain the 2018 repeal of Sections 70–74, and distinguish employer notification from situations requiring separate authorization.

**A company selling work-permit assistance should not present restrictions abolished in 2018 as current legal requirements in a guide supposedly reviewed in June 2026.**

## E-Visa FAQ Falsely Claims Most Thai Embassies Still Issue Physical Visa Stickers

Thai Visa Centre's *Do Thai Embassies Accept Online Applications?* FAQ, marked reviewed June 2026, publishes incorrect instructions concerning the issuance of Thai electronic visas.[^530]

Although the article correctly identifies Thailand's centralized e-Visa application system, its section describing procedures after approval claims that mailing passports to embassies remains common in the United States and Europe.

It further states that some diplomatic missions require applicants to appear for biometric processing or physical visa stickers, while characterizing digital approvals as rare and claiming that most embassies still place visas directly into passports.

**These assertions fundamentally misrepresent Thailand's stickerless e-Visa procedures, which had already operated at major diplomatic missions for several years before TVC's claimed June 2026 review.**

### Official Embassy Instructions Explicitly Contradict TVC

The Royal Thai Embassy in Washington confirms that its electronic visa system does not require applicants to mail passports or submit original supporting documents in person.[^531]

The embassy explains that successful applicants receive their approved visas electronically and should present the confirmation to airline personnel and Thai immigration authorities.

The Royal Thai Embassy in Budapest, Hungary, provides equivalent instructions.[^532]

Its published guidance, updated January 13, 2026, confirms that applicants have been required to use the online e-Visa system since November 2022. It explicitly states that passports and original supporting documents are no longer submitted by post or in person.

Successful applicants receive confirmation electronically.

Thailand's official e-Visa platform also describes a digital approval process in which confirmation documents are delivered by email.[^533]

**TVC's assertion that most embassies still affix physical visa stickers is incompatible with these official procedures. Its claim that passport mailing remains common at American and European posts is directly contradicted by the diplomatic missions responsible for those applications.**

An embassy may still request additional documents or an interview in particular circumstances. Such exceptions do not make physical passport submission the ordinary method for obtaining an approved e-Visa.

### The Misinformation Creates Unnecessary Document-Handling Risks

Incorrectly instructing visa applicants to mail original passports has consequences beyond administrative inconvenience.

Passports are essential identity and travel documents. Unnecessary international mailing creates avoidable risks of loss, theft, delayed travel, and additional courier expenses.

Applicants following TVC's instructions could also attempt to send passports to diplomatic missions that explicitly no longer accept them for ordinary e-Visa processing.

The error is particularly serious because TVC presents the article as guidance for applicants navigating embassy jurisdiction, document uploads, and application approval.

**A commercial visa agency advertising professional assistance should not direct customers toward obsolete passport-handling procedures that the responsible embassies expressly discontinued years earlier.**

### The Same FAQ Also Denies the Availability of Online Work Permits

TVC's inaccurate description of electronic government services extends beyond visa issuance.

In the same FAQ, its table of procedures that cannot be completed online lists work permits as unavailable electronically, directing readers instead to Thailand's Department of Employment.[^530]

However, the Ministry of Labour launched its nationwide e-WorkPermit system on October 13, 2025, permitting employers and foreign workers to register and submit applications electronically.[^152]

Physical identity verification and permit collection may still be required after approval, but these steps do not negate the availability of electronic applications.

The existing investigation documents TVC's failure to explain e-WorkPermit in several other guides. This FAQ supplies another explicit example of the same outdated claim.

**TVC therefore incorrectly describes two established electronic government procedures within a single article supposedly reviewed in June 2026.**

The evidence does not establish that individual customers actually mailed unnecessary passports or paid avoidable service charges because of these instructions.

It establishes that Thai Visa Centre continues publishing obsolete application procedures despite readily available government guidance contradicting them.

The combination of incorrect passport-mailing instructions and the categorical denial of online work-permit applications raises substantial questions about the accuracy of TVC's claimed editorial reviews and the reliability of its professional immigration advice.

## Permanent-Residency Guides Misstate Application Dates and Filing Procedures

Thai Visa Centre advertises professional assistance with Thailand's permanent-residency applications, including eligibility assessments, document preparation, and immigration coordination.

However, its published guidance contains two significant discrepancies concerning when and where applications must be submitted.[^158][^159]

**The agency repeatedly identifies October–December as the annual application period, despite Thailand's Immigration Bureau accepting applications from March 9 to April 3, 2026.**

Its separate permanent-residence guide also directs applicants toward embassy and e-Visa submission channels that do not handle ordinary permanent-residency applications.

### TVC Advertises an Incorrect Application Window

Thai Visa Centre's *Thailand Permanent Residency* service page identifies October–December as the annual application period.[^158]

Its eligibility requirements state:

> Must apply during annual quota period (October-December)

The page repeats the restriction under its quota requirements and tells unsuccessful applicants they can reapply during the following October–December period.

However, Thailand's Immigration Bureau announced a different timetable for its latest documented intake.

Under the government's February 26, 2026 announcement covering the **2025 permanent-residency quota**, applications were accepted from **March 9 through April 3, 2026**.[^160]

The announcement established quotas of 100 applicants per nationality and 50 stateless applicants.

Permanent-residency application periods are determined by government announcements and can change between quota years.

October–December may describe earlier application arrangements, but it was not the filing period used for the 2025 quota intake conducted in 2026.

**TVC presents a historical filing window as a fixed annual requirement rather than directing applicants to the government's actual announced dates.**

### Its Application Guide Gives Incorrect Filing Channels

The discrepancy extends to TVC's *Permanent Residence Thailand Guide*, marked reviewed June 2026.[^159]

Under its application stages, the guide instructs readers to:

> Submit at embassy, e-Visa portal, or immigration with originals and fees.

Its common-mistakes section separately warns applicants against missing a December deadline.

However, the Immigration Bureau's March 2026 announcement identified specific domestic application locations.[^160]

Bangkok applicants were directed to Sub-Division 1, Immigration Division 1, at the Government Complex on Chaeng Watthana Road.

Applicants elsewhere could submit through the appropriate provincial immigration office or a nearby immigration office.

The ordinary Thai e-Visa portal is used for qualifying visa applications, not permanent-residency applications under the Immigration Bureau's annual quota.

**TVC's own Thailand e-Visa guide acknowledges that permanent residence follows a separate procedure outside the standard e-Visa system.**[^161]

Its permanent-residence guide therefore provides filing instructions that conflict with both the government's published procedure and the company's explanation elsewhere.

### Why These Errors Matter

Permanent residence involves extensive supporting documentation, eligibility requirements, government fees, and a limited annual filing period.

Applicants may spend months preparing financial records, employment evidence, translations, and other supporting documents.

Incorrect guidance about filing dates or submission channels could cause applicants to miss the actual intake or prepare for an unavailable application route.

The June 2026 review date is particularly relevant because the March–April application period had already concluded.

The available evidence does not establish that Thai Visa Centre has missed filing deadlines for customers or submitted permanent-residency applications through an invalid channel.

Its actual case-handling procedures may differ from its public instructions.

**What is established is that the agency advertises professional permanent-residency assistance while publishing application dates and filing instructions inconsistent with the government's documented 2026 procedures.**

Thai Visa Centre should correct its filing-window descriptions, identify the appropriate government application channels, and explain why its June 2026 guidance continued referencing a December deadline after the March–April intake.

### Retirement Visa Marketing Implies an Unsupported Path to Permanent Residency

Thai Visa Centre promotes permanent residency as a benefit of its retirement-visa services, despite Thailand's immigration rules not recognizing retirement alone as a qualifying category for permanent residence.

Its *Thailand Retirement Visa* page advertises a path to permanent residency among the benefits of the Non-Immigrant O-A retirement route.[^189]

Its separate *Thailand 5-Year Retirement Visa* page goes further, describing the Non-Immigrant O-X visa as providing a **clearer path to permanent residency**.[^190]

Neither page explains the distinction between residing in Thailand on a retirement visa and independently qualifying for permanent-resident status.

#### Retirement Is Not an Independent Application Category

Thailand's official permanent-residence guidance identifies five application categories:[^191]

1. Investment.
2. Employment.
3. Humanitarian reasons, including qualifying family relationships.
4. Expertise.
5. Exceptional circumstances.

**Retirement is not listed as a standalone qualification.**

The Royal Thai Embassy in London independently confirms these categories and explains that applicants must satisfy requirements specific to their chosen application route.[^192]

The ordinary residence requirement generally includes at least three consecutive years of qualifying non-immigrant status with annual extensions.

However, satisfying a minimum residence period does not automatically establish eligibility for permanent residence.

Applicants must also meet the financial, employment, investment, family, or other requirements associated with an eligible category.

#### TVC's Own Permanent-Residency Page Confirms the Distinction

Thai Visa Centre's dedicated permanent-residency service page separately identifies investment, business, employment, expertise, and family-based applications.[^158]

It does not advertise retirement as an independent application category.

**TVC therefore promotes permanent residency as a benefit of its retirement products while its own permanent-residency page identifies a different set of qualifying pathways.**

A retiree might independently qualify through substantial investment, an eligible family relationship, or another recognized basis.

The problem is not that retirement-visa holders are categorically prohibited from obtaining permanent residence.

It is that **holding or renewing a retirement visa does not, by itself, establish a pathway to permanent residency**.

#### The O-X Claim Requires Particular Scrutiny

The five-year O-X visa is marketed to retirees from selected countries who meet substantial financial and insurance requirements.

Those conditions differ from the qualifications for permanent residence.

The official residence-permit criteria also refer to qualifying residence supported by one-year non-immigrant extensions, raising additional questions about how a five-year O-X permission would satisfy the ordinary eligibility framework.

TVC does not explain that distinction or identify a specific government provision supporting its claim that O-X holders have a clearer route to permanent residency.

#### Consequences for Applicants

Foreigners considering retirement in Thailand may choose between visa categories partly because they hope eventually to obtain permanent residence.

That decision can involve substantial financial commitments and years of immigration planning.

TVC's marketing could create unrealistic expectations for retirees who meet retirement-visa requirements but have no independent qualification for permanent residency.

The available evidence does not establish that Thai Visa Centre has submitted ineligible permanent-residence applications or deliberately misled individual customers.

However, **its retirement-visa advertising promotes permanent residency without explaining that retirement status alone is insufficient to qualify**.

Thai Visa Centre should clarify the distinction, identify the recognized residence-permit categories, and substantiate its particular claim that the O-X visa provides a clearer route to permanent residency.

### Permanent-Residency Page Invents an Annual Immigration Reporting Obligation

Thai Visa Centre's permanent-residency service page repeatedly claims that foreigners granted permanent residence must submit annual immigration reports to maintain their status.[^193]

However, Thailand's immigration legislation distinguishes permanent residence from temporary-stay permissions, and the agency does not identify a government rule establishing the annual reporting obligation it advertises.

#### TVC Presents Annual Reporting as Mandatory

Under its visa-validity information, Thai Visa Centre states:

> Annual reporting required to maintain status

Its restrictions section separately specifies:

> Must report annually to Immigration

The same page advertises annual reporting assistance among its additional services.[^193]

These statements present annual reporting as a continuing legal condition of permanent residence, rather than an optional service or a requirement associated with another visa category.

#### Immigration Law Establishes Different Obligations

Section 37 of Thailand's Immigration Act B.E. 2522 (1979) governs foreigners admitted under temporary permission to stay.[^194]

It establishes residence-notification requirements, including ordinary 90-day reporting, for qualifying temporary residents.

Permanent residence is governed separately under Chapter 5 of the Act.

Once granted, permanent residence permits indefinite residence subject to applicable legal conditions. Section 48 establishes that a residence certificate remains valid indefinitely unless circumstances such as departure without the necessary endorsement affect its validity.

**The ordinary temporary-stay reporting framework does not automatically apply to foreigners who have obtained permanent-resident status.**

A separate annual reporting obligation exists for certain long-term visa categories.

For example, Thailand's Board of Investment explicitly requires qualifying Long-Term Resident (LTR) visa holders to report their address annually using Form TM95.[^195]

An LTR visa is not the same legal status as permanent residence.

#### Permanent Residents Have Different Registration Duties

Permanent residents are not exempt from every administrative requirement.

Thailand's Alien Registration Act establishes a separate registration system involving an alien identity certificate issued through the appropriate police authorities.

The Thai government's official instructions confirm that these certificates can be issued or renewed for either one year or five years.[^196]

Permanent residents must also comply with applicable changes-of-address requirements and obtain the necessary endorsements before international travel.

Under Sections 48 and 50 of the Immigration Act, residence-preserving departure and re-entry documentation is particularly important for permanent residents leaving Thailand.[^194]

**These obligations should not be confused with a universal annual address report to Immigration.**

#### Relevance to TVC's Paid Services

Thai Visa Centre advertises immigration reporting assistance as part of its broader commercial service network.

The distinction therefore matters financially as well as procedurally.

A customer relying on TVC's permanent-residency page could incorrectly believe that maintaining permanent residence requires purchasing annual reporting assistance or arranging an unnecessary immigration appointment.

The available evidence does not establish that Thai Visa Centre has charged permanent residents for unnecessary reports or submitted such reports on their behalf.

However, **the agency repeatedly presents an annual immigration reporting obligation without identifying the legal provision requiring permanent residents to comply**.

Thai Visa Centre should remove or substantiate this claim, distinguish permanent residence from LTR annual reporting, and accurately explain the separate alien-registration and travel-document requirements applicable to permanent residents.

### Permanent-Residency Travel Guidance Omits Status-Preserving Documents

Thai Visa Centre's permanent-residency service page tells customers that multiple international entries are permitted with a re-entry permit. Its restrictions section repeats that a re-entry permit is required for travel.[^193]

However, the page does not explain the distinct departure and re-entry procedure applicable to permanent residents.

Its separate re-entry guide describes Form TM8 as the means of preserving an existing visa or temporary extension of stay.[^229]

**Thailand's Immigration Act establishes a different procedure for permanent residents, with potentially serious consequences if it is not followed.**

#### Residence Certificates Require a Separate Endorsement

Sections 48 and 50 of the Immigration Act B.E. 2522 (1979) establish that permanent residents intending to travel abroad must have their residence certificates endorsed before departure.[^194]

Section 48 further provides that a residence certificate ceases to be valid when its holder leaves Thailand without the required endorsement.

Even with an endorsement, the resident must return within one year to preserve the certificate's validity.

The Immigration Bureau maintains distinct application forms:

- **TM8:** Re-entry permit for temporary visa or extension holders.
- **TM13:** Endorsement of departure and re-entry for residence-certificate holders.
- **TM22:** Application for a Non-Quota Immigrant Visa.[^230]

These documents serve different legal purposes and should not be treated as interchangeable.

#### Non-Quota Immigrant Visa Requirements Are Also Omitted

The Immigration Bureau's official Non-Quota Immigrant Visa handbook identifies Form TM22 and supporting documents, including the applicant's passport, alien registration book, and certificate of residence.[^231]

The published government fees distinguish:

| Procedure | Government fee |
|---|---:|
| Residence-certificate endorsement | ฿1,900 |
| Non-Quota Immigrant Visa, single entry | ฿1,900 |
| Non-Quota Immigrant Visa, multiple entry | ฿3,800 |
| Ordinary TM8 re-entry permit, single entry | ฿1,000 |

These are separate charges under the Immigration Bureau's published schedule.[^232]

**TVC's general description of re-entry permits does not explain the documentary requirements or potential costs specific to permanent residents.**

#### Consequences of Incorrect Travel Advice

The distinction matters because permanent residence is considerably harder and more expensive to obtain than an ordinary temporary extension.

A permanent resident relying on generic TM8 guidance could misunderstand which documents are necessary before departing Thailand.

Under Section 48, departing without the required residence-certificate endorsement can invalidate permanent-resident status. Restoring residence may require a separate discretionary procedure under Section 51.[^194]

The available evidence does not establish that TVC has issued incorrect travel instructions to individual permanent residents or caused anyone to lose residency.

Nor does its service page explicitly instruct permanent residents to use TM8 instead of TM13.

**The documented problem is that TVC markets permanent-residency and re-entry assistance while omitting the distinct statutory procedure essential to preserving permanent residence during international travel.**

TVC should identify the TM13 endorsement and applicable TM22 requirements, distinguish them from ordinary TM8 permits, and explain the one-year return requirement under the Immigration Act.

### November 2026 Update: TVC Corrects Application Dates but Introduces Inaccurate Fee Guidance

By November 27, 2026, Thai Visa Centre had substantially rewritten its principal permanent-residency service page, incorporating information from its expanding legal archive.[^158][^663]

The revised publication correctly identifies the March 9–April 3, 2026 application period for the 2025 permanent-residency quota and acknowledges that applications must be submitted using Form TM.9 rather than through Thailand's e-Visa portal.

However, the rewrite introduces another discrepancy by replacing previously accurate government fee information with the higher statutory figures reproduced in its legal archive.

| Government charge | Previously advertised fee | Revised advertised fee |
|---|---:|---:|
| Application | 7,600 baht | 8,000 baht |
| Standard residence certificate | 191,400 baht | 200,000 baht |
| Reduced residence certificate | 95,700 baht | 100,000 baht |

Thai Visa Centre's own November 22, 2026 archive of Bangkok Immigration's official fee schedule confirms the lower operational charges.[^672]

**In rewriting its permanent-residency service page, Thai Visa Centre has corrected certain historical inaccuracies while replacing previously accurate fee information with misleading figures.**

Meanwhile, its separate permanent-residency application guide continues directing applicants toward embassies and the e-Visa portal, warning about a December deadline, and including ordinary temporary-stay compliance instructions in its post-approval guidance.[^159]

That publication now incorporates fifteen legal references without resolving these fundamental procedural errors.

The contradictory instructions demonstrate why expanding a legal archive and adding statutory citations cannot substitute for properly reviewing the accuracy of customer-facing immigration advice.

The precise timing and motivation behind these revisions remain unverified. The evidence does not establish that TVC has collected excessive government fees from individual customers.

## Marriage and Retirement Visa Pages Give Incorrect Renewal Instructions

Thai Visa Centre's commercial marriage-visa and retirement-visa pages contain incorrect instructions concerning annual extensions of stay.

Both pages identify **TM47 as a required renewal form**, despite Thailand's Immigration Bureau designating TM7 for extension applications and TM47 for 90-day address reporting.[^162][^163][^165]

The marriage-visa page also describes an incorrect bank-deposit maintenance period.

These errors appear on pages advertising professional immigration assistance, rather than simply in general informational articles.

### Both Service Pages Identify the Wrong Form

Thai Visa Centre's *Thailand Marriage Visa* page tells customers:

> You can renew annually at Thai immigration with updated financial proof, current passport, TM.47 form, photos, and proof of continuing marriage.

Its *Thailand Retirement Visa* page similarly instructs customers to provide a TM47 form when renewing their annual permission to stay.[^163]

However, the Immigration Bureau's official form directory distinguishes the two procedures:

- **TM7:** Application for extension of temporary stay.
- **TM47:** Notification of remaining in Thailand longer than 90 days.

The distinction is explicit in official immigration guidance.[^165]

A recent 90-day reporting receipt may be required as supporting documentation for certain extension applications. Pathum Thani Immigration's marriage-extension checklist, for example, requests a copy of the applicant's latest reporting receipt.[^164]

That does not make TM47 the application form used to obtain an annual extension.

**TVC's service pages confuse immigration address reporting with the separate procedure for renewing permission to stay.**

### Marriage-Visa Deposit Instructions Add Another Error

The marriage-visa page introduces a separate inconsistency concerning financial qualification.

Its frequently asked questions state:

> For renewal, funds must be maintained for 3 months before application.

However, official immigration guidance identifies **400,000 baht maintained for the preceding two months** as the ordinary deposit requirement for qualifying marriage-based extensions.[^164]

The same government checklist also requests three months of bank statements and requires the qualifying deposit to remain available during the application-review period.

These are different requirements.

Providing three months of transaction records does not necessarily mean the full qualifying balance must have been maintained throughout all three months.

TVC's own marriage-visa page elsewhere correctly identifies the two-month deposit period, creating an internal contradiction.

### Relevance to TVC's Professional Services

These discrepancies are significant because TVC advertises annual extensions, financial-document preparation, bank-account assistance, and immigration coordination as paid services.

Incorrect form instructions can cause customers to prepare the wrong application paperwork.

Incorrect deposit-maintenance advice can also affect financial planning, particularly for couples arranging substantial bank transfers before an extension deadline.

The evidence does not establish that TVC submits incorrect forms for customers or that its internal processing follows the published mistakes.

However, **its commercial service pages provide incorrect or contradictory instructions concerning two fundamental aspects of annual visa extensions: the required application form and the financial qualification period.**

The company should correct both renewal checklists, distinguish TM47 reporting receipts from TM7 extension applications, and reconcile its marriage-visa deposit instructions with official immigration requirements.


### Marriage-Visa Financial Checklists Ignore Different Spouse Categories

Thai Visa Centre's commercial marriage-visa page presents a 400,000-baht Thai bank deposit or 40,000-baht monthly income as standard financial requirements for foreigners married to Thai nationals.[^209]

Its separate *Thai Marriage Visa Requirements* FAQ, marked reviewed June 2026, similarly tells foreign spouses that approval depends on financial evidence in the applicant's own name.[^210]

Neither checklist adequately distinguishes the different requirements for foreign husbands, foreign wives, and marriages between persons of diverse genders.

**Thailand's Immigration Bureau expressly differentiates these cases.**

#### Foreign Wives Are Not Subject to the Same Deposit Requirement

Immigration Bureau Order 12/2568, Clause 2.18(6), applies the standard financial test specifically to a foreign husband married to a Thai woman.[^211]

It requires qualifying monthly income, a bank deposit maintained for the prescribed period, or an accepted combination.

The corresponding clause does not impose that same financial threshold on a foreign wife married to a Thai husband.

Foreign wives must still satisfy the applicable immigration, marriage-registration, residence, and documentation requirements. Individual immigration offices may request additional supporting evidence.

However, **TVC's generalized financial checklist presents a requirement applicable to foreign husbands as though it automatically applies to foreign wives.**

#### Different Rules Apply to Gender-Diverse Marriages

Clause 2.18(7) of the same Immigration Bureau order separately addresses marriages between persons of diverse genders.

It permits the prescribed financial requirement to be satisfied by either spouse individually or by both spouses together.[^211]

This distinction matters because TVC's FAQ emphasizes financial evidence held in the foreign applicant's own name.

That instruction does not accurately reflect the alternative arrangements expressly permitted under the applicable category.

#### Financial Consequences for Applicants

Incorrectly applying the same financial test to every foreign spouse can affect decisions about:

- Transferring or maintaining 400,000 baht in a Thai bank account.
- Preparing income certifications and financial documentation.
- Delaying an immigration application while arranging funds that may not be required.
- Purchasing professional assistance based on an inaccurate assessment of eligibility.

The available evidence does not establish that TVC has rejected eligible applicants, demanded unnecessary deposits, or collected fees because of these instructions.

Nevertheless, its published checklists omit distinctions that directly affect the financial obligations of prospective customers.

TVC should explain the separate requirements under Clauses 2.18(6) and 2.18(7), distinguish initial Non-Immigrant O visa applications from annual extensions of stay, and stop presenting the foreign-husband financial test as a universal requirement for every foreign spouse.

### November 2026 Update: TVC Revises Its Marriage Visa Advertising but Retains Misleading Financial Instructions

By November 27, 2026, Thai Visa Centre had substantially rewritten its principal marriage-visa service page.[^162]

The revised publication removes the previously incorrect TM47 renewal instructions and acknowledges that Immigration Bureau Order 12/2568 establishes specific financial requirements for foreign husbands married to Thai women.

Its expanding legal archive also includes a dedicated marriage-stay guide supported by 59 legislative and official references.[^683]

However, the company's separate marriage-visa FAQ continues presenting the 400,000-baht deposit or 40,000-baht monthly income requirement as generally applicable to foreign spouses.[^210]

The FAQ now incorporates fifteen official legal references, including Immigration Bureau Order 12/2568, without adequately explaining the separate financial provisions applicable to foreign wives and gender-diverse marriages.

**Thai Visa Centre has revised its principal commercial publication while leaving materially inconsistent financial instructions elsewhere on its website.**

The discrepancy is financially significant because the company advertises professional document reviews, bank-account assistance, and marriage-based extensions.

Customers relying on its generalized financial checklist could incorrectly conclude that transferring or maintaining 400,000 baht in their own Thai bank account is compulsory for their particular application category.

Notably, the FAQ continues identifying June 2026 as its last review date despite incorporating references to government measures published as recently as November 2026.

The addition of recent legislative references therefore does not establish that the underlying commercial advice has received an equally thorough substantive review.

The precise timing and motivation behind the revisions remain unverified, as does whether individual customers incurred unnecessary expenses because of the earlier instructions.

## Business Visa Page Incorrectly Requires Applicants to Leave Thailand

Thai Visa Centre advertises professional assistance with Non-Immigrant B visas, including business sponsorship, employment documentation, visa extensions, and work-permit coordination.

However, its commercial business-visa page incorrectly states that applicants cannot change from a tourist visa to a Non-Immigrant B visa while remaining in Thailand.[^166]

**This directly contradicts the Immigration Bureau's published procedure permitting qualifying applicants to obtain Non-B status without leaving the country.**

### TVC Claims Overseas Applications Are Mandatory

Thai Visa Centre's *Thailand Business Visa* service page lists the following eligibility requirement:

> Must apply from outside Thailand

Its FAQ reinforces that restriction.

Under *Can I convert from a tourist visa?*, the company answers:

> No, you must apply for the Non-Immigrant B Visa from outside Thailand.

The answer then instructs applicants to leave Thailand and apply through a Thai embassy or consulate.[^166]

The page's application checklist similarly describes overseas embassy submission as the required route.

These instructions present international travel as unavoidable rather than explaining the available domestic application procedure.

### Immigration Bureau Explicitly Permits In-Country Conversion

Thailand's official government guidance, sourced from Immigration Division 1, describes the procedure for changing immigration status to **Non-Immigrant B for employment with a Thai company or partnership**.[^167]

It distinguishes two application routes:

- **TM86:** For holders of tourist or transit visas requesting a change to Non-Immigrant status.
- **TM87:** For eligible foreigners admitted without a visa who subsequently request a Non-Immigrant visa.

The procedure requires supporting employer documentation, proof of company registration, financial and tax records, and a government application fee of **2,000 baht**.

Applicants generally must have more than 15 days remaining on their current permission to stay and cannot apply while overstaying.

Approval is discretionary and initially provides permission for no more than 90 days.

The Ministry of Foreign Affairs independently confirms that tourist and transit visa holders wishing to conduct business in Thailand may apply for a visa-status change at the Immigration Bureau in Bangkok.[^168]

**The government therefore provides an established domestic procedure that TVC's business-visa page incorrectly tells customers does not exist.**

### Consequences for Customers

An applicant who believes TVC's instructions could unnecessarily arrange international travel, accommodation, embassy appointments, and additional visa-processing expenses.

Those costs are especially relevant to foreigners already residing in Thailand who obtain qualifying employment or business sponsorship.

Not every applicant will qualify for an in-country conversion. Immigration authorities retain discretion, and documentary requirements can be substantial.

Some applicants may still need to obtain their visas through Thai diplomatic missions abroad.

However, **those qualifications do not justify TVC's categorical claim that tourist visa holders must leave Thailand to obtain Non-B status**.

The available evidence does not establish that TVC has unnecessarily sent customers abroad or charged them for avoidable embassy applications.

It establishes that the agency's commercial guidance excludes a legitimate government procedure directly relevant to its advertised visa services.

Thai Visa Centre should correct its application instructions, distinguish overseas visa issuance from in-country status changes, and explain the eligibility requirements for both routes.


### Changing Employers Does Not Always Require Leaving Thailand

Thai Visa Centre's business-visa page also provides incorrect blanket instructions for foreigners changing employers.

Its FAQ states that employees must cancel their existing work permit and visa, **exit Thailand**, and apply for a new Non-Immigrant B visa through an overseas embassy or consulate.[^166]

However, a June 2026 guide published by the Australian-Thai Chamber of Commerce explains that qualifying employees may complete an employer transition entirely within Thailand.[^282]

The process can involve cancelling the previous employment-based permission, obtaining a temporary period to complete new work-permit arrangements, and coordinating immigration documentation with the incoming employer.

The Chamber describes a potential **21-day transition period**, subject to conditions including the former employer's registration in Bangkok. Other applicants may still need to leave Thailand and obtain new visas overseas.

TVC's own employer-change FAQ separately describes work-permit cancellation, new employer sponsorship, and immigration updates without explaining the supposedly mandatory overseas departure.[^283]

The distinction has significant financial and legal consequences. Employees following TVC's categorical advice could incur unnecessary international travel, accommodation, and visa expenses.

Conversely, attempting an in-country transition without proper coordination could immediately jeopardize immigration status. Thailand's official guidance confirms that employment-based permission may terminate when the underlying employment ends.[^284]

**Thai Visa Centre presents overseas departure as mandatory when established procedures permit qualifying employees to change employers within Thailand.**

The agency should distinguish situations requiring departure from those eligible for coordinated domestic transfers rather than recommending unnecessary international travel.

### November 2026 Update: TVC Publishes Regulations Contradicting Its Business Visa Advice

On November 19, 2026, Thai Visa Centre's legal archive reportedly transcribed a 15-page Immigration Bureau regulation governing visa issuance and changes of immigration status, dated September 28, 2023.[^684]

The publication describes the domestic visa-conversion procedure, including the use of Form TM.86 and the applicable 2,000-baht government fee.

Thai Visa Centre's expanding immigration forms directory also explicitly identifies Non-Immigrant B as one of the visa categories available through qualifying in-country conversions.[^685]

However, the company's recently indexed commercial business-visa service page categorically instructs tourist visa holders to leave Thailand and apply through an overseas diplomatic mission.[^166]

**Thai Visa Centre is now publishing the government regulations and application forms for a procedure that its own commercial guidance claims is unavailable.**

Thailand's official government portal independently confirms that qualifying applicants may change their immigration status to Non-Immigrant B for employment with a Thai company without leaving the country.[^167]

Approval remains discretionary and subject to documentary requirements, employer qualifications, and the applicant's remaining permission to stay.

The discrepancy is commercially significant because incorrect advice could lead customers to incur unnecessary international travel, accommodation, and overseas visa-processing expenses.

Publishing extensive legislative material does not compensate for providing contradictory instructions to prospective paying customers.

The available evidence does not establish that TVC has deliberately directed customers toward unnecessary overseas applications or profited from such arrangements.

Whether the company has subsequently corrected its commercial business-visa page remains unverified.

## Non-Immigrant Visa Page Incorrectly Requires Conversion to Non-B for Employment

Thai Visa Centre's commercial *Thailand 90-Day Non-Immigrant Visa* page incorrectly tells applicants that obtaining a Non-Immigrant B visa is mandatory before working in Thailand.[^212]

Under its frequently asked questions, the agency states:

> No, working is strictly prohibited. You must first convert to a Non-Immigrant B visa and obtain a work permit.

The page covers several Non-Immigrant categories, including family and marriage visas, without qualifying that instruction.

**Thailand's Ministry of Labour expressly recognizes work-permit applications by qualifying Non-Immigrant O holders married to Thai nationals.**

### Ministry of Labour Recognizes Non-O Marriage Visa Holders

The Ministry of Labour's official employment guidance identifies two relevant categories when explaining applications for skilled foreign workers:

- Non-Immigrant B visa holders.
- Non-Immigrant O visa holders married to Thai nationals.[^213]

It explains that eligible foreigners already in Thailand can apply for a work permit using the prescribed application procedure.

This does not mean every Non-O visa authorizes employment. Retirement and ordinary dependent categories have different restrictions, and the applicant must satisfy the applicable work-permit requirements.

However, **conversion to Non-B is not universally mandatory for foreigners holding Non-O status through marriage to a Thai citizen**.

### BOI Documentation Confirms the Distinction

The Board of Investment's Single Window guidance also expressly recognizes Non-O (Thai Spouse) alongside Non-B, Non-IB, and permanent-resident status for eligible applications under BOI employment privileges.[^214]

Its instructions impose additional immigration and eligibility conditions for applicants relying on Thai-spouse status.

That distinction reinforces the central point: the government does not require every qualifying foreign spouse to abandon Non-O status and obtain Non-B before pursuing employment authorization.

### Thai Visa Centre Contradicts Its Own Marriage-Visa Page

TVC's separate commercial marriage-visa page correctly describes the Non-Immigrant O marriage visa as providing work-permit eligibility.[^215]

Its FAQ confirms that a marriage-visa holder can work after obtaining the necessary work permit.

The two commercial pages therefore provide conflicting instructions:

- **90-day Non-Immigrant visa page:** Requires conversion to Non-B before employment.
- **Marriage-visa page:** Recognizes work-permit eligibility under the existing Non-O marriage category.

Both pages advertise professional visa assistance, including immigration-status changes and work-permit services.

### Consequences for Foreign Spouses

The incorrect instruction could lead eligible foreign spouses to believe they must change immigration categories before applying for employment authorization.

Unnecessary conversion could involve additional applications, government fees, document preparation, administrative delays, or complications with an existing marriage-based extension.

The available evidence does not establish that TVC has unnecessarily converted customers' visas or charged fees for conversions that were not required.

Nevertheless, its published guidance incorrectly presents a category-specific employment procedure as a universal legal requirement.

TVC should distinguish employment authorization from visa classification, explain when Non-O marriage holders can obtain work permits, and reconcile its 90-day visa FAQ with its own marriage-visa service page.

## SMART Visa Service Advertises Categories Discontinued in 2025

Thai Visa Centre continues advertising Thailand's SMART Visa program as an immigration option for highly skilled professionals, investors, senior executives, and startup entrepreneurs.

However, **three of the four principal visa categories advertised on its commercial service page were discontinued for new applicants in February 2025**.[^169][^170]

The discrepancy is significant because TVC presents the outdated categories as currently available services and encourages prospective customers to begin applications.

### BOI Restricted New SMART Visas to Startup Entrepreneurs

On February 18, 2025, Thailand's Board of Investment issued Announcement No. Por 5/2568, following a Cabinet resolution dated January 13, 2025.[^170]

The government explicitly decided to retain SMART Visa eligibility for startup entrepreneurs while eliminating overlap with Thailand's Long-Term Resident (LTR) visa program.

The announcement repealed the previous SMART Visa qualification framework established in December 2018.

For new applications, it identifies only:

- **SMART S:** Qualifying entrepreneurs operating certified startups in Thailand.
- **SMART O:** Eligible spouses and children of those entrepreneurs.

The former SMART Talent (T), Investor (I), and Executive (E) categories are no longer available to new applicants under the revised framework.

The announcement specifies that the replacement rules apply to applications submitted after the change.

It does not establish that existing SMART Visa holders automatically lost their previously granted immigration status.

### TVC Continues Advertising Discontinued Categories

Thai Visa Centre's *Thailand SMART Visa* service page nevertheless presents the program as a four-year immigration solution for professionals, investors, executives, and startup founders.[^169]

Its eligibility and application sections separately advertise:

- **SMART Talent (T):** Highly skilled professionals earning at least 100,000 baht monthly, with certain exceptions.
- **SMART Investor (I):** Investors committing 20 million baht to technology companies or 5 million baht to qualifying startups.
- **SMART Executive (E):** Senior executives earning at least 200,000 baht monthly.
- **SMART Startup (S):** Startup founders meeting investment, ownership, and financial requirements.

The page also advertises four-year stays, qualification endorsements, visa conversions, and government liaison services.

These descriptions reflect the former SMART Visa framework rather than the program established by the BOI's February 2025 announcement.

**TVC is advertising government visa categories that the responsible government agency no longer accepts for new applications.**

### The Four-Year Visa Claim Is Also Outdated

The distinction affects more than the names of the eligible categories.

TVC prominently describes SMART Visas as allowing stays of up to four years.

That duration was associated with certain former SMART Talent, Investor, and Executive categories.

The replacement announcement instead provides for qualifying startup entrepreneurs to receive permission for a period not exceeding two years, subject to the applicable requirements.[^170]

Consequently, **the four-year duration used to promote TVC's current SMART Visa services no longer describes the program available to new applicants under the revised rules**.

### Consequences for Prospective Customers

A professional or investor relying on TVC's page could spend time preparing financial evidence, employment contracts, investment documents, or qualification endorsements for a discontinued visa category.

The outdated information could also lead applicants to compare immigration options using benefits and stay durations that are no longer available.

The BOI's decision specifically sought to reduce overlap between SMART Visas and the LTR program, making accurate distinctions between those pathways particularly important.

The available evidence does not establish that Thai Visa Centre has submitted applications under abolished categories, collected fees for unavailable visas, or caused a particular customer financial loss.

However, **the company's commercial website continues promoting discontinued immigration categories more than a year after the government formally restricted the program**.

Thai Visa Centre should update its advertised categories, remove obsolete four-year eligibility claims, and explain which immigration pathways remain available to professionals and investors affected by the 2025 changes.


### TVC's Own Legal Archive Acknowledges the Revised Eligibility Rules

As of November 27, 2026, Thai Visa Centre's dedicated SMART Visa legal archive identifies BOI Announcement No. Por. 5/2568 as the applicable qualification framework.[^676]

Its reference table explicitly describes the announcement as establishing a startup-only SMART Visa pathway.

However, the same publication's plain-English explanation continues identifying highly skilled professionals, investors, executives, and startup entrepreneurs as eligible categories without clearly distinguishing historical eligibility from current application requirements.

### Obsolete Application Materials Remain in the Archive

The legal guide also includes historical qualification criteria and application checklists for discontinued categories, including:

- SMART Talent (T).
- SMART Investor (I).
- SMART Executive (E).

Preserving historical documents is not inherently misleading. However, presenting them alongside current application materials without consistently identifying their superseded status creates ambiguity for prospective applicants.

The BOI announcement expressly repealed the previous qualification framework and restricted new applications to qualifying startup entrepreneurs and their eligible dependents.[^170]

### Contradiction Between Legal Knowledge and Commercial Advertising

**Thai Visa Centre's own legal archive acknowledges the startup-only framework while its commercial website continues advertising the former eligibility categories.**

This establishes an inconsistency between the legislation the company publishes, its explanatory guidance, and its commercial immigration services.

The evidence does not establish deliberate deception or that customers have paid for unavailable immigration services.

Nevertheless, the company cannot resolve the discrepancy merely by publishing additional legislative references. Its customer-facing eligibility explanations must accurately reflect those references.


## Tourist Visa Page Understates Financial Requirements and Misstates Insurance Rules

Thai Visa Centre's commercial tourist-visa page provides incorrect guidance concerning financial evidence and travel insurance.

The page identifies minimum funds of **10,000 baht per individual or 20,000 baht per family**, while Thailand's Ministry of Foreign Affairs specifies **20,000 baht per individual or 40,000 baht per family** in its general tourist-visa requirements.[^171][^172]

The same service page also presents valid travel insurance as mandatory, despite the absence of a universal insurance requirement for standard tourist visas.

### Published Financial Requirements Are Half the Official Amounts

Thai Visa Centre's *Thailand Tourist Visa* page identifies the following requirements for single-entry tourist visas:

- Bank statements showing at least 10,000 baht per person.
- Bank statements showing at least 20,000 baht per family.

The amounts appear in both its visa-category checklist and general financial requirements.[^171]

However, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs identifies adequate financial evidence for tourist-visa applications as **20,000 baht per person and 40,000 baht per family**.[^172]

The Royal Thai Embassy in Budapest independently confirms these amounts in its February 2026 tourist-visa guidance.[^173]

The discrepancy is significant because TVC's figures match the lower amounts published for Thailand's separate Visa on Arrival scheme, rather than the standard tourist visa.[^174]

**TVC appears to have applied Visa on Arrival financial thresholds to a different immigration category.**

Although individual embassies may impose additional documentary or financial requirements, this does not support advertising a lower general threshold than the Ministry of Foreign Affairs specifies.

An applicant relying on TVC's figures could prepare inadequate financial evidence and encounter requests for additional documentation or an unsuccessful application.

### Insurance Presented as a Mandatory Tourist-Visa Condition

The same page lists the following restriction:

> Must maintain valid travel insurance

However, standard Thai tourist visas do not carry a general government requirement to maintain travel insurance throughout the stay.

The Ministry of Foreign Affairs' published tourist-visa documentation requirements do not identify insurance as a universal prerequisite.[^172]

More significantly, Thai Visa Centre's own *Do I Need Health Insurance for Thailand?* FAQ acknowledges that standard tourist visas do not generally require mandatory health insurance.[^175]

That article recommends obtaining insurance for medical expenses, evacuation, and other travel risks.

**Recommending insurance is materially different from presenting it as a compulsory immigration condition.**

Specific diplomatic missions may request additional documentation in individual circumstances. The available evidence does not establish that every tourist-visa application is exempt from every possible insurance-related request.

Nevertheless, TVC's categorical requirement is inconsistent with both the general government guidance and its own published explanation.

### Tourist Visa Guide Falsely Promises a Guaranteed 60-Day Stay

Thai Visa Centre's *Single-Entry Tourist Visa Thailand* guide repeatedly describes the TR visa as an option for travelers seeking a **"guaranteed 60-day stay with extension options."**[^534]

The claim appears in the article's introduction, eligibility explanation, application stages, and multiple additional-guidance sections.

**This guarantee directly contradicts Thailand's official visa and immigration procedures.**

The Royal Thai Consulate-General in Mumbai explicitly states that possession of a visa does not guarantee entry into Thailand. Immigration officers determine whether travelers are admitted and the period of stay authorized upon arrival.[^535]

For tourist visas, the ordinary authorized period is **up to 60 days**, not an unconditional entitlement to remain for the full period.

TVC's own guide acknowledges this distinction elsewhere. Its requirements table describes the permitted stay as "Up to 60 days," while another paragraph acknowledges that final decisions rest with Thai immigration and consular authorities.[^534]

**The company therefore repeatedly advertises a guaranteed outcome while publishing contradictory qualifications on the same page.**

This distinction matters to travelers making financial commitments based on their expected immigration status. An approved tourist visa does not eliminate the possibility of refused entry or guarantee the duration of the permission granted at the border.

The evidence does not establish that TVC has caused a traveler to be refused entry or that any customer relied on the inaccurate guarantee.

It establishes that **a commercial immigration agency repeatedly promises a guaranteed stay that Thai authorities expressly do not guarantee**.

TVC should remove the unconditional guarantee and accurately distinguish visa issuance, permission to enter Thailand, and the maximum period of stay ordinarily available to tourist-visa holders.

### Implications for Applicants

These errors concern three basic questions applicants must resolve before paying for professional visa assistance:

1. How much financial evidence must they prepare?
2. Is purchasing travel insurance compulsory or optional?
3. Does an approved tourist visa guarantee entry and a full 60-day stay?

TVC's financial guidance could leave customers underprepared, while its insurance statement could lead customers to believe they must purchase coverage to satisfy an immigration requirement that does not generally exist. Its separate guaranteed-stay claim could also lead travelers to mistake a visa's ordinary maximum stay period for an unconditional immigration entitlement.

The available evidence does not establish that TVC has sold unnecessary insurance, caused visa refusals, submitted applications with insufficient financial evidence, or caused travelers to rely on its guaranteed-stay claim.

It establishes that **TVC publishes financial thresholds inconsistent with Ministry of Foreign Affairs requirements, an insurance obligation contradicted by its own guidance, and a guaranteed-stay claim that conflicts with official entry procedures**.

Thai Visa Centre should correct its tourist-visa financial checklist, distinguish tourist visas from Visa on Arrival requirements, explain insurance as a recommendation except where a specific applicable rule requires it, and remove the unconditional 60-day stay guarantee.


## One-Year Visa Page Misstates Reporting and Eligibility Requirements

Thai Visa Centre's commercial *Thailand One-Year Non-Immigrant Visa* page incorrectly instructs frequent international travelers to continue filing 90-day immigration reports even when they regularly leave and re-enter Thailand.[^176]

This contradicts both the Immigration Bureau's official reporting rules and the agency's own published guidance.

This is another instance of TVC misrepresenting when TM47 reporting applies. The consolidated findings above document its separate LTR, Thailand Privilege, and paid-reporting claims.

**The discrepancy is particularly relevant because Thai Visa Centre also sells paid 90-day reporting assistance through an affiliated service.**

### TVC Claims Reporting Remains Mandatory

Thai Visa Centre advertises its one-year multiple-entry Non-Immigrant Visa as suitable for foreigners making frequent visits to Thailand.

The service page explains that the visa generally allows stays of up to 90 days per entry.

Under its FAQ, however, the company answers the question *What about 90-day reporting?* by stating:

> Yes, you must still report to immigration every 90 days, even if you regularly leave and re-enter Thailand.

Its restrictions section also lists 90-day reporting as mandatory, without explaining that the obligation depends on the length of continuous residence.[^176]

**This incorrectly suggests that the reporting schedule continues regardless of international departures and subsequent re-entry.**

### Immigration Bureau Rules Reset the Reporting Clock

Thailand's official government guidance, sourced from Immigration Division 1, explains that foreigners staying in Thailand for more than 90 days must notify immigration of their residence.[^177]

Crucially, it states:

> If a foreigner leaves the Kingdom and re-enters, the day count starts at 0 in every case.

The Royal Thai Consulate-General in Los Angeles independently publishes the same rule.[^178]

Consequently, a foreigner who leaves Thailand before reaching the reporting deadline does not need to submit a report for that interrupted period.

The 90-day count begins again following the next entry.

A traveler making repeated visits shorter than 90 consecutive days may therefore never become obligated to file a TM47 report during those visits.

This does not eliminate reporting requirements for foreigners who remain in Thailand continuously for the relevant period, or excuse violations that occurred before departure.

### TVC's Own FAQ Contradicts Its Service Page

The inconsistency is especially clear because Thai Visa Centre publishes a separate FAQ titled *Do I Need to Do 90-Day Reporting While Outside of Thailand?*[^179]

That article correctly explains that:

- No report is required for a reporting period interrupted by departure before its deadline.
- Each re-entry restarts the 90-day count.
- Reporting resumes only after the applicable continuous-stay period.

**The agency therefore gives customers opposite answers about the same immigration obligation, depending on which page they consult.**

Both publications promote TVC's immigration assistance.

### Relevance to Paid Reporting Services

Thai Visa Centre advertises paid 90-day reporting assistance, including through its affiliated `90day.in.th` service.

Customers following the one-year visa page could incorrectly believe that they must purchase reporting assistance despite regularly departing Thailand before reaching the reporting threshold.

There is no evidence that TVC has charged a customer for an unnecessary report or deliberately misrepresented the rule to generate business.

However, **its commercial service page incorrectly presents a conditional immigration obligation as mandatory for travelers whose repeated departures may eliminate the need to report altogether**.

The agency should correct the FAQ, distinguish continuous residence from repeated short visits, and reconcile its commercial visa guidance with its own accurate explanation of the reporting clock.


### One-Year Visa Page Falsely Presents Retirement-Visa Conditions as Universal Requirements

Thai Visa Centre's commercial *Thailand One-Year Non-Immigrant Visa* page advertises assistance for business, education, retirement, and family applicants while imposing a single set of eligibility conditions across these distinct immigration categories.[^176]

Its general eligibility checklist requires applicants to have:

- A passport valid for at least 18 months.
- No criminal record.
- Valid travel insurance.
- An application submitted from outside Thailand.

The page's documentation section repeats the 18-month passport requirement and states that insurance must cover the entire stay.

**These are not universal requirements for the Non-Immigrant visa categories TVC advertises.**

Thailand's Ministry of Foreign Affairs specifies a minimum passport validity of six months for ordinary Non-B business and employment applications.[^543]

Official Non-O family-visa guidance likewise specifies six months and does not impose a general mandatory health-insurance condition.[^544]

By contrast, the separate Non-O-A long-stay retirement category expressly requires an 18-month-valid passport, qualifying medical insurance, and a criminal-record clearance certificate.[^545]

**TVC therefore appears to have generalized requirements associated with the O-A retirement category across unrelated immigration programs.**

The company's instruction that all applicants must apply from outside Thailand introduces another discrepancy. As documented separately in this investigation, Thailand permits qualifying applicants to request certain Non-Immigrant visa conversions through the Immigration Bureau without leaving the country.[^167]

Individual embassies may impose additional documentation, police-clearance, or insurance requirements depending on the visa category, nationality, and application circumstances. These exceptions do not establish a blanket requirement applicable to every category advertised on TVC's page.

The consequences are practical. Applicants could incorrectly believe their passports are ineligible, purchase insurance that is not generally required, or pursue unnecessary overseas applications.

**The company markets specialized immigration expertise while presenting materially different visa categories under a misleading common eligibility checklist.**

TVC should distinguish category-specific requirements rather than treating conditions associated with one visa program as mandatory for all applicants.

## Five-Year Retirement Visa Page Advertises Work Rights Contrary to Official Restrictions

Thai Visa Centre advertises Thailand's Non-Immigrant O-X retirement visa as a long-term immigration option for qualifying foreigners aged 50 and older.

However, its commercial service page promotes remote employment and volunteer activities as benefits of the visa, despite official Thai diplomatic guidance expressly prohibiting employment under the O-X category.[^180][^181]

**The agency markets work-related benefits while simultaneously acknowledging that O-X holders are prohibited from employment.**

### TVC Promises Remote Work and Volunteer Opportunities

Thai Visa Centre's *Thailand 5-Year Retirement Visa* page lists the following benefits:

- Remote work permitted.
- Volunteer work options.

Its frequently asked questions reinforce those claims.

When asked whether O-X holders can work, TVC initially acknowledges that employment is prohibited.

However, it immediately states:

> However, you can work remotely for foreign companies and volunteer for approved activities.

The same page identifies the visa as a retirement category and lists employment in Thailand as a restriction.[^180]

**These statements create the impression that working remotely for overseas employers is an established exception to the visa's employment restrictions.**

### Thai Diplomatic Missions State That Employment Is Prohibited

The Royal Thai Consulate-General in Los Angeles publishes official O-X guidance, updated October 1, 2026.[^181]

It identifies the visa as intended for qualifying foreigners aged 50 and older who wish to reside in Thailand without undertaking employment.

The guidance states:

> Employment of any kind is strictly prohibited.

The Royal Thai Embassy in Kuala Lumpur independently repeats that restriction in its O-X instructions, updated July 17, 2026.[^182]

Neither publication identifies a general exemption allowing O-X holders to undertake remote employment for foreign companies.

The distinction matters because an employer's location outside Thailand does not, by itself, establish that work performed while physically present in Thailand is authorized under a particular immigration category.

**TVC presents remote employment as an affirmative visa benefit without identifying any government authorization supporting that claim.**

### Volunteering Requires Separate Consideration

The agency's reference to volunteer opportunities introduces a related concern.

Unpaid activity is not automatically exempt from Thailand's rules governing work by foreign nationals.

Whether a particular volunteer arrangement requires permission depends on the activity and applicable immigration and employment regulations.

TVC does not identify which volunteer activities it considers permissible, what authorization may be required, or how those activities would remain consistent with the O-X visa's restrictions.

Its broad marketing language therefore risks implying that volunteering is an ordinary benefit of retirement status rather than an activity requiring separate legal assessment.

### Consequences for Retirement Applicants

Customers considering the O-X visa must satisfy substantial financial and insurance requirements.

The visa generally requires a qualifying Thai bank deposit of at least 3 million baht, or a qualifying combination of deposits and annual income.

An applicant who intends to continue remote employment may view TVC's advertised work rights as an important reason to select this expensive long-stay arrangement.

However, **the government's published O-X conditions do not support presenting remote employment as an unrestricted benefit of the visa**.

The available evidence does not establish that TVC has arranged unauthorized employment, advised a particular customer to violate work restrictions, or caused an immigration violation.

It establishes that the agency's commercial service page promotes work-related activities while failing to reconcile those claims with the explicit employment prohibition published by Thai diplomatic missions.

Thai Visa Centre should remove the unqualified remote-work claim, clarify the separate rules governing volunteer activities, and distinguish retirement status from visa categories specifically intended for qualifying employment or remote work.


## Full-Refund Guarantee Conflicts With Published Refund Exclusions

Thai Visa Centre prominently advertises a refundable-deposit model intended to reassure customers paying in advance for immigration services.

Its homepage promises that customers receive a **full refund of their deposit if the agency does not complete the agreed service**.[^183]

However, the company's separately published Refund Policy allows certain payments to be retained, including government application fees following submission and transaction charges of 2–7% in specified circumstances.[^184]

**The public documents do not clearly explain how these deductions operate alongside the company's advertised full-refund guarantee.**

### September 2021 Immediate Refund and Receipt Guarantees

On September 24, 2021, Thai Visa Centre responded to customer criticism concerning its immigration-service pricing and anticipated changes affecting visa agencies.[^668]

The agency expressly stated:

> We also guarantee full immediate refund if we cannot obtain the extension.

Thai Visa Centre additionally promised not to increase agreed prices after accepting payment, provided customers had disclosed existing immigration complications.

It also stated that it aimed to provide official immigration receipts within 72 hours and advised prospective customers to obtain government receipts when dealing with competing agencies.

The discussion followed a complaint from forum participant problemfarang, who reported receiving a 30,000-baht quotation and criticized the agency's pricing.

Another participant subsequently reported a positive experience with Thai Visa Centre, including receiving receipts despite an immigration processing delay.

### Comparison With the Subsequent Published Refund Policy

Unlike the company's February 2025 Refund Policy, which primarily describes applications submitted to embassies and consulates, the September 2021 guarantee expressly addressed unsuccessful immigration extensions.

**Thai Visa Centre therefore previously provided a more explicit public refund assurance concerning domestic immigration extensions than appears in its subsequently published general refund policy.**

The historical statement also promised immediate repayment, rather than merely establishing eligibility for a refund.

The available evidence does not establish when these specific assurances changed, whether they remain applicable under individual customer agreements, or whether customers have experienced difficulties obtaining refunds.

Nevertheless, the distinction demonstrates the importance of clearly identifying the refund conditions applicable to domestic immigration services.

### Homepage Promises Full Refunds for Uncompleted Services

Under *Transparent payments, refundable deposits, and nationwide service*, Thai Visa Centre states that all visa-service payments are accepted as refundable deposits under written agreements.

It explains that successful completion allows the company to retain the deposit as agreed.

For unsuccessful services, the homepage promises:

> If we do not complete the service as agreed, you receive a full refund of your deposit.

The company also states that refund terms may vary slightly by service but that every customer receives a written agreement before work begins.[^183]

This language presents the refundable-deposit arrangement as a general customer protection rather than an exception available to selected visa categories.

### Published Policy Excludes Certain Payments

Thai Visa Centre's Refund Policy, updated February 9, 2025, establishes more restrictive conditions.[^184]

For applications denied after submission, it distinguishes between two categories of charges:

- **Government application fees:** Nonrefundable once submitted to the relevant embassy or consulate.
