A Thai co-administrator may be useful where an estate includes substantial local assets, complicated administrative procedures, or beneficiaries living in multiple countries.

However, practical convenience does not make joint appointment legally mandatory.

TVC's warnings do not adequately explain that distinction.

#### Supreme Court Decision 626/2541 Recognizes Foreign Administrators

Thailand's Supreme Court has directly addressed whether foreign nationality prevents a person from administering an estate.

In Decision 626/2541, a foreign woman sought appointment as administrator of her deceased husband's estate.

The deceased's child opposed the petition.

The Supreme Court held that foreign nationality did not disqualify the petitioner under Section 1718 of the Civil and Commercial Code.[^394]

The court considered her suitability for administration and appointed her jointly with the deceased's child.

The decision establishes two important principles.

First, **a foreign national can lawfully qualify to administer a Thai estate**.

Second, the court retains discretion to determine whether a particular person is suitable and whether a joint appointment is appropriate.

The fact that the court appointed joint administrators in that particular case does not create a universal requirement for foreign administrators to have Thai co-executors.

Subsequent judicial guidance confirms that courts assess suitability, good faith, competing interests, and the circumstances of the estate.

Nationality alone does not determine the outcome.

**TVC's advice presents Thai co-administration as a routine necessity without identifying the statutory rules or the Supreme Court decision recognizing foreign administrators.**

#### Appointing Joint Administrators Can Create Additional Complications

Naming a local co-administrator may be sensible in some circumstances.

A person based in Thailand may be better positioned to deal with banks, the Land Department, courts, translation requirements, and local beneficiaries.

Nevertheless, appointing multiple administrators introduces its own legal considerations.

Section 1726 of the Civil and Commercial Code provides rules governing how joint administrators exercise their authority.[^393]

Unless the will provides otherwise, decisions among multiple administrators generally require majority agreement.

Disagreements between two administrators may consequently require intervention by the court.

A testator should therefore consider:

- Whether a single administrator can perform the necessary duties.
- Whether the proposed administrator is eligible and capable of acting.
- Whether local assistance can be arranged without appointing another administrator.
- Whether joint appointment would create divided authority or additional administrative costs.
- Whether the will should expressly establish decision-making arrangements.

The recommendation to appoint a Thai co-administrator should be presented as a possible estate-planning choice, not an unavoidable condition imposed on foreigners.

#### The Land Department Allows Inheritance Transfers Without an Estate Administrator

TVC's probate publications also leave out an important alternative to court-supervised administration.

Section 81 of Thailand's Land Code establishes a procedure through which qualifying heirs may apply to register inherited rights directly with the competent Land Office.[^395]

Under that procedure, the applicant submits evidence establishing entitlement to the inherited property.

The official reviews the documentation and ordinarily publishes an inheritance notice.

The statutory notice period is generally 30 days.

If no qualifying objection is raised and the officer is satisfied that the applicant is entitled to the inheritance, registration may proceed according to the applicable requirements.

Where objections arise, the officer may investigate the competing claims.

Unresolved disputes can require court proceedings.

**The existence of this procedure means that obtaining a court order appointing an administrator is not universally required before an inheritance can be registered.**

The Department of Lands explicitly publishes a public service procedure titled *Registration of Inheritance Transfer — Without an Estate Administrator*.[^396]

Its instructions address inherited land, buildings, and condominium ownership documents.

The procedure includes:

1. Submission of the deceased person's property documents.
2. Evidence of death and the applicant's legal entitlement.
3. Examination of the applicant's inheritance rights.
4. Publication of the required notice.
5. Consideration of any objections.
6. Registration of the inheritance if the applicable conditions are satisfied.

The Department of Lands has also published explanatory guidance distinguishing inheritance transfers with an administrator from transfers without one.[^397]

In its October 2026 guidance, a provincial Land Office describes the procedure without an administrator as involving an heir's application, examination of inheritance rights, a **30-day public notice**, confirmation by the heir, and registration. It gives an **approximate total of 47 days, including the notice period**, for that administrative route.[^397] This is an illustrative processing estimate rather than a guarantee for every estate; disputes, incomplete evidence, or complex inheritance rights can require additional steps or court proceedings.

The guidance treats direct registration without an administrator as an established process involving additional verification and notification requirements.

**TVC's court-focused presentation overlooks this officially recognized alternative.**

#### Court-Appointed Administrators Remain Necessary in Many Cases

The existence of direct inheritance registration does not mean that heirs can always avoid court proceedings.

Court appointment can be required or practically necessary where:

- The heirs disagree about entitlement or distribution.
- An existing administrator cannot perform the required duties.
- A will contains disputed or ineffective provisions.
- Banks or other institutions require judicial confirmation of authority.
- Assets must be sold or administered before distribution.
- The Land Office cannot resolve competing claims through its administrative procedure.
- Complex cross-border succession issues prevent straightforward registration.

Section 1713 of the Civil and Commercial Code identifies the circumstances in which an heir, interested person, or public prosecutor may petition for the appointment of an administrator.[^393]

Foreign families may also encounter additional documentation and authentication requirements.

A foreign will, overseas death certificate, or foreign-issued proof of relationship may need translation and legalization before the relevant Thai institution accepts it.

Accordingly, TVC is not necessarily wrong when it states that many inheritance matters involve court orders.

**The problem is its failure to explain that court appointment is not invariably required and that the correct procedure depends on the assets, beneficiaries, and circumstances of the estate.**

#### Administrative Authority Is Different From Beneficial Ownership

TVC's discussion also risks confusing the nationality of the estate administrator with the nationality of the heir.

An administrator manages and distributes assets on behalf of the estate.

An heir acquires rights under the applicable succession provisions.

These are distinct legal roles.

A foreigner may lawfully serve as estate administrator without necessarily being permitted to acquire every asset personally.

For example, the Land Code imposes separate restrictions on foreign ownership of land.

The Condominium Act contains different rules governing foreign ownership and inherited condominium units.

A foreign administrator's eligibility to perform administrative duties therefore does not establish personal entitlement to inherited land.

Conversely, restrictions on a foreigner's right to own particular property do not automatically prevent that person from administering the estate.

**The correct legal analysis separates the administrator's eligibility, the beneficiaries' inheritance rights, and the registration requirements for each asset.**

#### Financial Consequences for Foreign Families

These distinctions matter for foreign retirees, spouses, and families holding Thai property, bank deposits, company shares, and other investments.

A family relying on TVC's guidance might incorrectly believe that it must:

- Appoint an additional Thai co-executor.
- Replace a foreign executor already designated in a valid will.
- Commence court proceedings before investigating direct Land Office registration.
- Retain additional professional representatives for an otherwise straightforward inheritance.
- Delay distribution while seeking a court order that the relevant government agency may not require.

In complex estates, these steps may be appropriate.

In simpler circumstances, they may create unnecessary expense, delays, and divided administrative authority.

The available evidence does not establish that Thai Visa Centre has charged customers for unnecessary probate proceedings, improperly arranged estate administrators, or caused financial losses through these publications.

Nevertheless, **its published instructions can create an exaggerated impression of the legal barriers facing foreigners administering Thai estates.**

#### Recommended Corrections

Thai Visa Centre should revise its probate and succession publications to explain:

- Sections 1711–1718 of the Civil and Commercial Code and the statutory eligibility rules for administrators.
- Supreme Court Decision 626/2541 confirming that foreign nationality is not an automatic disqualification.
- The distinction between optional Thai co-administration and a legally mandatory appointment.
- The circumstances in which court appointment is appropriate under Section 1713.
- Direct inheritance registration under Section 81 of the Land Code.
- The Department of Lands' procedure for transfers without a court-appointed administrator.
- The separate rules governing foreign administrators, foreign heirs, and foreign ownership of immovable property.

**The agency should avoid portraying Thai co-executors and court-appointed probate administration as universal requirements when Thailand's legislation and published Land Department procedures establish more flexible alternatives.**

### Commercial Significance Across Estate Planning and Probate

TVC promotes estate-planning coordination, legal-service referrals, and assistance with inherited Thai assets while publishing blanket statements about compulsory inheritance shares, lawyers, Thai co-administrators, and court proceedings. The underlying legal questions differ: a testator's freedom to dispose of an estate, the formal requirements for making a will, eligibility to administer an estate, and the transfer process for particular property must each be assessed on their own terms.

**The cumulative problem is that the agency presents optional professional assistance and procedures needed in some cases as generally unavoidable legal requirements.** That can discourage lawful testamentary choices or encourage unnecessary paid services, additional administrators, and court applications. The published material does not establish that TVC has charged particular customers for unnecessary services, administered estates improperly, or caused financial losses.

TVC should correct the cited guides together, identify when lawyers and court procedures are genuinely needed, and disclose the government alternatives rather than leaving mutually reinforcing overstatements scattered across its publications.

## Misleading Marriage, Prenuptial, and Divorce Requirements

Thai Visa Centre publishes misleading and sometimes contradictory instructions concerning marriage registration, remarriage, prenuptial agreements, and divorce in Thailand. Across its family-law guides, the agency presents optional legal representation as mandatory, gives outdated or overly restrictive registration instructions, misstates statutory marriage conditions, and overlooks administrative procedures available to qualifying couples seeking an uncontested divorce.

These are distinct legal requirements, but the recurring problem is that **TVC presents administrative procedures as more restrictive, expensive, or dependent on professional intervention than Thai law generally requires**. The inaccuracies are commercially relevant because the agency advertises marriage-related immigration assistance, certified translations, document preparation, and referrals to family-law professionals.

### Family-Law Guide Falsely Claims Marriage Registration Requires Licensed Thai Lawyers

Thai Visa Centre's *Contact a Thai Family Lawyer* guide, marked reviewed June 2026, incorrectly tells prospective spouses that marriage registration in Thailand requires licensed Thai family lawyers.[^475]

The guide states:

> Registration, prenuptials, custody, and divorce require licensed Thai family law counsel.

It repeats the assertion in its FAQ while advertising consultations and referrals to family-law professionals.

**The blanket claim misrepresents Thailand's ordinary marriage-registration procedures, which do not require couples to retain private lawyers.**

#### Government Permits Direct Marriage Registration

Thailand's Department of Provincial Administration publishes official instructions for registering marriages at district and municipal district offices. Eligible couples may apply directly to the registrar.[^476]

Its procedures identify required evidence and witnesses, rather than a lawyer's signature or representation as a general condition.

For a marriage involving a foreign national, the government's published guidance addresses:

- Valid identification, including passports for foreign applicants.
- Evidence of the foreign applicant's marital status.
- Applicable translation and authentication of foreign-language documents.
- Two witnesses.
- Any additional consent or authorization required by the parties' circumstances.[^477]

The registrar examines the documents and legal eligibility, records the marriage, and issues the official marriage certificates.

**The published government procedure does not identify licensed legal counsel as a mandatory participant in ordinary marriage registration.**

#### Marriage Registration Is Free at District Offices

The Department of Provincial Administration explicitly states that registering a marriage at a district registration office carries **no government registration fee**.[^476]

The same official fee schedule lists a separate charge for marriage registration performed outside the office and for additional certified copies.

Foreign couples may also incur costs for translations, document authentication, and other preparation. None of those charges is a compulsory lawyer fee.

**A couple satisfying the applicable statutory and documentary requirements can register their marriage without purchasing private legal representation.**

#### TVC Confuses Administrative Registration With Legal Disputes

Marriage registration, prenuptial agreements, contested divorce, and child-custody litigation are legally distinct procedures.

Complex property arrangements or contested family-law proceedings may justify qualified professional advice or representation. Ordinary marriage registration, by contrast, is an administrative process handled by a government registrar.

TVC's guide groups these activities together as though they all impose the same professional-representation requirements.[^475]

The distinction matters because the agency advertises assistance with marriage documents, embassy coordination, translation, and marriage-based immigration applications.

**Offering those services does not establish that licensed Thai lawyers are legally required to register marriages.**

#### Misleading International Couples About Costs

TVC's statements could lead couples to believe they cannot legally marry in Thailand without hiring a private family lawyer.

That impression may encourage unnecessary professional expenses or discourage eligible couples from using the government's direct registration procedure.

The available evidence does not establish that TVC charged individual couples unnecessary legal fees. It establishes that the agency publishes an incorrect blanket requirement while marketing paid professional coordination.

**Thai Visa Centre should correct its family-law guide to acknowledge that eligible couples can register marriages directly at district offices without hiring licensed Thai lawyers.**

It should disclose the government's free in-office registration procedure and distinguish optional professional assistance from genuine statutory requirements.


### American Marriage Guide Ignores 2026 Registration Rules and Embassy Affidavit Changes

Thai Visa Centre's *How US Citizens Get Married in Thailand*, marked reviewed June 2026, gives American couples categorical instructions about obtaining an Affirmation of Freedom to Marry from the U.S. Embassy in Bangkok.

The guide states:

> A legal marriage requires Affirmation of Freedom to Marry from the US Embassy Bangkok before any amphoe will register the union.

It further warns:

> Without it, Thai registration stops at document check.[^508]

**These instructions fail to reflect Thailand's May 2026 civil-registration guidance and the U.S. Embassy's documented effort to replace its marriage-affidavit procedure.**

#### Thailand's May 2026 Circular Does Not Universally Require Embassy-Issued Evidence

On May 22, 2026, Thailand's Department of Provincial Administration issued Circular มท 0309.3/ว15381 concerning measures to prevent fraudulent marriage registration.[^509]

The circular provides instructions to district registrars handling marriages between Thai nationals and foreign nationals lawfully present in Thailand.

It directs officials to examine evidence of the foreign applicant's marital status issued by an authority legally empowered under the applicant's national law.

Crucially, it explains that the issuing authority **does not necessarily have to be the applicant's embassy or consulate**.

The document must establish the applicant's marital status or legal capacity to marry and comply with applicable translation and Thai Ministry of Foreign Affairs authentication requirements.

**The government's published instruction is broader than TVC's categorical claim that every American must obtain a specific document from the U.S. Embassy before a Thai district office can register the marriage.**

The precise documents accepted may depend on the foreign applicant's nationality and the authority competent to issue them. The circular does not mean that unverified declarations must be accepted automatically.

It means the embassy-only requirement cannot accurately be presented as universal.

#### U.S. Embassy Announced Plans to Discontinue Marriage Affidavits

The U.S. Embassy had already identified problems with the traditional affidavit procedure.

In a diplomatic note dated May 15, 2025, the Embassy advised Thai authorities that the U.S. Mission intended to stop notarizing self-sworn marriage affidavits from August 1, 2025.

The proposed cessation was subsequently postponed.

In a further diplomatic note dated October 24, 2025, the Embassy explained that the deadline had been temporarily extended while American and Thai officials worked toward an acceptable replacement.[^510]

The Embassy identified a possible alternative: a standardized, non-notarized letter that American citizens could download rather than obtain through a paid consular appointment.

The proposed letter would explain that the U.S. government does not issue the type of official marital-status confirmation assumed by the existing system.

**TVC's June 2026 guide does not explain this officially documented change in policy or the negotiations concerning alternative documentation.**

The diplomatic correspondence does not establish that notarization had permanently ceased by June 2026. It establishes that the Embassy had formally announced plans to discontinue the practice and that Thai authorities were already considering alternatives.

#### Embassy Notarization Does Not Independently Verify Marital Status

TVC states:

> The Affirmation of Freedom to Marry confirms you are free to marry under US law.[^508]

That description is misleading without explaining the nature of the document.

The traditional American marriage affidavit is a self-sworn declaration made by the applicant.

Consular notarization establishes the formal execution of the declaration. It does not mean the Embassy has independently searched American marriage records and certified that the applicant is unmarried.

Indeed, the U.S. Embassy's proposed replacement letter expressly explains that it does not possess information concerning the marital status of the American presenting it.[^510]

U.S. Department of State guidance similarly distinguishes the documentation and procedures required by foreign marriage authorities from a U.S. consular officer's ability to certify an individual's marital status under the laws of individual states.[^511]

**TVC confuses the notarization of an applicant's declaration with government verification of the underlying facts.**

That distinction is especially important in a process designed to prevent fraudulent or bigamous marriages.

#### The Problem Extends Beyond One Guide

TVC's general marriage-registration publications repeatedly present a fixed sequence involving an embassy affirmation, Thai translation, MFA legalization, and district-office registration.

The procedure can be appropriate for applicants whose nationality requires those particular documents.

However, Thailand's May 2026 instructions recognize that legally competent authorities outside embassies may issue acceptable marital-status evidence.

TVC's American-specific guide goes further by insisting that no Thai district office can register the marriage without the particular U.S. Embassy affirmation.

That categorical assertion is not justified by the government's published guidance.

#### Potential Consequences for American Couples

Americans planning marriages in Thailand may rely on TVC to coordinate embassy appointments, translations, legalization, and district-office registration.

Inaccurate requirements could lead applicants to:

- Book unnecessary embassy appointments.
- Assume they cannot marry without a specific consular document.
- Misunderstand the evidentiary value of a notarized affidavit.
- Overlook alternative documentation recognized by Thai registration authorities.
- Budget time and professional-service expenses around an unnecessarily rigid procedure.

The available evidence does not establish that TVC charged particular customers for unnecessary embassy visits or caused any marriage application to fail.

It establishes that the agency publishes categorical instructions inconsistent with the government's more flexible documentary framework and omits substantial diplomatic developments affecting Americans seeking marriage registration.

#### Required Corrections

Thai Visa Centre should revise its American marriage guide to acknowledge the May 22, 2026 Department of Provincial Administration circular.

It should distinguish evidence from legally competent foreign authorities from documents specifically issued or notarized by embassies.

It should explain the U.S. Embassy's announced plans to discontinue marriage-affidavit notarization, the temporary extension recorded in October 2025, and the need to verify any subsequent implementation decision.

Finally, it should clarify that notarizing a self-sworn declaration is not equivalent to independently certifying an American citizen's marital status.

**An agency claiming to coordinate American marriage registration daily should not insist that a particular embassy document is universally mandatory when Thailand's own registration authority has issued broader instructions and the Embassy has formally sought to replace that procedure.**

### Remarriage FAQ Denies Statutory Waiting Period and Uses Obsolete Marriage Age

Thai Visa Centre's FAQ *Divorcee Getting Married in Thailand*, marked reviewed June 2026, incorrectly tells foreign customers that Thai law imposes no waiting period after divorce.[^241]

The page repeatedly makes categorical claims, including:

> No statutory waiting period for remarriage once divorce is final.

It also describes marriage as available from age 17 with parental consent.

**Both statements conflict with Thailand's Civil and Commercial Code as amended by the Marriage Equality Act, effective January 23, 2025.**

#### Section 1453 Retains a 310-Day Remarriage Restriction

Section 1453 establishes a 310-day waiting period for women remarrying men following the termination of a previous marriage, subject to four statutory exceptions:[^242]

1. The woman has given birth during the waiting period.
2. She is remarrying her former spouse.
3. A qualified medical practitioner certifies that she is not pregnant.
4. A court authorizes the marriage.

The provision does not impose an equivalent waiting period on divorced men. Its amended wording also distinguishes women remarrying men from other marriage configurations.

Thailand's Government Public Relations Department confirmed the continuing 310-day restriction when explaining the January 2025 Marriage Equality Act.[^243]

**TVC's assurance that no statutory waiting period exists is therefore incorrect for applicants covered by Section 1453.**

#### TVC's Own Separate FAQ Explains the Rule Correctly

The contradiction is particularly clear because TVC publishes another FAQ titled *Waiting Period After Divorce - Remarriage in Thailand*.[^244]

That page correctly explains the 310-day restriction, the non-pregnancy certificate exception, and the different treatment of male divorcees.

It also warns that district offices can refuse premature marriage-registration applications.

Both FAQs claim to reflect June 2026 requirements, yet they provide opposite answers to the same legal question.

The incorrect FAQ further provides a suggested 10–14-day remarriage timeline without incorporating the applicable waiting-period requirements.

#### Marriage Age Was Raised to 18 in January 2025

The incorrect FAQ also describes the ordinary marriage-age threshold as 17 with parental consent.[^241]

TVC's separate *Marriage in Thailand* guide repeats the outdated minimum age of 17.[^245]

However, the 2024 amendment to Section 1448, effective January 23, 2025, raised the normal minimum marriage age to 18 for both parties.[^242]

Marriage below 18 requires court permission based on appropriate grounds. Parental consent alone does not replace that judicial authorization.

Separate consent requirements may still apply to persons aged 18 or 19.

**TVC therefore continues publishing pre-2025 marriage-age guidance in materials claiming to explain current registration requirements.**

#### Consequences for Customers

These discrepancies matter because TVC advertises marriage-document preparation, embassy coordination, district-office registration, and immigration assistance.

Customers relying on the incorrect guidance could arrange travel, embassy appointments, translations, and registration dates without satisfying the applicable marriage requirements.

A refused registration could also disrupt plans to apply for marriage-based immigration status.

The available evidence does not establish that TVC has caused failed registrations or financial losses through this guidance.

Nevertheless, the company presents contradictory legal instructions while advertising professional marriage-registration assistance.

TVC should correct its remarriage FAQ, identify the scope and exceptions of Section 1453, update its minimum-age guidance, and reconcile its published marriage-registration instructions.

### Family-Law Guide Misstates Prenuptial Registration Timing

Thai Visa Centre's *Thailand Family Law Questions – Marriage, Divorce, and Visas* guide, marked reviewed June 2026, advises couples that a prenuptial agreement must be registered at the same district office as their marriage and completed before the wedding day.[^331]

The guidance repeats this requirement in its family-property overview.

**This wording confuses preparing a prenuptial agreement before marriage with the legally required registration of its terms at the time of marriage registration.**

#### Civil and Commercial Code Requires Registration at Marriage

Section 1466 of Thailand's Civil and Commercial Code establishes two methods for recording a valid prenuptial agreement.[^332]

The parties may:

1. Record the agreed prenuptial terms directly in the marriage register when registering their marriage.
2. Prepare a written agreement signed by both spouses and at least two witnesses, annex it to the marriage register, and have the annexation recorded at the same time.

These alternatives establish the importance of simultaneous registration.

A couple may prepare and review their agreement before the wedding, but the statute does not require a separate prenuptial registration completed on an earlier day.

Indeed, an agreement prepared beforehand without the prescribed registration formalities will not satisfy Section 1466 merely because it was signed before the marriage.

**The legally significant deadline is the registration of the marriage, not the preceding calendar day.**

#### TVC's Specialist Guide Gives Different Instructions

The inconsistency is particularly apparent in Thai Visa Centre's dedicated *Prenuptial Agreements in Thailand* guide.[^333]

That publication correctly explains that registration occurs alongside marriage registration at the district office.

Its workflow describes the couple bringing their agreement to the marriage appointment, signing the necessary documents, and having the registrar record both matters together.

TVC therefore publishes conflicting explanations:

- Its general family-law overview tells couples that the prenuptial agreement must be completed before the wedding day.
- Its specialist guide correctly explains simultaneous registration with the marriage.

Preparing an agreement in advance is prudent, particularly when substantial assets or business interests are involved.

However, the timing of preparation must not be confused with the statutory registration requirement.


#### Prenuptial Agreement Guides Falsely Claim Licensed Thai Lawyers Are Mandatory

Thai Visa Centre also incorrectly tells couples that licensed Thai lawyers are legally required for prenuptial agreements.

Its *Thailand Law Firms and TVC Office Locations* guide presents a table distinguishing services requiring lawyers from those an immigration agent may coordinate. For marriage to a Thai national, the lawyer column states:

> Required for prenuptial and property agreements

A separate comparison on the same page identifies a prenuptial agreement as a situation in which an attorney is needed, while its legal-coordination section says marriage prenuptials require licensed Thai attorneys.[^467]

TVC's dedicated *Prenuptial Agreement in Thailand* guide repeatedly recommends drafting with a Thai family lawyer and asserts that its guide's subject requires licensed advocates for binding legal work.[^468]

**These statements portray optional legal assistance as a mandatory prerequisite to an enforceable prenuptial agreement.**

##### Section 1466 Does Not Require Legal Representation

As explained above, Section 1466 of Thailand's Civil and Commercial Code identifies two ways to satisfy the registration formalities: record the prenuptial terms in the marriage register at marriage registration, or annex a written agreement signed by the parties and at least two witnesses and record that annexation at the same time.[^332]

Neither route imposes a blanket requirement that a licensed lawyer draft, approve, sign, or register the agreement.

The legal requirements concern the agreement's permissible content and the prescribed execution and registration formalities, not the occupational status of a person helping the couple prepare it.

##### Government Procedures Allow Couples to Present Agreements Directly

Thailand's Department of Provincial Administration includes a prenuptial agreement, *if any*, among the documents couples may bring to a district office when registering their marriage.[^469]

The same government instructions describe the witnesses and identity documents needed for marriage registration. They do not list a lawyer's certificate or legal representation as a universal condition for submitting a prenuptial agreement.

Couples must still comply with the legal requirements for valid terms, execution, registration, and any applicable translation or document authentication.

**The government process does not require couples to hire a private lawyer merely to present and register an otherwise valid prenuptial agreement.**

##### Optional Advice Is Not a Statutory Requirement

Independent legal advice may be especially valuable when a prenuptial agreement affects valuable property, business ownership, overseas assets, or competing national laws. Separate counsel can help each party understand the agreement and reduce the risk of future disputes.

But recommending professional review is fundamentally different from claiming every prenuptial agreement requires a licensed Thai attorney.

TVC advertises referrals to family lawyers while presenting their involvement as compulsory in its office guide. That could mislead international couples into purchasing legal services they are not generally required to obtain.

The available evidence does not establish that TVC charged any particular couple unnecessary legal fees or caused an agreement to be invalid.

**Thai Visa Centre should remove its blanket mandatory-lawyer claim, explain the registration methods recognized by Section 1466, and distinguish optional legal drafting and independent advice from compulsory statutory formalities.**

#### Financial Consequences for International Couples

Prenuptial agreements may govern significant property interests, including condominium ownership, business shares, investment accounts, and the management of marital assets.

Couples misunderstanding the registration requirements might:

- Believe they have missed their opportunity when they have not yet registered their marriage.
- Attempt to register an agreement separately before marriage rather than ensuring it is properly recorded with the marriage.
- Rely on an agreement signed beforehand without verifying its inclusion in the official marriage register.
- Incur unnecessary legal or administrative expenses correcting misunderstandings about the process.

Section 1467 also restricts subsequent alteration of registered prenuptial agreements without court authorization, making compliance with the original formalities particularly important.

The available evidence does not establish that Thai Visa Centre has caused an invalid prenuptial agreement, improperly charged customers for registration, or produced financial losses through this guidance.

Nevertheless, its conflicting instructions concern a legally significant property-planning procedure that the agency advertises coordinating for international couples.

**TVC should correct its family-law overview to explain that prenuptial terms must be recorded simultaneously with marriage registration, distinguish advance preparation from official registration, and reconcile the instructions across its published guides.**

### Divorce Guides Incorrectly Require Joint Attendance and Misstate Court Procedures

Thai Visa Centre's *Divorce in Thailand: Amphoe and Family Court Options for Foreigners* guide, marked reviewed June 2026, repeatedly instructs couples that both spouses must attend the same district office to register a divorce by mutual consent.[^346]

Its frequently asked questions explicitly advise that when one spouse cannot attend, a contested court divorce is required.

The guide also suggests that couples normally return to the district office where their marriage was registered, warning against booking an appointment elsewhere.

TVC's separate *Thailand Divorce FAQs* and *Uncontested Divorce in Thailand* guides reinforce the impression that both spouses must attend together for administrative divorce registration.[^347]

**These instructions omit an established government procedure allowing mutually consenting spouses to register their divorce at separate registry offices, including qualifying cases involving different countries.**

#### Government Expressly Permits Divorce Registration at Different Offices

Thailand's Bureau of Registration Administration, under the Department of Provincial Administration, publishes official instructions for registering divorce.[^348]

The government recognizes two methods of registration by mutual consent:

1. **Same-office registration:** Both spouses appear before the same registrar and complete their divorce registration together.
2. **Different-office registration:** Each spouse completes the required registration procedure before a different registrar, with the offices exchanging documentation.

The official manual explains the second procedure in detail.

One spouse initially submits the application, identification documents, marriage-registration evidence, and written divorce agreement.

The first registrar records the application and forwards the necessary documents to the second registrar.

The other spouse subsequently appears before the second registrar, reviews the documents, and confirms consent.

**The divorce becomes effective after the second registrar completes the required registration.**

This is not a proxy divorce or an exemption from personal consent.

Both spouses must still participate in the legally prescribed registration process.

However, they are not categorically required to appear together at the same office.

#### Thai Embassy Confirms Cross-Border Registration

The Royal Thai Embassy in Washington publishes separate instructions for divorces registered at different registrar offices.[^349]

Its guidance explicitly addresses mutually consenting spouses who cannot travel to the same registration office.

For eligible cases involving one spouse in Thailand and the other residing within the embassy's consular jurisdiction, the published procedure permits registration through:

- A district registrar in Thailand.
- The consular registrar at the Royal Thai Embassy in Washington.

Either spouse may initiate the process under the applicable procedure.

Documents are subsequently transmitted through official channels, enabling the other spouse to complete registration.

The embassy confirms that both registrations must be completed before the divorce becomes effective.

Its guidance also warns that U.S. immigration authorities may not recognize certain consular or different-office divorces for Green Card purposes.

This is an important limitation: a divorce validly registered under Thai procedures may face separate recognition requirements in another jurisdiction.

**Nevertheless, the embassy's published procedure directly contradicts TVC's categorical suggestion that spouses unable to attend the same office must obtain a contested Thai court divorce.**

#### Couples Do Not Have to Return to Their Original Marriage District

TVC's general divorce guide also suggests that couples should normally register their divorce at the district where they originally married.

Its FAQ tells couples who married in Phuket but now live in Bangkok that they often need to return to the original district office.[^346]

However, the Bureau of Registration Administration's official instructions permit applications at authorized district registration offices without restricting them to the office where the marriage was originally registered.[^348]

The U.S. Department of State likewise identifies the issuing authority for Thai divorce certificates as the original marriage district or any other district registration office.[^350]

Individual offices may require document verification or advance appointments.

Nevertheless, those practical arrangements do not establish a general legal requirement to return to the original marriage district.

TVC should distinguish obtaining historical marriage-registration documents from choosing an authorized office to register a divorce.

#### Separate Guide Misidentifies the Governing Civil Code Section

The problems extend to TVC's *Thai Divorce Law: Grounds, Property Division, and Court Procedure* guide.[^351]

Under its administrative-divorce section, the publication identifies **Section 1516** as the legal pathway for divorce by mutual consent.

This is incorrect.

Thailand's Civil and Commercial Code distinguishes:

- **Section 1514:** Divorce by mutual consent or court judgment, including the written-agreement and witness requirements for consensual divorce.
- **Section 1515:** Registration requirements for a divorce by mutual consent.
- **Section 1516:** Statutory grounds on which a spouse may seek divorce through court proceedings.[^352][^353]

Section 1516 does not establish the ordinary administrative-divorce procedure.

Indeed, TVC's separate contested-divorce guide correctly identifies Section 1516 as the basis for court divorce claims.

The inconsistent citations reinforce a broader failure to distinguish consensual administrative registration from litigation involving contested divorce.

#### Civil Registration Is Not Administered by the Judiciary

TVC's general divorce guide also describes district-office divorce registration as operating under the Office of the Judiciary's civil-registration system.[^346]

However, district-level marriage and divorce registration falls within Thailand's civil-registration administration under the Ministry of Interior, through the Department of Provincial Administration and its Bureau of Registration Administration.[^348]

The Office of the Judiciary has a separate role in proceedings requiring judicial determination, including contested divorce litigation.

**Administrative registration and judicial divorce proceedings are distinct government functions.**

Confusing the responsible authorities can make already complicated family-law procedures harder for foreign residents to navigate.

#### Financial and Immigration Consequences

These errors matter particularly for Thai-foreign couples living in different provinces or countries.

Spouses relying on TVC's instructions might:

- Believe they must initiate contested litigation despite agreeing to divorce.
- Incur unnecessary travel costs returning to the district where they married.
- Arrange international flights merely to attend the same registration appointment.
- Overlook a potentially available consular registration procedure.
- Delay divorce registration while seeking court representation.
- Misunderstand when their marriage legally ends and how that affects marriage-based immigration permission.

The available evidence does not establish that Thai Visa Centre has directed specific customers into unnecessary litigation, received referral commissions from divorce lawyers, or caused financial losses through these instructions.

Nor does the separate-office procedure apply automatically in every international situation. Eligibility, consular jurisdiction, documentary requirements, and foreign recognition must be individually assessed.

Nevertheless, **TVC repeatedly presents joint attendance and contested litigation as mandatory alternatives while omitting an established administrative procedure expressly recognized by Thai government authorities.**

#### Recommended Corrections

Thai Visa Centre should revise its divorce publications to:

1. Explain same-office and different-office registration by mutual consent.
2. Distinguish a spouse who cannot attend the same location from one who refuses to consent or cannot complete the required registration.
3. Clarify that eligible couples may register at authorized offices other than their original marriage district.
4. Identify the Department of Provincial Administration as the authority responsible for civil registration.
5. Correct the references to Sections 1514, 1515, and 1516.
6. Explain the separate recognition requirements potentially affecting divorces registered through Thai consular offices.

These corrections would provide materially more accurate information to couples making significant legal, financial, and immigration decisions.

### Commercial Significance for International Couples

TVC's guidance does not consistently distinguish ordinary civil registration from contested legal proceedings or optional professional advice from compulsory statutory requirements. Eligible couples can pursue administrative marriage registration directly, while requirements for remarriage, prenuptial registration, and consensual divorce depend on the applicable law and the couple's circumstances. A lawyer may be useful for complex documents, disputed rights, or litigation without being a universal prerequisite for straightforward registry procedures.

The difference matters especially for cross-border couples: registration under Thai law does not automatically settle whether another country will recognize a marriage, divorce, or related documentation for immigration or other purposes. Embassy practices, eligibility for separate-office or consular registration, and foreign recognition must be assessed separately rather than assumed.

**Taken together, the five findings could cause couples to misunderstand their legal options or purchase unnecessary assistance, but they do not establish that TVC actually charged any particular customer unnecessary fees, caused failed registrations, or directed customers into avoidable litigation.** The agency should reconcile the source guides and clearly distinguish mandatory formalities, permitted administrative alternatives, and circumstances warranting specialist legal advice.

## Misleading Foreign Property Ownership, Leasehold, and Condominium Transfer Requirements

Thai Visa Centre publishes misleading and sometimes contradictory guidance about foreign property ownership, leasehold security, condominium purchases, resale, and inheritance in Thailand. Across its guides, the agency presents optional legal services as mandatory, omits narrow exceptions to foreign land-ownership restrictions, exaggerates the security of prearranged lease renewals, and applies documentation or ownership-quota requirements to transactions that follow different legal rules.

TVC advertises property-related legal referrals and due-diligence assistance. **The recurring problem is its failure to distinguish general restrictions from statutory exceptions, and mandatory registration requirements from optional professional assistance.** The findings below address six related errors without suggesting that the same rules govern every type of property transaction.

### Property Guides Falsely Claim Licensed Thai Lawyers Are Mandatory for Condominium Purchases

Thai Visa Centre tells foreign property buyers that licensed Thai lawyers are essential for condominium purchases and that legal representation is mandatory before paying a deposit.

Its *Thailand Law Firms and TVC Office Locations* guide identifies condominium and land purchases as requiring a Thai lawyer:

> Licensed Thai lawyer essential

The same table asserts that due diligence and title review must be conducted by a Thai attorney.[^478]

Its separate *Real Estate Lawyer in Thailand* guide expressly answers the question *When is a lawyer mandatory?*:

> Before any deposit on condo, leasehold villa, or land-backed deal.

That publication also directs buyers to instruct a property lawyer before paying any reservation fee and says even simple condominium resales require counsel.[^479]

**These statements present professional legal representation as compulsory when Thailand's ordinary condominium registration procedures impose no blanket requirement to hire a lawyer.**

#### Department of Lands Permits Direct Ownership Transfers

The Department of Lands administers registration of condominium ownership through the competent Land Office.

The Thai government's published instructions identify the documents needed for registration by eligible foreign purchasers, including title documentation, identification, information about the parties or their authorized representatives, and applicable evidence of foreign ownership and funding eligibility.[^480]

Condominium transfers may also require the building's foreign-ownership quota confirmation and certification concerning outstanding common-area expenses.

The official documentary requirements do not impose a universal requirement that a licensed Thai lawyer prepare, approve, or attend a condominium transfer.

The Department of Lands separately explains that parties to an ordinary property sale can attend the Land Office themselves with the original registration documents; representation is an option where circumstances require it.[^481]

**Proper Land Office registration, compliance with condominium ownership restrictions, and hiring a private property lawyer are three different matters.**

#### TVC's Own Due-Diligence Guide Acknowledges the Distinction

TVC's *10 Things to Look for in a Thai Property Due Diligence Review* provides a revealing contrast.

In response to the question *Is due diligence mandatory by law?*, the guide answers:

> Not legally mandatory, but prudent and standard for any foreign buyer.

It nevertheless tells buyers to commission licensed lawyers and describes standard condominium due-diligence fees of **15,000–40,000 baht**.[^482]

The statement that due diligence is not legally mandatory does not mean due diligence is unimportant. It demonstrates, however, that TVC knows how to distinguish a prudent service from an actual statutory obligation.

Its categorical statements elsewhere about mandatory lawyers fail to make that distinction.

#### Independent Legal Advice Can Still Be Valuable

Foreign condominium acquisitions carry substantial financial and legal risks. Independent counsel can help investigate title defects, mortgages, developer obligations, condominium quota restrictions, unpaid common-area charges, and contract provisions.

Such assistance is particularly sensible for off-plan developments, contested transfers, high-value resales, or complex foreign-funding arrangements.

**Recommending independent legal review is sound risk-management advice; claiming that the law universally requires a lawyer is a different assertion.**

The responsible government authority decides whether the transaction satisfies the statutory registration requirements. A lawyer's involvement does not replace those requirements, and their absence does not automatically invalidate an otherwise eligible transaction.

#### Potentially Misleading Foreign Buyers About Costs

TVC advertises property-lawyer referrals while describing licensed counsel as essential for condominium purchases.

Its own guides identify professional due-diligence costs in the tens of thousands of baht, before any separately charged transfer-attendance services.[^479][^482]

Foreign buyers could therefore believe they are legally obliged to incur private legal fees before paying a deposit or completing an otherwise lawful condominium acquisition.

The evidence does not establish that TVC charged individual customers unnecessary fees or mishandled their transactions. It establishes that the company's public guidance presents optional legal representation as mandatory, even while acknowledging that due diligence itself is not legally required.

**Thai Visa Centre should correct its property guides to distinguish mandatory ownership and registration requirements from optional independent legal review.**

It should acknowledge that eligible buyers and sellers can use the Department of Lands' prescribed transfer procedures without universally retaining private attorneys, while continuing to explain why professional due diligence may be advisable.

### Property Guides Omit Statutory Exceptions to Foreign Land Ownership

Thai Visa Centre's *Thailand Property FAQ for Expats*, marked reviewed June 2026, categorically states that foreigners cannot legally own land in Thailand.[^339]

When directly asked whether a foreigner can own land, the guide answers:

> No. Direct land ownership by foreigners is prohibited under the Land Code.

Its separate *Buying a House in Thailand as a Foreigner* guide similarly describes foreign freehold ownership as prohibited and presents leasehold arrangements, usufructs, and Thai-spouse ownership as the available alternatives.[^340]

The agency's broader *Property Law in Thailand for Expats* guide also categorizes direct foreign land ownership as unavailable in its property comparison table.[^341]

**These categorical statements omit express statutory exceptions under which qualifying foreigners can legally acquire land in Thailand.**

#### Land Code Section 96 Bis Permits Limited Foreign Freehold Ownership

Section 96 bis of Thailand's Land Code establishes a specific exception to the ordinary restrictions on foreign land ownership.

Under this provision, a qualifying foreign individual may acquire up to **one rai (1,600 square metres)** of land for residential purposes, subject to substantial investment and government approval requirements.[^342]

Thailand's official government guidance identifies the principal conditions:

- Investment of at least **40 million baht** in qualifying Thai assets or activities.
- Maintenance of the qualifying investment for the prescribed period, identified in government guidance as at least five years.
- Explicit permission from the Minister of Interior.
- Land restricted to designated geographic areas, including Bangkok, Pattaya, qualifying municipalities, and approved residential zones.
- Residential use consistent with the statutory conditions.

Qualifying investments may include specified government bonds, property funds, investments in promoted companies, and other approved activities.

These requirements are exceptionally restrictive, and obtaining approval is not automatic.

Nevertheless, **the law expressly permits foreign freehold land ownership in circumstances that TVC's guides describe as categorically impossible.**

#### Government Publishes Registration Procedures for Foreign Owners

The omission is particularly significant because Thailand's government publishes specific documentation requirements for foreigners seeking land ownership under the permitted exceptions.[^343]

The published requirements include:

- Identification and nationality documentation.
- Evidence of qualifying investment funds.
- Certificates establishing the nature and amount of the investment.
- Town-planning confirmation that the land is situated in a permitted residential area.
- Confirmation that the property is outside restricted military safety zones.
- Land-title documentation and location plans.

These requirements demonstrate that the statutory exception is accompanied by an identifiable administrative application procedure.

The existence of the procedure does not guarantee approval or establish that ordinary foreign buyers can qualify.

However, it directly contradicts an explanation that excludes all foreign individuals from freehold land ownership.

#### BOI-Promoted Foreign Companies Have Separate Land Rights

A second exception exists under Thailand's Investment Promotion Act.

Section 27 authorizes qualifying BOI-promoted entities to own land needed for their approved investment activities.[^344]

The Board of Investment expressly recognizes that companies treated as foreign under Thai land law may obtain permission to hold qualifying land.

The permitted amount depends on the promoted activities, applicable BOI criteria, and the authorization granted.

This is not equivalent to unrestricted private land ownership.

A foreign shareholder does not personally own land simply because a BOI-promoted company holds its title.

Likewise, ordinary Thai companies using nominee shareholders to conceal foreign control do not acquire lawful land ownership through these exceptions.

Nevertheless, the BOI framework establishes another legally recognized category of foreign land ownership that should be distinguished from prohibited nominee arrangements.

#### Land Department Recognizes the Exceptions

The distinction is not merely theoretical.

A May 26, 2026 report concerning the Land Department's enforcement of foreign land-ownership restrictions identified three principal exceptions:

1. Qualifying inheritance under Section 93 of the Land Code.
2. Residential acquisition under Section 96 bis following the required investment and ministerial approval.
3. Ownership authorized under separate legislation, including the Investment Promotion Act and other applicable investment laws.[^345]

The report also described intensified scrutiny of nominee arrangements and suspicious land transfers.

These enforcement measures do not abolish the statutory exceptions.

Rather, they underscore the importance of distinguishing legitimate, expressly authorized foreign ownership from prohibited arrangements designed to evade nationality restrictions.

**A comprehensive property guide should explain both the prohibition and its narrowly defined legal exceptions.**

#### Financial Implications for Foreign Buyers

The distinction matters particularly for wealthy foreign investors, business owners, and long-term residents considering substantial property investments in Thailand.

Foreigners relying on TVC's categorical statements might incorrectly conclude that direct land ownership is legally impossible regardless of investment size or government authorization.

This could affect decisions concerning:

- Residential investment structures.
- Foreign-owned business expansion.
- BOI investment-promotion applications.
- Long-term property and estate planning.
- Whether to pursue freehold ownership or alternative arrangements involving leases and registered rights.

The statutory exceptions are unlikely to benefit most ordinary property buyers.

Indeed, for many foreigners, a registered condominium or carefully structured lease remains considerably more practical.

Nevertheless, the existence of substantial eligibility requirements does not justify describing a lawful ownership route as nonexistent.

#### Relevance to Thai Visa Centre's Commercial Services

Thai Visa Centre advertises property-related legal referrals, immigration coordination, and assistance for foreign investors establishing businesses in Thailand.

Its property publications repeatedly direct readers toward leasehold structures and other alternatives without explaining the Section 96 bis investment exception.

The available evidence does not establish that Thai Visa Centre has improperly directed customers into leasehold investments, earned commissions from particular property transactions, or caused financial losses through these omissions.

However, its categorical explanations fail to distinguish the general prohibition from recognized statutory exceptions that could be material to some prospective customers.

**Thai Visa Centre should revise its property guides to explain Section 96 bis, identify the applicable investment and ministerial-approval requirements, distinguish BOI-promoted corporate ownership from personal freehold rights, and clarify that nominee structures remain unlawful.**

### Property Guide Promotes 90-Year Lease Structure Despite Supreme Court Ruling

Thai Visa Centre's *Property Law in Thailand for Expats* guide, marked reviewed June 2026, prominently advertises **30+30+30 years** as a leasehold term available to foreign property buyers.[^306]

The guide states that registered leases can extend in successive blocks but does not explain the significant legal restrictions affecting arrangements intended to secure 90 years of occupancy from the outset.

**This presentation risks giving foreign buyers an exaggerated impression of the security provided by prearranged lease renewals.**

#### Civil and Commercial Code Limits Ordinary Leases to 30 Years

Section 540 of Thailand's Civil and Commercial Code generally limits ordinary immovable-property leases to 30 years, while permitting subsequent renewals subject to legal requirements.[^307]

The distinction between a valid initial lease and promised future renewals is fundamental.

A registered 30-year lease does not automatically secure another 60 years of legally enforceable occupancy.

#### Supreme Court Invalidated a 30+30+30 Arrangement

In **Supreme Court Decision 4655/2566**, issued in 2023, the court examined a Phuket property lease involving:

- An initial registered 30-year term.
- Promises of two additional 30-year terms made when the original lease was signed.
- Advance payment covering all three periods.
- Renewal terms agreed at the outset rather than renegotiated when the initial lease expired.

The Supreme Court held that the arrangements for the additional 60 years were intended to circumvent Section 540 and were therefore void.[^308]

The initial 30-year lease remained effective. However, after its expiration, the tenant could not compel the promised renewal. The court upheld eviction and awarded damages for continued occupation without legal entitlement.

A 2026 analysis published by Chambers and Partners confirms the substantial enforceability risks associated with prearranged long-term lease structures.[^309]

The decision does not establish that every future renewal agreement is invalid. Its reasoning particularly concerns attempts to secure successive 30-year periods on predetermined terms that circumvent the statutory limit.

**The legally important distinction is between a renewable 30-year lease and a supposedly secured 90-year property interest.**

#### TVC's Separate Lease Guide Acknowledges the Problem

The inconsistency is especially apparent because Thai Visa Centre's dedicated *Property Leases in Thailand* guide expressly warns against trusting 90-year lease marketing.[^310]

That publication acknowledges that only the initial 30-year term enjoys ordinary statutory protection and that later renewal promises have uncertain enforceability.

It also advises foreigners to obtain independent legal review before paying deposits.

TVC therefore gives readers conflicting impressions:

Its property overview advertises **30+30+30 years** as a prominent leasehold term, while its specialist guide recognizes that the additional periods are not assured.

#### Financial Implications for Foreign Buyers

The distinction matters particularly for foreigners considering expensive leasehold villas in Phuket, Samui, Pattaya, and other property markets.

A buyer might pay a substantial upfront premium for what appears to be 90 years of occupation, even though the enforceable rights could be limited to the initial registered term.

This can affect property valuation, resale expectations, inheritance planning, and the financial security of retirement investments.

The available evidence does not establish that Thai Visa Centre has sold invalid leasehold arrangements or received commissions from developers promoting unenforceable renewals.

Nevertheless, the agency advertises property-related legal referrals and immigration coordination while presenting a legally sensitive lease structure without adequately explaining the relevant Supreme Court decision.

**Thai Visa Centre should replace its simplified 30+30+30-year presentation with an explanation distinguishing enforceable registered lease terms, conditional renewal agreements, and arrangements that courts may invalidate as attempts to evade Section 540.**

### Condominium Guides Incorrectly Require Overseas Remittances From Permanent Residents

Thai Visa Centre's guide *Condo in Thailand*, marked reviewed June 2026, presents foreign-currency remittances and Foreign Exchange Transaction (FET) documentation as mandatory requirements for every foreign condominium purchase.[^246]

The guide identifies FET documentation as a statutory requirement and warns that transferring Thai baht from a domestic bank account instead of remitting foreign currency from abroad is a purchasing mistake.

Its separate *Thailand Property FAQ for Expats* repeats that foreigners must remit purchase funds from abroad and obtain an FET form.[^247]

**These categorical instructions omit an express exception for foreign permanent residents under Thailand's Condominium Act.**

#### Government Confirms Permanent Residents Can Use Thai Baht

Thailand's official government website explains that foreign buyers holding permanent-resident status may purchase condominium units using Thai baht without requesting an FET form.[^248]

Section 19 of the Condominium Act B.E. 2522 (1979) establishes separate eligibility categories, including:

1. **Section 19(1):** Foreigners holding permission to reside permanently in Thailand under immigration law.
2. **Section 19(2):** Foreigners admitted under applicable investment-promotion legislation.
3. **Section 19(5):** Foreigners qualifying through specified foreign-currency remittances or permitted account withdrawals.[^249]

Section 19 ter prescribes different supporting evidence for these categories.

A qualifying permanent resident provides evidence of immigration residence permission, rather than relying on foreign-currency remittance evidence under Section 19(5).

All applicants remain subject to applicable ownership restrictions, including the condominium building's foreign-ownership quota.

**The government's FET exemption does not eliminate ordinary property-registration requirements. It establishes that overseas remittance is not universally mandatory.**

#### TVC Repeats the Requirement in Its Legal-Services Advertising

The discrepancy extends beyond general property information.

TVC's *Thailand Property Legal Services* guide describes FET compliance as a standard part of professional assistance and states that foreign buyers must transfer condominium purchase funds from abroad in foreign currency.[^250]

Its due-diligence materials similarly identify overseas remittance as mandatory without explaining the permanent-resident exception.

These publications promote document preparation, property-lawyer referrals, banking coordination, and Land Department transfer assistance.

However, they do not adequately distinguish permanent residents from foreign purchasers who must establish eligibility through qualifying remittances.

#### Financial Consequences for Buyers

Incorrectly presenting overseas remittance as compulsory could lead foreign permanent residents to arrange unnecessary international transfers.

For substantial condominium purchases, those arrangements may involve:

- Foreign-exchange conversion charges and unfavorable exchange rates.
- International bank-transfer fees and administrative delays.
- Unnecessary movement of funds already lawfully held in Thailand.
- Additional document preparation or professional assistance based on an incorrect eligibility assessment.

The available evidence does not establish that TVC has instructed individual permanent residents to transfer funds unnecessarily or collected fees for avoidable transactions.

Nevertheless, its published instructions omit a significant statutory distinction affecting how qualifying foreigners may finance property purchases.

TVC should distinguish Section 19(1) permanent-resident purchases from Section 19(5) remittance-based purchases, explain the different Land Department documentation requirements, and correct its categorical statements that every foreign condominium buyer must transfer funds from overseas.

### Condominium Resale Guide Incorrectly Requires Unused Foreign Quota for Existing Foreign-Owned Units

Thai Visa Centre's *Condominium Rights in Thailand: Foreign Owner Obligations and Bylaws*, marked reviewed June 2026, gives misleading advice concerning the resale of condominiums already registered in foreign ownership.[^381]

When asked what happens if the foreign ownership quota is full when an owner wants to sell, the guide states:

> You can sell to a Thai national. Selling to another foreigner requires an available foreign quota slot in the building.

The same publication lists buying when foreign ownership approaches 49% as a common mistake, warning that insufficient remaining quota can restrict future resale opportunities for foreign owners.

**These statements confuse the acquisition of additional foreign-owned condominium floor area with transferring an existing foreign-owned unit to another qualifying foreign purchaser.**

#### Condominium Act Limits Aggregate Foreign Ownership, Not Successive Foreign Owners

Section 19 bis of Thailand's Condominium Act B.E. 2522 (1979), as amended, generally limits qualifying foreign ownership to 49% of the aggregate floor area of all units in a registered condominium building.[^382]

The restriction concerns the proportion of the building held by foreigners.

It does not establish a separate requirement for an additional unused foreign ownership allocation every time a unit already owned by a foreigner changes hands.

Sections 19 ter and 19 quater establish the requirements for registering transfers to qualifying foreign purchasers.[^382]

They require the relevant ownership information and supporting documents to be submitted to the Land Office.

The registrar must verify that the resulting foreign ownership proportion complies with the statutory ceiling.

For an ordinary transfer of a foreign-owned unit to another qualifying foreign buyer, the seller ceases to hold the unit as the buyer acquires it.

The same floor area remains included in the building's foreign ownership total.

**The transaction does not ordinarily increase the foreign-owned proportion and therefore does not require additional unused quota merely because the building has already reached 49%.**

#### A Full Foreign Quota Does Not Automatically Prevent Foreign-to-Foreign Resale

Consider a condominium building with 10,000 square metres of total unit floor area.

The ordinary statutory ceiling permits foreigners to hold up to 4,900 square metres.

Assume that the entire foreign allocation is already occupied.

If a foreign owner sells an existing 80-square-metre unit to another qualifying foreigner:

- The selling foreign owner's 80 square metres leave their ownership.
- The purchasing foreign owner's 80 square metres enter their ownership.
- The building's aggregate foreign-owned area remains 4,900 square metres.
- The resulting foreign ownership ratio remains 49%.

The transaction does not increase the building's aggregate foreign ownership.

The result differs when a foreign purchaser seeks to acquire a unit currently owned by a Thai national.

In that situation, transferring the Thai-owned unit into foreign ownership would increase the building's foreign-owned area.

If the building has already exhausted the permitted allocation, that additional acquisition ordinarily cannot be registered.

**TVC's guidance fails to distinguish these fundamentally different transactions.**

The distinction is confirmed in published analyses of condominium resale procedures, which explain that an existing foreign-owned unit can ordinarily pass to another qualifying foreign purchaser without increasing the foreign ownership ratio.[^383]

#### TVC's Separate Selling Guide Contradicts Its Own Warning

The inconsistency is especially clear because Thai Visa Centre's separate *Selling Your Condominium in Thailand: Foreign Owner Resale Guide*, also marked reviewed June 2026, provides a different explanation.[^384]

That publication states that an existing foreign quota allocation normally passes to the next qualifying foreign buyer when the ownership transfer is registered.

Its resale workflow further explains that the condominium juristic person must update the foreign ownership register after completion.

This is substantially more consistent with the statutory framework.

TVC therefore gives conflicting advice:

1. Its condominium-rights guide suggests an unused quota allocation is required when an existing foreign owner sells to another foreigner.
2. Its dedicated selling guide correctly describes the existing allocation transferring to the next qualifying foreign owner.

Both guides target foreign condominium owners making property and investment decisions.

**The company should reconcile these instructions rather than presenting the foreign ownership ceiling as an additional restriction on the resale of units already counted within that ceiling.**

#### Foreign Buyers Must Still Satisfy Independent Registration Requirements

The fact that a foreign-to-foreign resale does not ordinarily increase aggregate foreign ownership does not make registration automatic.

The incoming purchaser must independently qualify under Section 19 of the Condominium Act.

Depending on the buyer's qualifying category, the relevant supporting evidence may include:

- Proof of eligible immigration residence or investment-promotion status.
- Evidence of qualifying foreign-currency remittances or withdrawals from accounts recognized by Section 19.
- The condominium juristic person's confirmation of the building's foreign ownership position.
- The unit title deed and required transfer documents.
- A certificate confirming that applicable common-property expenses have been paid.

The Department of Lands must still verify the purchaser's eligibility and the building's ownership ratio before registering the transfer.[^385]

An existing foreign seller's original Foreign Exchange Transaction documentation does not automatically establish that a new purchaser satisfies their own funding requirements.

The new buyer must provide the evidence applicable to their own statutory eligibility category.

The buyer and seller should also confirm the current registration position, particularly where historic ownership arrangements, unusual title circumstances, or applicable statutory exceptions complicate the foreign ownership calculation.

**The important distinction is that a purchaser's separate eligibility and documentation requirements are not the same as a requirement for newly available foreign-owned floor area.**

#### Selling to a Thai National Has Different Quota Consequences

The ownership ratio can change when a foreign-owned condominium unit is sold to a Thai national.

Once the transfer is properly registered, the former foreign-owned floor area ordinarily ceases to count toward the building's foreign ownership allocation.

This can make additional foreign ownership capacity available for subsequent transfers involving other units.

Conversely, transferring an existing Thai-owned unit to a foreigner consumes otherwise available foreign ownership capacity.

These distinctions explain why the nationality and eligibility of both the existing owner and prospective purchaser matter.

TVC's guidance treats a full quota primarily as an obstacle to foreign purchasers without properly distinguishing whether the particular unit is already counted within that quota.

A clear explanation should address all three situations:

1. Foreign-owned unit sold to another qualifying foreigner: ordinarily no increase in foreign-owned area.
2. Foreign-owned unit sold to a Thai national: foreign-owned area ordinarily decreases.
3. Thai-owned unit sold to a foreigner: foreign-owned area increases and requires sufficient available capacity.

The Land Office must ultimately verify the relevant ownership and registration requirements.

#### Financial Implications for Foreign Property Owners

The distinction can materially affect a foreign owner's understanding of their property rights and resale options.

Owners relying on TVC's warning might incorrectly believe that a building reaching 49% foreign ownership makes their existing foreign-owned condominium impossible to sell to another foreigner.

This could lead them to:

- Exclude otherwise qualified foreign purchasers from negotiations.
- Assume they must wait for another foreign owner to sell to a Thai national.
- Accept an unnecessarily restricted potential buyer pool.
- Consider replacing freehold ownership with a leasehold arrangement.
- Make inaccurate assumptions about the resale value or liquidity of their investment.

These are significant considerations for foreign retirees and investors holding condominium assets worth millions of baht.

The available evidence does not establish that Thai Visa Centre has caused a failed condominium transaction, advised a particular seller to accept an undervalued offer, or received compensation from property transactions affected by this guidance.

Nevertheless, its published advice could mislead owners about the legal marketability of property they already hold.

#### Recommended Corrections

Thai Visa Centre should amend its condominium-rights guide to distinguish:

- The 49% aggregate foreign ownership ceiling.
- Transfers of existing foreign-owned units to qualifying foreign purchasers.
- Acquisitions of previously Thai-owned units that would increase aggregate foreign ownership.
- The purchaser-specific documentary requirements under Sections 19 and 19 ter.
- The Land Office's responsibility to verify the resulting ownership proportion under Section 19 quater.

The company should also remove its categorical implication that a foreign owner requires additional unused foreign quota to resell an existing foreign-owned unit.

**TVC should reconcile its condominium-rights guide with its own dedicated condominium-selling guide, which already acknowledges that an existing foreign ownership allocation can pass to the next qualifying purchaser.**

### Condominium Inheritance Guides Omit Land Department Ruling Protecting Foreign Heirs

Thai Visa Centre's *Foreigner Making a Will in Thailand* guide, marked reviewed June 2026, warns that foreign beneficiaries must satisfy condominium foreign-ownership quotas and Foreign Exchange Transaction (FET) documentation requirements when inheriting property.[^314]

The guide states that foreign heirs need available ownership quota and may require FET documentation. It also warns that inadequate estate planning can result in forced property sales.

TVC's separate *Last Will and Testament Under Thai Law* guide similarly presents qualification under the foreign-ownership quota as a requirement for retaining inherited condominiums, warning that foreign heirs may otherwise have to sell.[^315]

**These descriptions omit a significant Land Department interpretation recognizing that certain foreign heirs succeed to the deceased owner's existing condominium ownership rights.**

#### Land Department Recognizes Inherited Ownership Rights

Section 19 of Thailand's Condominium Act B.E. 2522 (1979) establishes several categories of foreigners eligible to own condominium units, including persons who acquire property using qualifying foreign-currency remittances under Section 19(5).[^316]

Section 19 septies requires foreigners outside the qualifying statutory categories who inherit condominium units to notify the authorities within 60 days and dispose of the property within one year.
