However, the Land Department has addressed an important distinction involving foreign heirs of existing foreign condominium owners.

In a published interpretation, the department applied Sections 1599 and 1600 of the Civil and Commercial Code, which provide that a deceased person's estate passes to the heirs together with the deceased's transferable rights and obligations.[^317]

The department concluded that a foreign heir succeeding to a condominium previously acquired by a qualifying foreign owner may inherit the deceased owner's status under Section 19(5).

**Such an heir is not automatically required to sell the condominium within one year under Section 19 septies merely because the heir is a foreign national.**

This interpretation was examined by the Thai law firm Tilleke & Gibbins in its analysis of condominium inheritance by foreigners.[^318]

The analysis confirms that heirs may succeed to the deceased owner's qualifying rights rather than necessarily establishing an entirely new foreign-currency-funded acquisition.

#### Foreign Ownership Quotas Still Apply

The Land Department's interpretation does not eliminate Thailand's condominium foreign-ownership restrictions.

Section 19 bis generally limits foreign ownership to 49% of the building's total unit floor area.

Section 19 quinquies also provides for disposal when inherited ownership causes the applicable foreign-ownership proportion to be exceeded.

These requirements must be distinguished from the separate question of whether an heir qualifies to retain a previously foreign-owned condominium.

For example, transferring an existing foreign-owned unit to another eligible foreign owner does not, by itself, increase the building's aggregate foreign-owned floor area.

Nevertheless, inheritance registration, evidence of the deceased owner's ownership status, the building's quota, and other applicable requirements remain relevant.

The government also identifies specific documentation for inherited property transfers, including death certificates, wills, heir identification, and condominium ownership records.[^319]

#### Foreign-Currency Requirements Are Not Automatically Repeated

TVC's assertion that foreign heirs must satisfy FET requirements also fails to distinguish an ordinary condominium purchase from inheritance.

A foreign buyer acquiring condominium ownership under Section 19(5) normally establishes eligibility through qualifying financial transactions.

An heir succeeding to that owner's existing rights occupies a different legal position.

Evidence supporting the deceased owner's original qualification may remain relevant during registration, but the Land Department's interpretation does not establish a universal requirement that the heir personally remit the property's purchase price into Thailand again.

**The distinction is between proving the legal basis of an inherited ownership right and treating an inheritance as an entirely new foreign-funded purchase.**

TVC's guidance does not adequately explain this difference.

#### Financial Implications for Foreign Families

These omissions matter because condominiums often represent substantial portions of foreign residents' estates in Bangkok, Pattaya, Phuket, and other Thai property markets.

Families misunderstanding the rules might:

- Believe an inherited condominium must be sold within one year when the Land Department's interpretation permits continued ownership.
- Assume fresh foreign-currency remittances are necessary to receive inherited property.
- Make unnecessary changes to estate plans or property ownership structures.
- Incur additional legal expenses resolving avoidable misunderstandings about inheritance eligibility.

The available evidence does not establish that Thai Visa Centre has caused an inheritance to be refused, compelled a property sale, or obtained payments through incorrect advice.

Nevertheless, the agency markets property-law referrals and succession-planning coordination while omitting a legally significant interpretation that directly affects the rights of foreign heirs.

**Thai Visa Centre should update its condominium inheritance guidance to distinguish original purchase requirements, inherited Section 19(5) rights, foreign-ownership quota restrictions, and the circumstances in which mandatory disposal provisions actually apply.**

### Distinguishing Four Condominium Acquisition Scenarios

The requirements for a qualifying foreigner to acquire or retain a condominium depend on **how the person qualifies under the Condominium Act and how the unit changes hands**:

1. **Purchase supported by qualifying overseas funds:** A foreign purchaser relying on Section 19(5) must supply the applicable evidence of foreign-currency remittances or other permitted transactions. The purchase remains subject to the building's foreign-ownership limit and ordinary registration requirements.[^249]
2. **Purchase by a foreign permanent resident:** A qualifying permanent resident may rely on Section 19(1) and evidence of permanent residence rather than Section 19(5) remittance documentation; Thai-baht funds may be used. The foreign-ownership quota and other registration rules still apply.[^248][^249]
3. **Foreign-to-foreign resale of an existing foreign-owned unit:** Replacing one qualifying foreign owner with another ordinarily does not consume *additional* foreign-owned floor area, even where the building's ordinary quota is fully allocated. The incoming buyer must nevertheless establish independent eligibility and satisfy Land Office documentation and verification requirements.[^382][^385]
4. **Inheritance by a qualifying foreign heir:** The Land Department's published interpretation permits certain heirs to succeed to the deceased foreign owner's existing Section 19(5) rights without making a new purchase or automatically becoming subject to the one-year disposal rule. That interpretation does not dispense with applicable inheritance documentation, ownership limits, or examination of the particular circumstances.[^317][^319]

**These are not interchangeable transactions.** An ordinary purchase's funding documentation should not automatically be imposed on permanent residents or qualifying heirs; likewise, a foreign-to-foreign resale should not be treated as an acquisition requiring a new, unused slice of the building's quota. None of these distinctions eliminates the need to verify lawful ownership and registration.

### Commercial Significance of the Property Guidance Errors

TVC markets professional property-law referrals, document reviews, and related assistance while publishing statements that could cause foreign buyers or heirs to misunderstand their rights or incur unnecessary steps or costs. The evidence establishes misleading public guidance, **not** that any specific customer paid unnecessary fees, lost property rights, or received incorrect individual advice.

TVC should correct the source guides consistently: distinguish optional legal review from mandatory Land Office requirements, identify the narrow land-ownership exceptions, avoid presenting contingent lease renewals as a secured 90-year right, and explain the different condominium eligibility and transfer scenarios described above.

## Misleading Tax, Accounting, and Corporate Incentive Guidance

Thai Visa Centre publishes inaccurate and sometimes contradictory instructions concerning Thai property-transfer charges, the taxation of foreign-source income, tax-residency documentation, company audits and filing deadlines, and discontinued corporate tax incentives. Though these subjects involve separate statutes and agencies, the errors share a practical consequence: customers may misunderstand obligations, miscalculate costs, or purchase professional assistance on the basis of outdated government procedures.

TVC advertises immigration, relocation, property-related legal referrals, work-permit coordination, and links to accounting or other professional services. The five investigations below retain their distinct legal requirements while examining the reliability of the financial guidance presented alongside those services.

### Property Tax Guides Misstate Exemptions and Calculation Rules

Thai Visa Centre's guide *Thailand Property Transfer Tax and Fees*, marked reviewed June 2026, presents incomplete guidance about when sellers owe Thailand's 3.3% Specific Business Tax (SBT).[^256]

Under its exemption checklist, TVC identifies individual sellers who owned property for more than five years and used it as their primary residence.

Its separate *Property Transfer Taxes and Fees in Thailand* guide also presents an overly broad explanation of how the Land Office calculates taxes and registration fees.[^257]

**These instructions obscure important distinctions that can materially affect the cost of selling Thai property.**

#### The Five-Year and Principal-Residence Rules Are Separate

Thailand's Revenue Department identifies ordinary private property sales made within five years of acquisition as potentially subject to Specific Business Tax, subject to statutory exceptions.[^258]

Two important situations must be distinguished:

1. **Property held beyond five years:** An ordinary private sale generally falls outside the five-year SBT provision, without a separate requirement that the property was the seller's primary residence.
2. **Qualifying principal residence:** Even within five years, an eligible individual may qualify for an exception where the property was used as a principal residence and the seller was named in its house registration for at least one year after acquisition.

The second exception is expressly recognized in Thailand's official government guidance.[^259]

These rules are not universally applicable to every transaction. Developer sales, sales by corporate entities, and other transactions classified as commercial property sales may remain subject to different treatment.

**TVC's checklist presents independent tax considerations together without explaining that satisfying both is not generally necessary.**

#### The Land Office Does Not Use One Valuation Rule for Every Charge

TVC's property-tax FAQ also states:

> Land office uses government appraised value or declared price, whichever is higher.

This is an incomplete description of Thailand's transfer-tax system.[^257]

The Department of Lands distinguishes the calculation bases:

| Charge | Standard calculation basis |
|---|---|
| Transfer registration fee | 2% of government-assessed value |
| Specific Business Tax, where applicable | 3.3% of the higher of assessed value or declared sale price |
| Stamp duty, where applicable | 0.5% of the higher value |
| Individual seller's withholding tax | Calculation using assessed value and applicable income-tax rules |
| Corporate seller's withholding tax | Generally 1% of the higher value |

The Department of Lands publishes these distinctions in its official guidance.[^260]

Certain transactions may qualify for reduced transfer-registration rates or other exceptions.

TVC correctly identifies some individual charges elsewhere in its guides, but its general explanation does not clearly distinguish their respective tax bases.

#### Financial Consequences for Customers

The errors matter because SBT can represent a substantial expense when property changes hands.

A seller relying on the combined exemption checklist might incorrectly believe that an investment property held for more than five years remains subject to SBT because it was never their primary residence.

Conversely, someone selling a qualifying principal residence within five years might overlook the available exemption.

Incorrect assumptions about assessed value and sale price can also distort transfer-fee estimates.

TVC advertises property-related legal referrals, document preparation, and coordination for foreigners buying or selling Thai real estate.

The available evidence does not establish that TVC has caused customers to overpay taxes or submitted incorrect declarations to the Land Office.

Nevertheless, **its published guidance fails to distinguish important statutory exemptions and calculation methods that could materially affect property-transfer costs**.

TVC should explain the separate five-year and principal-residence rules, identify transactions that remain taxable, and distinguish the valuation bases applicable to registration fees, SBT, stamp duty, and withholding tax.

### Personal Income Tax Guide Repeats Foreign-Remittance Rule Abolished in 2024

Thai Visa Centre's guide *Personal Income Tax Rates in Thailand*, marked reviewed June 2026, incorrectly describes the taxation of foreign-source income brought into Thailand by tax residents.[^261]

Under its explanation of who pays personal income tax, TVC states that residents are taxed on foreign income:

> remitted to Thailand in the year earned.

The guide's FAQ repeats the same limitation.

**This wording reflects an obsolete interpretation of Thailand's Revenue Code that ceased to apply to qualifying income earned from January 1, 2024.**

#### Revenue Department Removed the Same-Year Restriction

Revenue Department Order P.161/2566, issued September 15, 2023, changed the administrative interpretation of Section 41, paragraph two, of the Revenue Code.[^262]

The order requires qualifying foreign-source income to be included in the calculation of Thai personal income tax when brought into Thailand, regardless of whether remittance occurs during the year of receipt or a subsequent year.

Order P.162/2566, issued November 20, 2023, clarified that this interpretation does not apply to income arising before January 1, 2024.[^263]

The distinction is important:

| Foreign income | General treatment under the revised guidance |
|---|---|
| Earned before January 1, 2024 | Outside the revised remittance rule |
| Earned from 2024 onward while a Thai tax resident, remitted that year | Potentially taxable |
| Earned from 2024 onward while a Thai tax resident, remitted in a later year | Potentially taxable |

Thailand's Revenue Department explicitly confirms these distinctions in its published guidance for foreigners.[^264]

The applicable residence test generally concerns spending at least 180 days in Thailand during the calendar year in which the income arises.

Tax treaties, exemptions, foreign tax credits, and the nature of the funds may affect the final tax liability.

**Simply delaying a remittance into a subsequent calendar year no longer automatically removes qualifying foreign income from Thai taxation.**

#### Official 2026 Guidance Confirms the Rule

PwC Thailand's *Thai Tax 2026/27 Booklet* independently confirms that assessable foreign income earned from January 1, 2024, may be taxable when remitted in the same year or a later year.[^265]

The Revenue Department's own foreign-income guidance reaches the same conclusion.[^264]

TVC's personal-income-tax guide therefore continues presenting an outdated restriction more than two years after the underlying administrative change.

Its June 2026 review date makes the discrepancy particularly notable.

#### TVC's Own Law Archive Contradicts Its Commercial Tax Guide

The error is especially clear when compared with TVC's own separate *Foreign-sourced income & tax residency* law archive.[^637] That archive explicitly discusses Revenue Department Orders P.161/2566 and P.162/2566 and explains that qualifying foreign-source assessable income earned from January 1, 2024, may be taxed when a Thai tax resident remits it in a subsequent calendar year.

By contrast, TVC's *Personal Income Tax Rates in Thailand* guide—marked reviewed June 2026—continues to say that residents may owe personal income tax on foreign income remitted **in the year earned**, repeating the limitation in its FAQ.[^261]

**TVC thus publishes conflicting descriptions of the same foreign-income rule on its commercial guidance page and its own legal-reference archive.** The correct legal treatment still depends on the taxpayer's residence in the relevant year, the type and timing of income, applicable exemptions and treaty provisions, and the evidence supporting a remittance. The contradiction does not establish that TVC prepared incorrect returns or caused any customer to underpay tax.

#### Potential Consequences for Foreign Residents

The error could materially affect retirees, foreign employees, business owners, and other long-term residents maintaining income or investments overseas.

For example, a qualifying taxpayer who earned foreign investment income in 2025 while resident in Thailand might incorrectly assume that transferring those earnings in 2026 avoids Thai tax.

The revised rule does not support that assumption.

Incorrect planning could lead to omitted assessable income, inaccurate personal income tax returns, additional assessments, interest, or penalties.

Not every international transfer is taxable income. Previously accumulated savings, qualifying pre-2024 income, exempt funds, and applicable treaty relief must be assessed separately.

#### Relevance to Thai Visa Centre's Services

Thai Visa Centre advertises immigration, relocation, work-permit, and tax-document coordination for foreigners living in Thailand.

Although it states that it is not a tax-accounting firm and refers tax preparation to qualified professionals, its published guidance addresses tax residency and foreign-income remittances.

The available evidence does not establish that TVC has prepared incorrect tax returns, advised individual customers to defer remittances, or caused anyone to incur tax penalties.

Nevertheless, **its June 2026 guide repeats a superseded interpretation of foreign-income taxation that could materially influence customers' financial planning**.

TVC should remove the same-year limitation, explain Orders P.161/2566 and P.162/2566, distinguish pre-2024 income from later earnings, and clarify that the tax treatment depends on residence status in the year the income arises and the applicable remittance rules.

### Tax Residency Certificate Guide Repeatedly Identifies the Wrong Government Form

Thai Visa Centre's *Tax Residency Certificates in Thailand: Form 205 Guide*, marked reviewed June 2026, repeatedly identifies **Form 205** as the official document used to apply for or establish Thai tax residency.[^320]

The guide prominently displays Form 205 in its summary, describes it as the Revenue Department's tax residency certificate, and instructs both individual and corporate applicants to submit the same numbered form.

Its application checklist and frequently asked questions repeat this designation.

**These instructions contradict the Revenue Department's published application procedures and certificate classifications.**

#### Revenue Department Identifies R.O.01 and R.O.22

Thailand's Revenue Department distinguishes between the application used to request an English-language tax certificate and the certificate subsequently issued.

Revenue Department Region 2 identifies:

- **R.O.01 (ร.อ.01):** The application form used to request English-language tax certificates.
- **R.O.21 (ร.อ.21):** Income Tax Payment Certificate.
- **R.O.22 (ร.อ.22):** Certificate of Residence for Thai tax purposes.[^321]

The Revenue Department's Region 1 office independently instructs applicants requesting a Certificate of Residence to submit **R.O.01**, specifying the country in which the certificate will be used.[^322]

Its published procedures identify supporting documents for individual and corporate applicants.

The Revenue Department's English-language website also confirms that tax residence certificates are issued upon written application with supporting documents to the responsible Revenue Office.[^323]

None of these official instructions identifies Form 205 as the application or certificate required for Thai tax residency confirmation.

**TVC's use of Form 205 is therefore unsupported by the published Revenue Department procedures.**

#### Application and Certificate Are Different Documents

The distinction is important because applying for a certificate and receiving one involve separate administrative steps.

An applicant ordinarily submits R.O.01 with supporting evidence, after which the Revenue Department may issue an R.O.22 Certificate of Residence if the applicable requirements are satisfied.

The department's published instructions identify documentation that can include:

- Relevant personal or corporate income tax returns.
- Tax payment receipts.
- Identification or passport documentation.
- Evidence of physical presence in Thailand for individual applicants.
- The foreign treaty country for which the certificate is requested.
- Additional records required by the responsible Revenue Office.

Revenue Department Region 2 specifically requests an individual's passport entry-and-exit records and a travel-history summary for the relevant tax year.[^321]

Corporate applicants face different supporting-document requirements, including corporate registration and tax filings.

TVC's guide does not clearly explain these official distinctions.

Instead, it repeatedly presents Form 205 as though the same numbered document serves both the application and certification functions.

#### Treaty Benefits Depend on Correct Documentation

Thai tax residency certificates can be important when individuals or companies claim relief under double-taxation agreements.

A certificate may be requested by a foreign tax authority, employer, bank, investment institution, or other payer when determining eligibility for treaty benefits.

Applicants following incorrect form instructions could:

- Search for or attempt to submit a form that is not identified in the Revenue Department's published procedure.
- Prepare incomplete applications or omit required supporting documents.
- Confuse the application with the actual certificate issued.
- Experience delays obtaining documentation needed for foreign tax filings or treaty-based withholding relief.
- Incur unnecessary professional or administrative expenses correcting paperwork.

The consequences may be particularly significant when a taxpayer faces filing deadlines or needs a certificate to support reduced foreign withholding taxes.

#### Relevance to TVC's Professional Services

Thai Visa Centre markets immigration coordination, relocation planning, and related administrative guidance to foreign residents.

Its tax residency guide directs readers toward its immigration services while explaining how tax documentation intersects with long-term residence and international financial obligations.

The available evidence does not establish that Thai Visa Centre has submitted invalid tax certificate applications, caused rejected filings, or collected fees for nonexistent forms.

Nevertheless, the agency presents an incorrect or unsupported form designation throughout a guide specifically intended to explain the official application procedure.

**TVC should replace its repeated references to Form 205 with the Revenue Department's documented R.O.01 application and R.O.22 Certificate of Residence, clearly distinguish the two documents, and provide accurate supporting-document instructions for individuals and companies.**

### Corporate Compliance Guides Misstate Mandatory Audits and Tax Deadlines

Thai Visa Centre's guides *Accounting and Tax Compliance in Thailand* and *Corporate Tax in Thailand*, both marked reviewed June 2026, contain incorrect instructions concerning mandatory audits, corporate tax prepayments, and social-security contributions.[^266][^267]

These discrepancies are significant because TVC advertises company-registration coordination, work-permit assistance, and referrals to accounting professionals.

#### Thai Limited Companies Are Not Exempt From Annual Audits Based on Size

TVC's accounting guide answers its FAQ about mandatory audits by stating that the requirement depends on company size and that many startups are initially exempt.[^266]

Its corporate-tax guide similarly advises:

> Companies meeting size thresholds must appoint an auditor

It subsequently suggests that small companies may follow simpler requirements.[^267]

**This is incorrect for ordinary Thai private limited companies.**

The Board of Investment's official accounting guidance requires limited companies to prepare annual financial statements audited by an authorized auditor.[^268]

The relevant small-business exemption applies to qualifying Thai registered partnerships meeting specified capital, revenue, and asset thresholds.

It does not establish a general audit exemption for newly incorporated or small private limited companies.

Consequently, a newly registered limited company cannot dispense with its annual statutory audit merely because it has few employees, modest assets, limited revenue, or has not yet begun significant operations.

#### TVC Also Misstates the Annual Filing Schedule

TVC's accounting guide combines the statutory audit, corporate income tax return, and Department of Business Development (DBD) financial-statement submission under a single deadline of 150 days after the accounting year ends.[^266]

However, the Board of Investment identifies separate obligations for private limited companies:[^269]

1. Arrange the annual shareholders' meeting to approve audited financial statements within four months after the financial year ends.
2. Submit the approved audited statements and supporting documents to the commercial registrar within one month after that meeting.
3. File the annual corporate income tax return, Form PND.50, with the Revenue Department within 150 days after the accounting year ends.[^270]

These deadlines are related but should not be presented as identical.

The DBD deadline follows shareholder approval, whereas PND.50 has its own statutory filing period.

#### PND.51 Is Not Payable in Two Midyear Installments

TVC's accounting guide describes PND.51 corporate income tax prepayments as occurring twice yearly.[^266]

Its corporate-tax guide similarly describes two midyear installments based on estimated profits, normally due around months six and eleven.[^267]

Thailand's Revenue Department prescribes a different arrangement.

For companies subject to the ordinary net-profit taxation system, Form PND.51 is a **single half-year prepayment** calculated using the applicable estimate of annual taxable profits.[^270]

The filing and payment are generally due within two months after the first six months of the accounting period.

The prepayment is subsequently credited against the company's annual tax liability when it files PND.50.

**There is no standard second PND.51 installment in the eleventh month.**

This distinction matters for cash-flow forecasting and scheduling corporate tax payments.

#### Social-Security Payments Follow a Separate Deadline

TVC's accounting FAQ states that withholding tax, VAT, and social-security contributions are due by the seventh day of the following month, or the fifteenth when filed electronically.[^266]

However, Thailand's Social Security Office operates under a separate statutory contribution framework.

Official Board of Investment guidance confirms that employers must remit monthly employer and employee social-security contributions by the fifteenth day of the following month.[^271]

The fifteenth is the ordinary contribution deadline, not merely an electronic-filing extension from the seventh.

Different Revenue Department forms also have different filing schedules, so combining all three obligations under one generalized deadline is misleading.

#### Consequences for Foreign Business Owners

Incorrect compliance guidance could expose foreign entrepreneurs to avoidable problems.

Founders relying on the supposed small-company audit exemption could fail to engage an auditor, obtain shareholder approval of audited accounts, or submit the required financial statements.

Incorrect PND.51 schedules could distort corporate cash-flow planning, while unclear contribution deadlines could complicate payroll administration.

These issues are especially relevant to foreigners maintaining Thai companies as employers for Non-Immigrant B visas and work permits.

The available evidence does not establish that TVC has instructed specific customers to skip mandatory audits, caused late filings, or prepared incorrect corporate tax returns.

TVC also states that independent accountants handle actual tax filings and financial statements.

Nevertheless, **its published corporate-compliance guidance gives incorrect explanations of several fundamental obligations while advertising professional coordination for foreign business owners**.

TVC should remove the supposed general startup audit exemption, distinguish limited companies from qualifying registered partnerships, correct the PND.51 schedule, and separate DBD, Revenue Department, and Social Security Office filing deadlines.

### Regional Operating Headquarters Guide Advertises Tax Incentives Abolished Years Earlier

Thai Visa Centre's *Regional Operating Headquarters (ROH) in Thailand: Tax and Visa Guide*, marked reviewed June 2026, presents Thailand's former Regional Operating Headquarters program as an active tax-incentive scheme available to businesses.[^334]

The publication describes ROH as a status granted by the Revenue Department, outlines corporate and personal income tax benefits, and provides a six-step application process culminating in government approval.

Its frequently asked questions explicitly tell readers that newly incorporated Thai companies can apply for ROH status if they satisfy the relevant capital, staffing, and service requirements.

The guide also advertises Thai Visa Centre's assistance with visas and work permits for executives employed under approved ROH arrangements.

**These instructions present a discontinued tax-incentive regime as though new companies could still obtain approval in 2026.**

#### Thailand Replaced ROH With the International Business Centre Regime

Thailand originally established Regional Operating Headquarters incentives to encourage multinational businesses to locate management and support functions in the country.

The government subsequently introduced International Headquarters incentives in 2015 and replaced the earlier arrangements with the International Business Centre (IBC) regime in 2018.

Thailand's official government website confirms that the Revenue Department introduced IBC tax incentives to replace the former ROH, International Headquarters (IHQ), and International Trading Centre (ITC) programs.[^335]

New registrations under the old ROH arrangements had already ceased:

- New ROH2 applications stopped being accepted in November 2015.
- New ROH1 applications stopped being accepted in October 2018.
- The government subsequently revoked major remaining ROH tax incentives in 2019.

Grant Thornton Thailand documented the March 2019 Cabinet decision withdrawing the old regimes' corporate income tax concessions effective June 1, 2019, and their expatriate personal income tax reductions effective January 1, 2020.[^336]

**TVC's instructions for obtaining new ROH tax status are therefore incompatible with the government's replacement of that program years before its purported June 2026 review.**

#### BOI Directs New Headquarters Projects Toward IBC

Thailand's Board of Investment provides guidance addressing businesses previously promoted under ROH or IHQ arrangements.[^337]

It identifies IBC as the successor framework and explains that businesses seeking the relevant new tax incentives must satisfy IBC requirements.

The BOI also distinguishes between companies retaining previously granted investment-promotion rights and those seeking new approval under the replacement program.

This distinction is important.

Discontinuation of new ROH registrations does not mean every company historically operating as an ROH immediately lost its legal existence, ordinary business permissions, or unrelated investment-promotion rights.

However, those historical arrangements do not establish a currently available ROH application pathway for newly incorporated companies.

**TVC makes no meaningful distinction between legacy ROH operations and applications for incentives under the replacement IBC framework.**

#### IBC Has Different Eligibility and Tax Requirements

The International Business Centre regime is not merely the old ROH program operating under a different name.

The applicable IBC requirements include conditions concerning:

- Minimum paid-up capital.
- Qualified full-time personnel.
- Eligible services provided to associated enterprises.
- Qualifying expenditure incurred in Thailand.
- Separate approval and compliance requirements for relevant tax incentives.

PwC's published Thailand corporate-tax guidance identifies reduced corporate income tax rates of 8%, 5%, or 3% for qualifying IBC income, depending on annual operating expenditure and other conditions.[^338]

It also explains that companies converting from certain historical ROH or IHQ arrangements may qualify for special transitional treatment.

These incentives differ materially from the historical ROH benefits advertised in TVC's guide.

Consequently, companies following TVC's ROH checklist could misunderstand both the relevant application authority and the financial qualifications necessary to obtain available tax benefits.

#### TVC Advertises Professional Immigration Coordination for the Obsolete Program

The discrepancy extends beyond historical corporate-tax commentary.

TVC's guide tells readers that it assists ROH executives and specialists with Non-Immigrant B visas and work permits.

It also instructs applicants to ensure their work-permit descriptions match activities approved under their ROH status.

Foreign executives employed by legitimate Thai businesses may still qualify for appropriate visas and work permits.

Likewise, companies previously granted ROH-related permissions may have continuing obligations or surviving non-tax rights.

However, **these possibilities do not make the discontinued ROH tax-approval program available to new applicants.**

TVC should distinguish ordinary employment authorization and legacy corporate permissions from incentives newly obtainable through the IBC framework.

#### Financial Implications for Multinational Businesses

Incorrect advice concerning investment incentives can have substantial financial consequences.

Businesses considering Thailand as a regional headquarters may make decisions involving significant capital, hiring commitments, office leases, professional fees, and multinational tax planning.

Companies relying on TVC's outdated ROH instructions could:

- Pursue an application process that is no longer available to new entrants.
- Budget for historical tax concessions that cannot be newly obtained under the former regime.
- Misunderstand the expenditure and staffing thresholds for IBC incentives.
- Engage consultants or prepare corporate documents for the wrong government program.
- Delay investment decisions while attempting to resolve an obsolete approval procedure.

The available evidence does not establish that Thai Visa Centre has submitted invalid ROH applications, collected fees for nonexistent government approvals, or caused financial losses to corporate customers.

Nevertheless, the agency presents detailed instructions for an abolished tax-incentive arrangement while advertising professional visa and work-permit coordination for businesses using that structure.

**Thai Visa Centre should withdraw its ROH application instructions for new companies, identify the historical termination of the program, distinguish legacy approvals from currently available incentives, and direct prospective headquarters investors toward the applicable IBC framework.**

### Commercial Significance of the Financial-Guidance Errors

The findings concern different kinds of compliance: transfer taxes depend on the transaction and the applicable tax base; personal income tax on foreign-source remittances depends on residency, timing, and exemptions; residency certificates require the proper Revenue Department procedure; ordinary limited companies have statutory audit and filing obligations; and newly established headquarters cannot obtain incentives through an obsolete ROH application route.

**Treating these requirements as interchangeable or repeating superseded instructions risks misdirecting both individual residents and foreign-owned businesses.** TVC should reconcile its commercial guides with its own legal archive and current government instructions, identify which legal rules apply to each customer situation, and avoid implying that optional professional services substitute for correct filing procedures. The evidence documents published inaccuracies and inconsistencies, not actual unnecessary payments, tax penalties, or incorrect applications by individual customers.

## Misleading Company Dissolution, Liquidation, and Insolvency Procedures

Thai Visa Centre's company-dissolution and bankruptcy guides describe closely connected but legally distinct procedures affecting foreign business owners, creditors, directors, and employees. The former misstates the sequence and deadlines for closing a private limited company, while the latter conflates ordinary bankruptcy and business rehabilitation.[^374][^354]

The connection between the guides is particularly important when liquidators discover that a company's assets cannot satisfy its debts. Thailand's Civil and Commercial Code establishes a specific duty to seek bankruptcy in qualifying circumstances, rather than allowing ordinary liquidation to continue as though the company could settle all its obligations.[^378][^639]

### Company-Dissolution Guide Misstates Shareholder Meetings and Statutory Filing Deadlines

Thai Visa Centre's *Company Dissolution in Thailand: Liquidation and Deregistration Guide*, marked reviewed June 2026, presents an incorrect sequence for voluntarily dissolving a Thai private limited company.[^374]

Under its section *Stage 1: Register Dissolution*, the guide instructs company owners to:

1. Hold a shareholders' meeting approving a special resolution to dissolve.
2. Hold a second meeting to confirm the resolution and appoint liquidators.
3. Register the dissolution with the Department of Business Development (DBD).
4. Begin liquidation.

The publication does not identify the statutory 14-day deadline for the initial DBD registration or the corresponding deadlines for notifying creditors.

It also repeatedly suggests that the company becomes legally dissolved only after the final liquidation registration.

**These instructions confuse the shareholder resolution that dissolves a company, the subsequent winding-up process, and the registration that completes liquidation.**

#### Thailand Abolished the Two-Meeting Special Resolution Requirement in 2008

Section 1194 of Thailand's Civil and Commercial Code establishes the voting threshold for special resolutions.[^375]

Under the current provision, a special resolution requires approval by at least three-quarters of the votes of shareholders present and entitled to vote at a properly convened general meeting.

The statute no longer requires two successive shareholders' meetings to approve the same special resolution.

The historical distinction is important.

Before the amendments to the Civil and Commercial Code enacted in 2008, companies generally needed two separate shareholders' meetings to pass a special resolution.

The first required approval by a three-quarters majority. A second meeting, held within the prescribed period, then confirmed the resolution.

Thailand abolished this arrangement through the Civil and Commercial Code Amendment Act (No. 18), which took effect on July 1, 2008.[^376]

Price Sanond's contemporary legal analysis expressly confirms that special resolutions could thereafter be approved at a single shareholders' meeting.

**TVC's requirement for a separate meeting to confirm the dissolution resolution resembles a procedure eliminated nearly two decades before the guide's claimed June 2026 review.**

The company's articles of association may impose additional governance requirements, and properly convened meetings remain essential.

However, those possibilities do not establish a general statutory obligation to repeat the dissolution vote.

#### Dissolution Registration Must Occur Within 14 Days

The more consequential omission concerns the first statutory filing deadline.

Section 1236(4) of the Civil and Commercial Code identifies a special resolution to dissolve as a legal ground for dissolution.[^377]

Section 1254 subsequently requires the liquidators to register the company's dissolution and their names within **14 days after the dissolution date**.[^378]

Thailand's Department of Business Development publishes official procedures confirming this deadline.[^379]

The DBD identifies the following initial sequence:

1. Convene a properly notified shareholders' meeting.
2. Pass the special resolution approving dissolution.
3. Prepare and submit the dissolution-registration application.
4. Publish the required dissolution notice and notify known creditors.
5. Complete the initial registration and notification requirements within the applicable 14-day period.

This is not merely an administrative preference.

The deadline arises from the Civil and Commercial Code itself.

A company cannot safely postpone initial dissolution registration while arranging an unnecessary second meeting to reconfirm the original resolution.

**TVC gives the correct general instruction to register dissolution but omits the deadline and places an additional meeting before that registration.**

#### Creditor Notifications Have Their Own 14-Day Deadline

Section 1253 imposes separate duties on the liquidator following dissolution.[^378]

Within the statutory period, the liquidator must:

- Publish notice of the company's dissolution in the prescribed manner.
- Notify known creditors through registered correspondence.
- Inform creditors that claims should be submitted to the liquidator for payment.

The DBD's published instructions confirm that these creditor-notification steps must be completed within **14 days of dissolution**.[^379]

These notices matter because liquidation is not simply the process of closing bank accounts and distributing assets to shareholders.

The liquidator must identify liabilities, collect receivables, settle creditor claims, and preserve funds required to discharge the company's obligations.

TVC warns against distributing assets before the creditor-notification period has been completed.

However, its published checklist does not clearly explain when the notification obligation begins or how it relates to the company's dissolution date.

**A guide claiming to describe company closure in strict chronological order should identify these mandatory deadlines at the start of the process, rather than focusing almost exclusively on the final registration deadline.**

#### The Later Shareholders' Meeting Serves a Different Purpose

The existence of a later shareholders' meeting requires careful distinction.

Sections 1255 and 1256 of the Civil and Commercial Code require liquidators to prepare an opening liquidation balance sheet, arrange an audit, and convene a general meeting.[^378]

That meeting serves specific purposes:

- Adopting the audited balance sheet.
- Confirming the directors as liquidators or appointing replacements.
- Considering additional directions relating to the winding-up of the company.

It is therefore possible for a company to have a shareholders' meeting after the original dissolution resolution.

**But this meeting is not a second vote legally required to confirm the original dissolution resolution before DBD registration.**

The Department of Business Development documents the liquidation meeting separately from the procedure for registering the company's dissolution.[^380]

This distinction reconciles the statutory provisions without incorrectly suggesting that all subsequent shareholder meetings are unnecessary.

A company may also need annual meetings while liquidation remains unfinished and a final meeting to approve the completed liquidation accounts.

Those meetings have different statutory purposes and should not be combined into one generic confirmation requirement.

#### Dissolution and Completion of Liquidation Are Different Legal Events

TVC's guide states that a company is not fully dissolved after the first shareholders' resolution.

It later claims that the company becomes legally dissolved once the final liquidation registration is completed.

This wording conflates dissolution with the termination of the company's remaining legal existence.

Section 1236 establishes the events that cause a company to be dissolved, including a valid special resolution.[^377]

Section 1249 separately provides that a dissolved company is deemed to continue in existence insofar as necessary for liquidation.[^378]

Consequently:

1. **Dissolution:** The company enters the winding-up process following the legally effective dissolution event.
2. **Liquidation:** Its legal existence continues for the limited purposes of settling debts, realizing assets, and completing statutory obligations.
3. **Completion:** The final liquidation accounts are approved and completion is registered, ending the winding-up process.

Section 1270 governs the final meeting and registration of completed liquidation.[^378]

The company therefore does not remain an ordinary operating company until the final registration.

Nor does its legal personality necessarily disappear immediately upon dissolution.

**The distinction is between a dissolved company continuing for liquidation and a company whose liquidation has been completed.**

TVC should use those terms consistently rather than suggesting that the special resolution has no legal dissolving effect until the entire liquidation is finished.

#### Insolvent Liquidation Requires an Immediate Bankruptcy Application

TVC's dissolution checklist correctly includes calling unpaid share capital and applying to the bankruptcy court if company assets are insufficient to pay debts. However, it does not explain the statutory conditions or urgency of that obligation.[^374]

Section 1265 of the Civil and Commercial Code permits liquidators to call outstanding capital contributions. Section 1266 requires them to apply immediately to the court for bankruptcy if assets still cannot meet liabilities after all contributions or shares have been fully paid.[^378][^639]

This is not simply an optional strategy for directors deciding whether to close or reorganize a business. It is a specific statutory duty imposed on the liquidators in the circumstances defined by Section 1266.

Section 1269 also limits distributions to shareholders to property not required to discharge the company's obligations.[^378]

**A company undergoing voluntary dissolution cannot treat final deregistration or distribution of shareholder assets as a substitute for the bankruptcy procedure where the statutory conditions require a liquidator's application.**

This provides the legal connection between the dissolution procedure and the separate bankruptcy investigation below.

#### Potential Financial and Compliance Consequences

The errors could matter for foreign entrepreneurs, directors, shareholders, and creditors involved in closing Thai businesses.

A company owner relying on TVC's instructions might:

- Schedule an unnecessary confirmation meeting before filing with the DBD.
- Miss the 14-day dissolution-registration deadline.
- Delay the statutory publication and creditor-notification requirements.
- Misunderstand when directors become liquidators and which powers they retain.
- Confuse initial dissolution registration with registration of completed liquidation.
- Make incorrect assumptions about ongoing contracts, employee obligations, or corporate authority.
- Incur avoidable accounting, legal, or administrative expenses.

These risks are particularly relevant where foreign employees and directors also hold Non-Immigrant B visas or work permits linked to the business.

Company dissolution, termination of employment, and immigration permissions do not necessarily take effect at identical times.

Each requires separate review under the applicable rules.

### Bankruptcy Guide Conflates Liquidation, Rehabilitation, and Automatic Stay Protections

Thai Visa Centre's *Bankruptcy: Guide for Foreigners*, marked reviewed June 2026, presents Thailand's bankruptcy and business rehabilitation procedures as parts of a generalized insolvency process.[^354]

Its procedural overview states that either a creditor or debtor files a petition with the Central Bankruptcy Court, followed by an absolute receivership order, creditor meetings, and either reorganization or liquidation.

The guide also prominently claims that filing triggers a stay on most creditor enforcement while a receiver or planner administers the estate. Elsewhere, its table and FAQ instead describe enforcement restrictions arising after a bankruptcy order.[^354]

**These explanations fail to distinguish ordinary bankruptcy liquidation from business rehabilitation, which have different filing rights, legal requirements, and consequences for creditors.**

#### Ordinary Bankruptcy Is Generally Initiated by Creditors

Section 9 of Thailand's Bankruptcy Act B.E. 2483 (1940) establishes the ordinary bankruptcy petition procedure.[^355]

Under the provision, a creditor may seek bankruptcy proceedings where:

- The debtor is insolvent.
- An individual debtor owes at least 1 million baht to one or more petitioning creditors.
- A juristic-person debtor owes at least 2 million baht to one or more petitioning creditors.
- The relevant debt is definite and legally determinable.

The court must examine the statutory conditions before ordering absolute receivership under Section 14.

**An insolvent individual cannot ordinarily initiate voluntary personal bankruptcy merely by submitting a petition to obtain protection from creditors.**

Chambers and Partners confirms that ordinary Thai bankruptcy proceedings are creditor-initiated rather than a voluntary bankruptcy procedure available to debtors.[^357]

The statutory liquidator application described above is a separate exception: Section 1266 requires immediate application when liabilities remain greater than assets after all contributions or shares have been paid up.[^639]

That exception should not be confused with a general right for individuals or company directors to initiate ordinary bankruptcy proceedings.

TVC's generalized petition-filing sequence fails to explain these distinctions.

#### Business Rehabilitation Uses a Separate Statutory Procedure

Sections 90/2, 90/3, and 90/4 of the Bankruptcy Act establish a separate procedure for business rehabilitation.[^356]

Unlike ordinary bankruptcy liquidation, qualifying rehabilitation proceedings may be initiated by:

1. The debtor.
2. One or more qualifying creditors.
3. Authorized government supervisory authorities in specified circumstances.

Under the ordinary corporate rehabilitation framework, Section 90/3 provides for a minimum indebtedness of 10 million baht and requires reasonable grounds and prospects for successfully rehabilitating the business.

Separate rules may apply to qualifying small and medium-sized enterprises.

The objective of rehabilitation is to preserve a viable business while restructuring its financial obligations under court supervision.

Ordinary bankruptcy instead focuses on receivership, realizing the debtor's assets, and distributing proceeds among creditors.

KPMG's May 2026 analysis independently identifies the debtor, creditors, and relevant government authorities as potential rehabilitation petitioners, subject to the statutory conditions.[^358]

**A rehabilitation petition filed by a company is not equivalent to an ordinary bankruptcy petition filed against that company.**

TVC's guide acknowledges that rehabilitation can preserve a business, but its combined procedural explanation does not adequately distinguish the separate statutory pathways.

#### Automatic Stay Does Not Arise From Every Bankruptcy Filing

The discrepancy is particularly significant in TVC's description of creditor enforcement.

The guide's summary prominently states that filing triggers a stay on most collection activity, while its comparison table and FAQ tie the restriction to a bankruptcy order and its process overview refers to an order being issued.[^354]

These descriptions give different answers about the event that triggers protection. The guide does distinguish rehabilitation from liquidation in its FAQ, but it does not consistently identify which order and which statutory procedure govern the respective enforcement restrictions.

However, Section 90/12 of the Bankruptcy Act specifically establishes automatic-stay protections in business rehabilitation proceedings.[^356]

The protections arise when the court issues an order accepting the rehabilitation petition for consideration, subject to the statutory limitations and exceptions.

They can restrict:

- New civil litigation against the debtor concerning qualifying obligations.
- Continuation of certain pending proceedings.
- Enforcement against the debtor's property.
- Enforcement of specified security interests without the necessary authorization.
- Certain transfers, disposals, and payments by the debtor.

Thailand's Legal Execution Department independently illustrates this sequence in its official business rehabilitation flowchart.[^359]

**The legally significant event is the court's acceptance of the rehabilitation petition, not merely a debtor or creditor preparing or submitting paperwork.**

Ordinary bankruptcy proceedings operate differently.

The filing of a creditor's bankruptcy petition does not automatically create the broad rehabilitation moratorium under Section 90/12.

The court may impose specific provisional measures where statutory conditions are met. A receivership order gives the official receiver statutory authority over the debtor's assets under Section 22; an absolute receivership order also triggers the separate bankruptcy claims procedure.

These measures are legally distinct from the automatic stay applicable during business rehabilitation.

TVC's generalized descriptions risk obscuring that distinction.

#### Receivership and Rehabilitation Planning Are Different Stages

The guide's procedural sequence also places absolute receivership before discussions of either rehabilitation or asset distribution.

This presentation conflates the administration of a bankrupt estate with the appointment and functions of a rehabilitation plan preparer.

In ordinary bankruptcy, the official receiver assumes statutory authority over the debtor's assets following the relevant court order.

Business rehabilitation follows a different framework involving the court, official receiver, appointed plan preparer, creditors, and subsequent plan administration.

The court-supervised rehabilitation procedure can allow a qualifying debtor business to continue operating while its debts are restructured.

The existence of a bankruptcy petition does not mean a company automatically proceeds through absolute receivership and then chooses between liquidation and rehabilitation.

**These are different statutory processes, not interchangeable outcomes of a single filing sequence.**

#### Financial Consequences for Foreign Creditors and Business Owners

The distinction matters for foreign entrepreneurs, investors, directors, lenders, and employees involved with financially distressed Thai businesses.

A company owner relying on TVC's guidance could mistakenly believe that submitting an ordinary bankruptcy petition would immediately prevent creditors from taking enforcement action.

A creditor might similarly misunderstand whether a debtor has obtained statutory protection, whether a separate claim can proceed, or when enforcement restrictions take effect.

Potential consequences include:

- Incorrect assumptions about whether a company qualifies for rehabilitation.
- Failure to distinguish voluntary corporate rehabilitation from creditor-initiated bankruptcy.
- Unnecessary litigation or procedural expenses.
- Misunderstanding the enforceability of security interests.
- Mistaken decisions concerning asset transfers, debt repayment, or ongoing operations.
- Delays securing legal advice before insolvency proceedings affect visas and work permits.

These are substantial financial and legal risks, particularly where company assets, employee wages, personal guarantees, or immigration sponsorship depend on the outcome.

### Distinguishing Corporate Closure From Insolvency Proceedings

| Procedure | Legal framework and effect |
| --- | --- |
| Voluntary dissolution | A valid special resolution under Sections 1194 and 1236 triggers dissolution; initial registration and creditor notices follow within 14 days under Sections 1253–1254.[^375][^377][^378] |
| Ordinary liquidation | The dissolved company continues to exist for winding-up purposes under Section 1249. Liquidators settle obligations and register completion under Section 1270.[^378] |
| Insolvent liquidation and bankruptcy | Section 1266 mandates an immediate court application by liquidators if assets remain insufficient after all capital contributions have been paid. Ordinary bankruptcy otherwise generally begins with a qualifying creditor petition under Section 9 of the Bankruptcy Act.[^639][^355][^357] |
| Business rehabilitation | A separate court-supervised restructuring procedure for qualifying businesses, with distinct petitioner eligibility and automatic-stay protections arising on acceptance of the petition under Section 90/12.[^356][^358][^359] |

These procedures are not successive stages of every company closure. A solvent company may complete ordinary liquidation without bankruptcy, while a qualifying distressed business may pursue rehabilitation under the applicable statutory framework.

### Commercial Significance for Foreign Business Owners and Creditors

Thai Visa Centre advertises immigration coordination, work-permit assistance, and referrals to Thai insolvency lawyers for foreign directors and employees affected by company closure or financial distress. Its bankruptcy guide expressly states that it is not a law firm and does not handle bankruptcy court representation.[^374][^354]

The guides should identify the separate statutory events that determine when a company dissolves, when initial filings and creditor notifications are due, when liquidation is completed, when liquidators must seek bankruptcy, and when creditor-enforcement restrictions take effect. In particular, an unnecessary second dissolution vote must not delay initial registration, and the mere filing of an ordinary bankruptcy petition must not be confused with the automatic stay applicable to accepted rehabilitation petitions.

The timing of corporate closure, employment termination, work-permit changes, and immigration permissions also requires separate assessment. These events do not necessarily occur simultaneously.

The available evidence establishes misleading procedural descriptions, not that TVC has caused late filings, failed to notify creditors, filed improper petitions, arranged unnecessary paid procedures, advised customers to disregard enforcement restrictions, or caused financial losses.

**TVC should distinguish the statutory routes for voluntary dissolution, ordinary liquidation, mandatory liquidator bankruptcy applications, creditor-initiated bankruptcy, and qualifying rehabilitation rather than presenting them as interchangeable corporate closure or insolvency procedures.**


## Misleading Court Procedures, Jurisdiction, and Litigant Rights

Thai Visa Centre publishes misleading and sometimes contradictory guidance concerning litigation procedures, court jurisdiction, statutory appeal deadlines, and access to justice in Thailand. Its guides misidentify courts, conflate distinct legal procedures, overstate the need for professional representation, and omit protections affecting consumers, employees, businesses, and class-action claimants.

**These are different areas of law, but they share a recurring problem: TVC offers professionally presented litigation guidance without consistently identifying the correct courts, filing procedures, deadlines, and rights.** The discrepancies are commercially relevant because the agency advertises referrals to litigation lawyers and legal-document assistance.

### Class-Action Guide Misstates Statutory Framework and Opt-Out Rights

Thai Visa Centre's *Class Action Lawsuits: Guide for Foreigners*, marked reviewed June 2026, repeatedly presents Thai class-action litigation as a collection of separate statutory procedures with different rules governing participation.[^360]

Its introductory explanation states that Thailand permits grouped claims under specific legislation, including consumer protection and securities laws, rather than through a general class-action framework.

The publication also claims that opt-in and opt-out requirements vary depending on the governing statute.

Its checklist warns potential claimants about missing opt-in registration deadlines, while its frequently asked questions similarly suggest that participation may require individuals to register before becoming class members.

**These explanations misrepresent Thailand's established civil-procedure framework and confuse automatic class membership with the separate process for claiming compensation after judgment.**

#### Thailand Established a General Class-Action Framework in 2015

Thailand introduced formal class-action proceedings through the Civil Procedure Code Amendment (No. 26) B.E. 2558 (2015).

The amendments inserted Sections 222/1 through 222/49, establishing a general mechanism for representative civil litigation.[^361]

Thailand's Securities and Exchange Commission explains that the initiative originally arose from concerns about securities investors' access to compensation.

However, the Office of the Council of State expanded the framework beyond securities disputes to accommodate multiple claimants suffering harm from torts, contractual breaches, and violations of other laws.[^361]

The resulting mechanism is part of Thailand's Civil Procedure Code rather than a collection of unrelated opt-in schemes.[^366]

The framework can apply to qualifying disputes involving:

- Contractual breaches.
- Wrongful acts and tortious conduct.
- Consumer protection.
- Securities and financial markets.
- Environmental damage.
- Labour rights.
- Trade competition.

The Legal 500's comparative guide independently confirms the broad statutory framework and identifies Section 222/8 as governing the categories of claims potentially suitable for collective proceedings.[^362]

Specialized statutes and procedural rules may affect jurisdiction, remedies, and the handling of individual disputes.

Nevertheless, **the existence of specialized legislation does not eliminate the general class-action provisions of the Civil Procedure Code.**

TVC's guide fails to explain this distinction.

#### Class Membership Is Generally Automatic, Not Opt-In

The more consequential discrepancy concerns participation rights.

Thailand's Civil Procedure Code establishes an **opt-out** class-action system.

Once the court certifies a class, qualifying persons falling within its definition are generally included without having to submit individual applications to join.

Section 222/15 establishes notice requirements following certification.[^363]

These include notification of known class members, public notice, and an explanation of the period during which affected persons may elect to leave the class.

Section 222/16 grants class members the right to opt out by submitting written notice to the court within the prescribed period.[^364]

The ordinary opt-out period cannot be shorter than 45 days.

Class members who do not exercise that right generally remain bound by the resulting judgment.

Thailand's Securities and Exchange Commission explicitly confirms that qualifying affected persons become class members by operation of law and must notify the court if they wish to exclude themselves.[^361]

**This directly contradicts TVC's repeated implication that prospective class members must determine whether their particular statute requires them to opt in before participating.**

The distinction is fundamental.

Failure to join an opt-in proceeding ordinarily means that a person remains outside the participating group.

Failure to opt out of Thailand's general class-action procedure can instead mean that the person remains included and bound by the court's decision.

Those outcomes have materially different legal consequences.

#### Compensation Claims Are Not Applications to Join the Class

TVC's guidance also contains a more subtle problem.

It repeatedly warns that missing registration deadlines may prevent individuals from receiving compensation.

Deadlines for claiming compensation can indeed be important.

However, **the requirement to submit a compensation claim following judgment is not the same as a requirement to opt in to the lawsuit.**

Section 222/39 of the Civil Procedure Code governs the enforcement of qualifying class-action judgments involving monetary compensation.[^365]

Following the judgment, the court notifies class members and appoints an execution officer for the relevant payment procedure.

The court establishes a period during which class members must submit requests for payment.

A member who fails to submit the required request within that period may lose entitlement to participate in the distribution, subject to the statutory exceptions.

This creates two legally distinct stages:

1. **Class membership:** Qualifying persons are included following certification unless they exercise their statutory right to opt out.
2. **Payment claims:** Following a favorable monetary judgment, members must comply with the applicable compensation-claim procedure to receive their individual shares.

A person can therefore remain bound by the class-action judgment while losing the opportunity to receive money through the subsequent distribution process.

TVC's guide fails to distinguish these stages and instead repeatedly characterizes participation deadlines as opt-in requirements.

#### Court Certification Remains Mandatory

The existence of a general statutory framework does not mean that every group of people sharing similar grievances can automatically pursue a class action.

Section 222/12 establishes requirements that courts must consider when determining whether class proceedings are appropriate.

Relevant considerations include common factual and legal issues, the suitability of the representative plaintiff, the adequacy of legal counsel, and whether collective litigation is more appropriate than separate actions.[^362]

The courts retain authority to refuse class certification.

A disappointed claimant cannot simply declare a dispute to be a class action, nor does having multiple complainants eliminate ordinary evidentiary and jurisdictional requirements.

TVC correctly recognizes that judicial certification is necessary.

The problem is its failure to connect that requirement with the broader Civil Procedure Code framework and the statutory consequences of certification.

#### Financial and Procedural Consequences

These errors could affect foreign consumers, investors, property buyers, and business owners participating in litigation involving multiple claimants.

A person relying on TVC's instructions might:

- Incorrectly believe that failing to register initially means they are not part of a class action.
- Overlook a critical opportunity to opt out and preserve an individual legal claim.
- Confuse a court's certification notice with a later compensation-payment notice.
- Misunderstand whether an unfavorable class judgment will bind them.
- Assume that collective proceedings are unavailable for ordinary contractual or tortious claims.
- Miss the separate deadline for claiming their share of a monetary judgment.

The potential consequences are not limited to administrative inconvenience.

They can involve the loss of individual litigation rights, missed financial recovery, and misunderstanding the binding effect of court judgments.

The general class-action framework applies regardless of whether the claimant is Thai or foreign, although jurisdiction, standing, and the substantive cause of action must still satisfy Thai law.

#### Relevance to Thai Visa Centre's Commercial Services

Thai Visa Centre advertises immigration coordination, document preparation, and referrals to Thai litigation lawyers for foreigners involved in collective disputes.

Its class-action guide specifically targets investors, consumers, and foreign residents who may require professional assistance navigating the litigation process.

The company states that it is not a law firm and does not itself represent customers in class-action proceedings.

The available evidence does not establish that TVC has improperly enrolled customers in litigation, caused claimants to miss statutory deadlines, or collected fees for unnecessary proceedings.

Nevertheless, its published instructions repeatedly confuse the procedural rights that determine whether affected persons are legally bound by a class-action judgment.

**TVC should revise its guide to identify Sections 222/1–222/49 of the Civil Procedure Code, explain automatic class membership and the right to opt out, distinguish certification notices from post-judgment compensation claims, and clarify the separate deadlines governing each stage.**

### Trade Disputes Guide Names a Nonexistent Commercial Court and Misstates Customs Appeal Procedures

Thai Visa Centre's *Trade Disputes in Thailand: Import, Export, and Customs Litigation*, marked reviewed June 2026, repeatedly identifies a supposed "Bangkok Commercial Court" as the appropriate forum for commercial trade litigation.[^367]

The guide directs foreign importers and exporters toward this court for claims involving letters of credit, contract enforcement, international shipments, and distribution agreements.

Its procedural checklist specifically instructs readers to file complaints at the Bangkok Commercial Court.

The same publication repeatedly directs customs classification and valuation disputes toward administrative-court proceedings, without explaining the statutory Customs Appeal Committee process.

**These instructions misidentify Thailand's judicial institutions and omit critical procedures governing challenges to customs duty assessments.**

#### Thailand Has No Court Officially Named the Bangkok Commercial Court

Thailand's Office of the Judiciary publishes the structure of its national court system.[^368]

The recognized courts include ordinary Courts of First Instance and specialized courts established to handle particular categories of disputes.

There is no separate institution officially named the "Bangkok Commercial Court."

The actual courts relevant to the types of cases TVC describes include:

- **Ordinary civil courts:** Certain domestic contractual and commercial disputes.
- **Central Intellectual Property and International Trade Court:** Qualifying international trade transactions and associated disputes.
- **Central Tax Court:** Tax and customs matters falling within its statutory jurisdiction.
- **Administrative Court:** Qualifying public-law disputes within its separate jurisdiction.

The judiciary's published descriptions identify the Central Intellectual Property and International Trade Court and Central Tax Court as specialized courts with distinct responsibilities.[^369]

Simply describing a dispute as commercial does not establish which court has jurisdiction.

Instead, the applicable law, nature of the transaction, parties, requested remedy, and jurisdictional rules must be examined.

**TVC repeatedly uses the name of a court that does not exist as a formally established Thai judicial institution, without identifying the actual forum in which the relevant claim should be filed.**

#### International Trade and Letter-of-Credit Cases Have Specialized Jurisdiction

The discrepancy is especially clear for international letters of credit.

Section 7 of the Act on the Establishment of and Procedure for the Intellectual Property and International Trade Court B.E. 2539 (1996) defines the specialized court's jurisdiction.[^370]

The provision includes qualifying civil disputes concerning:

- International sales and exchanges of goods.
- International services and carriage.
- Related insurance and commercial transactions.
- Letters of credit connected with international trade.
- Associated remittances, trust receipts, and guarantees.

These matters may therefore fall within the jurisdiction of the Central Intellectual Property and International Trade Court, rather than the unspecified commercial court described by TVC.

Published analysis of Thai letter-of-credit litigation confirms that qualifying disputes under Section 7(6) belong before the specialized international trade court.[^371]

However, this does not mean every unpaid invoice, domestic distribution agreement, or business-contract dispute automatically belongs there.

Ordinary commercial disputes outside the specialized court's jurisdiction may instead be heard by the appropriate civil court.

**The correct legal distinction is between the jurisdiction of established courts, not between a generic Bangkok Commercial Court and all other forums.**

#### Customs Duty Assessments Follow a Statutory Appeal Process

The more consequential problem concerns TVC's explanation of customs disputes.

Its guide identifies customs valuation and tariff-classification challenges as matters requiring administrative-court review.

However, the Customs Act B.E. 2560 (2017) establishes a specific appeal procedure for importers and exporters disputing customs duty assessments.[^372]

Under Section 37, the importer or exporter generally has **30 days from receipt of the assessment notification** to appeal to the Customs Appeal Committee.

This is a formal statutory procedure.

It is not simply an informal negotiation with Customs officials, nor should it be confused with filing a new administrative-court lawsuit.

The Customs Appeal Committee reviews the assessment and issues its ruling under the applicable statutory provisions.

Section 48 provides a further right to commence court proceedings within **30 days after receiving the Appeal Committee's ruling**, subject to the statutory requirements and exceptions.

Section 42 separately addresses the ability to commence proceedings when the committee does not complete its review within the applicable statutory period.

These provisions establish distinct administrative and judicial stages.

**TVC's guide does not explain the Section 37 appeal requirement or the separate time limit for challenging an adverse Appeal Committee ruling.**

#### Customs Appeals Do Not Automatically Suspend Payment

The omission extends to the financial consequences of disputing an assessment.

Section 38 of the Customs Act establishes that appealing a duty assessment does not automatically defer payment.[^372]

An importer or exporter generally remains subject to the assessment unless the Director-General permits payment to be deferred pending the relevant appellate decision or final judgment.

This distinction can be financially significant.

A business facing a substantial disputed assessment cannot safely assume that filing an objection, appointing a lawyer, or commencing proceedings automatically prevents customs enforcement.

Additional statutory rules govern unpaid duty, surcharges, penalties, detention of goods, and enforcement measures.

An importer must therefore consider payment obligations and any available deferral procedure separately from the merits of its appeal.

**TVC emphasizes that customs litigation can affect substantial shipments and business operations but fails to explain this essential distinction between appealing an assessment and obtaining relief from immediate payment.**

#### The Central Tax Court Handles Civil Customs Duty Litigation

Thailand's Central Tax Court has statutory jurisdiction over qualifying tax disputes, including civil customs duty matters.

The judiciary identifies the court's responsibilities as including appeals involving decisions by competent tax officials or committees and disputes over tax obligations and collection.[^369]

Professional guidance on Thai tax litigation likewise identifies customs duty-assessment disputes among the matters falling within the tax-court framework.[^373]

Consequently, a typical dispute over an additional customs duty assessment does not automatically proceed to the Administrative Court merely because Customs is a government agency.

An importer contesting the assessment must consider the particular statutory appeal procedure and the jurisdiction of the relevant court.

That does not mean the Administrative Court can never hear a customs-related dispute.

Certain disputes involving different administrative acts, regulatory decisions, or public-law questions may fall under separate jurisdictional rules.

Likewise, customs criminal proceedings can involve additional statutory provisions and jurisdictional requirements.

However, **TVC's broad advice omits the central distinction between appealing a customs duty assessment and challenging a different administrative decision.**

#### Foreign Importers Face Strict Deadlines and Significant Financial Risks

The combined errors matter because foreign traders frequently operate under short filing deadlines while dealing with substantial financial exposure.

A business relying on TVC's trade-disputes guide could:

- Seek legal representation for proceedings in an incorrectly named court.
- Prepare an administrative-court claim rather than following the required Customs Appeal Committee procedure.
- Miss the initial 30-day duty-assessment appeal period.
- Miss the separate court-filing deadline following an adverse committee ruling.
- Assume that appealing automatically suspends payment obligations.
- Misunderstand whether an international letter-of-credit dispute belongs before a specialized court.
- Incur additional legal expenses correcting procedural errors or jurisdictional misunderstandings.

These risks are particularly relevant to importers facing reassessed customs duties, exporters disputing trade payments, and companies whose ongoing operations depend on timely release of goods.

Failure to comply with a mandatory appeal procedure or statutory filing period can affect the availability of legal remedies.

#### Relevance to Thai Visa Centre's Commercial Services

Thai Visa Centre advertises immigration coordination, translation assistance, and referrals to licensed Thai commercial litigation lawyers.

Its trade-disputes guide specifically targets foreign importers, exporters, company directors, and investors facing customs or commercial disputes.

The company states that it does not provide courtroom representation itself.

The available evidence does not establish that TVC has filed proceedings in an incorrect court, caused a customer to miss a Customs Appeal Committee deadline, or generated legal fees through these errors.
