Nevertheless, its publications provide procedural instructions that repeatedly name an unrecognized court while overlooking statutory customs appeal requirements.

**TVC should correct the court names in its trade-disputes guide, distinguish ordinary civil litigation from specialized international trade proceedings, explain the Customs Appeal Committee process under Sections 37–48 of the Customs Act, and clarify the separate payment and enforcement consequences of disputing customs duty assessments.**


### Administrative Litigation Guide Misidentifies Tax Court Jurisdiction and Appeal Deadlines

Thai Visa Centre's *Administrative Litigation in Thailand*, marked reviewed June 2026, incorrectly presents Revenue Department tax assessments as disputes ordinarily handled through Thailand's Administrative Court system.

The guide opens:

> Administrative litigation challenges government orders such as visa cancellations, licence revocations, and tax assessments in the Administrative Court system, separate from ordinary civil courts.

The publication lists taxpayers disputing Revenue Department assessments among those needing advice about administrative litigation, while prominently advertising **90 days typical** as its filing window.[^493]

**This presentation overlooks Thailand's specialized Tax Court system and could mislead taxpayers about both the correct forum and the time available to appeal.**

#### Administrative Court Expressly Excludes Tax Court Cases

Section 9 of the Act on Establishment of Administrative Courts and Administrative Court Procedure B.E. 2542 (1999) defines the Administrative Court's jurisdiction and its statutory exclusions.

Thailand's Administrative Court explains on its official website that it does not hear cases assigned to specialized courts, expressly including the Tax Court, Labour Court, Intellectual Property and International Trade Court, and Bankruptcy Court.[^494]

**A dispute does not automatically belong in Administrative Court merely because the challenged decision was made by a government agency.**

Ordinary disputes arising from Revenue Department assessments generally follow the separate tax appeal and litigation framework, rather than an application to the Administrative Court.

#### Revenue Code Establishes Separate 30-Day Appeal Periods

TVC's guide prominently displays a typical **90-day** deadline and explains that many administrative complaints must be filed within 90 days of the written order.[^493]

That general Administrative Court timeframe cannot safely be applied to Revenue Department assessments.

Section 30 of Thailand's Revenue Code generally requires taxpayers challenging assessments outside the separate Amphur-assessment procedure to appeal to the competent Commission of Appeal **within 30 days of receiving the assessment**.[^495]

Subject to applicable conditions, a taxpayer dissatisfied with the Commission of Appeal's decision must generally bring a further appeal to the competent court **within 30 days of receiving that decision**.

The Revenue Code contains different deadlines for certain other assessments, including the 15-day provisions under Section 29. The exact applicable procedure therefore depends on the assessment and governing tax provisions.

**TVC's failure to distinguish these statutory routes creates a risk that taxpayers will mistake a 90-day Administrative Court period for the deadline controlling their actual tax appeal.**

#### Specialized Tax Courts Hear Ordinary Assessment Litigation

Thailand's Act for the Establishment of and Procedure for the Tax Court B.E. 2528 (1985) creates a specialized judicial framework for tax disputes, supplemented by the Regulation on Tax Cases B.E. 2568 (2025).[^496]

The Central Tax Court handles qualifying tax-assessment appeals, tax debt claims, refund disputes, and other matters within the Tax Court's statutory jurisdiction. Its jurisdiction and procedures are distinct from those of the Administrative Court.

Taxpayers ordinarily must complete any administrative objection or appeal required by the applicable tax law before bringing an assessment dispute to the Tax Court.

Judgments and orders of the Central Tax Court follow the specialized appellate route through the Court of Appeals for Specialized Cases, with further petitions to the Supreme Court where permitted—not the ordinary path to the Supreme Administrative Court.[^496]

**TVC's guidance conflates two different court systems with different jurisdictions, filing requirements, and deadlines.**

#### The Guide Repeatedly Reinforces the Wrong Framework

The problem is not limited to the opening sentence.

TVC groups tax assessments with visa and licence cancellations in its examples of administrative disputes, tells taxpayers disputing Revenue Department assessments to seek advice within its Administrative Court discussion, and includes tax cases in its expected Administrative Court litigation timelines.[^493]

Although its table briefly acknowledges dedicated review paths for tax and customs assessments, it does not identify the Tax Court's specialized jurisdiction or provide the Revenue Code's relevant appeal deadlines.

That partial qualification does not resolve the guide's broader presentation of tax assessment disputes as Administrative Court matters.

#### Incorrect Deadlines Can Have Serious Consequences

Foreign residents and business operators may face assessments involving substantial income tax, VAT, penalties, or surcharges.

A taxpayer who assumes that a 90-day filing window applies to an assessment subject to Section 30's 30-day appeal requirement could lose an important opportunity to challenge the assessment.

Section 31 further provides that filing an appeal generally **does not defer payment of assessed taxes**, except where the taxpayer receives the specified permission to await the appeal or court decision.[^495]

That makes accurate guidance concerning both deadlines and financial obligations especially important.

The available evidence does not establish that TVC caused a particular taxpayer to miss an appeal deadline. It establishes that the agency publishes misleading jurisdictional guidance while advertising referrals to licensed litigation professionals.

**Thai Visa Centre should correct its Administrative Litigation guide to identify the Tax Court's specialized jurisdiction, explain the relevant Revenue Code appeal procedures, and distinguish ordinary tax assessments from disputes genuinely within Administrative Court jurisdiction.**

Professional litigation guidance should not direct readers toward the wrong judicial framework or omit statutory deadlines that can determine whether a tax assessment remains challengeable.

### Labour Litigation Guide Misstates Court Fees, Lawyer Requirements, and Appeals

Thai Visa Centre's *Labor Litigation: Guide for Foreigners*, marked reviewed June 2026, contains several misleading statements about Thailand's Labour Court procedures, including the cost of filing claims, the necessity of legal representation, and the court responsible for hearing appeals.[^398]

#### Labour Court Proceedings Are Exempt From Court Fees

The guide tells readers that Labour Court fees are "modest compared with Civil Court."

However, Section 27 of the Act on the Establishment of Labour Courts and Labour Court Procedure B.E. 2522 (1979) expressly exempts the filing of claims and proceedings in Labour Courts from court fees.[^399]

Although litigants may incur legal representation, translation, travel, and other expenses, the statutory exemption is substantially different from the guide's suggestion that filing fees are merely reduced.

#### Lawyers Are Not Mandatory for Every Labour Court Claim

The guide's frequently asked questions state that Thai counsel is required for proceedings conducted in Thai. Its procedural checklist similarly describes Thai lawyers preparing the necessary pleadings.

Section 35 of the Labour Court legislation expressly allows plaintiffs to submit written claims or present their complaints orally before the court. Where a complaint is presented orally, the court may question the plaintiff and record the claim.[^399]

Legal representation may be advisable, particularly for complex disputes or foreign litigants requiring language assistance, but it is not a universal statutory requirement for bringing a Labour Court claim.

**The distinction matters because incorrect advice about mandatory legal representation could discourage workers from pursuing claims they are legally entitled to file themselves.**

#### Appeals Are Directed to the Wrong Court

The guide also instructs readers that appeals proceed to a "Labour Court appellate division."

Thailand instead operates a **Court of Appeal for Specialized Cases**, which includes a Labour Case Division. The court was inaugurated on October 1, 2016, and hears qualifying appeals from the country's specialized courts.[^400]

Thai Visa Centre's description fails to identify the actual appellate institution and omits important procedural restrictions governing Labour Court appeals.

#### Relevance to Thai Visa Centre's Professional Claims

These errors appear in a publication specifically intended to explain legal procedures to foreign residents and workers.

The guide repeatedly recommends professional legal assistance while failing to explain that Labour Court proceedings are exempt from court fees and that individuals may initiate claims without retaining an attorney.

**The combination of incorrect cost information, overstated representation requirements, and misidentified appellate procedures raises serious questions about the accuracy of Thai Visa Centre's professionally presented legal guidance.**

The discrepancies do not establish deliberate deception. However, they undermine confidence in a guide presented as reviewed and suitable for readers navigating potentially consequential employment disputes.


### Consumer Protection Guide Falsely Claims Lawyers Are Mandatory and Omits Free Court Filing

Thai Visa Centre's *Consumer Protection Act: Guide for Foreigners*, marked reviewed June 2026, misrepresents fundamental consumer rights under Thai law. The guide incorrectly tells foreigners that lawyers are required to pursue consumer litigation, fails to disclose statutory court-fee exemptions, and invents documentary requirements that could discourage legitimate complaints.[^401]

#### Falsely Claims Thai Lawyers Are Mandatory

The guide explicitly states:

> You still need Thai counsel to file and conduct proceedings in Thai.

Its procedural checklist repeatedly instructs consumers to use Thai lawyers to prepare and file complaints.

**Section 20 of Thailand's Consumer Case Procedure Act B.E. 2551 (2008) directly contradicts this advice.** Consumers may file complaints orally or in writing. Court officials must record oral complaints and obtain the plaintiff's signature.[^402]

Thai law deliberately provides a filing procedure accessible to consumers without mandatory legal representation.

TVC instead presents paid legal assistance as a prerequisite.

#### Conceals Statutory Court-Fee Exemptions

Section 18 of the same legislation exempts qualifying consumer plaintiffs from court fees when filing and conducting consumer proceedings.[^402]

The exemption does not eliminate possible liability for costs at the conclusion of proceedings, but it removes a significant upfront financial barrier.

**TVC's guide omits this fundamental consumer protection entirely**, despite extensively discussing litigation costs, legal representation, and referrals to professional lawyers.

#### Invents Mandatory Receipt and Warranty Requirements

The guide further claims that consumer complaints involving defective electronics fail without Thai-language receipts and warranty cards.

Neither Section 20's complaint-filing procedure nor the legislation's general consumer-case provisions impose these documents as universal filing requirements.[^402]

Receipts and warranty cards may provide useful evidence, but their absence does not automatically invalidate a consumer complaint.

TVC converts recommended supporting evidence into purported mandatory documentation.

#### Misleading Consumers While Promoting Legal Referrals

These errors consistently make consumer litigation appear more expensive, complicated, and dependent on professional representation than Thai law actually requires.

The pattern is particularly troubling because Thai Visa Centre advertises referrals to licensed lawyers while providing inaccurate information about consumers' ability to pursue remedies independently.

**A business promoting legal services should not be telling consumers that lawyers are mandatory when the law expressly provides procedures for filing claims without them.**

The publication should be corrected to disclose the available filing procedures, statutory fee exemptions, and actual evidentiary requirements.

### Distinguishing the Correct Procedural Routes

The governing court, procedural prerequisites, and costs depend on the particular dispute; the following routes should not be conflated:

| Dispute | Relevant procedural distinction |
|---|---|
| Class-action lawsuit | A court must certify the class; qualifying members generally fall within the class unless they exercise their opt-out rights, while compensation claims after judgment involve a separate process.[^361][^365] |
| Customs duty assessment | Statutory Customs Act objections and appeals, payment obligations, and the specialized Tax Court route must be distinguished from ordinary commercial litigation.[^372][^373] |
| Revenue Department assessment | Revenue Code administrative appeals and applicable 30-day periods differ from a generalized 90-day Administrative Court filing framework; the specialized Tax Court handles qualifying assessment disputes.[^494][^495] |
| Labour dispute | Labour Court procedures include court-fee exemptions and do not invariably require counsel; appeals follow the specialized appellate system.[^399][^400] |
| Consumer claim | Applicable consumer-case procedures permit direct or oral filing and statutory court-fee exemptions, without a universal requirement to hire a lawyer.[^402] |

**No single court, deadline, filing method, or lawyer requirement governs all five categories.** The particular statutory conditions and factual circumstances must still be checked before bringing or defending a claim.

### Commercial Significance for Litigants

Incorrect litigation guidance can have consequences beyond the cost of professional assistance. Readers could select the wrong court, misunderstand a mandatory administrative appeal, miss a filing deadline, assume they must retain counsel when direct filing is permitted, or fail to exercise class-action, employment, or consumer rights.

TVC promotes referrals to legal professionals while publishing this procedural information. **The documented problem is the unreliability of its public instructions, not proof that any customer actually lost a case, missed a deadline, incurred unnecessary legal fees, or received defective individual advice.** TVC should correct the underlying guides together while distinguishing optional professional representation from actual procedural requirements.

## Litigation Guides Invent a Recognition Procedure for Foreign Court Judgments

Thai Visa Centre publishes misleading instructions about enforcing foreign court judgments in Thailand, repeatedly suggesting that overseas judgments can be recognized through a Thai court procedure rather than requiring fresh litigation.

The errors appear across multiple guides marked reviewed June 2026 and concern a fundamental distinction in Thai civil procedure.[^403][^404][^405]

### Inventing a Foreign Judgment Recognition Procedure

In its *Injunction and Restraining Order* guide, Thai Visa Centre states:

> Creditor must first obtain Thai recognition or sue on underlying debt

The publication presents judicial recognition and filing a new lawsuit as alternative procedures available to foreign judgment creditors.[^403]

**Thailand does not provide a general recognition or registration procedure for foreign civil court judgments.**

A creditor who obtains a judgment in the United States, United Kingdom, Singapore, or another foreign jurisdiction cannot simply apply to a Thai court to recognize that judgment and authorize execution against assets in Thailand.

The creditor must instead commence fresh proceedings before a competent Thai court.

### Contradictory Instructions Across Litigation Guides

The company's *Litigation in Thailand FAQs* states that enforcement of foreign judgments depends on treaties and Thai law, suggesting that a separate registration or recognition process may be available.[^404]

Its *Debt Recovery* guide similarly warns against attempting to enforce foreign judgments without following a recognition procedure.[^405]

These publications repeatedly fail to explain the actual legal position.

**Foreign court judgments are not directly enforceable in Thailand through an ordinary recognition application.**

The distinction is particularly important for international creditors, business owners, and investors attempting to recover debts or enforce damages awards against Thai assets.

### Established Thai Law Requires Fresh Litigation

International law firm Tilleke & Gibbins confirmed in December 2025 that foreign court judgments must effectively be litigated again before a Thai court.[^406]

The foreign judgment may serve as evidence, but it does not bind the Thai court or eliminate the need to prove the underlying claim.

CMS independently explains that Thailand has no general statutory procedure for enforcing foreign judgments and identifies Supreme Court decisions addressing the evidentiary treatment of overseas judgments.[^407]

By contrast, qualifying **foreign arbitral awards** can be recognized and enforced under Thailand's Arbitration Act B.E. 2545 (2002) and the New York Convention.

Thai Visa Centre's guidance fails to explain this fundamental distinction between foreign court judgments and arbitral awards.

### Serious Consequences for Foreign Creditors

The difference between recognizing an existing judgment and initiating fresh litigation is substantial.

Foreign creditors following TVC's guidance could misunderstand the proceedings required, underestimate legal costs, and make incorrect assumptions about how quickly Thai assets can be seized or attached.

**A business publishing professional litigation guidance should not present a nonexistent general judgment-recognition procedure as an available legal remedy.**

Thai Visa Centre should correct its litigation, injunction, and debt-recovery guides to explain that ordinary foreign civil judgments require fresh Thai proceedings, while qualifying arbitral awards follow a separate statutory enforcement process.


## Intellectual Property Guides Misstate Court Jurisdiction and Copyright Damages

Thai Visa Centre publishes incorrect instructions about intellectual-property litigation in Thailand, misidentifying the courts responsible for infringement cases and suggesting copyright remedies that do not correspond to the country's statutory framework.

The errors appear in two guides marked reviewed June 2026, both intended to help foreign businesses and intellectual-property owners navigate enforcement proceedings.[^408][^409]

### Incorrectly Directs IP Claims Toward Ordinary Civil Courts

TVC's *Intellectual Property: Guide for Foreigners* tells readers:

> IP and Trade Court or Civil Court handles civil damages.

Its separate *Intellectual Property Disputes in Thailand* guide repeats this distinction, presenting the Intellectual Property and International Trade Court and ordinary civil courts as alternative forums for infringement litigation.[^408][^409]

**Thailand established specialized jurisdiction over intellectual-property infringement proceedings through the Intellectual Property and International Trade Court.**

The Central Intellectual Property and International Trade Court handles civil and criminal IP infringement cases under the Act on the Establishment of and Procedure for Intellectual Property and International Trade Court B.E. 2539 (1996).

The Legal 500's Thailand IP litigation guide confirms that this specialized court has jurisdiction over intellectual-property cases.[^410]

**TVC's instructions wrongly suggest that ordinary civil courts provide an interchangeable filing option for IP infringement claims.**

### Misrepresents Statutory Copyright Damages

The *Intellectual Property* guide also states:

> Statutory damages apply in some copyright matters.

This description incorrectly suggests a system of predetermined statutory compensation comparable to copyright regimes in jurisdictions such as the United States.

Thailand's Copyright Act instead provides a different damages framework.

Under Section 64, courts determine appropriate compensation by considering the seriousness of the infringement, lost benefits, and reasonable enforcement expenses.[^411]

The 2015 amendment additionally permits enhanced damages of up to twice the assessed amount in specified circumstances involving intentional infringement and widespread public access.[^412]

**Neither provision establishes a general schedule of fixed statutory copyright damages.**

TVC fails to distinguish judicially assessed compensation and enhanced damages from predetermined statutory awards.

### Consequences for Foreign Rights Holders

These errors concern fundamental aspects of litigation strategy.

A foreign business following TVC's instructions could misunderstand which court has jurisdiction, incorrectly assess available monetary remedies, or pursue legal advice based on expectations unsupported by Thai law.

The mistakes are particularly serious because the guides target trademark owners, copyright holders, franchise operators, and businesses facing potentially expensive disputes.

**A company advertising professional legal referrals should accurately identify the competent court and available remedies before directing customers toward litigation services.**

Thai Visa Centre should correct both intellectual-property guides, remove the misleading references to ordinary civil-court jurisdiction, and explain the actual damages framework established by Section 64 of the Copyright Act.


## Alternative Dispute Resolution Guide Misstates Statutory Limitation Periods

Thai Visa Centre's *Alternative Dispute Resolution in Thailand FAQs*, marked reviewed June 2026, incorrectly explains how limitation periods operate during arbitration and mediation.

The publication repeatedly warns foreign businesses that legal deadlines continue running during alternative dispute resolution proceedings, overlooking statutory provisions that expressly protect claimants.[^413]

### Arbitration Directly Interrupts Prescription

The guide states:

> Limitation periods continue during mediation and arbitration.

It subsequently advises readers to determine whether their contracts pause legal deadlines.

**This advice directly contradicts Section 193/14(4) of Thailand's Civil and Commercial Code.**

The provision expressly establishes that prescription is interrupted when a creditor submits a dispute to arbitration.[^414]

Section 193/15 further provides that time elapsed before an interruption does not count toward the limitation period and that a fresh period begins when the interruption ends.[^415]

This protection arises from legislation, not merely from contractual arrangements between the disputing parties.

TVC's blanket claim that limitation periods continue during arbitration ignores this fundamental statutory rule.

### Mediation Also Provides Statutory Deadline Protections

The guide separately tells readers:

> Limitation periods continue running during mediation

However, Section 6 of Thailand's Dispute Mediation Act B.E. 2562 (2019) expressly provides a 60-day extension where qualifying mediation fails and the limitation period expired during mediation or would expire within 60 days afterward.[^416]

The extension runs from the termination of the unsuccessful mediation.

Thailand's Civil Procedure Code also provides protection for qualifying court-supervised mediation before litigation.

Amendments introduced in 2020 allow parties to petition courts for mediation before filing a lawsuit. Where mediation fails and the relevant limitation period has expired or is approaching expiration, the legislation provides an additional 60 days to pursue the claim.[^417]

International law firm Tilleke & Gibbins specifically documented this protection when explaining the amendments.

**TVC's advice fails to distinguish informal settlement negotiations from statutory mediation procedures that provide additional time to initiate litigation.**

### Misleading Businesses About Their Legal Rights

These errors affect one of the most consequential aspects of commercial dispute resolution: whether a creditor or claimant can still pursue a legally enforceable claim.

Foreign investors, contractors, creditors, and business owners rely on limitation rules when choosing between arbitration, mediation, and court proceedings.

TVC's guide incorrectly presents the passage of time during these proceedings as a uniform legal risk while overlooking statutory protections specifically designed to preserve claims.

The mistakes also contradict the guide's stated purpose of helping foreign businesses choose appropriate dispute resolution procedures.

**A commercial visa agency publishing professional litigation guidance should not misstate statutory deadlines that determine whether customers can enforce their legal rights.**

Thai Visa Centre should correct its ADR guide to explain interruption of prescription under Sections 193/14–193/15, the 60-day extension under the Dispute Mediation Act, and the separate protection available through pre-litigation court mediation.


## Cybercrime Guide Falsely Treats Online Defamation as a Computer Crime Act Offence

Thai Visa Centre's *Cyber Crime Lawyers: Guide for Foreigners*, marked reviewed June 2026, repeatedly misrepresents Thailand's Computer Crime Act by presenting ordinary online defamation as a computer crime.[^418]

The publication ignores a major legislative amendment introduced in 2017 specifically to prevent the misuse of computer-crime charges against people accused of defamation.

### Incorrectly Claims Defamation Falls Under the Computer Crime Act

TVC's guide identifies online defamation among Computer Crime Act violations and describes social media posts damaging someone's reputation as potential computer-crime offences.

Its section on overlapping criminal charges explicitly states:

> Social media insults may simultaneously attract Computer Crime Act and criminal defamation charges.

The guide further suggests that a single online post can expose its author to multiple criminal proceedings under both laws.[^418]

**This presentation ignores the explicit defamation exclusion introduced into Section 14(1) of the Computer Crime Act in 2017.**

### The 2017 Amendment Expressly Excludes Defamation

Thailand amended its Computer Crime Act through the Computer-Related Crime Act (No. 2) B.E. 2560 (2017).

The revised Section 14(1) criminalizes specified dishonest or deceitful submissions of false or distorted computer data, while expressly excluding conduct constituting defamation under the Criminal Code.[^419]

The amendment took effect on May 24, 2017.

Human Rights Watch documented that the previous legislation had frequently been used alongside criminal defamation charges to prosecute individuals expressing criticism online. The amendment was intended to prevent this overlap under Section 14(1).[^420]

**Publishing criticism, accusations, or allegedly defamatory statements on Facebook, Google Reviews, or another online platform does not automatically transform criminal defamation into a Computer Crime Act violation.**

A separate computer-crime charge requires conduct satisfying the elements of an applicable offence, not merely publication through the internet.

### Thai Courts Have Already Applied the Exclusion

The distinction is supported by actual Thai court proceedings.

In litigation brought by PTT Public Company Limited against activist Itthaboon Onwongsa over critical Facebook posts, the Bangkok South Criminal Court dismissed Computer Crime Act charges in December 2017 after applying the amended legislation.

On November 21, 2018, the appellate court upheld the dismissal, emphasizing the amended requirements for dishonest or deceitful conduct and the exclusion of defamation from Section 14(1).[^421]

**The courts rejected the attempt to use computer-crime provisions against online criticism without establishing the additional statutory elements.**

### Misleading Readers About Criminal Exposure

TVC's guide fails to explain this established legal distinction despite advertising professional assistance to foreigners facing online defamation complaints and criminal investigations.

The error could lead readers to believe that ordinary social media criticism automatically exposes them to separate Computer Crime Act prosecution simply because the statements appeared online.

It also exaggerates the potential criminal consequences of negative reviews and public accusations.

**Thai Visa Centre should correct its cybercrime guide to acknowledge the 2017 defamation exclusion, explain the actual elements of Section 14(1), and stop presenting ordinary online defamation as an automatic computer-crime offence.**


## Bail Guide Falsely Claims Cash or Property Is Mandatory and Denies Insurance Options

Thai Visa Centre's *Bail and Bail Bonds: Guide for Foreigners*, marked reviewed June 2026, incorrectly describes Thailand's criminal bail system, falsely presenting cash deposits and pledged property as mandatory requirements while denying the existence of commercial bail bond insurance.[^422]

These claims contradict Thailand's Criminal Procedure Code, judicial reforms promoting release without financial security, and insurance products publicly offered by Thai companies.

### Incorrectly Claims Bail Requires Cash or Property

The guide states:

> Release requires cash paid at court or pledged registered assets.

It repeats this restriction throughout its explanations of bail applications, payment methods, and release conditions.[^422]

**Section 110 of Thailand's Criminal Procedure Code expressly permits provisional release without a bail bond or financial security in qualifying cases.**[^423]

For offences carrying a maximum imprisonment exceeding ten years, a bail bond is required, but additional financial security is not automatically mandatory.

For other offences, provisional release may be granted without a bond, with a bond alone, or with both a bond and security.

TVC's blanket description incorrectly turns discretionary financial requirements into universal conditions.

### Ignores Judicial Reforms Promoting Release Without Security

Thailand's judiciary has actively promoted alternatives to cash-based bail to reduce unnecessary pretrial detention.

The Thailand Institute of Justice documented a judicial initiative introducing simplified provisional-release applications and risk assessments that do not require applicants to declare assets or provide financial security.[^424]

The initiative specifically sought to reduce unequal treatment between defendants who could afford bail deposits and those who could not.

Courts may instead impose appropriate reporting, supervision, or electronic-monitoring conditions.

**TVC's guide ignores these established alternatives and continues portraying financial deposits as essential to obtaining release.**

### Commercial Bail Bond Insurance Exists in Thailand

The publication also states that Thailand has no commercial bail bond insurance industry and that defendants must instead provide cash or registered assets.[^422]

However, **Thai Pattana Insurance Public Company Limited explicitly advertises Bail Bond Insurance** on its official website.[^425]

The insurer describes policies under which the company guarantees a defendant's provisional release and assumes specified financial obligations if the defendant breaches bail conditions.

It also publishes procedures for premium refunds, court documentation, and insurance claims.

Siam Legal International independently explains that defendants and their families can purchase bail insurance after charges are filed, with insurance certificates potentially serving as security accepted by Thai courts.[^426]

Thailand's arrangements are not identical to the American commercial bail bondsman industry, but that distinction does not justify denying the existence of Thai bail insurance products.

### Consequences for Foreign Defendants

These errors could cause foreign defendants and their families to believe they must urgently raise substantial cash deposits or pledge property before provisional release is possible.

They also conceal alternatives involving unsecured release, insurance-backed guarantees, and judicial risk-assessment procedures.

The consequences are serious because bail decisions directly affect personal liberty, family finances, and the ability to prepare a criminal defence.

**A commercial visa agency advertising criminal-law referrals should not falsely tell defendants that cash or property is mandatory while overlooking legally recognized alternatives.**

Thai Visa Centre should correct its bail guide to explain Section 110, disclose the possibility of release without financial security, and acknowledge the commercial bail insurance products available in Thailand.


## Misleading Lease Enforceability, Landlord Obligations, and Tenant Protections

Thai Visa Centre publishes misleading and incomplete guidance about two distinct aspects of renting property in Thailand: whether an enforceable lease survives a change of ownership, and when additional consumer-protection rules govern residential rental businesses. Its lease guides wrongly suggest that unregistered leases do not bind new owners, while its landlord guidance omits important contract-control requirements introduced in 2025.

**General lease-enforceability rules and the additional requirements imposed on qualifying residential landlords are not interchangeable.** The findings below distinguish tenants' rights under the Civil and Commercial Code from the statutory obligations of covered rental businesses, including the three-unit threshold under the updated regulations.

### Property Guides Falsely Claim Unregistered Leases Do Not Bind New Owners

Thai Visa Centre publishes misleading advice about the legal protection afforded to tenants when leased property changes ownership.

Its *Property Lease Agreement in Thailand* and *Property Leases in Thailand* guides, both marked reviewed June 2026, incorrectly suggest that unregistered leases cannot bind subsequent property owners.[^427][^428]

#### Incorrectly Claims Unregistered Leases Do Not Survive Property Sales

The property lease agreement guide explicitly states:

> Unregistered leases do not bind future owners or rank against creditors.

Its separate property leases guide similarly warns that an unregistered lease may not survive a sale to a third party.[^427][^428]

**These blanket statements contradict Section 569 of Thailand's Civil and Commercial Code.**

Section 569 expressly provides that a contract for the hire of immovable property is not extinguished when ownership transfers to another person.

The new owner assumes the previous owner's rights and obligations toward the tenant.[^429]

The statute does not categorically exclude unregistered leases from this protection.

#### Unregistered Leases Remain Enforceable for Up to Three Years

Section 538 establishes a separate rule governing lease documentation and registration.[^430]

Leases of immovable property require written evidence signed by the party against whom enforcement is sought.

Where an agreement provides for more than three years but has not been registered with the competent authority, it is enforceable for only three years.

**Failure to register a long-term lease does not automatically invalidate the entire agreement or eliminate the tenant's rights when ownership changes.**

For example, a properly documented five-year lease that was never registered may remain enforceable during its first three years, including against a subsequent purchaser under Section 569.

The remaining two years cannot ordinarily be enforced without registration.

TVC's advice incorrectly conflates the inability to enforce an unregistered lease beyond three years with the complete absence of protection against future owners.

#### Supreme Court Decision Confirms the Distinction

Thailand's Supreme Court addressed this issue in Decision No. 1564/2558 (2015).

The dispute involved a 15-year land-leasing arrangement structured through five consecutive three-year contracts.

The Supreme Court determined that the arrangement attempted to circumvent Section 538's registration requirements.

Consequently, the lease was enforceable for only three years.

However, the court also applied **Section 569**, recognizing that the purchaser of the property succeeded to the previous owner's position as landlord.[^431]

The ruling demonstrates that registration requirements and the survival of an enforceable lease after a property transfer are distinct legal questions.

#### Misleading Foreign Tenants About Their Rights

These errors could lead foreign tenants to believe they automatically lose their right to occupy rented homes, commercial premises, or leased land when a landlord sells the property.

They could also encourage tenants to surrender possession, renegotiate existing agreements, or accept unfavorable financial settlements without understanding their statutory protections.

**Thai Visa Centre should correct its property guides to explain that Section 569 protects enforceable leases following ownership transfers, while Section 538 separately limits the enforceability of unregistered long-term agreements.**

The distinction is fundamental to Thai property law and particularly important for foreigners entering expensive residential or commercial leasing arrangements.


### Landlord Guide Omits Mandatory Residential Lease Protections Introduced in 2025

Thai Visa Centre's *Becoming a Landlord in Thailand*, marked reviewed June 2026, provides incomplete guidance on residential tenancy law while overlooking major consumer-protection regulations introduced in September 2025.[^432]

The guide advertises advice for foreign condominium owners renting properties in Thailand, covering lease agreements, security deposits, tenant disputes, and landlord obligations.

However, it fails to explain Thailand's updated contract-control regulations despite specifically presenting itself as a guide to landlord compliance.

#### Fails to Identify the 2025 Residential Lease Regulations

Thailand's Contract Committee introduced new regulations under the Consumer Protection Act through its Notification on Residential Building Rental Businesses B.E. 2568 (2025).

The regulations replaced the earlier 2019 framework and expanded coverage from landlords operating at least five residential units to those offering **three or more units**, including properties spread across different buildings.[^433]

The rules apply to qualifying residential rental businesses, including houses, condominiums, and apartments, while excluding accommodation regulated separately under hotel and dormitory legislation.

**TVC's guide never identifies this threshold or explains which landlords must comply with the mandatory contract requirements.**

#### Omits Mandatory Deposit Limits and Refund Deadlines

TVC's lease checklist describes deposits as:

> Typically two months rent plus one month advance; residential caps apply

It does not explain the actual statutory limits or mandatory refund procedures.

For qualifying residential rentals, the 2025 regulations generally prohibit collecting security deposits and advance rent exceeding three months' monthly rent combined. Different limits apply to qualifying long-term leases with annual payments.[^434]

The Office of the Consumer Protection Board also confirms that security deposits must be returned within seven days where no damage is found, or within 14 days where documented damage requires deductions.

Normal wear and tear cannot be treated as tenant-caused damage.

**These are binding consumer protections, not merely recommended contract practices.**

TVC's guide discusses deposit disputes and warns landlords against retaining deposits without documentation, yet omits the statutory deadlines governing repayment.

#### Ignores Prohibited Utility Charges and Contract Terms

The regulations also prohibit covered landlords from:

- Charging electricity or water rates exceeding the amounts billed by utility providers.
- Increasing rent or service charges during an existing lease.
- Entering rented premises without appropriate notice, except in emergencies.
- Seizing tenants' belongings or denying access without lawful grounds.
- Requiring tenants to pay for ordinary deterioration of the premises.
- Using prohibited clauses that unfairly restrict tenants' statutory rights.

Qualifying landlords must use contracts incorporating the mandatory provisions of the prescribed standard forms.[^435]

Unlawful contractual clauses can be treated as unenforceable under the Consumer Protection Act.

Violations of applicable contract-control requirements can expose landlords to criminal penalties, including fines of up to 200,000 baht and imprisonment of up to one year.[^435]

#### TVC's Legal Archive Repeats the Superseded Five-Unit Threshold

TVC's separate *Tenants' Rights, Residential Leases & Property Tax* law archive expressly identifies the 2025 residential rental notification and its September 4, 2025 effective date.[^638] Nevertheless, its *In plain English* summary says that the contract-control rules bind residential landlords operating **five or more rental units**, with an additional reference to digital rental platforms.

That numerical threshold belongs to the superseded 2019 framework. The 2025 notification expanded coverage to qualifying residential rental businesses offering **three or more units**, whether located together or across different properties.[^433][^435] The archive itself identifies the older 2019 notification as regulating landlords with five or more units, yet repeats that figure in the summary of the rules currently in force.[^638]

**The misleading summary could cause an operator renting three or four qualifying residential units to believe the mandatory contract rules do not apply.** The agency's commercial landlord guide omits the revised threshold altogether, while the law archive acknowledges the new notification but retains the old numerical test.

The evidence does not establish that TVC has advised any particular landlord to disregard these requirements or caused a tenant to lose legal protections. It establishes contradictory and incomplete public guidance about which landlords are subject to the rules.

#### Published Review Dates Raise Further Questions

The regulations had already been in force for approximately nine months when TVC's guide was supposedly reviewed in June 2026.

Yet its discussion of residential lease compliance remains focused on general Civil and Commercial Code requirements, Land Department registration, deposits, and tax reporting.

**A supposedly reviewed landlord guide that overlooks mandatory contract regulations, deposit refund deadlines, and prohibited rental practices fails to explain some of the most important legal obligations facing professional landlords.**

Thai Visa Centre should update its publication to identify the 2025 regulations, distinguish covered rental businesses from smaller private landlords, and explain the mandatory protections governing residential lease agreements.

### Distinguishing Ordinary Lease Rights From Residential Contract Controls

The two legal frameworks address different questions:

| Situation | Relevant legal protection or obligation |
|---|---|
| An enforceable lease when a property is sold | Section 569 generally preserves the lease against the new owner; the absence of long-term registration does not alone extinguish the enforceable portion of the lease.[^429] |
| A lease for more than three years that was not registered | Section 538 generally limits enforceability to three years despite a longer written term.[^430][^431] |
| A qualifying residential rental business offering three or more units | The 2025 contract-control notification imposes additional requirements concerning rental terms, deposits, refunds, utilities, and prohibited landlord conduct.[^433][^434][^435] |
| A smaller landlord outside the contract-control notification | The notification's additional duties do not automatically apply merely because premises are rented; applicable Civil and Commercial Code provisions and other legal obligations may still protect the tenant. |

**A lease can be enforceable after a property sale without the landlord falling within the three-unit residential-business regime; likewise, mandatory residential contract terms do not replace the separate registration rules for long leases.** TVC should explain both distinctions consistently across its property guides and law archive.

### Commercial Significance for Landlords and Tenants

Misstating lease survival could lead tenants to abandon enforceable rights after a sale. Omitting or misstating the scope of residential contract controls could lead covered landlords to use noncompliant terms, with consequences for deposits, utility bills, and disputes. Both problems can affect foreign residents and property owners seeking reliable compliance advice.

The available evidence documents published inaccuracies, not particular unlawful rental transactions, financial losses, or lost tenancy rights caused by TVC. Its publications should accurately explain Sections 538 and 569, the 2025 three-unit threshold, and the additional protections available to tenants of qualifying residential rental businesses.

## TVC's Civil Litigation Guides Misstate Court Costs, Procedure, Representation, Appeals, and Enforcement

TVC's *Civil Cases: Guide for Foreigners*, *Civil Case Filing in Thailand*, *Civil Procedure in Thailand*, *Trial, Appeals, and Execution of Judgment in Thailand*, and *Debt Recovery: Guide for Foreigners* publish inaccurate instructions about court costs, court filing, legal representation, appeal deadlines, and judgment enforcement.[^436][^440][^441][^446][^489][^512]

The findings below compare those publications' instructions with the governing statutes and court procedures.

### Civil Case Filing and Civil Procedure Guides Falsely Present Lawyers as Mandatory

The *Civil Case Filing in Thailand* guide states:

> Court filing requires licensed Thai attorneys.

Its introductory explanation similarly insists that foreign claimants cannot file effectively without a licensed Thai advocate. The guide's procedural instructions describe lawyers preparing complaints, submitting documents, and conducting all courtroom advocacy.[^440]

The separate *Civil Procedure in Thailand* guide goes further:

> Foreigners must operate through licensed Thai advocates.

It repeats this supposed requirement throughout its discussion of civil litigation.[^441]

**These statements turn optional professional representation into a purported legal obligation.**

### Section 60 Expressly Permits Self-Representation

Section 60 of Thailand's Civil Procedure Code provides that parties may conduct their own cases and undertake procedural steps personally or appoint legal counsel to act on their behalf.[^442]

The provision establishes self-representation and representation through appointed lawyers as alternative lawful methods of conducting civil proceedings.

It does not impose a blanket requirement that foreign plaintiffs or defendants hire Thai lawyers.

Foreign litigants must comply with Thai procedural rules, including language, filing, evidence, and court requirements. Those obligations do not eliminate their statutory right to conduct their own cases.

**TVC's categorical claims about mandatory legal representation directly contradict the governing legislation.**

### TVC Contradicts Itself in the Same Guide

Remarkably, the *Civil Procedure in Thailand* guide acknowledges the correct legal position elsewhere.

Its frequently asked questions include:

**Can I represent myself?**

The answer begins:

> Legally possible but strongly discouraged for foreigners.

This admission directly contradicts the same publication's repeated insistence that foreigners must use licensed Thai advocates.[^441]

The contradiction is especially revealing because both statements appear in a guide supposedly reviewed for legal accuracy in June 2026.

**TVC acknowledges that self-representation is lawful in one paragraph while presenting legal representation as mandatory elsewhere on the same page.**



### Trial and Appeals Guide Falsely Says Lawyers Are Required

The *Trial, Appeals, and Execution of Judgment in Thailand* guide extends TVC's false mandatory-lawyer claims to appeals and post-judgment enforcement. Its FAQ states: “Appeals and execution require licensed Thai attorneys.”[^512]

Section 60 permits parties to conduct their own cases and take procedural steps personally, or appoint one or more attorneys.[^442] It imposes no blanket lawyer requirement for civil appeals or execution.

The claim repeats the same misrepresentation already documented in TVC's *Civil Case Filing in Thailand* and *Civil Procedure in Thailand* guides.

### Trial and Appeals Guide Repeats the False Enforcement Instruction

The guide's checklist warns readers against filing for execution while an appeal is pending, its timeline says execution begins only after final judgment, and its FAQ says execution generally waits until judgment is final.[^512]



### Trial Guide Misstates the Appeal Deadline and Extension Rule

The guide describes the ordinary civil appeal period as 30 days, refers to judgment delivery or the date printed on a judgment copy as the trigger, and says foreigners receive “no extension” if they miss the deadline.[^512]

Section 229 sets the ordinary appeal period at **one month from pronouncement of the judgment or order**. Section 193/5 requires periods expressed in months to be calculated according to the calendar.[^513][^514] A fixed 30-day count and a delivery-date trigger do not accurately state that rule.

The guide's categorical claim that foreigners cannot obtain an extension is also false. Section 23 authorizes courts to extend procedural deadlines, including appeal deadlines, in special circumstances. As a rule, the request must be made before the deadline expires; force majeure is an exception.[^515] An extension is not automatic, but Section 23 contains no bar for foreign litigants.

### Civil Procedure Guide Repeats the False 30-Day Deadline

*Civil Procedure in Thailand* independently says the judgment copy bears the date that starts the appeal period, “often thirty days.” It also tells readers to file immediately after judgment delivery and describes the deadline as running from delivery.[^441]

Section 229 instead sets the ordinary appeal period at one month from pronouncement, with calendar-based calculation under Section 193/5.[^513][^514] TVC therefore repeats the inaccurate deadline and trigger in separate litigation guides.

The combined instructions risk causing readers to calculate the wrong filing date or believe that no extension can be requested, while presenting lawyer representation as compulsory where Section 60 permits self-representation.

### Financial Consequences of Misstating Representation Requirements

The distinction has immediate financial consequences.

Foreign residents, property owners, creditors, and business operators may believe they cannot initiate or defend ordinary civil proceedings without first paying for professional legal representation.

Thai Visa Centre promotes referrals to licensed litigation lawyers while publishing guidance that exaggerates the necessity of hiring those professionals.

Although representation can be valuable in complicated disputes, it is not a universal prerequisite for civil litigation.

**A commercial business advertising legal referrals should not misrepresent optional paid legal representation as a statutory requirement.**



### Debt-Recovery Guide Falsely Requires Appeals to Finish Before Judgment Enforcement

Thai Visa Centre's *Debt Recovery: Guide for Foreigners*, marked reviewed June 2026, incorrectly tells creditors that they must wait for appeal periods to expire or appeals to conclude before enforcing civil court judgments.[^446]

This advice directly contradicts Section 231 of Thailand's Civil Procedure Code, which establishes that filing an appeal does not automatically suspend enforcement of a judgment.

#### Incorrectly Claims Appeals Must Be Resolved Before Enforcement

In its explanation of the debt-recovery process, TVC states:

> Appeal periods must expire or be resolved before execution begins.

The same instruction appears again in the publication's practical action checklist.[^446]

The guide presents this as a mandatory procedural step between obtaining judgment and commencing enforcement against a debtor's assets.

**There is no general requirement under Thai civil procedure that creditors wait until all appeals have concluded before enforcing a judgment.**

#### Section 231 Expressly Rejects Automatic Suspension

Section 231 of Thailand's Civil Procedure Code provides that filing an appeal does not suspend enforcement of the judgment or order of the court of first instance.[^447]

Instead, an appellant seeking to prevent enforcement must pursue the appropriate procedure for obtaining a stay of execution.

The appellate court may grant a stay where justified, potentially subject to conditions.

Depending on the circumstances, the court may require the appellant to deposit funds, provide security, or give undertakings concerning the disposal of assets.

The law also provides mechanisms involving payment or security sufficient to cover the judgment debt and associated costs.

**The critical distinction is between an appeal, which does not automatically prevent enforcement, and an effective stay of execution, which can suspend enforcement.**

TVC's guide does not explain this distinction.

#### Thai Litigation Practitioners Confirm the Rule

The law firm's published translation of Section 231, reproduced by WSR Law Group, confirms that an appeal does not itself prevent enforcement.[^448]

Louis Group's 2026 debt-recovery guidance independently explains that a creditor may pursue enforcement after judgment where the debtor has failed to comply and no applicable stay prevents execution.[^449]

The firm also identifies the separate procedural steps required to obtain a writ of execution and pursue seizure or attachment through Thailand's Legal Execution Department.

Therefore, a favorable judgment does not mean money is collected automatically, but neither does a pending appeal necessarily prevent collection.

#### Misleading Creditors About Their Enforcement Rights

TVC's incorrect instruction could cause creditors to delay enforcement unnecessarily while debtors continue holding or disposing of assets.

That delay matters particularly in disputes involving insolvent businesses, unpaid commercial invoices, investment losses, and debtors who may move money or property before enforcement.

An unsuccessful defendant's appeal might continue for months or years, but **the existence of that appeal alone does not automatically deprive the successful claimant of enforcement rights**.

The practical consequences of waiting can be substantial if assets disappear or become more difficult to locate.

#### Another Error in a Supposedly Reviewed Legal Guide

Thai Visa Centre advertises referrals to licensed Thai litigation lawyers while publishing instructions that misstate a fundamental rule of civil procedure.

Its debt-recovery publication acknowledges that winning a judgment is insufficient without execution, yet simultaneously instructs creditors to delay that execution until appeal proceedings are complete.

**Thai Visa Centre should correct its debt-recovery guide to explain Section 231, distinguish appeals from stays of execution, and identify the procedures for enforcing first-instance judgments while an appeal is pending.**

This is a basic procedural distinction that a publication offering guidance on Thai debt recovery should not misrepresent.


### Civil Case Filing Guide Misidentifies Case Numbers and Omits Mandatory Electronic Procedures

Thai Visa Centre's *Civil Case Filing in Thailand*, marked reviewed June 2026, contains basic errors about how Thai courts register lawsuits and how litigants submit documents.

The guide incorrectly identifies the case number assigned when proceedings begin, calls physical courthouse submission the dominant filing method, and describes electronic filing as limited without explaining the judiciary's established digital procedures.[^489]

**These discrepancies concern fundamental aspects of Thailand's civil justice system, not obscure technicalities.**

#### Confuses Black and Red Case Numbers

TVC's filing instructions state:

> Court registers the case and issues a red case number.

This reverses the terminology ordinarily used by Thailand's judiciary.

When a court registers a new case, it assigns a **black case number (คดีหมายเลขดำ)**. A **red case number (คดีหมายเลขแดง)** is assigned when the case is decided or otherwise formally disposed of.

The judiciary's own case-tracking portal identifies the number used to search existing proceedings as the *black case number*, confirming the initial registration terminology.[^490]

**A newly registered lawsuit does not ordinarily receive a red case number merely because a complaint has been filed.**

Case-number terminology matters when litigants contact the court, track hearings, request records, and communicate with counsel.

#### Physical Filing Is Not the Dominant Procedure TVC Describes

TVC's procedural summary states:

> Physical filing at court is dominant. E-filing is limited. Your lawyer handles submission.

It subsequently instructs plaintiffs to pay court fees at the filing counter and submit complaints through a lawyer in person.[^489]

Thailand's Office of the Judiciary, however, has operated e-Filing for years. In its January 2026 announcement about a major platform upgrade, the judiciary reported that **more than 85% of civil cases were already being filed electronically** through its existing e-Filing infrastructure.[^491]

That official statistic directly contradicts TVC's categorical assertion that physical filing dominates, although individual court procedures and eligible case types still vary.

#### The Judiciary Introduced an Upgraded System in January 2026

On January 5, 2026, Thailand's Office of the Judiciary launched an upgraded e-Filing platform with enhanced identity verification, electronic forms, notifications of court orders and hearings, and expanded digital case management.[^491][^492]

The initial rollout covered two specific categories:

- Consumer cases in which business operators bring claims before courts authorized to hear consumer disputes.
- Applications for recognition of a child where the mother consents, heard in juvenile and family courts.

For **consumer cases filed by business operators from January 5, 2026**, the judiciary directed new filings through the upgraded e-Filing system. Cases already filed before that date could continue using the previous e-Filing v.3 system until completion.[^491]

For qualifying child-recognition cases, the upgraded electronic channel was introduced as an **additional option**, rather than a universal electronic-filing mandate.

**These distinctions are essential: not every court proceeding became subject to compulsory electronic filing, but certain newly filed consumer claims did.**

#### Electronic Preparation and Payment Follow Different Schedules

The judiciary's announcement also distinguishes between preparing an electronic complaint and completing payment or submission steps.

Applicants can enter and save draft complaint information electronically at any time, but payments must be completed during official working hours, generally **8:30 a.m. to 4:30 p.m.** Certain other electronic submissions likewise remain subject to official service hours.[^491]

TVC's blanket instruction to pay at a physical courthouse counter omits these procedures and could mislead applicants about how and when to complete a valid filing.

The changes were already in effect when TVC's guide was supposedly reviewed in June 2026.

#### Consequences for Litigants

Black and red case numbers identify different procedural stages. Confusing them can complicate case tracking and communications with courts.

Likewise, incorrectly describing physical filing as dominant and omitting electronic-filing requirements can cause unnecessary courthouse visits, incorrect submission preparations, and confusion about the correct channel.

The evidence does not establish that TVC caused individual customers to miss deadlines or that its own lawyers filed cases improperly.

It establishes that the agency's published guide contains an elementary case-numbering error and overlooks substantial procedures publicly confirmed by the judiciary.

**Thai Visa Centre should correct its black-versus-red case-number explanation, retract its assertion that physical filing dominates, and identify the judiciary's applicable electronic-filing platforms and January 2026 case-specific requirements.**

An agency advertising litigation referrals should accurately explain how Thai courts receive, register, and track proceedings.


### Civil Litigation Guide Misrepresents Recovery of Court Fees

Thai Visa Centre's *Civil Cases: Guide for Foreigners*, marked reviewed June 2026, misrepresents Thailand's rules governing litigation expenses by suggesting that successful plaintiffs generally cannot recover their court fees.[^436]

The guidance contradicts Section 161 of Thailand's Civil Procedure Code and the cost-recovery principles established under Thai civil procedure.

#### Incorrectly Claims Court Fees Are Rarely Recoverable

In its frequently asked questions, TVC states:

> Factor fees into settlement math because they are rarely fully recoverable even if you win.

Elsewhere, the same publication warns readers about spending money on filing fees that opposing parties may never repay.[^436]

These statements present limited recovery as the normal outcome, without explaining the statutory rule governing responsibility for litigation costs.

**Thailand does not operate under a general rule requiring successful civil litigants to absorb their own court fees.**

#### Section 161 Establishes the Losing-Party-Pays Principle

Section 161 of Thailand's Civil Procedure Code expressly establishes that the unsuccessful party is generally responsible for court fees and litigation costs.[^437]

The legislation also gives courts discretion to allocate costs differently based on the circumstances, including the reasonableness and good faith of the parties' conduct.

This means a successful litigant may recover court fees through a costs order, while the amount and allocation remain subject to judicial determination.

International law firm DLA Piper confirms that court fees are recoverable in principle when a civil claim succeeds.[^438]

Tilleke & Gibbins likewise identifies the losing-party-pays principle as Thailand's general rule for litigation costs.[^439]

**TVC's suggestion that court fees are rarely fully recoverable reverses the statutory starting position.**

#### Confuses Court Fees With Private Legal Expenses

A critical distinction exists between court fees and the actual fees paid to privately retained lawyers.

Thai courts may award legal costs against the losing party, but recoverable attorney fees are commonly limited by statutory schedules and judicial assessment.

Consequently, even successful litigants may recover only a fraction of their actual legal representation expenses.

However, this limitation does not establish that court filing fees are generally unrecoverable.

**TVC's guidance fails to distinguish court fees, recoverable litigation costs, and private attorney fees**, leaving readers with a misleading picture of the financial consequences of civil litigation.

#### Financial Consequences for Foreign Litigants

Foreign investors, tenants, creditors, and business owners assessing whether to pursue civil claims need accurate information about potential litigation costs.

Misrepresenting cost recovery can distort settlement calculations and discourage claimants from pursuing legitimate remedies.

The problem is especially relevant because Thai Visa Centre advertises referrals to licensed litigation lawyers while publishing procedural guidance for prospective claimants.

**A business presenting itself as a source of professional legal guidance should accurately explain the statutory cost-recovery framework rather than suggest that winning parties ordinarily cannot recover their court fees.**

Thai Visa Centre should correct its civil litigation guide to explain Section 161, distinguish court fees from privately incurred attorney expenses, and acknowledge the judiciary's discretion when allocating costs.

Across these guides, TVC misstates court-registration and electronic-filing procedures, presents lawyers as mandatory, describes the appeal deadline as 30 days instead of one month, tells creditors they must wait for appeals to end before enforcing judgments, and says court fees are rarely recoverable.[^436][^440][^441][^446][^447][^489][^512][^513][^514] Several of the pages carry June 2026 review dates.

## Misleading Document Authentication, Notarial Services, and Power-of-Attorney Requirements

TVC's document-authentication and power-of-attorney guides misstate professional requirements in opposite directions. Its notary guidance omits the specialist registration required for formal notarial services, while its power-of-attorney guidance presents lawyer involvement as a prerequisite without explaining when individuals can complete the relevant documents and transactions directly.[^443][^450]

These distinctions matter because preparing a legal authorization, certifying a signature or document, registering a property transfer, and legalizing documents for international use are separate procedures governed by different requirements. The forthcoming Apostille framework adds a further distinction between present and future international authentication procedures.[^444][^451][^452][^453][^454][^456][^457]

### Notary Guide Omits Mandatory Specialist Registration for Notarial Services Attorneys

Thai Visa Centre's *Notary Public in Thailand: Document Authentication Guide*, marked reviewed June 2026, provides incomplete and potentially misleading advice about the qualifications required to authenticate signatures and documents in Thailand.[^443]

The publication correctly explains that Thailand does not have a standalone notary public profession equivalent to the American system.

However, it fails to identify Thailand's established system of specially trained and registered **Notarial Services Attorneys**, instead repeatedly presenting ordinary Thai lawyer licensing as the relevant qualification.

#### Incorrectly Treats Ordinary Lawyer Licensing as Sufficient

TVC's document-authentication instructions state:

> A licensed Thai lawyer certifies copies or witnesses signatures.

The guide's frequently asked questions also address whether any lawyer can certify documents:

> Licensed Thai lawyers certify copies and witness signatures.

It recommends verifying a lawyer's ordinary professional registration with the Lawyers Council of Thailand.[^443]

**This advice omits a critical distinction between ordinary legal practice and performing formal notarial certification.**

Although lawyers may witness signatures or certify certain documents in other professional contexts, formal notarial services are subject to additional registration requirements.

#### Lawyers Council Regulations Establish a Specialist Qualification

Thailand's Lawyers Council maintains a separate regulatory system for lawyers authorized to certify signatures and documents as Notarial Services Attorneys.

Its regulations, published in the Royal Gazette on May 13, 2024, require applicants to:

- Hold qualifications to practise as lawyers.
- Complete the Lawyers Council's prescribed specialist training.
- Apply separately for registration as a Notarial Services Attorney.
- Obtain an official registration certificate, registration number, and seal.

The registration certificate is valid for three years, subject to renewal requirements.[^444]

**An ordinary lawyer's professional licence does not, by itself, establish that the lawyer holds the specialist authorization required for formal notarial services.**

#### Requirements Were Reaffirmed in 2026

On March 9, 2026, the Lawyers Council announced amendments to its specialist training regulations.

The revised curriculum covers certified document copies, signature authentication, sworn statements, legal-entity certification, electronic notarization, professional responsibilities, and the use of official seals.

It also addresses the relationship between Thai document certification, international legalization, and the Apostille Convention.[^445]

These requirements were already established when TVC's publication was supposedly reviewed in June 2026.

Nevertheless, the guide neither identifies the specialist qualification nor instructs customers to verify it.

### Guides Ignore Thailand's Confirmed Apostille Convention Accession

Thai Visa Centre's document-authentication guidance also omits a major development in Thailand's international legalization system.

Its *Notary Public in Thailand* guide describes Thailand as lacking a comprehensive Apostille system and presents Ministry of Foreign Affairs legalization followed by embassy authentication as the standard process.[^443]

Its separate *Notary Public Services in Thailand* publication similarly states:

> Thailand is not full apostille state for all docs.

Neither guide explains that Thailand has already formally acceded to the Hague Apostille Convention or identifies the scheduled implementation date.[^455]

**Thailand's participation in the convention is no longer merely a proposal. Its accession has been officially completed.**

#### Thailand Deposited Its Instrument of Accession in June 2026

On June 30, 2026, Thailand deposited its instrument of accession to the 1961 Hague Convention Abolishing the Requirement of Legalisation for Foreign Public Documents.

The Hague Conference on Private International Law officially announced the accession that day.[^456]

Thailand's Ministry of Foreign Affairs separately confirmed the deposit and explained that the convention is scheduled to enter into force for Thailand on **February 28, 2027**, following the prescribed eight-month objection period.[^457]

The convention simplifies international authentication of eligible public documents by replacing traditional consular legalization requirements with an Apostille certificate between countries where the convention applies.

Its operation remains subject to the convention's rules, including any objections to Thailand's accession.

#### Current Rules and Upcoming Changes Must Be Distinguished

As of November 2026, Thailand has not yet reached the convention's scheduled entry-into-force date.

Consequently, TVC is not necessarily incorrect to describe existing consular legalization procedures as the current standard.

**The omission is its failure to explain that a confirmed transition is approaching.**

This distinction matters for foreigners preparing documents for international property transactions, immigration applications, marriages, academic qualifications, and corporate registrations.

Applicants whose deadlines fall before February 28, 2027, generally need to follow the existing authentication requirements.

Applicants preparing documents for use after the convention takes effect should check whether the Apostille procedure will apply to their documents and destination country.

Not every document is covered, and the convention does not eliminate separate translation requirements or the receiving authority's substantive documentation rules.

#### Another Gap in TVC's Document-Authentication Guidance

Thai Visa Centre promotes assistance coordinating document preparation and legalization, yet its guides omit one of Thailand's most consequential developments in cross-border document authentication.

The agency's principal notary guide is marked reviewed June 2026. Because the accession occurred on June 30, that review date alone does not establish whether the omission was present when the guide was last reviewed.

Nevertheless, the omission remains relevant to readers consulting the publications in November 2026.

**Thai Visa Centre should update both guides to acknowledge Thailand's completed accession, identify February 28, 2027 as the scheduled effective date, and distinguish current consular legalization procedures from the forthcoming Apostille framework.**

Accurate guidance should explain both what applies today and the confirmed legal changes affecting customers' upcoming document submissions.

#### Misleading Customers About Document Authentication

Foreigners commonly require authenticated documents for international banking, property transactions, immigration applications, corporate filings, and proceedings before foreign authorities.

A person following TVC's advice might verify that a lawyer is licensed without determining whether that lawyer is authorized to perform the particular notarial certification required.

This creates a risk of paying for documents that do not satisfy the receiving authority's requirements.

The distinction also matters because formal notarial certification, Ministry of Foreign Affairs legalization, and embassy authentication are separate procedures. Completing one does not automatically satisfy the others.

**Thai Visa Centre should correct its notary guide to explain the Notarial Services Attorney qualification, distinguish ordinary lawyer certifications from formal notarial acts, and advise customers to verify specialist registration and the requirements of the receiving authority.**

A publication specifically advertising expertise in document authentication should not omit the professional credential central to the service it describes.


### Power-of-Attorney Guide Misrepresents Lawyer Requirements for Property Transfers

Thai Visa Centre's *Power of Attorney in Thailand: Property, Court, and Corporate Uses*, previously marked reviewed June 2026, presents lawyer involvement as a requirement for document preparation and binding legal work without adequately distinguishing optional advice from mandatory formalities.[^450]

The publication repeatedly promotes lawyer involvement while failing to explain that individuals can execute valid powers of attorney themselves and generally conduct property transactions directly through Thailand's Department of Lands.

#### Presents Licensed Lawyers as a Prerequisite

The earlier version of this investigation recorded the following statement from the guide:

> POA drafting and property transfers require licensed Thai attorneys.

That exact sentence could not be reproduced in the accessible version checked on November 24, 2026, and should not be treated as a verified current quotation.

However, the accessible guide still directs readers to draft their power of attorney with Thai counsel as a step in its recommended procedure. It also says the subject requires licensed Thai advocates for binding legal work, recommends retaining counsel before filing at an amphoe or court, and advertises referrals to partner firms.[^450]

**The guide still fails to distinguish optional legal assistance from the mandatory formalities applicable to the particular transaction.**

A power of attorney is a legal authorization allowing one person to act on another person's behalf. It does not inherently require preparation by a lawyer.

Likewise, transferring ownership of property involves registration with the competent Land Office, not a general requirement to retain a licensed attorney.

#### The Civil and Commercial Code Does Not Require Lawyer Preparation

Sections 797 and 798 of Thailand's Civil and Commercial Code govern agency appointments and their formal requirements.[^451]

Section 797 establishes that one person may authorize another to act as an agent.

Section 798 requires written authorization where the underlying transaction must itself be made or evidenced in writing.

Neither provision establishes a blanket requirement that the document must be drafted by a licensed lawyer.

Section 801 further distinguishes ordinary management authority from special transactions, including selling or mortgaging immovable property, which require specific authorization.

**The legal issue is whether the agent has valid authority in the required form, not whether a lawyer was paid to prepare the document.**

#### Thailand's Department of Lands Provides Official Forms

The Department of Lands publishes standardized power-of-attorney documents directly on its official website.

These include:

- Form Tor Dor 21 for transactions involving titled land.
- Power-of-attorney forms for condominium transactions.
- Additional forms for other Land Office registration procedures.

The Department's downloadable forms were available in October 2025, well before TVC's claimed June 2026 review.[^452]

Property owners can complete the appropriate forms and follow the relevant identity verification, witnessing, and certification procedures.

Foreign-executed documents may require additional authentication, but these requirements do not establish mandatory lawyer involvement.

#### Thai Embassies Confirm Direct Execution Procedures

The Royal Thai Embassy in Canberra provides instructions and downloadable forms for individuals granting powers of attorney from overseas.

Its guidance specifically covers land transactions using Tor Dor 21 and condominium transactions using Or Chor 21.[^453]

The embassy explains how applicants can sign documents before consular officials or complete the prescribed authentication process when applying by post.

These procedures provide an official route for executing powers of attorney without engaging a Thai lawyer merely to draft the document.

The receiving Land Office ultimately determines whether the completed authorization satisfies its requirements.

#### Property Transfers Are Registered by Land Officials

Thailand's Board of Investment also publishes a procedural explanation of property registration.

Its guidance describes applications being submitted to the relevant Land Office, where government officials examine documents, prepare the transfer contract, calculate fees, and register the transaction.[^454]
