The buyer and seller sign the documents, pay the applicable charges, and receive the registered title.

The procedure does not prescribe mandatory representation by a licensed Thai lawyer.

Qualified legal assistance may be advisable for complex transactions, disputed ownership, contractual risks, or due diligence. However, **professional advice being advisable does not make it legally compulsory**.

#### Misleading Customers About Paid Legal Services

TVC's broad instructions could lead property owners and foreign residents to believe they must purchase legal services before preparing a power of attorney or transferring eligible property.

The publication also advertises referrals to Thai legal professionals, creating a direct commercial context for its instructions concerning lawyer involvement.

**Thai Visa Centre should correct its guide to distinguish mandatory legal formalities from optional professional assistance.**

It should explain the Department of Lands' official forms, the applicable Civil and Commercial Code provisions, embassy authentication procedures, and the circumstances in which specialist legal advice is useful.

Presenting optional lawyer involvement as a statutory requirement is particularly misleading in a guide supposedly intended to help foreigners understand their legal options.


### Distinguishing Mandatory Formalities From Optional Legal Assistance

| Procedure | Actual requirement |
| --- | --- |
| Preparing a power of attorney | Valid authorization in the required form, including specific authority where necessary; no general requirement for lawyer drafting.[^451][^452] |
| Formal notarial certification | A lawyer holding the specialist Notarial Services Attorney registration where that form of certification is required.[^444][^445] |
| Property ownership transfer | Compliance with ownership and transaction requirements and registration by the competent Land Office; no general requirement for lawyer representation.[^454] |
| Overseas document execution | The applicable witnessing, consular authentication or legalization requirements of the receiving authority.[^453] |
| Apostille certification | A forthcoming framework for eligible public documents between applicable countries, scheduled to take effect for Thailand on February 28, 2027, subject to the Convention's rules.[^456][^457] |

### Commercial Significance for Customers

The guides promote professional referrals but do not consistently explain which qualifications and formalities the relevant procedures actually require. A customer may therefore overlook a necessary specialist credential when seeking formal notarial certification or mistakenly believe that ordinary document preparation and property registration require private legal representation.

TVC should distinguish the scope of each service, the receiving authority's requirements, and the circumstances in which professional assistance is useful or legally necessary. The published guidance establishes these risks of misunderstanding, not evidence that a particular customer incurred unnecessary costs or submitted an invalid document.


## Misleading Privacy Policies, Data Protection Responsibilities, and PDPA Compliance

Thai Visa Centre publishes conflicting and incomplete guidance concerning legal representation for privacy compliance, eligibility of younger customers to submit personal data, and the responsibilities of organizations that collect and process customer information. Its own policies also raise unresolved questions about retention, indirect collection, immigration documents, and rights claimed over submitted material.

These issues involve different provisions of Thailand's Personal Data Protection Act (PDPA) and different contractual documents. **The common problem is that TVC advertises services handling confidential immigration information and professional privacy-related assistance while its public guidance does not consistently explain the agency's obligations or customers' rights.** The findings below distinguish contradictions in published terms from evidence of actual misuse of personal information.

### PDPA Guide Falsely Claims Privacy Compliance Requires Licensed Thai Lawyers

Thai Visa Centre's *PDPA Consulting and Training in Thailand*, marked reviewed June 2026, incorrectly presents licensed Thai legal representation as a mandatory requirement for complying with Thailand's Personal Data Protection Act.[^458]

The claim misrepresents who is responsible for privacy compliance and potentially encourages businesses to purchase professional legal services they are not generally required to obtain.

#### Explicitly Claims Licensed Lawyers Are Required

The guide concludes:

> PDPA compliance requires licensed Thai attorneys.

Elsewhere, the publication states that PDPA consulting requires licensed Thai advocates for binding legal work.

It repeatedly directs employers and technology businesses toward lawyer referrals while describing routine privacy compliance activities, including data mapping, consent records, staff training, security measures, and vendor agreements.[^458]

**These activities do not inherently require licensed Thai attorneys.**

Qualified legal advice can be valuable when interpreting complicated statutory obligations, handling regulatory investigations, or defending litigation.

However, the practical advantages of legal assistance do not establish a general legal requirement to retain a lawyer.

#### The PDPA Assigns Compliance Duties to Organizations

Thailand's Personal Data Protection Act B.E. 2562 (2019) expressly identifies the persons and organizations responsible for compliance.

Section 37 establishes duties for data controllers, including appropriate security measures, data retention and deletion procedures, and reporting qualifying personal data breaches.

Section 39 establishes recordkeeping obligations, subject to applicable exceptions.

Section 40 separately establishes duties for data processors, including following lawful controller instructions, implementing security measures, and maintaining processing records.[^459]

**None of these provisions requires organizations to hire licensed lawyers to perform ordinary compliance activities.**

Companies may implement appropriate policies through their management, information technology departments, human resources personnel, privacy specialists, and other qualified employees or service providers.

The legal obligation concerns compliance with the statute, not purchasing a particular category of professional services.

#### Data Protection Officers Are Not Required to Be Lawyers

Section 41 establishes circumstances in which data controllers and processors must designate a Data Protection Officer (DPO).

The statutory triggers concern public authorities, certain large-scale monitoring activities, and processing involving categories of sensitive personal data.

Crucially, Section 41 expressly permits the DPO to be either an employee or an external service provider.[^459]

Section 42 describes the DPO's responsibilities, including advising on compliance, monitoring organizational practices, cooperating with regulators, and maintaining confidentiality.

**The legislation does not require DPOs to hold Thai lawyers' licences.**

On November 17, 2026, Thailand's Personal Data Protection Committee opened public consultation on draft DPO qualification standards. The draft would require a bachelor's degree or equivalent in any field, relevant data-protection knowledge and expertise, at least one year of related work experience, approved DPO training, and continuing professional development. It also proposes a 180-day preparation period after the final notification is published.[^460]

These are proposed DPO qualifications, not requirements currently in force. The listed criteria do not include a Thai lawyer's licence. They concern the qualifications of a Data Protection Officer where an organization must appoint one; they do not turn ordinary PDPA compliance work into a service that only lawyers may perform.

#### Misleading Businesses About Compliance Costs

TVC's assertion could lead employers, website operators, and foreign-owned businesses to believe they must retain lawyers before implementing ordinary privacy protections.

That is particularly misleading because organizations remain responsible for compliance regardless of whether they engage external counsel.

Hiring a lawyer does not replace the need for appropriate technical safeguards, internal policies, data inventories, staff training, or operational procedures.

Indeed, TVC's own guide correctly acknowledges that publishing a privacy policy alone is insufficient.

Yet its concluding assertion about mandatory licensed attorneys incorrectly converts a broader organizational responsibility into an apparent professional-services requirement.

#### Commercial Referrals and Editorial Accuracy

Thai Visa Centre advertises referrals to licensed legal professionals while publishing compliance guidance for prospective business customers.

This creates a commercial context in which inaccurate statements about mandatory lawyer involvement deserve scrutiny.

The publication does not distinguish situations requiring qualified courtroom representation from everyday regulatory compliance activities.

**Thai Visa Centre should correct its PDPA guide to acknowledge that organizations can manage compliance internally or through appropriate external specialists without a general obligation to hire licensed Thai attorneys.**

The guide should distinguish statutory DPO appointment requirements, optional legal advice, regulatory representation, and operational privacy responsibilities.

A business promoting professional legal referrals should not present optional legal services as universally mandatory under Thai law.


### TVC's Age Requirements Contradict Its Own Privacy Policy

Thai Visa Centre publishes conflicting eligibility requirements in its Terms & Conditions and Privacy Policy, both updated February 9, 2025.[^519][^520]

The Terms & Conditions expressly permit individuals aged 16 and older to use the website and services.

However, the Privacy Policy's *Privacy of Children* section instructs individuals under 20 not to submit personal information through those same services.

**TVC therefore permits 16- to 19-year-olds to use its services while simultaneously instructing them not to provide the personal information necessary to obtain many of those services.**

#### Thailand's PDPA Does Not Establish a Universal Prohibition

Section 20 of Thailand's Personal Data Protection Act establishes specific rules for obtaining consent involving minors.[^459]

These rules distinguish between circumstances requiring parental authorization and activities for which minors can lawfully act independently. Special provisions apply to children younger than ten.

The law does not establish a universal prohibition against collecting information concerning everyone under 20.

Indeed, TVC's own Privacy Policy acknowledges that parents may submit their children's information when applying for immigration services.

#### Conflicting Instructions for Customers and Families

The company's published policies fail to distinguish clearly between:

- Individuals permitted to browse the website.
- Minors permitted to purchase or request services.
- Individuals authorized to submit personal information.
- Parents or legal guardians submitting information for children.

These distinctions are particularly important for immigration services involving families and dependent applicants.

**TVC's policies leave eligible teenage users with contradictory instructions about whether they may actually use the services advertised to them.**

The evidence does not establish unlawful processing of minors' information or improper handling of any particular application.

It establishes that the company's published contractual and privacy requirements are inconsistent.

Thai Visa Centre should reconcile its minimum service age with its personal-information submission rules and explain how parental authorization and applications involving minors are handled under Thailand's PDPA.

### TVC's Privacy Policy Incorrectly Assigns Data Controller Responsibilities to Customers

Thai Visa Centre's Privacy Policy, updated February 9, 2025, incorrectly describes the legal relationship between the company and customers submitting personal information through its website.[^520]

Under *Use and Processing of Collected Information*, the policy states:

> In such instances, the User providing Personal Information acts as a data controller.

The same passage claims that TVC acts merely as a data processor when users submit personal information through its services, because the company supposedly does not control that information.

#### Thailand's PDPA Establishes Different Legal Responsibilities

Section 6 of Thailand's Personal Data Protection Act defines a **data controller** as the person or organization responsible for deciding how personal information is collected, used, or disclosed.

A **data processor** handles information under the instructions of a separate data controller.[^459]

**A customer does not become a data controller merely by submitting their own personal information to a commercial service.**

Ordinary visa applicants supplying their personal details are generally data subjects, not organizations directing TVC's processing activities.

Different arrangements may apply where a business customer supplies information about other individuals under a genuine controller-processor relationship. However, TVC's broad statement does not explain that distinction.

#### TVC's Own Policy Contradicts Its Processor Classification

Elsewhere, the Privacy Policy explicitly states that TVC determines the purposes and methods of processing information necessary for access to its services.

The company also identifies its own purposes for collecting customer information, including:

- Managing accounts and service orders.
- Processing payments and delivering services.
- Publishing customer testimonials.
- Marketing, customer feedback, and service improvements.

These activities demonstrate that TVC determines at least some purposes for processing personal information.

**The company cannot accurately characterize every customer submitting personal information as its data controller while simultaneously describing independent decisions about how that information is used.**

#### Published Privacy Policies Omit Required Retention and Data-Subject Rights Information

Thai Visa Centre's published Privacy Policy describes the personal information it collects and identifies various purposes for processing that information. However, it omits important disclosures expressly required by Thailand's Personal Data Protection Act.[^520]

Section 23 of the PDPA generally requires data controllers to inform individuals, before or when collecting personal information, about:

- The purposes and necessity of collection.
- The categories of information collected and applicable retention periods.
- The categories of recipients to whom information may be disclosed.
- The identity and contact details of the responsible data controller.
- The statutory rights available to the individual.[^459]

**TVC's Privacy Policy identifies no retention period or expected retention standard for the personal information it collects.**

The document also fails to explain the full range of statutory data-subject rights, including rights concerning erasure, objection, restriction of processing, and data portability.

Although the introduction mentions accessing and updating information, and the policy separately discusses deletion requests involving children, these limited references do not adequately explain the broader rights established by the PDPA.

#### Privacy Policy Permits Collection From Unidentified Marketing Partners

Thai Visa Centre's Privacy Policy expressly permits the company to collect personal information from sources other than its customers.[^520]

Under *Collection of Personal Information*, the company identifies two additional sources:

- Public databases.
- Joint marketing partners.

**The policy does not identify those marketing partners, explain what categories of personal information they provide, or describe the arrangements under which customer information is obtained from them.**

This disclosure deserves scrutiny because it potentially extends TVC's information collection beyond individuals who knowingly contact the agency or submit immigration applications.

#### Thailand's PDPA Restricts Indirect Collection

Section 25 of Thailand's Personal Data Protection Act specifically regulates collecting personal information from sources other than the data subject.[^459]

The provision generally requires a data controller to:

- Inform the individual about the indirect collection without delay, within 30 days.
- Obtain the individual's consent, unless an exception under Sections 24 or 26 applies.
- Provide the applicable information notices required under Sections 21 and 23, subject to statutory exceptions.

**The fact that information is available in a public database or obtained through a commercial marketing relationship does not automatically exempt its collection from the PDPA.**

TVC's published Privacy Policy does not explain how it satisfies these requirements when obtaining information from marketing partners or public databases.

Nor does it distinguish information collected for an existing immigration-service relationship from information acquired for marketing or customer acquisition.

#### The Disclosure Leaves Important Questions Unanswered

The company's published wording raises several questions:

1. Which marketing partners provide personal information to TVC?
2. What information is obtained from those partners?
3. What lawful basis supports the collection?
4. How are affected individuals notified?
5. Can information about individuals who have never contacted TVC be added to its customer or marketing records?

These questions are especially relevant because the same Privacy Policy identifies customer communications, feedback requests, testimonials, and service updates among its processing purposes.[^520]

**TVC publicly reserves the ability to collect information from outside sources without adequately explaining the accompanying privacy safeguards or notification procedures.**

The available evidence does not establish that TVC has actually purchased marketing databases, collected information without a lawful basis, or failed to notify particular individuals. Additional private notices or consent procedures may exist.

What is established is that the company expressly contemplates indirect collection while its public privacy disclosures leave the applicable procedures unexplained.

For an immigration business handling identity and financial records, **unidentified marketing partnerships should not obscure where personal information originates or which organization is accountable for collecting it lawfully**.

#### Privacy Policy Omits Passport and Financial Documents Requested From Customers

Thai Visa Centre's Privacy Policy describes the personal information it collects using three general categories:

- Account details, including usernames and passwords.
- Contact information.
- Basic personal information, including names and countries of residence.[^520]

However, the agency's own *Contact Thai Visa Centre* guide directs prospective customers to prepare substantially more detailed documentation before beginning immigration services.[^522]

Its published consultation workflow requests:

- Copies of passport identification pages.
- Current visa stamps and immigration records.
- Embassy or immigration refusal notices.
- Supporting financial or employment records.

The guide also explicitly describes a document-upload stage during which customers provide passport and immigration documentation.

**None of these immigration-specific document categories is explicitly identified in TVC's published Privacy Policy.**

#### Terms & Conditions Authorize Additional Financial Documentation

TVC's Terms & Conditions introduce another category of potentially sensitive customer records.

Under *Billing and payments*, the company reserves the right to demand copies of government-issued photo identification and recent bank statements when it classifies a purchase as a high-risk transaction.[^519]

These records go beyond the basic account and contact information emphasized in its Privacy Policy.

Although the policy's examples are not presented as an exhaustive list, **TVC does not clearly explain the categories of identity, immigration, and financial documentation its own service instructions contemplate collecting**.

Section 23 of Thailand's Personal Data Protection Act requires appropriate disclosure of the personal information being collected, its purposes, and applicable retention periods.[^459]

The discrepancy is significant because passport records, immigration histories, and financial documents can expose customers to identity theft, financial fraud, and other harms if improperly disclosed.

The available evidence does not establish that TVC has mishandled these records or that additional notices are absent from its private application process.

It establishes that **TVC's public-facing Privacy Policy does not adequately describe several important categories of documentation its own published customer instructions request**.

Thai Visa Centre should reconcile its privacy disclosures with its actual document-collection procedures, including the purposes, retention standards, and responsible legal entity for handling immigration and financial records.

#### Disclaimer Claims Perpetual Publication Rights Over Submitted Information

Thai Visa Centre's published Disclaimer contains an extraordinarily broad provision granting the agency rights over information submitted through its website or otherwise provided to the company.[^518]

Under *Content and postings*, the company claims an **unlimited, perpetual right** to distribute, display, publish, reproduce, reuse, and copy submitted information.

**The provision does not expressly exclude confidential customer records, passport documents, immigration applications, or financial information.**

#### The Agency Requests Confidential Immigration Documents

Thai Visa Centre's own consultation instructions ask prospective customers to submit passport copies, visa stamps, immigration refusal notices, and supporting financial or employment records.[^522]

These are not ordinary public comments or marketing testimonials. They are confidential documents provided for specific immigration and professional-service purposes.

Although the disputed provision appears under a heading concerning website content and postings, its wording refers broadly to information uploaded or otherwise made available to the company.

**That wording creates an alarming ambiguity over whether TVC claims publication and reuse rights extending beyond public testimonials to confidential customer submissions.**

The available evidence does not establish that TVC has actually published private immigration documents under this clause.

#### Perpetual Publication Rights Conflict With Privacy Protections

Thailand's Personal Data Protection Act imposes restrictions on collecting, using, and disclosing personal information.

Section 19 establishes requirements for valid consent, including clear disclosure of processing purposes and the ability to withdraw consent, subject to applicable exceptions.[^459]

Section 21 generally restricts processing to previously notified purposes unless a lawful exception applies.

**A sweeping contractual permission to publish submitted information does not automatically override these statutory protections.**

TVC's Privacy Policy separately describes its commitment to safeguarding customer information and limiting unauthorized disclosures.[^520]

However, neither document clearly reconciles those assurances with the Disclaimer's unlimited, perpetual publication language.

#### The Agency Must Clarify What Information Is Excluded

Thai Visa Centre should explain whether its publication rights apply solely to voluntary public comments, testimonials, and other material expressly intended for publication.

It should also explicitly exclude confidential customer documents and personal information submitted for immigration services.

**A visa agency handling passports, financial records, and sensitive immigration histories should not leave customers guessing whether its legal terms reserve perpetual rights to publish their submissions.**

The documented problem is the breadth and ambiguity of TVC's contractual language, not proof that confidential customer information has already been improperly disclosed.

#### Separate Data Protection Policy Does Not Resolve the Omissions

Thai Visa Centre also publishes a *Data Protection Policy*, updated February 9, 2025, promising internal safeguards, responsible management of personal information, and compliance with Thailand's PDPA.[^521]

However, that document likewise provides no specific retention periods, expected retention standards, or meaningful explanation of the statutory rights available to customers.

**Publishing general assurances about data protection does not substitute for disclosing the information required by Section 23.**

This omission is particularly significant for a visa agency handling identity documents, immigration applications, financial information, and other sensitive customer records.

The available evidence does not establish whether TVC provides additional privacy notices during individual applications or through separate customer agreements.

It does establish that **neither of its publicly published privacy policies supplies several disclosures specifically contemplated by Thailand's data-protection legislation**.

Thai Visa Centre should identify the responsible controller, explain its applicable retention standards, and publish a clear account of customers' statutory rights.

#### Consequences for Customer Privacy and Accountability

The distinction matters because data controllers have statutory responsibilities concerning lawful processing, security, retention, data-subject requests, and qualifying breach notifications.

Misidentifying customers as controllers obscures which organization is responsible for those obligations.

The problem is particularly significant for a visa agency handling sensitive identity documents and immigration-related information.

The published wording does not establish that TVC has unlawfully processed customer data or violated particular individuals' privacy rights.

It does establish that **the agency's own Privacy Policy misrepresents a fundamental distinction under Thailand's data-protection legislation**.

Thai Visa Centre should identify the legal entity acting as controller for its ordinary customer services, explain circumstances involving genuine third-party processors, and stop presenting routine customers as controllers merely because they submit their information.

### Litigation Referrals and Unresolved Legal Confidentiality Protections

Thai Visa Centre advertises litigation support services covering criminal defence, employment disputes, family proceedings, commercial litigation, and other legal matters.[^652][^653]

The agency expressly acknowledges that it is not a litigation law firm.

Nevertheless, its advertised service workflow involves:

- Collecting details of prospective clients' legal disputes.
- Preparing supporting evidence and documentation.
- Coordinating police-related documentation.
- Transferring case information to partner attorneys.
- Maintaining communications between customers and legal counsel.

The agency also claims to verify attorney registration before making referrals but does not identify the participating law firms in the reviewed service descriptions.

#### Professional Confidentiality Versus Commercial Privacy

Thailand's legal professional confidentiality framework establishes specific obligations and protections for licensed lawyers.

As explained by international law firm Baker McKenzie, the applicability of professional privilege to individuals outside the legal profession depends on their status, responsibilities, and the relevant statutory provisions.[^654]

**Information disclosed to an immigration agency before establishing a relationship with licensed legal counsel cannot automatically be assumed to receive the same professional protections as confidential attorney-client communications.**

#### General Commercial Policies Applied to Litigation Customers

Thai Visa Centre's general Privacy Policy permits collected personal information to be used for product development, customer feedback, service updates, and testimonials.[^520]

The policy does not specifically address criminal allegations, litigation evidence, confidential legal correspondence, or the handling of privileged communications.

Its separate Disclaimer also claims broad rights over submitted material, as documented elsewhere in this investigation.[^518]

Neither of the reviewed litigation service descriptions explains whether legal case information is governed by separate confidentiality agreements or excluded from ordinary commercial data-processing activities.

#### Unidentified Legal Partners and Commercial Responsibilities

Thai Visa Centre should identify the law firms participating in its referral network, clarify whether referral compensation is involved, and explain when responsibility for confidential case information passes from the immigration agency to licensed counsel.

The distinction is particularly important for customers facing criminal investigations, immigration enforcement, or sensitive personal disputes.

The available documents do not establish that confidential legal information has been improperly disclosed.

They establish that the agency advertises the collection and transfer of sensitive litigation information without adequately explaining the specific confidentiality arrangements governing that service.

### Conflicting Customer Protections Across TVC's Published Policies

The discrepancies are easier to see when the agency's documents are compared directly:

| TVC document | Documented concern |
|---|---|
| Terms & Conditions | Permits use of its website and services from age 16, without reconciling that permission with the Privacy Policy's different age instruction.[^519] |
| Privacy Policy — children | Instructs individuals under 20 not to submit personal information, creating a conflict for some customers aged 16–19.[^520] |
| Privacy Policy — data processing | Broadly labels users who submit information as controllers and TVC as a processor, despite describing the agency's own decisions about processing purposes.[^520][^459] |
| Privacy Policy and Data Protection Policy | Do not clearly disclose retention standards and several data-subject rights in the published text examined here.[^520][^521] |
| Disclaimer — submitted material | Claims broad perpetual publication and reuse rights without expressly excluding confidential immigration and financial documents.[^518][^522] |
| PDPA consulting guide | Portrays licensed Thai lawyers as legally required for ordinary compliance work, despite no general statutory lawyer-licensing requirement for such activities.[^458][^459][^460] |

**The existence of these clauses does not establish that TVC actually publishes confidential records, unlawfully collects information, or refuses data-subject requests.** The concern is that its public-facing documents do not explain their respective scopes and legal responsibilities consistently.

### Commercial Significance for Immigration Customers

Immigration assistance can involve passports, visa and residence records, financial statements, family information, and other confidential supporting documents. Clear explanations of who determines processing purposes, what information is collected, how long it is retained, and when it may be disclosed therefore matter to prospective customers.

TVC should harmonize its age restrictions and consent arrangements, identify the controller for its ordinary customer services, distinguish genuine processor relationships, clarify notices for direct and indirect collection, and expressly exclude private client records from any blanket publication licence unless a separate lawful basis and appropriate authorization apply. It should also correct its assertion that all ordinary PDPA compliance activities require licensed lawyers.

**The evidence establishes contradictions, ambiguities, and gaps in the agency's published guidance; it does not prove a violation affecting a particular customer.** Private application notices or contractual terms could provide further detail not covered by these public documents.

## TVC Repeatedly Misrepresents Optional Legal Services as Mandatory

TVC makes the same categorical claim across guides covering unrelated services: that customers must hire licensed Thai lawyers to pursue appeals, file labour or consumer claims, register companies, complete property transfers, prepare wills, register marriages, or comply with the PDPA.[^440][^441][^398][^401][^470][^450][^465][^475][^458]

The sections above document the different laws and government procedures governing each subject. Taken together, they show a recurring editorial pattern: TVC presents lawyer involvement as a legal prerequisite even where the cited rules provide no general requirement to hire counsel.

This wording can make readers believe they cannot use available court or government procedures without first paying for private legal services. That is especially significant across guides that also promote referrals to lawyers.

The evidence establishes what TVC’s publications tell readers. It does not establish that a particular customer paid unnecessary fees because of those claims. But the repetition across unrelated legal topics is not an isolated wording error: **TVC repeatedly turns optional professional assistance into an apparent legal mandate.**

## Misleading Thai Embassy and Consular Visa Guidance

Thai Visa Centre publishes an extensive collection of country-specific guides concerning Thai visa applications at overseas diplomatic missions. These guides repeatedly misrepresent official application procedures, consular jurisdiction, eligibility requirements, payment methods, and processing times.

### Malaysia: Contradictory Consular Instructions and Residency Requirements

Thai Visa Centre's Penang guide identifies the Royal Thai Embassy in Kuala Lumpur as the assigned diplomatic mission for applicants in Penang, Perak, Kedah, and Perlis.

However, its application checklist instructs applicants to select the Royal Thai Consulate in Penang instead.[^640]

Both the Penang and Kuala Lumpur guides incorrectly present legal residency as a general application requirement.[^640][^641]

The respective diplomatic missions expressly accept Malaysian immigration arrival records as evidence of an applicant's current location, without universally requiring Malaysian residency status.[^642][^643]

The company's Kuala Lumpur guide also advertises typical processing times of three to ten business days, whereas the embassy specifies seven to fourteen working days for ordinary tourist and non-immigrant visas, and approximately one month or longer for DTV applications.[^643]

### Laos: Incorrect Payment Procedures and Processing Times

Thai Visa Centre's Vientiane and Savannakhet guides repeatedly instruct applicants to pay visa application fees online.[^644][^645]

However, neither diplomatic mission accepts online payments. Applicants must arrange payment in cash using Thai baht, either personally or through a representative.[^646][^647]

| Diplomatic mission | Payment hours |
|---|---|
| Vientiane | 09:00–12:00 |
| Savannakhet | 09:00–11:00 |

Both missions accept payments on weekdays, excluding official holidays.

The company's guides additionally advertise processing times of three to ten business days, whereas both diplomatic missions specify approximately five to ten working days beginning after payment.

The Vientiane guide also incorrectly presents legal residency as a general eligibility requirement, confusing physical presence with category-specific residency conditions. Revised DTV requirements effective August 31, 2026, separately require applicants to be nationals or permanent residents of the country of application.[^646]

### Cambodia: Incorrect Residency Requirements and Processing Times

Thai Visa Centre's Phnom Penh guide explicitly identifies legal residency in Cambodia as a prerequisite for applying for Thai visas, excluding ordinary foreign visitors.[^648]

However, the Royal Thai Embassy in Phnom Penh expressly acknowledges applications from foreigners traveling to Cambodia specifically to obtain Thai visas. Its nationality-specific instructions also establish procedures for processing applications from non-residents.[^649][^650]

The company's guide advertises tourist visa processing times of three to ten business days, while its separate visa-processing FAQ advertises an even shorter timeframe of two to five days.[^651]

The embassy specifies ten working days, excluding the submission date, with expedited processing reserved for documented medical or family emergencies.[^649]

The same Thai Visa Centre FAQ further contradicts official processing timelines in Laos and Malaysia, advertising one to three days for Vientiane and Savannakhet and three to seven days for Kuala Lumpur.

These discrepancies demonstrate that the company's inaccurate processing estimates extend across multiple publications rather than being confined to individual embassy guides.

### Commercial Significance of the Consular Guidance Errors

Thai Visa Centre claims to have verified official sources in June 2026 while advertising professional assistance with applications at these diplomatic missions.

Yet its published guides contain contradictory mission-selection instructions, incorrect payment procedures, overstated eligibility requirements, and understated processing times.

These errors can result in unnecessary travel, delayed applications, wasted non-refundable government fees, and avoidable commercial assistance costs.

**Thai Visa Centre presents itself as a specialist in preventing immigration application mistakes while repeatedly publishing those same mistakes in its own consular guides.**
